## 📚 Paper List

- **[Roz: A Taste Test for World Model Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00612)**  `arXiv:2611.00612`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Kenneth Ge, Dhruv Sheth, Kunvar Thaman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State of the art latent dynamics world models achieve impressive performance for robot planning. But do they actually encode the physics of the world?In this paper, we show that popular world models today achieve strong results on tasks that reduce to image similarity but forget key details about objects in the scene like mass, deformation, friction, and bounciness. We achieve this by defining precisely what ought to be encoded in such a world model and introducing two new evaluation methods that can directly tease physical properties and easily scale to new ones. Our evaluation, which we call Roz, includes 10 contrastive tasks and 3 continuous tasks, totaling 1734 individual scenarios. We validate our eval on control tasks. On our own model, AnonWM, we find that performance roughly improves with training steps, while not necessarily being correlated with reconstruction MSE.We argue current model failures are a symptom of evaluation strategies (probes and planners) that are opaque, model-specific, and high-compute. Roz requires only a model that can ingest still frames or video, a way to extract latents, and a distance function between them, and it can be run in one pass. We hope future evaluations will adopt Roz's evaluation techniques. More broadly, we hope Roz can be a case study for a future where benchmarks allow rapid iteration and comparison across models.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Build: Autonomous Robotic Assembly of Stable Structures Without Predefined Plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23934)**  `arXiv:2602.23934`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingwen Wang, Johannes Kirschner, Paul Rolland, Luis Salamanca, Stefana Parascho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a novel autonomous robotic assembly framework for constructing stable structures without relying on predefined architectural blueprints. Instead of following fixed plans, construction tasks are defined through targets and obstacles, allowing the system to adapt more flexibly to environmental uncertainty and variations during the building process. A reinforcement learning (RL) policy, trained using deep Q-learning with successor features, serves as the decision-making component. As a proof of concept, we evaluate the approach on a benchmark of 15 2D robotic assembly tasks of discrete block construction. Experiments using a real-world closed-loop robotic setup demonstrate the feasibility of the method and its ability to handle construction noise. The results suggest that our framework offers a promising direction for more adaptable and robust robotic construction in real-world environments.
  </details>

- **[PlanContext: Benchmarking In-Context Driving Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03067)**  `arXiv:2611.03067`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Ruikai Li, Naiyu Gao, Hao Shan, Yizhe Fan, Yanhao Wu, Han Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world driving requires context-aware decision-making. Existing end-to-end driving benchmarks primarily assess trajectory quality through safety, compliance, comfort, and driving progress, leaving context-dependent decision-making insufficiently characterized. To fill this gap, we present PlanContext, a benchmark for evaluating in-context driving decision-making. PlanContext constructs paired evaluations by varying high-level driving intentions while keeping the observed scene fixed, enabling a natural and controllable assessment of context-dependent decision-making. We further define six representative driving contexts and jointly evaluate contextual decision correctness, road compliance, and dynamic safety with class-balanced metrics. Experiments on 9,561 scene-condition instances from NAVSIM and 23 public end-to-end driving models show that high conventional driving-quality scores do not consistently correspond to strong route-level in-context driving decision-making. Further analysis shows that model behavior varies with decision timing, road structure, and traffic interaction depending on the decision required by the current context. Our results highlight in-context driving decision-making as an important complementary dimension for evaluating end-to-end driving systems.
  </details>

- **[Answering Without Arriving: An Evidence Audit for Embodied Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01958)**  `arXiv:2611.01958`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Shubh Garg, Divyansh Pandey, Niyati Rawal, Vedant Khandelwal, Amit Sheth_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied question answering asks an agent to navigate, observe, and then answer questions about what it saw, yet answer accuracy alone cannot tell whether any of this happened. A plausible answer can come from the question, the room label, or the answer choices, and a correct answer does not show that the supporting observation was ever made. We present a multi-room benchmark whose 200 test episodes and 800 questions are stratified by route extent, with an evaluation ladder that holds the questions fixed while varying only the evidence supplied to the answerer, from text-only probes through closed-loop agents to replay of the reference trajectory. Questions are generated from symbolic records of rooms, visible objects, and frames, so every answer can be traced to the observations that support it. A closed-loop vision-language agent reaches no target in 200 episodes yet answers 45.5% of questions, within 0.1 points of the same model given no images; replaying the reference trajectory raises the same model to 57.4%, so the evidence exists along the route but is not gathered. A family-level audit then shows that access to evidence is not the same as use of it. Four of eight families are largely answerable from text, one cites frames that cannot establish its own answer, and the oracle conditions reach 59.5% on Rooms-visited by counting the supplied images. Evidence acquisition, answer coverage, and shortcut sensitivity must therefore be established before an accuracy gain can be credited to memory.
  </details>

- **[PREM: Prediction-Regulated Memory for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00634)**  `arXiv:2611.00634`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Huang, Ya Zhang, Weidi Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot's next action can depend on recent motion or earlier visual evidence absent from its current observation. A recurrent visual-memory architecture can retain this history in a visual state, but its usefulness depends on what the state represents and how it is updated with new observations. We introduce Prediction-Regulated Memory (PREM), a framework that uses action-conditioned future prediction to guide both memory learning and updating in vision-language-action policies. During training, predicting future visual features provides temporal supervision for memory learning. During execution, PREM compares current visual features with the prediction made at the preceding decision and uses the spatially normalized prediction discrepancy to regulate updates to the visual state at each spatial location. The updated visual state supplies temporal context for action generation alongside current observations. Built on $\pi_{0.5}$, PREM improves average success rate from 9.63% to 20.74% on DOMINO dynamic manipulation tasks and from 14.4% to 83.8% on the RMBench M(1) memory-dependent setting. Further evaluations on LIBERO and LIBERO-Pro show improvements in general manipulation and robustness to spatial perturbations. PREM incurs a mean inference overhead of 7.7 ms on a single NVIDIA H800 GPU.
  </details>

- **[Pose accuracy is not grasp reliability: residual pose posteriors improve simulated robotic grasp planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13235)**  `arXiv:2609.13235`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Md Selim Sarowar, Sungho Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model-based robotic grasping estimates an object's six-dimensional (6D) pose and plans on its mesh as if that pose were exact. We show that this assumption reduces simulated grasp success even when the pose passes the standard accuracy test. We built LM-Grasp, a benchmark of 15{,}800 real LINEMOD RGB-D images with grasp outcomes simulated on scanned meshes. FoundationPose meets the ADD(-S) criterion (mean model-point error below 10\% of the object diameter) on 0.997 of test scenes, yet planning on its estimate succeeds in 0.791 of scenes, compared with 0.856 using the annotated pose. Because the estimator reports no uncertainty, we build a pose posterior from its own errors on annotated training frames, resampled from frames with similar observable fit, and score each grasp by its mean success across sampled poses and four equally weighted physics scenarios. Without retraining the estimator, posterior planning yields 0.859 (difference from the annotated-pose result +0.003; 95\% scene-wise paired-bootstrap interval [$-$0.003, +0.009]). This interval is compatible with no difference and a small shortfall; we did not conduct an equivalence test. For pose errors of 5 to 10\% of the diameter, simulated success rises from 0.796 to 0.984. Among test scenes assigned singleton-success conformal prediction sets, observed held-out precision is 0.972, compared with 0.904 for the point-estimate planner; these are empirical precision estimates, not conditional guarantees. On occluded LM-O scenes never used for tuning, posterior planning closes 66\% of the gap to the annotated pose.
  </details>

- **[CoEnv: Code-Driven Exploration in Real-to-Sim Digital Twins for Multi-Arm Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05484)**  `arXiv:2604.05484`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Li Kang, Yutao Fan, Rui Li, Heng Zhou, Yiran Qin, Zhemeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose coding agents can now write, debug, and refine entire robot programs, but only if they have somewhere to practice. Physical hardware is a poor practice ground: exploratory rollouts are slow to reset and, when several arms share a workspace, unsafe. Generic simulation benchmarks are safe but do not match the geometry, assets, or viewpoints of the workspace where the robot must actually work. We present CoEx (Code-driven Exploration), which lets the agent practice in an executable digital twin of its own deployment workspace. The twin is built by CoEnv, our real-to-sim framework, which reconstructs the physical scene from multi-view RGBD into a physics simulation. Inside it, a centralized code agent explores at the program level, writing a complete multi-arm program, running it in the twin, and refining it against grounded verification (privileged state checks, checkpoint predicates on risky steps, and adaptive viewpoints against inter-arm occlusion). Verified programs transfer to hardware through collision-checked deployment. On five real-world multi-arm tasks with up to three heterogeneous robots, CoEx reaches 70% average success, against 50% for the same system without the code agent (where a VLM must choose every primitive step by step), outperforms two prior VLM-planning frameworks, and produces verified episodes at 3.8-11.7x the ablated rate. Because every verified rollout is also a demonstration, exploration leaves reusable experience behind: a $\pi_{0.5}$ policy fine-tuned purely on 200 agent-generated rollouts reaches 100% and 70% real-robot success on two tasks, with no human demonstrations.
  </details>

- **[Tactile MNIST: Benchmarking Active Tactile Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06361)**  `arXiv:2506.06361`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tim Schneider, Guillaume Duret, Cristiana de Farias, Roberto Calandra, Liming Chen, Jan Peters_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile perception has the potential to significantly enhance dexterous robotic manipulation by providing rich local information that can complement or substitute for other sensory modalities such as vision. However, tactile sensing is an inherently local sensor modality, providing information only at the points of contact. This strict locality necessitates the use of active perception techniques to acquire useful, task-relevant information from manipulated objects or the environment in general. Hence, the agent must actively guide sensors toward regions with more informative or significant features and integrate this information over time to understand a scene or complete a task. While active perception and tactile sensing methods have received significant attention recently, both fields lack standardized benchmarks. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Tactile MNIST Benchmark Suite, an open-source, Gymnasium-compatible benchmark specifically designed for active tactile perception tasks, including classification, localization, pose estimation, and volume estimation. Our benchmark suite offers diverse simulation scenarios, from simple toy environments to complex tactile perception tasks using vision-based tactile sensors. Furthermore, we offer a comprehensive dataset comprising 13,580 synthetic 3D MNIST digit models and 153,600 real-world tactile samples collected from 600 3D-printed digits. Using this dataset, we train a CycleGAN for realistic tactile simulation rendering. By providing standardized protocols and reproducible evaluation frameworks, our benchmark suite facilitates systematic progress in tactile sensing and active perception more broadly.
  </details>

- **[Do Embodied VLMs Actually Use Their Scene-Graph Memory?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02056)**  `arXiv:2611.02056`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Shubh Garg, Divyansh Pandey, Vedant Khandelwal, Niyati Rawal, Amit Sheth_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents that answer questions about their route need a memory of what they saw, and scene graphs are a natural form for it. A vision-language model (VLM) can receive such a graph as text or through a graph encoder, but accuracy alone cannot show whether the graph holds the episode's facts, reaches the model, or is actually relied on. In this diagnostic study, we introduce a graph-swap test for the last of these: each episode is given another episode's graph, and we count how many answers change. On an embodied question-answering benchmark with routes through two to seven rooms, we use it to compare ways of delivering scene-graph memory to a fine-tuned VLM. As text, the memory is used strongly, adding 30 points on route questions. As a graph, it is not, and we trace why: the encoder preserves the information, but standard pooled and late fusion discard it. Giving each node its own input token recovers part of it, a gain of 8.3 points on route questions that the swap test confirms comes from the graph. The test also exposes a shortcut that accuracy hides: when the same facts appear in the text, the model ignores its graph. The test thus offers a cheap check of whether a VLM uses its scene-graph memory, and our analysis points to how that memory should be delivered.
  </details>

- **[OpenNWM: A Generalist Navigation World Model with Latent Action Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01844)**  `arXiv:2611.01844`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Dujun Nie, Ruiqi Song, Zixuan Wang, Hanqing Wang, Yifan Han, Baiyong Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Navigation world models predict future observations conditioned on navigation actions to support planning, but their generalization across real-world environments remains constrained by the limited coverage of action-labeled data. Action-free navigation videos provide diverse visual experience, yet lack the recorded actions required for action-conditioned training. We introduce OpenNWM, a generalist navigation world model with latent action pretraining. It learns universal latent actions from large-scale action-free videos through visual prediction pretraining, and further aligns them with physical motions via action-conditioned post-training. To support video pretraining, we curate NavAnywhere, a large-scale action-free navigation video dataset comprising 17.5 million visual observations from 15 heterogeneous sources. It captures navigation by ground robots, humans, and drones, with diverse viewpoints and motion patterns across five broad scene types: residential areas, public and commercial spaces, urban settings, parks, and natural landscapes. Experiments demonstrate improved visual prediction on in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, together with improved navigation performance. These results show that latent-action pretraining enables diverse, action-free videos to improve dynamics learning and generalization for open-world navigation.
  </details>

- **[Rollout then Recycle: 3,718 Labelled Real-Robot Episodes from Benchmarking 7 Policies on 12 Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03168)**  `arXiv:2611.03168`  `cs.RO`  
  _Praveen Selvaraj, Lorenzo Uttini, Ville Kuosmanen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We release all 3,718 episodes from the ArmnetBench v0.1 evaluation (arXiv:2607.24481), which contains 600 teleoperated demonstrations and 3,118 rollouts of seven imitation-learning and vision-language-action policies on 12 tabletop tasks, recorded on low-cost SO-101 arms; 73% of the rollouts are failures. Every policy for a task was trained or fine-tuned on the same 50 demonstrations and rolled out on the same cell, so the corpus contains many policies' failures on shared tasks. An operator labelled every rollout successful, suboptimal or failure. Each episode has three camera views, joint states and actions and is available on the Hugging Face Hub in LeRobot v3.0 and a reward-model format. This note documents how the episodes were collected and the limitations that could affect their use.
  </details>

- **[VLALagBench: Understanding When and Why VLA Inference Latency Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03103)**  `arXiv:2611.03103`  `cs.RO`  
  _Qi Wen, Borui Xu, Hussein Amro, Hongshi Tan, Hanmin Lin, Chaoyi Ruan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models offer a promising path toward general-purpose robot manipulation. While inference latency can affect task success in a constantly evolving physical world, the relationship between latency and task success rate remains poorly understood. In this paper, we introduce VLALagBench, a comprehensive benchmark for studying VLA inference latency sensitivity across task types, object speeds, execution modes (synchronous and asynchronous), and VLA policies. Using VLALagBench, we find that even a modest inference latency of 100 ms can nearly halve task success on moving targets, with sensitivity varying substantially across tasks and policies. These differences reflect task-specific requirements for precision and continuous motion. By overlapping inference with action execution, asynchronous execution can recover some of the lost success at the cost of more frequent inference requests. Building on these findings, we develop an empirical model that predicts task success from limited calibration data, achieving root-mean-square prediction errors of only 5.7-7.8% across various VLA policies. Given an inference system's latency and per-robot throughput, these predictions guide the selection of system-specific VLA algorithm configurations to maximize task success. We believe VLALagBench and these findings provide a solid foundation for future robotics algorithm-system co-design.
  </details>

- **[KlaskTron-IL: A Contact-Rich Adversarial Imitation Learning Benchmark with a Validated Sim-to-Real Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03091)**  `arXiv:2611.03091`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nicklas Sohm, Aswin Ramachandran, Raffaello D'Andrea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KlaskTron is an open-hardware physical platform for two-player adversarial reinforcement learning, with a learned actuator model that supports zero-shot sim-to-real transfer of trained policies. Building on this platform, we contribute KlaskTron-IL: a multi-tier imitation learning dataset (80k human demonstration timesteps collected directly on the physical robot, 320k symmetry-augmented timesteps, and synthetic expert trajectories up to 5M steps generated by the zero-shot-validated PPO policy), and diagnostic baselines that expose three characteristic failure modes of standard IL methods under contact-rich adversarial dynamics: BC's data scaling plateau, GAIL's interpretable but weak-gradient discriminator (Spearman $\rho = -1.0$ peg-ball proximity recovery, but a logit span under 0.1), and an asymmetric BC-prior x reward-density interaction. Because the policy interface used throughout this paper is the same one the actuator model bridges to hardware, a policy trained on this benchmark can run unchanged on the physical robot, as we demonstrate zero-shot for the strongest BC baseline, so long as its commands respect the actuator model's $\pm 0.2$ m/s identification range.
  </details>

- **[RESONATE: a Real Social Navigation Testbed for Autonomous Service Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02937)**  `arXiv:2611.02937`  `cs.RO`  
  _Stefano Trepella, Andrea Ostuni, Mauro Martini, Irene Borgini, Marcello Chiaberge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social navigation is essential for autonomous service robots operating in human environments, but progress remains constrained by the lack of extensive real-world benchmarks. This paper presents the Real Social Navigation Testbed (RESONATE), a reproducible benchmark for human-aware navigation based on a unified robotic platform, a shared ROS 2 software stack, precise trajectory ground truth, and structured social interaction scenarios. We evaluate eight controller configurations, combining classical and predictive local planners with different social modeling strategies, over eight representative scenarios with five repetitions each, yielding 320 physical experiments. The benchmark combines quantitative navigation and social metrics with participant-based qualitative assessments to jointly measure efficiency, safety, comfort, and perceived social behavior. Results reveal scenario-dependent trade-offs among the tested methods. The algorithms that stands out for their performance are the DWA with Gaussian social costmaps for its reliability, and the SFM-MPPI to provide the best balance between robustness, navigation efficiency, and social acceptance. Overall, RESONATE supports the robotics community with a real-world benchmark, reusable baselines, data, and testbed, and a consistent evaluation protocol for advancing socially aware navigation research.
  </details>

- **[DexArena: A Unified Benchmark for Long-Horizon Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02629)**  `arXiv:2611.02629`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haonan Ping, Jianshu Hu, Yimin Wang, Yi Zhu, Pai Liang, Qiyan Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon dexterous manipulation remains a fundamental challenge for robot learning, requiring the composition of multiple skills, precise bimanual coordination, generalizable visual understanding, and robust skill transitions. Yet existing manipulation benchmarks largely emphasize isolated skills or support only a limited range of end-effectors, making these capabilities difficult to evaluate systematically. We introduce DexArena, a benchmark designed to evaluate learning and generalization in long-horizon bimanual dexterous manipulation. DexArena features 10 long-horizon tasks and 30 stage-level tasks spanning diverse household and industrial scenarios. Each long-horizon task is composed of explicitly defined stages with physically grounded success conditions, enabling reliable evaluation of both intermediate progress and end-to-end task completion. A unified benchmark interface and verified human-teleoperated demonstrations enable testing different policy learning algorithms, with standardized simulation configurations, scene randomization, trajectory replay, and task-level evaluation. Through extensive baseline experiments, we identify substantial challenges in precise manipulation, generalization to scene variation, and skill composition. DexArena establishes a challenging and reproducible benchmark for developing robot learning methods that move toward generalizable long-horizon dexterous behavior.
  </details>

- **[Directional Bounded-Confidence Matrix Alignment in Vicsek Flocking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02189)**  `arXiv:2611.02189`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hossein B. Jond_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper proposes a directional bounded-confidence variant of the discrete-time Vicsek multiagent model, in which pairwise heading alignment is modulated by a state-dependent, positive semidefinite matrix that independently gates orthogonal radial and tangential components in the instantaneous line-of-sight frame. We analyze the normalized, matrix-weighted system dynamics, proving a uniform non-vanishing bound on proposal vectors that guarantees global well-posedness across arbitrary network states. For local small-spread states under complete metric interaction, we prove linear geometric contraction of the heading diameter and establish an offline-verifiable spatial bootstrap condition that guarantees metric contact preservation and coincidence avoidance a priori. A counterexample demonstrates invariant polarized states on connected matrix graphs, delineating systemic structural limits. Numerical experiments against unfiltered Vicsek averaging, scalar tied confidence, trace-matched scalar baselines, and restricted-angle rules demonstrate component-selective filtering without rejected-channel leakage, along with improved fixed-bound alignment on multi-seed benchmarks across varying population densities.
  </details>

- **[ClothDojo: Learned Data Generation and Benchmarking for Bimanual Garment Flattening and Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01190)**  `arXiv:2611.01190`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zilong Huang, Zipeng Ye, Caicheng Wang, Yuchen Xie, Jiaxiang Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Garment flattening and folding require robots to identify garment structure during manipulation and adapt their actions as the cloth deforms. We present ClothDojo, a simulation platform that generates adaptive manipulation trajectories at scale across diverse garments and initial states and evaluates policies in closed loop. ClothDojo encodes each garment's canonical mesh coordinates as an NOCS texture, providing canonical surface-position cues through the visual interface of a pretrained vision-language-action model. We fine-tune the model on human demonstrations and corrections to obtain a data-generation policy. The policy autonomously generates simulated manipulation trajectories, and we retain rollouts that satisfy task-specific success criteria. Each retained trajectory is re-rendered under a randomly sampled appearance to provide RGB observations for downstream policies that do not receive NOCS. Across both tasks, the policy produces nearly 8,000 successful trajectories over hundreds of garments. RGB policies trained on these generated trajectories outperform those trained directly on the original human demonstrations and corrections across all evaluated garment splits and appearance settings. Evaluations across multiple policy models show that overall performance and appearance robustness vary across models and tasks, while generalization to unseen garments remains a common challenge.
  </details>

- **[A Reproducible Reconstruction and Evaluation Framework for Photorealistic Lunar Analog Scene Reconstruction Toward Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00870)**  `arXiv:2611.00870`  `cs.RO`  
  _Antar Mazumder, Ryan Hartzell, Luke Boyd, Alexander Robinson, Neha Sharma, Chance Carnival, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we present a reconstruction pipeline that empirically quantifies the photorealistic scene reconstruction accuracy from both human-controlled and rover mast camera monocular feeds against physical ground truth as a function of Lunar traversal effects (e.g., motion blur, haze due to lofted regolith, trajectory bias, and sparse views). First, we benchmark six variants of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) across three Lunar analog scenes on held-out test sets using standard photometric metrics. We then establish how under traversal effects, this test-set-specific Novel View Synthesis (NVS) performance does not proportionally scale to the same photometric metrics in the reconstructed DTs. Our results quantify that splat training metrics alone are insufficient predictors of Cross-Reconstruction Photorealistic NVS Fidelity (CRPNVS), and that factors such as rover trajectory bias and geometric scene coverage play a critical role in determining the photorealistic quality of the reconstructed scenes targeting Lunar analog Digital Twins. Additionally, we pilot study Coverage Score ($S_c$), a geometry-based diagnostic derived from COLMAP poses and point cloud structure that provides a meaningful heuristic about the highest achievable CRPNVS structural fidelity, independently of splat training performance in the absence of a human-controlled reference. These findings establish a standardized, human control-referenced evaluation pipeline for photorealistic scene reconstruction for Lunar-analog Digital Twin development and future planetary exploration. Code and project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[MOST: Memory of Object States and Task Progress for Long-Horizon Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00734)**  `arXiv:2611.00734`  `cs.RO`  
  _Diqi He, Jinbang Guo, Yijun Shen, Zhiwei Yu, Huan Yang, Dingwen Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon embodied manipulation requires policies to act on information that is no longer visible. Modified objects may leave the field of view, while completed subtasks continue to constrain future actions. Existing approaches extend temporal context through recurrent state updates, test-time training, or storage and retrieval of past observations. As horizons grow, these mechanisms can still leave the policy to infer task state from long, redundant, and viewpoint-dependent visual experience. We argue that long-horizon agents benefit not merely from remembering more, but from remembering task-relevant state changes and progress. We propose Memory of Object States and Task Progress (MOST), a policy-internal memory framework that compresses multi-view observations into temporal tokens. Historical and predictive future tokens are explicitly supervised with object states and subtask progress, structuring memory around what has changed in the world and what has already been accomplished. The resulting memory directly conditions action generation within a single end-to-end policy, without an external planner or retrieval stage. We further introduce RoboCasa-Long, a benchmark of multi-minute, multi-stage mobile manipulation tasks. Across RoboCasa365, RoboCasa-Long, and real-world robot experiments, MOST consistently outperforms Xiaomi-Robotics-1 (XR-1), the previous state-of-the-art model. It raises average success from 65.9% to 72.1% on RoboCasa365, average maximum task progress from 20.83% to 42.22% on RoboCasa-Long, and real-world success from 6.66% to 43.33%. MOST also outperforms a matched visual-history memory variant, showing that effective long-horizon memory depends not only on how much experience is retained, but on whether it preserves decision-relevant progress and world-state changes.
  </details>

- **[TacMind: Benchmarking and Modeling Tactile Interaction States for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00455)**  `arXiv:2611.00455`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiaxin Shi, Zibin Wu, Yichi Zhang, Jianghua Duan, Siyu Zhu, Weihao Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tactile sensing provides robots with direct observations of contact physics, local geometry, and interaction dynamics, making it a vital source of feedback for contact-rich manipulation. However, existing tactile manipulation studies are often developed and evaluated within specific task settings, making it difficult to systematically compare how tactile information contributes under different interaction conditions. In this work, we develop a benchmark in simulation comprising 128 tasks organized around three complementary evaluation regimes: Precise, Recognition, and Dynamic. The benchmark further includes controlled static and dynamic task variants and 12 perturbation settings for evaluating robustness under changes in visual and physical conditions. These settings motivate studying tactile information as a temporally evolving interaction representation rather than only as an instantaneous observation. We therefore propose an action-conditioned latent predictive world model that integrates tactile history and learns a tactile state through prediction of future tactile latents. Building on this representation, we design a model that conditions action generation on the learned tactile state and uses predicted future tactile information to refine candidate actions through predictive tactile reflection. Experiments on the proposed benchmark show consistent gains from incorporating tactile information across the evaluated regimes. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Does Validation Loss Predict Real-Robot Success? Evidence from Four Imitation-Learning Policies on a Low-Cost Arm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00283)**  `arXiv:2611.00283`  `cs.RO`  
  _Simon Cowling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Held-out validation loss is the standard offline signal for choosing checkpoints and comparing models, but its relation to closed-loop success on a real robot is rarely measured. We measure it for ACT, Diffusion Policy, SmolVLA and $\pi_{0.5}$ (action expert only), trained on nested subsets of 12 to 200 demonstrations of a pick-and-place task on an SO-101 arm. We score 82 checkpoints on 25 held-out demonstrations, with each policy's training loss and with the error of the actions it outputs, and compare the scores with 930 bench rollouts. We also score all 700 intermediate checkpoints of 70 training runs, and recover the ball positions of every training, held-out and bench episode from the front camera. Within each policy, hold-out action error correlates with bench success (Spearman $\rho$ from -0.23 to -0.88), but no more strongly than the number of training episodes alone, and not at all among seeds trained on the same data. Across policies the ranking is inverted ($\rho$ = +0.90 at 200 episodes): the policy with the lowest hold-out error succeeded in 3 of 110 rollouts. The most common failure, the arm never leaving its rest pose, ended 96% of rollouts for Diffusion Policy trained on untrimmed demonstrations and 47% for SmolVLA, and cannot occur in a hold-out set made of demonstrations. Among rollouts in which the arm moved, SmolVLA's hold-out action error predicts success well ($\rho$ = -0.81). For the diffusion and flow-matching policies, hold-out loss rises two- to five-fold during training while hold-out action error falls by up to 29%. Held-out metrics are a sound diagnostic of learning but no substitute for evaluation on the robot, and we recommend reporting initiation failure and success among started rollouts as separate bench outcomes.
  </details>

- **[Anchor 1: Precise and Stable Humanoid Control for Whole-Body Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00193)**  `arXiv:2611.00193`  `cs.RO`  
  _Archon Robotics, Caojun Wang, Checheng Yu, Gangcheng Jiang, Guangjun Zeng, Haoran Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid controllers have acquired increasingly diverse motor skills, yet translating these capabilities into precise and stable whole-body manipulation remains challenging. Manipulation tasks are sensitive to execution deviations -- even small errors or unintended steps can cause failure. Introducing Anchor 1, a whole-body controller for precise and stable whole-body manipulation, together with a benchmark spanning diverse coordination demands. Anchor 1 adopts calibrated sparse motion references to preserve task-relevant human motion structure without online joint-space retargeting, leaving kinematic freedom for precise control. To guide this freedom towards stable, physically plausible whole-body coordination, we shape the training distribution through offline curation of robot motions paired with these calibrated sparse references. We further introduce explicit error feedback decoupled from motion encoding, improving control precision while supporting flexible command interfaces. To achieve a systematic evaluation of these capabilities, a benchmark is proposed to assess precision, sustained execution stability, and reachability across diverse whole-body coordination demands. Evaluations in simulation and on hardware show stronger precision and stability across all evaluation tracks. Under the most strict real-world global scoring setting, Anchor 1 achieves a 30\% to 80\% decrease in tracking error with fewer look-ahead inputs, and without retargeting compared to all existing controllers. Downstream experiments demonstrate more efficient teleoperated data collection and higher task success using the same deployment pipeline as well. We believe Anchor 1 supports precise and stable whole-body control as a practical basis for humanoid task execution.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Target-State Risk-Aligned Conditional Execution for Embodied Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41275)**  `arXiv:2610.41275`  `cs.RO`  
  _Mingyang Lyu, Gongce Cao, Yiyang Jia, Lu Jia, Erliang Lin, Yuhao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to follow a language-specified person under occlusion, look-alike distractors, and ambiguous instructions. We show that improving tracking persistence can simultaneously increase safety violations. Using a compact 0.6B vision-language-action (VLA) tracker with a frozen backbone, we update only the planner head with proximal policy optimization (PPO). PPO improves success and following rates across all three EVT-Bench settings, but increases the collision rate on distracted tracking because episodes that previously ended in target loss can instead end in contact. We introduce TRACE, an execution-time target-state risk gate that preserves the learned policy and intervenes selectively. A lightweight risk head processes 84 cues, including candidate boxes, identity margins, a learned range-and-bearing probe, and the policy's commands. When the calibrated risk exceeds a threshold, TRACE rescales the translational component of the next waypoint while leaving yaw unchanged. The executed action is identical to PPO's on more than 98% of steps. In paired evaluations on identical episodes, TRACE reduces the distracted-tracking collision rate by 3.96 percentage points, while further improving success and following rates. Matched slowdown controls without target-state timing do not reproduce this improvement. Concurrent evaluation reproduces a collision reduction of 3.77 points, compared with 0.29 points for identical-arm null pairs, indicating that the effect exceeds evaluation variability. These results show that target-state-aware execution can recover the safety cost of policy post-training with minimal intervention.
  </details>

- **[LOGIV: Graph-Verified Execution with Causal Local Repair for Long-Horizon Robot Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40016)**  `arXiv:2610.40016`  `cs.RO`  
  _Enqiao Lu, Yuxiang Xie, Xingrui Yu, Yuyang Wang, Yipeng Gu, Zhirui Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic robots must turn a high-level goal into actions while keeping the physical conditions needed for future steps valid. A completed action does not ensure that its effects will persist: a later disturbance can invalidate support that the remaining plan still assumes. We present LOGIV, a graph engineering framework for agentic robot execution that uses an explicit task graph to connect symbolic planning, observation-based verification, and embodied action. LOGIV compiles a validated plan into condition-support and resource-ordering edges, checks their physical validity during execution, and uses dependency tracing to determine what must be restored after a failure. Repair is constrained by retained unfinished actions, and the combined continuation is symbolically validated before execution resumes. The framework operates above existing Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Model (WAM) policies without adding trainable components. Across 20 dual-arm tasks from RoboTwin V2 and BiCoord with three execution backbones, LOGIV improves mean task success without injected disturbances by 10.67 and 15.33 percentage points, respectively. Under predefined perturbations, it achieves higher task completion and condition restoration than three adapted recovery baselines for every backbone on both benchmarks. On three real-robot tasks, successful trials increase from 12/30 to 17/30. These results support explicit, continuously checked task dependencies as a practical interface for reliable agentic robot execution.
  </details>

- **[Surrogate Uncertainty for Black-box Visual Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39798)**  `arXiv:2610.39798`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hanbo Zhang, Tianrun Hu, Cedar Site Bai, Anxing Xiao, David Hsu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual grounding is crucial for robots to understand language instructions with visual observations. This paper addresses the problem of visual grounding uncertainties, where a robot uses a black-box vision-language model (VLM) to ground free-form natural-language instructions to target objects. Prevailing methods derive uncertainty from the VLM itself. Yet, outputs of the VLM, e.g., generated uncertainties, are from the same parameters and structures producing the predictions, and inherit the bias. We give a formal Bayesian definition of the problem that treats the VLM prediction as an observation. An external surrogate Bayesian model then quantifies per-query uncertainty over a local neighborhood of a held-out calibration set, decomposed into aleatoric and epistemic components. A hierarchical retrieval across input modalities realizes a compact joint-space neighborhood, mitigating the curse of dimensionality. The estimator further integrates with conformal prediction or model-based planning for active visual grounding, where robots are allowed to take verbal or physical actions to ground targets. Across grounding benchmarks and state-of-the-art VLMs, it consistently improves both calibration and task performance. On a real Fetch robot under open-world instructions, it enables principled trade-offs between confident action and information gathering, substantially improving robustness.
  </details>

- **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252)**  `arXiv:2610.35252`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shitong Liu, Xu Yang, Jingwei Zhang, Sheng Lian, Xinyu Zhuang, Kaiwen Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.
  </details>

- **[LLM4Robot-Bench: How Far Can GPT-6 Astra Go in Robotic Manipulation? A Hierarchical, Matched Sim-Real Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34527)**  `arXiv:2610.34527`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haibao Yu, Jiaqi Meng, Zijian Zhang, Bowen Jing, Lei Yang, Yue Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce LLM4Robot-Bench, a hierarchical, matched Sim-Real benchmark for evaluating physical interaction capabilities in contact-rich manipulation. Its 16 household tasks span three cumulative levels: contact selection and geometric alignment (L1), force regulation within sustained contact (L2), and coordinated contact reconfiguration and force regulation (L3). Each task is instantiated in physically and visually matched simulated and real-world environments, with shared task definitions and success criteria. We evaluate GPT-6 Astra zero-shot with three trials per task in each environment. Real-world success rates decline from 80.0% at L1 to 47.6% at L2 and 0.0% at L3, with corresponding simulation rates of 66.7%, 47.6%, and 0.0%. Ten of the 16 tasks have identical success counts across environments; the remaining six differ by one success out of three trials. Across both environments, recurring difficulties in spatial reasoning, contact selection, force regulation, and coordination often prevent local physical progress from accumulating into task completion. These findings characterize Astra's observed capability boundary across increasing interaction demands and, for this model, support matched simulation as a repeatable, scalable testbed for studying the sustained, coordinated physical interactions that limit reliable manipulation.
  </details>

- **[RynnVLA-Latent: Scaling Robotic Manipulation Foundation Models with 3D-Grounded Latent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34356)**  `arXiv:2610.34356`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Xingyue Zhao, Zhian Su, Bohan Hou, Yinzhou Tang, Fengyi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling vision-language-action (VLA) models is constrained by scarce action-labeled demonstrations and incompatible control spaces across robot embodiments. We present RynnVLA-Latent, a framework that turns heterogeneous interaction videos into scalable supervision for robot policy pretraining. At its core, RynnLAM learns continuous, 3D-grounded latent actions from visual transitions without requiring native robot controls. By combining residual future-feature prediction, camera-compensated 3D-flow supervision, and camera-motion decoupling, RynnLAM encourages representations that capture control-relevant motion rather than appearance changes alone. We apply RynnLAM to 30.1K hours of human, robot, and simulation videos, producing 745.52M latent-action descriptors. RynnVLA-Latent then learns to predict latent-action chunks from visual observations and language through flow matching, before adapting its pretrained backbone and action expert to embodiment-specific robot actions. The resulting 4B policy achieves 98.8% success on LIBERO, 84.7% on LIBERO-Plus without benchmark-specific adaptation, 51.9% average success across five VLABench tracks, and 88.6% average success on the RoboTwin 2.0 bimanual benchmark. It also achieves the highest observed success rates among the compared methods on six real-world manipulation tasks across dexterous-hand and mobile-gripper platforms. Together with representation ablations and data-scaling results, these findings demonstrate the potential of latent-action supervision for scaling robot policy learning beyond native action-labeled data.
  </details>

- **[WasserMan: Benchmark for Underwater Manipulation Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04536)**  `arXiv:2610.04536`  `cs.RO`  
  _Danil Belov, Artem Erkhov, Sergei Parsegov, Pavel Osinenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underwater contact manipulation couples visual decisions with vehicle stabilization and mechanism interaction. To our knowledge, WasserMan is the first multi-task simulation benchmark focused on visuomotor policy learning for manipulating articulated underwater mechanisms. The suite supports demonstration-based policy learning, alternative vehicle--arm platforms and bimanual manipulation. We compare ACT, diffusion policies and behavioral cloning under matched sample budgets, and evaluate adaptation of a pretrained SmolVLA baseline. Fixed-policy studies vary water appearance, lighting, contact parameters, actuator delay, arm properties and mechanism pose. RGB feedback controls and matched observation-history and expert-trajectory ablations examine observation and training choices. Expert references establish task feasibility under the same physical success tests. The experiments reveal task-dependent visual transfer and pose sensitivity, while contact, tracking and actuator measurements distinguish execution responses that completion alone hides. Complete expert candidate streams make the initial states associated with demonstration selection inspectable. Versioned code, demonstrations, checkpoints and per-episode physical evidence support reproducible comparisons within the simulation.
  </details>

- **[LocalNav: Distilling Frontier VLMs and Embodied RL for On-Device Object Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27871)**  `arXiv:2606.27871`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nicolas Baumann, Liam Boyle, Pu Deng, Edoardo Ghignone, Boyang Sun, Marc Pollefeys, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision Language Models (VLMs) have emerged in the robotic domain as a powerful tool that enables environmental perception with language context, serving as a catalyst for open- vocabulary tasks like Object Goal Navigation (ObjectNav). Yet, their computational footprint typically confines them to cloud execution, hindering low-latency inference with local deployment on resource-constrained robots. To address this challenge, we present a distillation strategy that aligns a lightweight, 4B- parameter local VLM to our spatial-semantic navigation interface via frontier model traces for edge execution on embedded GPU devices (e.g., Jetson Orin). While cloud models establish a baseline achieving 41.61% Success Rate (SR) on the HM3D OVON benchmark, zero-shot deployment of our proposed architecture with Qwen3.5-4B reaches only 25.97% SR. We demonstrate that distilling frontier reasoning traces and subsequent finetuning with Embodied Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (E- RLVR) obtains a 37.36% SR, substantially narrowing the gap to large cloud models. However, while deployable on memory- constrained devices, the model cannot compete with round-trip cloud inference time: we identify Token Generation (TG) as the main driver of latency and reduce it with Token Generation (TG)- regularized E-RLVR. The combined method reduces latency by 69.29% and retains a SR of 35.53%. Combined with quantization, this joint strategy yields a cumulative 80.21% reduction in overall inference latency without significantly sacrificing performance, presenting a viable paradigm for local, low-latency VLM execu- tion on mobile robots.
  </details>

- **[ArthroCut: Autonomous Policy Learning for Robotic Bone Resection in Knee Arthroplasty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03957)**  `arXiv:2603.03957`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xu Lu, Yiling Zhang, Wenquan Cheng, Longfei Ma, Fang Chen, Hongen Liao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid commercialization of surgical robots, their autonomy and real-time decision-making remain limited in practice. To address this gap, we propose ArthroCut, an autonomous policy learning framework that upgrades knee arthroplasty robots from assistive execution to context-aware action generation. ArthroCut fine-tunes a Qwen--VL backbone on a self-built, time-synchronized multimodal dataset from 21 complete cases (23,205 RGB--D pairs), integrating preoperative CT/MR, intraoperative NDI tracking of bones and end effector, RGB--D surgical video, robot state, and textual intent. The method operates on two complementary token families -- Preoperative Imaging Tokens (PIT) to encode patient-specific anatomy and planned resection planes, and Time-Aligned Surgical Tokens (TAST) to fuse real-time visual, geometric, and kinematic evidence -- and emits an interpretable action grammar under grammar/safety-constrained decoding. In bench-top experiments on a knee prosthesis across seven trials, ArthroCut achieves an average success rate of 86% over the six standard resections, significantly outperforming strong baselines trained under the same protocol. Ablations show that TAST is the principal driver of reliability while PIT provides essential anatomical grounding, and their combination yields the most stable multi-plane execution. These results indicate that aligning preoperative geometry with time-aligned intraoperative perception and translating that alignment into tokenized, constrained actions is an effective path toward robust, interpretable autonomy in orthopedic robotic surgery.
  </details>

- **[MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01728)**  `arXiv:2611.01728`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.
  </details>

- **[Cloudra: Geometric Reasoning and Scientific Validation for Autonomous Reaction Mechanism Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02909)**  `arXiv:2611.02909`  `cs.MA`  
  _Chengyi Yang, Qianxin Zhou, Yang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous reaction-mechanism exploration requires proposing reaction steps, preparing three-dimensional structures, and determining which reactant and product a computed saddle point connects. Large language model (LLM) agents can misjudge spatial relationships and confuse a computed structure with the species or step they intended to study.We present Cloudra, a multi-agent framework that constrains mechanistic output through a Delivery DAG of chemical objects. Species nodes carry geometries and bonding topology; elementary-step edges carry transition states and reaction connections between species. Cheminformatics algorithms establish molecular identities and atom correspondences. Deterministic routines measure and transform structures to prepare candidate geometries. Frequency and IRC calculations determine the stationary points found and the species they connect. Agents use these objects to test proposed steps, compare pathways, and revise the mechanism.CloudraBench evaluates the framework's end-to-end mechanism-calculation capability across 50 tasks covering elementary reactions, multistep mechanisms, stereochemical competition, and computational method limitations. Across 200 runs under four workflow configurations, the primary Guided workflow achieves a mean composite score of 0.805, compared with 0.584 for the same-model Standard baseline. On Hard-tier tasks, the Guided mean increases from 0.689 under Standard to 0.958. Under Guided, the self-consistency component reaches its maximum score in 24 of the 40 non-Impossible tasks, compared with seven under Standard. Behavioral analyses examine how the agents use bond and dihedral measurements, optimized structures, and IRC endpoints to revise local mechanistic assignments.
  </details>

- **[Coati: Host-Grounded Execution for Installable Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02644)**  `arXiv:2611.02644`  `cs.MA`  
  _Yudou Ying, Pu Shi, Hanlei Shi, Yuhua Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile GUI agents can carry out tasks across apps, yet most run on a separate computer that drives a phone over the Android Debug Bridge (ADB), out of reach of ordinary users and of the apps through which most people reach Web services. We present Coati, a source-available agent that users install as an Android app. Its runtime on the phone, the host, records what a model alone cannot see: the effect of each action at its target, result screens across apps, and the progress of repeated steps. VerifyLoop checks action outcomes and completion claims against host evidence and reports a completion it cannot audit as unverified. AppMemory builds per-app procedural memory from observations that the interface supports and reuses it on later visits. A routed fast executor lets a lightweight selection model take simple, confident steps and lets the host repeat loops of simple steps that the large model plans once. On all 116 AndroidWorld tasks, with Gemini 3.8 Flash as every agent's backbone, Coati succeeds on 86.5% of the tasks over three trials, ahead of the strongest open-source agent Artemis (77.6% with the same accessibility-only access, 76.5% even when allowed ADB) and far ahead of FinalRun and Midscene (about 59%), and VerifyLoop cuts false completion claims by about a third at comparable success. In everyday use, the fast executor cuts the median time of phone tasks by 39% at comparable success and runs three of four loop tasks 2.8-5.4x faster with 6-9x fewer model tokens, while AppMemory shortens a repeated deep route in an unfamiliar app from 18.5 steps to 6-7. On an Android 16 phone, routed execution completes all 27 benchmark runs with 76% fewer Reasoning calls on the critical path. Host evidence, not model self-assessment, makes an installable agent reliable and fast.
  </details>

- **[Automated Stateful Specialization for Adaptive Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01870)**  `arXiv:2611.01870`  `cs.MA`  
  _Myan Vu, Harrish Ayyanar, Jiang Pang, Anwiketh Reddy, Mayank Goel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current automated agent design frameworks produce either static workflows that lack adaptability or per-query optimizers that prevent the accumulation of deep, agent-level task expertise. We propose a new direction that reconciles these paradigms: creating stateful teams of specialist agents that accumulate knowledge over time and can be reconfigured for novel tasks entirely without human intervention. To this end, we introduce ASpec, a framework that manages this full agent lifecycle by first autonomously discovering specialist archetypes via evolutionary search and then cultivating their expertise through experience, mirroring how human experts learn through practice and reflection. We further introduce a lightweight hierarchical control policy, "retain-then-escalate," which governs when to leverage the established agent system versus when to adapt its structure. Through comprehensive experiments, we demonstrate that this approach leads to significant performance gains on expert-level scientific benchmarks like GPQA while matching the state-of-the-art on broader domain tasks, demonstrating a promising path toward agent systems that are simultaneously expert, adaptive, and efficient. We will release the code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MAServeBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Serving Systems with Hierarchical Collaboration Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40293)**  `arXiv:2610.40293`  `cs.MA`  
  _Shengxuan Qiu, Haochen Huang, Shuzhang Zhong, Zizhuo Fu, Zebin Yang, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate specialized agents to solve complex tasks, turning each user request into interdependent model and tool operations. Unlike independent requests, these operations become ready at different times and consume evolving context as agents coordinate and exchange results. This creates changing patterns of concurrency, computation, and memory demand that can expose different bottlenecks in LLM serving hardware. Yet benchmarking these effects requires capturing the collaboration rules that generate serving workloads, beyond the requests observed during execution. We present MAServeBench, a benchmark that treats multi-agent collaboration as a configurable dimension of serving workloads for LLM serving and hardware evaluation. Its composable stages capture how agents coordinate and exchange context in documented MAS workflows, enabling native executions to generate different workloads even when task inputs and tools remain unchanged. Hierarchical Semantic Traces preserve realized operations and dependencies for reproducible fixed-work replay and as inputs to architecture simulators. Task-linked measurements connect these workloads to serving execution and GPU/NPU hardware behavior, revealing why serving efficiency does not always translate into timely task completion. The benchmark code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MAPLE: A Multimodal Mobile Agent with Playbook-Learned Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01351)**  `arXiv:2611.01351`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Jun Wang, Aurora Wei, Lucas Spangher, Ming Zhang, Eugene Ie, Yiannis Kantaros_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents powered by vision-language models (VLMs) show great potential in navigating mobile graphical user interfaces (GUIs) to complete complex tasks. However, existing paradigms face fundamental scalability bottlenecks: fine-tuning requires massive high-fidelity datasets or prohibitive training time and compute, while gradient-free approaches rely on custom static heuristics or costly trial-and-error trajectory exploration where feedback is deferred until the end of execution. To address these limitations, we introduce MAPLE (Multimodal Agent with Playbook-Learned Experience), a novel gradient-free multi-agent framework that enables dynamic, step-wise experience sharing and execution-time prompt evolution. Unlike traditional action-reflection architectures that depend on costly, post-hoc self-reflection, MAPLE enables agents to cross-reference and distribute runtime insights in real time at each operational step. This dynamic exchange eliminates blind trial-and-error looping and steers agents along direct, grounded interaction paths. Extensive evaluations on the AndroidWorld and MobileWorld benchmarks demonstrate that MAPLE achieves state-of-the-art performance on complex, long-horizon mobile tasks, while proving scalable and adaptable across novel environments.
  </details>

- **[Learnable Hodge-Laplacian Driven Mixture of Experts for Steady-PDE Surrogates on Coarse Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20585)**  `arXiv:2610.20585`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Stavros Nousias, Akis Nousias, Konstantinos Gkrispanis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current approaches for steady partial differential equations surrogate models on unstructured meshes apply a single mixing mechanism to the whole solution. This paper proposes an architecture that partitions the solution among vanilla transformer experts, each focused on a different spectral scale. The partition is built from a discrete Hodge Laplacian $L_0=\star_0^{-1} d_0^{\top}\star_1 d_0$, that serves as a fitting layer measuring high-frequency content. A gating mechanism takes the mesh as input and produces the per-vertex weights that recombine the expert outputs. AirFRANS is a steady Reynolds-averaged Navier--Stokes (RANS) airfoil benchmark. We evaluate our approach in a reduced remeshed version of the benchmark with each unstructured mesh counting 9.6K faces and 4.9K vertices. We compare our approach with the Transolver on the same dataset and dataset slit. Our approach reduces relative $L_2$ error by 31% and interior mean squared error by 47%.
  </details>

- **[CAPT: Class-Aware Prompt Tuning for Federated Long-Tailed Learning with Vision-Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.06993)**  `arXiv:2503.06993`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Shihao Hou, Chikai Shang, Jiacheng Yang, Xinyi Shang, Shreyank N. Gowda, Yan Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effectively addressing the joint effects of non-IID data and long-tailed distribution remains a key challenge in federated learning. While fine-tuning vision-language models (VLMs) like CLIP has shown promise in tackling non-IID scenarios, it often degrades tail-class performance when both heterogeneity and long-tailed distributions are present, even leading to overall performance falling below the zero-shot baseline. To overcome this, we propose Class-Aware Prompt Tuning (CAPT), a novel framework leveraging pre-trained VLMs to effectively learn from non-IID and long-tailed data. Specifically, CAPT introduces a dual-prompt mechanism that synergizes class-aware and general prompts, enabling the model to preserve class-specific information while capturing global trends. Furthermore, to guide the prompts in learning targeted knowledge, we propose a heterogeneity-aware clustering strategy with two key components: similarity-based clusters aggregate class-aware prompts to reinforce tail-specific knowledge, while heterogeneity-based clusters aggregate general prompts to balance diverse representations and promote robust generalization. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CAPT significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods, e.g., by improving overall accuracy by 6.1% on ImageNet-LT and 8.8% on Places-LT over baselines, while also substantially enhancing tail-class performance across other benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[MulTaBench: Benchmarking Multimodal Tabular Learning with Text and Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10616)**  `arXiv:2605.10616`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Alan Arazi, Eilam Shapira, Shoham Grunblat, Mor Ventura, Elad Hoffer, Gioia Blayer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular Foundation Models have recently established the state of the art in supervised tabular learning, by leveraging pretraining to learn generalizable representations of numerical and categorical structured data. However, they lack native support for unstructured modalities such as text and image, and rely on frozen, pretrained embeddings to process them. On established Multimodal Tabular Learning benchmarks, we show that tuning the embeddings to the task improves performance. Existing benchmarks, however, often focus on the mere co-occurrence of modalities; this leads to high variance across datasets and masks the benefits of task-specific tuning. To address this gap, we introduce MulTaBench, a benchmark of 40 datasets, split equally between image-tabular and text-tabular tasks. We focus on predictive tasks where the modalities provide complementary predictive signal, and where generic embeddings lose critical information, necessitating Target-Aware Representations that are aligned with the task. Our experimental results demonstrate that the gains from target-aware representation tuning generalize across both text and image modalities, several tabular learners, encoder scales, and embedding dimensions. MulTaBench constitutes the largest image-tabular benchmarking effort to date, spanning high-impact domains such as healthcare and e-commerce. It is designed to enable the research of novel architectures which incorporate joint modeling and target-aware representations, paving the way for the development of novel Multimodal Tabular Foundation Models.
  </details>

- **[ViAdminShift: A Vietnamese Benchmark for Event-Level Knowledge Updates Built from the 2025 Administrative Reform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03171)**  `arXiv:2611.03171`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Phan Binh Nguyen Lam, Nguyen Thai Anh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On July 1, 2025, Vietnam reduced its 63 provinces to 34, abolished its 696 districts, and replaced its 10,035 commune-level units (wards) with 3,321. ViAdminShift, a Vietnamese language resource about this event, consists of 7,995 benchmark items, 2,281 retrieval passages from pre- and post-reform Wikipedia revisions and dated templates, and old-to-new tables that a released script rebuilds from the official conversion table (not redistributed). Changed items store the pre-reform answer (except 121 address items), a $2\times2$ design uses untouched units as controls, and 400 addresses appear without diacritics. The tables agree with an independent lineage on 10,564 of 10,565 official pairs, disagreements were settled against the resolutions, and the probing-track scorer agrees with both authors' blind labels (Cohen's $\kappa=0.91$ and $0.89$). Data, outputs, code, and a datasheet are released. In the probing track, at least 20 of 67 models know the reform. Fourteen of them give the new province alone for at most 11% of plain questions about old addresses (old province only: 14-100%); a bare date recovers up to 100%. Post hoc (80 province-free addresses, single runs), with reasoning off, today's date in the system prompt leaves 68-100% old-province-only answers for 10 of 12 models; with reasoning, an "as of" prefix in the question leaves none for seven of eight. On the 1,262-item benchmark subset, with same-source old and new passages, small models unaware of the reform give 14-30% outdated answers and the five benchmark-track knowers (also differing in size or provider) 0-5%.
  </details>

- **[Replay for LLM Continual Learning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02746)**  `arXiv:2611.02746`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqiao Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) distills domain experts into one student but needs every expert at once. When domains arrive one at a time and earlier experts are gone, the previous-stage student can teach replayed prompts of earlier domains inside every stage. Some pipelines instead store every stage snapshot for a final multi-teacher recovery stage. On five domains with a 1.5B student and five public experts, this paper compares both with joint MOPD. It varies the replay budget and the replay signal. The signal is token-level distillation alone or with alignment of hidden states, attention and value relations. Sequential distillation without replay or recovery is 6.2 points below 208-step joint MOPD on a 14-benchmark average and 3.5 points below on the 11 benchmarks outside safety. Without snapshots, replay toward the previous student closes 3.8 of the 6.2 points when the earlier domains share the replay budget of a stage. With a fixed share of seven replay steps per earlier domain and stage, it closes 5.8 over two training runs. This is level with the 5.9 of recovery from all snapshots, both at its default length and when given as many training steps as the fixed share. Alignment terms on replay steps change the average by about one point or less. If refusal and compliance count equally in safety, shared-budget replay ranks first among the trained configurations, and the fixed share stays at or above recovery. Against the shared budget, the fixed share lowers the score on the newest domain. Recovery lowers this score as well, in five of six cases. The replay steps of the fixed share grow quadratically with the number of stages.
  </details>

- **[When Rewards Depend on the Group, GRPO Plays a Tournament](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02404)**  `arXiv:2611.02404`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Junyi Gao, Yu Shi, Pingzhao Hu, Ewen M Harrison_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rewards for diversity, coverage and distribution matching make the reward of each sampled response depend on the rest of its group, and they are now routinely optimized with GRPO and RLOO. We prove that in this setting the group-relative baseline changes the objective. The expected update equals each response's own reward gradient plus $\alpha$ times its effect on its groupmates' rewards, with $\alpha=-1/(K-1)$ for groups of size $K$, so GRPO trains every response to beat its groupmates in a zero-sum tournament. It cannot see a pairwise diversity bonus when K=2, sees less than half of it at any K, and often does worse than using no baseline at all. Setting $\alpha=+1$ gives Team-GRPO, an unbiased estimator for any group-dependent reward that works with two samples per prompt and requires no extra model calls for pairwise rewards. The theory predicts training quantitatively. It locates the fixed points of a 9B policy to within 0.02 in $\ell_1$ and predicts how concentrated trained policies become with Pearson r=0.995. On GlobalOpinionQA, GRPO collapses onto the majority answer and ends further from human opinion than the untrained model, while Team-GRPO cuts the error from $0.20$ to $0.08$. On NoveltyBench, Team-GRPO avoids the diversity collapse of GRPO and significantly improves set utility.
  </details>

- **[When Does Adaptive Halting Win? Selection Rules Matter in Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02333)**  `arXiv:2611.02333`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Mahesh Reddy Pagadala_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models can stop iterating early, and recent work shows that post-hoc readouts of the loop trajectory often rival the learned exit gate of the looped model Ouro. Such comparisons reduce each method to one operating point chosen by a selection rule. We ask whether that rule decides the outcome. On a frozen Ouro-1.4B with four loops, we compared a trained stability probe ($\pi_1$-x) with Ouro's native gate, adapted to a per-question stopping decision, in pre-registered studies; the confirmatory study was designed after an exploratory finding on development data and evaluated once on a sealed test set of 8,517 questions. In the pre-registered confirmatory comparison, which selected operating points with a bootstrap-screened 1 pp accuracy-loss rule, the probe saved more of the four-loop budget than the native gate (+0.053 gross macro saving over three benchmarks, 95% CI [+0.049, +0.057]), mostly from CommonsenseQA, where the probe's test upper bound on accuracy loss exceeded 1 pp. Under a point-only rule, a pre-specified secondary analysis on the same test set reversed the preference (-0.022 [-0.026, -0.018]), with operating points that exceeded the budget. In pre-specified secondary comparisons, the probe showed no demonstrated advantage over an untrained KL divergence between successive loops (+0.00125 [-0.0041, +0.0065]); we emphasise this result post hoc and claim no equivalence. The probe also broke its budget on one of two out-of-distribution benchmarks (3.26 pp loss on Social IQa). Savings are estimated from stored full-depth traces, not measured inference time, and our screen carries no formal risk-control guarantee. We conclude that halting methods should be compared as signals together with selection procedures, with strong untrained baselines and out-of-distribution checks.
  </details>

- **[Who Should Score the Cache? Mid-Depth Layers Are the Best Judges of KV-Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02230)**  `arXiv:2611.02230`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ekaterina Seregina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Key-value (KV) cache eviction methods rank tokens by attention scores, and each layer scores its own cache. To our knowledge, no eviction method asks which layer should score. We find, across twenty-one models, that the depth of the scoring layer governs eviction quality. On the eighteen models with per-layer caches, spanning 0.5B to 109B, long-context retrieval accuracy collapses at shallow layers, peaks between 0.4 and 0.75 of depth, and degrades at the last layer. The curve arises because a layer judges tokens well only once it has used the question to find the answer, which takes several layers. Modeling eviction as a top-$k$ cut, we compute answer survival probability from prefill attention and reproduce the measured curves. This approach selects the scoring layer using calibration prompts with known answers, and the selected layer is the best layer measured on most models and never significantly below it. It improves on the own-layer default on sixteen of the eighteen models, with significant gains on seven, all at 14B or below, while on larger models the default is already close to the best mid-depth layer. Scoring every cache from the selected layer while keeping one set of tokens for all heads also beats per-head SnapKV by 18 to 43 points on five models up to 14B under tight budgets. This gain carries over to financial filings at 32K context, all at the same memory budget, with no extra forward pass. The same curve explains a penalty in shared-cache models, where several layers read one cache and the layer that writes it scores by default. On Gemma-4-E2B the writer is shallow and loses 28 points to a mid-depth reader at a 10% budget. We also find that in open-book question answering (LongBench) no mid-depth layer beats the own-layer default.
  </details>

- **[When Should an Agent Refresh? Adaptive Index Reuse for Efficient Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01429)**  `arXiv:2611.01429`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Guangyuan Wang, Yifan Zhang, Shilong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language agents read code, tool output, and dialogue in contexts that grow at every step. Dynamic sparse attention lets a small indexer choose the earlier tokens each query attends to, but the indexer still scores the whole prefix at every layer. Cross-layer reuse methods such as IndexCache rerun the indexer at only some layers, with one fixed pattern for every input.
