In an agent, the relevant evidence can change abruptly when a traceback or test result arrives, so fresh selections matter more at some steps than at others. We propose Adaptive Index Reuse (AIR), which keeps IndexCache's execution and learns, for each segment of an agent episode, how many layers refresh their selection. A small controller reads signals from completed computation and the latest tool event, chooses a pattern from a nested library, and learns from terminal task reward under an expected compute budget. On Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 with a 128K context, AIR improves success over the strongest static schedule at matched cost on SWE-bench Verified (27.0% to 32.4%), Terminal-Bench 2.0, and three $\tau^2$-bench domains, and it lowers time per successful task on all five. Across 50 backbone, context, and benchmark settings, it matches or exceeds that schedule in 44.
  </details>

- **[Geometry of Confabulation and Refusal in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01272)**  `arXiv:2611.01272`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Vladimir Kazei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Faced with an unanswerable question, small language models can either refuse or confabulate: confidently produce a wrong answer. Can the hidden state before the first generated token provide a clue? Can we flip confabulations into refusals? I introduce ConfabQA, a 784-item benchmark whose obscure-pre-cutoff category breaks the typical correctness/knowledge-cutoff confound and map the geometry of the hidden state with linear probes, a sparse autoencoder (SAE), and direct optimization of intervention directions. On Qwen3-1.7B and Gemma 2 2B the probe separates upcoming refusals from confabulations well beyond prompt features (+7.4/+16.3 pp); on Llama 3.2 3B the ConfabQA margin is within fold-to-fold spread, but a direct refusal probe on PopQA and TriviaQA recovers +18-25 pp. On Qwen I closely inspect three candidate directions for the refusal vector: probe-recovered, Qwen-Scope SAE feature, and directly optimized. Although the three directions are pairwise 50-81 degrees apart, a push at or below the state's own norm along any of them flips most confabulations into refusal openings dominated by the "As..." family. Forcing the opener without touching the hidden state yields the same one-in-three judged-refusal rate as any push. The opposite push collapses refusals mostly into confabulations, yet leaves originally correct answers intact, so the direction gates the opening speech act rather than the knowledge behind it. Confabulations do not resolve into a concentrated feature in a dictionary or a token signature on Qwen3-1.7B. Scoring every probe on the margin over the strongest of four prompt-feature baselines extracts the hidden state's own contribution. Correctness signal is model-dependent and portable only for Llama 3.2 3B: 81% of the probe's margin over test majority survives transfer between ConfabQA, PopQA, and TriviaQA. Benchmark, judge, and code are released.
  </details>

- **[Two is better than one: A Collapse-free Multi-Reward RLIF Training Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22620)**  `arXiv:2605.22620`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Shourov Joarder, Diganta Sikdar, Ahsan Habib Akash, Binod Bhattarai, Prashnna Gyawali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved the reasoning ability of LLMs, but often depends on external supervision from human annotations or gold-standard solutions. Reinforcement learning from internal feedback (RLIF) has recently emerged as a scalable unsupervised alternative, using signals extracted from the model itself. However, existing RLIF methods typically rely on a single internal reward, which can lead to reward hacking, entropy collapse, and degraded reasoning structure. We propose a multi-reward RLIF framework that decomposes the training signal into two complementary components: an answer-level reward based on cluster voting and a completion-level reward based on token-wise self-certainty. To combine these signals robustly, we apply GDPO-based normalization to reduce reward-scale imbalance. We further introduce KL-Cov regularization, which targets low-entropy token distributions responsible for disproportionate entropy reduction, preserving exploration and preventing late-stage collapse. Across mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, our method improves stability and robustness over prior unsupervised RL approaches, while achieving performance close to supervised RLVR methods. These results show that complementary internal rewards, combined with targeted regularization, can support stable long-horizon reasoning without relying on external ground-truth supervision. Code will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301)**  `arXiv:2611.03301`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PerNodeDrop: A Method Balancing Specialized Subnets and Regularization in Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12663)**  `arXiv:2512.12663`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Gelesh G. Omathil_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep neural networks possess strong representational capacity yet remain vulnerable to overfitting, primarily because neurons tend to co-adapt in ways that, while capturing complex and fine-grained feature interactions, also reinforce spurious and non-generalizable patterns that inflate training performance but reduce reliability on unseen data. Noise-based regularizers such as Dropout and DropConnect address this issue by injecting stochastic perturbations during training, but the noise they apply is typically uniform across a layer or across a batch of samples, which can suppress both harmful and beneficial co-adaptation.This work introduces PerNodeDrop, a lightweight stochastic regularization method. It applies per-sample, per-node perturbations to break the uniformity of the noise injected by existing techniques, thereby allowing each node to experience input-specific variability. Hence, PerNodeDrop preserves useful co-adaptation while applying regularization. This narrows the gap between training and validation performance and improves reliability on unseen data, as evident from the experiments.Although superficially similar to DropConnect, PerNodeDrop operates at the sample level. It drops weights at the sample level, not the batch level. An expected-loss analysis formalizes how its perturbations attenuate excessive co-adaptation while retaining predictive interactions. Empirical evaluations on vision, text, and audio benchmarks indicate improved generalization relative to the standard noise-based regularizer.
  </details>

- **[Can Large Language Model Agents Learn by Watching Tutorial Videos?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01460)**  `arXiv:2611.01460`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Zafeirios Fountas, Jun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans continuously acquire knowledge from carefully crafted materials such as books and tutorial videos, carrying that understanding into future tasks. Millions of such instructional videos are uploaded online every day, yet LLM agents remain unable to exploit this ever-expanding wealth of human teaching. Their capabilities are frozen after training, while parameter updates at scale remain prohibitively expensive. This raises the central question: can LLM agents learn from this vast instructional resource without parameter updates? We introduce Tutorial Agentic Learning (TAL), a nonparametric continual-learning paradigm that consolidates tutorial videos into a persistent, structured tutorialbook memory through a two-stage adversarial process that harvests hard, video-grounded questions and uses them to refine the memory. At inference time, TAL retrieves through a Table-of-Contents (TOC) tree, tying retrieval directly to the model's reasoning rather than to embedding similarity. To study this setting, we introduce VidLearn, a multimodal QA benchmark built from 72 chess tutorial videos spanning 12 openings. On VidLearn, TAL outperforms all evaluated baselines and improves monotonically as more videos are consumed, achieving up to 113% and 91% relative gains over the corresponding vanilla MLLMs on the training and test splits, respectively. In actual gameplay, TAL reaches a stronger opening-phase position than its vanilla counterpart in 69% of head-to-head games. Evaluation in a second domain further demonstrates the generality of TAL. Together, these results highlight instructional videos as a scalable resource for parameter-update-free agent continual learning.
  </details>

- **[Hinted Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02270)**  `arXiv:2611.02270`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Suze van Adrichem, Luke Bailey, Rohith Kuditipudi, Christine Ye, Neil Band, Ahmed Ahmed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling laws predict the outcome of large, expensive training runs by extrapolating trends from smaller, cheaper runs. On downstream accuracy evaluations, these predictions fail when the weaker models produced by the cheaper runs score near zero accuracy, because these scores give no trend to extrapolate to larger scales. We introduce hinted scaling laws, which use privileged information to turn a benchmark into a continuum of easier versions so that even the small models score above zero. We find that accuracy rises smoothly and predictably with the hint level, enabling new types of scaling laws that operate below the noise threshold. On the Delphi pretraining ladder, a hinted scaling law fit on models trained with at most $10^{20}$ FLOPs forecasts the GSM8K accuracy of a $10^{23}$ FLOP model (25.9%) to within 3 percentage points; a standard scaling law under-forecasts it by 13 percentage points when fit on the same models and by 12 even with models up to $10^{22}$ FLOPs in the fit. Across both this pretraining ladder and a collection of post-trained model families, hinted scaling laws reduce the median forecasting error on GSM8K, AIME and HLE from 21 percentage points to 4.3. Hinting also reduces the number of test examples needed to tell whether one model is better than another by widening the accuracy gaps between models while approximately preserving their order: a single hinted rollout per problem ranks nearly indistinguishable models as accurately as five unhinted rollouts. Our results suggest that hinting is a general primitive for improving the accuracy and cost of downstream scaling laws and other prediction tasks in model development.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Scaling for Open-Ended Reasoning at Small Model Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02208)**  `arXiv:2611.02208`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Adam Y. Ahmed, Abdullah G. Elafifi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A vote over a model's samples can only help when the samples disagree. For open-ended generation the binding question is therefore not how to combine samples but whether a model produces samples worth combining, a question test-time scaling has answered for math but not for free-form text. We study it at small scale with semantic cluster voting, a judge-free aggregator that groups a small instruct model's samples by meaning and returns the largest cluster's representative, with no reward model or self-judging. On a 250-prompt benchmark with three tiers of answer checkability, the same pattern holds across three model families (Qwen2.5-3B, Llama-3.2-3B, Gemma-2-2B) and two generation seeds: voting raises accuracy on the math control, where samples vary but stay checkable (a gain of +10 to +29 points across the three models), yet adds at most +0.30 judge points on open-ended text. On a larger 200-problem MATH-500 run with the primary model, the math gain is +8.5 points (95% CI [+4.0, +13.0]). Almost every open-ended sample falls into one meaning cluster, so there is little to select over. The collapse is consistent with instruction-tuning rather than model size, and raising decoding temperature does not recover usable diversity, because it costs more quality than it adds (net -0.64 judge points, 95% CI [-0.93, -0.37]).
  </details>

- **[Evolving Memory, Learning to Meta-Control: Test-Time Adaptation for Experience-augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01747)**  `arXiv:2611.01747`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyuan Zhao, Yuanyuan Shi, Peng Yu, Lingyue Fu, Shao Zhang, Ying Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving agents accumulate experience from task streams, but existing frameworks typically rely on fixed pipelines to retrieve and utilize this experience, neglecting when and how to deploy it. We introduce Online Meta-Control of Experience (OMCE), a framework that updates experience memory and learns a meta-control policy from task feedback during test-time adaptation. OMCE employs a lightweight contextual-bandit meta-controller that uses compact retrieval signals to select among four cognitive actions: DIRECT, EPISODE, GUIDE, and DISTILL. This routing mechanism operationalizes three coupled decisions: whether to invoke experience, which memory format to utilize, and when to consolidate new guidance. OMCE evolves online, leveraging task feedback to update the memory bank and refine the routing policy via contextual bandit updates. Experiments on four online adaptation benchmarks across four backbones show that OMCE improves first-attempt success by up to 20.5 percentage points over the strongest compared baseline, while reducing per-task token use by a factor of 5.7 to 62.5 relative to ACE with GPT-5.2. Controlled ablations further support the benefits of learned routing and continued routing policy adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks vs. Multi-Layer Perceptrons: A Rigorous Statistical Benchmark on Clinical Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02856)**  `arXiv:2611.02856`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Rafał Wodziński, Paweł Szczerbiak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) have recently been proposed as a promising alternative to Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs). However, empirical evaluations on structured medical datasets remain scarce, and commonly reported metrics like AUROC can yield overly optimistic performance estimates on imbalanced clinical cohorts. In this study, we present a standardized benchmark comparing eleven architectures - nine KAN variants (FastKAN, WavKAN, ReLUKAN, ChebyKAN, TaylorKAN, GramKAN, JacobiKAN, LegendreKAN, and TabKAN), a tuned StandardMLP, and a Random Forest baseline - across seven heterogeneous medical tabular datasets spanning oncology, cardiology, nephrology, neurology, endocrinology, and obstetrics. Our experimental protocol employs a 3x5 Repeated Stratified K-Fold cross-validation scheme with nested Bayesian hyperparameter optimization and evaluates discrimination via Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), calibration through the Brier Score, and computational efficiency. Formal equivalence testing is conducted using the Bayesian Correlated t-Test with a Region of Practical Equivalence (ROPE) at +/- 0.01. The results indicate that on datasets with severe class imbalance, dedicated KAN architectures (particularly TabKAN and ReLUKAN) can improve minority-class detection and achieve higher AUPRC than standard MLPs. Nevertheless, across pooled datasets, KAN variants demonstrate comparable overall predictive discrimination rather than a systematic advantage, while requiring 1.20x to 1.48x longer inference latency per sample and up to 12.5x more trainable parameters. Consequently, StandardMLP remains a strong baseline on the efficiency Pareto frontier, while KAN variants provide a viable alternative when precision on rare positive cases is the primary clinical objective. The code for reproducing the results is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DIKT: A Diagnostic and Interpretable Knowledge Tracing Framework for Prediction and Model-based Learning Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02774)**  `arXiv:2611.02774`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi-Qiang Wua, Shaohui Chen, Hanlin Li, Baolin Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge tracing is a key component of intelligent educational systems. However, most existing methods mainly focus on response prediction, providing limited support for explaining predicted outcomes and improving students'learning states. This paper proposes Diagnostic and Interpretable Knowledge Tracing (DIKT), which integrates response prediction, skill-level diagnosis, and model-based learning analysis. DIKT generates bounded cognitive-state trajectories and decomposes the final response probability into skill-wise probabilities. The trajectories reveal students'evolving strengths and weaknesses, while the skill-wise probabilities identify the skills that most strongly constrain the prediction. Based on these diagnostic results, DIKT supports direct cognitive-state intervention and effective-learning-duration-based what-if analysis to examine how hypothetical adjustments affect current predictions and subsequent state evolution. DIKT combines Bayesian multi-head attention, conditional variational recurrent inference, a logistic-growth decoder, and multiplicative skill-wise prediction. Experiments on three benchmark datasets show that DIKT outperforms all evaluated baselines, with an average absolute AUC gain of 2.04 percentage points. Other experiments further demonstrate that DIKT supports educationally meaningful diagnostic analysis and provides informative diagnostic indicators for downstream applications, extending knowledge tracing from response prediction to interpretable diagnosis and model-based learning analysis.
  </details>

- **[Iterative Gumbel Planning for Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02236)**  `arXiv:2611.02236`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shaohuai Liu, Weirui Ye, Yilun Du, Le Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Search-based planning remains a powerful policy improvement paradigm in reinforcement learning, driving milestone successes in discrete domains. However, extending this paradigm to continuous control exposes a structural mismatch: search evaluates a finite set of action candidates, whereas continuous RL typically relies on Gaussian policies. We show that winner-action distillation into a continuous Gaussian induces a \textit{projection error} and a stepwise variance-expanding gradient tendency when the selected action is sufficiently far from the current mean. Empirically, this tendency is associated with elevated terminal variance and a return gap on the evaluated tasks. To address this, we propose \textbf{Iterative Gumbel Planning (IGP)}. By adopting a per-dimension factorized categorical policy, IGP naturally aligns the policy support with the search candidates, eliminating the projection error and reducing the residual approximation to a bounded discretization error. To bypass the combinatorial explosion of high-dimensional discrete action spaces, IGP pairs Gumbel Top-$K$ candidate sampling with an MPPI-style multi-round iterative search to refine the policy at each state. Empirically, IGP achieves state-of-the-art aggregate performance and sample efficiency on DMControl and HumanoidBench. Furthermore, it produces more stable action sequences with lower temporal variance, demonstrating its practical potential for real-world robotic deployment.
  </details>

- **[Discrimination, Calibration, and Ranking: A Unified Matrix Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02215)**  `arXiv:2611.02215`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ronaldo Cristiano Prati, Farangis Johari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrimination, calibration, and ranking are the three pillars of classifier evaluation, yet no single algebraic language spans all three. A recent indicator-matrix account unifies discrimination measures (precision, recall, F1, Matthews correlation) across binary, multiclass, multilabel and related settings, using Boolean cell operations on indicator label matrices, generalised to soft labels by t-norms. Calibration measures (Brier score, log-loss) and ranking measures (nDCG, AUC) fall outside it. We close the gap with two further classes of cell operators: deviation operators phi(y - yhat), which give a Calibration Framework, and rank- and slice-dependent operators (positional gains for DCG, column concordance cells for AUC), which give a Ranking Framework. Both inherit the micro/macro/exemplar aggregation architecture. For calibration, the three Brier aggregations collapse to one scalar; the Murphy decomposition holds in matrix form (unbinned, binned and isotonic); and, within the Brier-type class-indexed family, a measure is skew-invariant if and only if it depends only on the class-conditional errors alpha_j and beta_j, the calibration analogues of recall and specificity. For ranking, raw DCG collapses across aggregations while nDCG does not; per-class nDCG and per-class AUC are related by a row-column duality; micro-AUC differs from pooled ROC-AUC; raw positional scores are never strictly proper, and nDCG and AUC need not be proper. The skew-invariant per-class building blocks share one class-conditional expectation template. Experiments on two multilabel benchmarks illustrate the distinctions. Many of the results are machine-checked in Lean 4; the proofs, the experiments and thethis http URLpackage are public:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Silent Reward and Environment Defects in EV Charging Simulation Benchmarks: A Diagnostic Checklist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01769)**  `arXiv:2611.01769`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Nour-Eddine Moumni, Rachid Alaoui, Driss Kiouach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement-learning (RL) benchmarks built on microscopic traffic simulators such as SUMO are increasingly used to compare agent architectures for electric vehicle (EV) charging assignment. Unlike crashes, defects in the reward function, the environment's stochastic model, or the state encoding often fail silently: the simulation completes, produces plausible numbers, and the comparison is biased. We report five such defects, found by auditing a five-agent benchmark on OpenStreetMap road networks, stated generically: a shared random generator that desynchronises seed-paired comparisons, an ablation-specific reward clip applied unconditionally, a normalisation mismatch that zeroes a reward term, a per-timestep penalty that should fire once per event, and a state dimensionality documented but never wired into the network. We quantify three: a rejection counter inflated up to 125x across agents/cities, a reward term bounded at about $10^{-4}$ of its intended scale, and a 16.6%/16.3% greedy-action divergence from a state-encoding mismatch. Re-analysing a historical ablation, no A1-A2 effect survives family-wise correction; a 56-run campaign confirms the RNG defect's mechanism directly while showing its correction does not change that conclusion. We provide an eight-item checklist as a low-cost first-pass audit; in a later regeneration, three checks caught two new defects while three further process/artefact defects fell outside its boundary.
  </details>

- **[MatMoE: Matryoshka Mixture-of-Experts with Dynamic Mixed-Precision Quantization for Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01704)**  `arXiv:2611.01704`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Renjie Wei, Haochen Huang, Chuyu Qiu, Jinqi Wen, Dehao Xu, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has been widely adopted in large language models (LLMs) since it reduces the computational cost through sparse expert activation. However, the massive parameter scale of MoE models hinders their deployment on resource-constrained edge devices. Although existing mixed-precision quantization reduces the model size, they are inherently static, fixing the bit-width of each expert across decoding steps. Such static schemes yield suboptimal performance as they overlook the dynamic variation in expert importance during decoding. Furthermore, they fail to adapt to the dynamic latency constraint driven by the fluctuating system utilization. To address these challenges, we propose MatMoE, a dynamic mixed-precision quantization framework for efficient MoE inference. MatMoE features: (1) a Matryoshka MoE PTQ algorithm that enables experts to operate flexibly at multiple precisions within a stringent memory budget; (2) a floor-and-compensation quantization method that eliminates redundant memory accesses in the Matryoshka representation; and (3) a dynamic bit-width allocation strategy that determines the precision for each expert based on their routing scores to satisfy the dynamic latency requirement. We implement our MatMoE system atopthis http URLfor both CPU and GPU platforms. Extensive evaluations across three modern MoE models and challenging benchmarks demonstrate that MatMoE achieves $4.0\% \sim 18.3\%$ higher accuracy than state-of-the-art static mixed-precision method. Compared tothis http URL, MatMoE delivers $1.3\times \sim 3.7\times$ speedup on Raspberry Pi 5, and $1.2\times \sim 1.3\times$ speedup on NVIDIA RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Orin.
  </details>

- **[ERIA: An Instruction-Optimized Agent for Clinical EEG Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01394)**  `arXiv:2611.01394`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmed Sameh, Gayal Kuruppu, Neeraj Wagh, Bridger Brundy, Yogatheesan Varatharajah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical EEG interpretation requires integrating technical quality, background and behavioral states, interictal and ictal abnormalities, and overall clinical context across long recordings. We introduce ERIA (EEG Reporting and Instruction-Optimized Agent), a locally deployable framework in which a single 27B language-model controller analyzes whole-recording EEGs by orchestrating specialized parametric and signal-processing tools. ERIA combines learned tools for background abnormality, sleep-wake state, seizure-like activity, and interictal epileptiform discharges with deterministic spectral, amplitude, symmetry, montage, and signal-quality analyses. Rather than fine-tuning the controller or using multi-agent orchestration, ERIA keeps the controller weights, specialist tools, and safety constraints fixed and optimizes only its natural-language analysis policy with GEPA (Genetic-Pareto), using feedback from expert-written EEG reports. GEPA improves physician-aligned whole-recording report quality over the raw policy, and the optimized policy transfers to external EEG benchmarks. On CerebraGloss-Bench and BrainBench Foundational Analysis, ERIA remains competitive with the models evaluated in the original benchmarks. These results show that specialist tools and language-level policy optimization can extend the clinical and general EEG capabilities of a single locally executable model while preserving local inference and traceable tool use.
  </details>

- **[The Verifier Is Part of the Simulator: Evidence-View Gaming in Agentic Equation Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01334)**  `arXiv:2611.01334`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Orestis Oikonomou, Bogdan Raonic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A growing number of agentic pipelines search for governing equations: an agent proposes laws and a verifier checks them against simulated trajectories. The verifier sees only a numerical summary: integrals against test functions on a time grid. That summary carries its own discretization error, making the verifier part of the simulator. We freeze the trajectory, candidate library, verifier and thresholds, leaving the agent one move: which permitted summary to admit. Without scores or language models, finer time grids reduce numerical discrepancies and false library rejections. On PDEBench, the verifier instead accepts equations missing diffusion on score-selected summaries. Maximizing a public score rewarding decisive verdicts and larger mismatches when rejecting the library wrongly rejects all 1,500 in-library cases; retrospectively retuning score constants on a separate bank reduces errors to 13/180 at best. We call this evidence-view gaming: reward hacking by choice of permitted summary. In single-turn forced-choice tests, Claude models rarely choose summaries giving wrong verdicts when asked to be accurate, but often do so when asked to maximize the score or report missing physics. In separate trust prompts, they often rank those harmful summaries least trustworthy. Requiring several summaries to agree reduces errors but often withholds a verdict and performs worse than a fixed fine view on PDEBench: a safeguard, not a certificate. Evidence chosen after its verdict is known cannot alone authorize a claim.
  </details>

- **[Lightweight Representation Learning for Low Tucker Rank Tensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01168)**  `arXiv:2611.01168`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hiroki Hasegawa, Yukihiko Okada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low Tucker rank tensors provide compact representations of multidimensional dependencies, but standard learning pipelines can incur substantial computational and memory overhead from redundant intermediate quantities. We reduce this overhead without modifying the underlying representation objective or parameterization. Our approach combines three complementary components: (i) sequential extraction of only task-required spectral components, avoiding unnecessary full decompositions; (ii) factor-wise multilinear contractions without materializing large Kronecker operators; and (iii) compact latent-state updates without storing full ambient tensor iterates. These components preserve the algebraic operations and learning trajectories of the original algorithms while reducing their computational and memory footprints. Across five low Tucker rank tensor estimators, our implementations match the original trajectories to numerical precision (relative discrepancies of $10^{-16}$ to $10^{-13}$), while reducing per-iteration runtime by up to $3.12\times$ and resident memory by up to $616\times$ ($99.8\%$) on the main order-3 benchmark at $n=100$, with memory reductions exceeding $900\times$ across the full evaluation suite. These results show that substantial computational redundancy can be eliminated from Tucker representation learning without changing the underlying model or learned representation.
  </details>

- **[HieraNTP: Structured Risk Representations for Time-Series Anomaly Detection via Hierarchical Next-Token Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00945)**  `arXiv:2611.00945`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Li, Zezhi Shao, Boyu Diao, Zhulin An, Yongjun Xu, Xueqi Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series anomaly detection commonly converts deviations from learned temporal regularities into anomaly scores. This practice can conflate violations of temporal regularity with task-specific anomaly judgments: the same deviation may be anomalous under one task definition and benign under another. Aggregating heterogeneous deviations into a single score before task-specific adaptation can obscure the evidence needed to distinguish these cases. We introduce HieraNTP, a Hierarchical Next-Token Prediction framework that separates self-supervised predictive modeling from task-specific risk interpretation. HieraNTP pretrains a hierarchical tokenizer and a causal next-token predictor to capture temporal regularities at multiple scales. It then organizes prediction discrepancies into a structured risk representation, preserving complementary evidence across temporal scales in token, local-statistic, and observation spaces. A task-adaptive detector uses anomaly labels to select and combine this evidence for the target task. Across anomaly detection benchmarks, HieraNTP improves VUS-PR by 12.0% on TSB-AD-U and 15.5% on TSB-AD-M relative to the strongest baselines, and achieves the highest composite score on both TAB tracks, with controlled comparisons supporting the value of retaining distinct evidence. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MarkovTS: Pretrained State-Transition Representations for Zero-Shot Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00941)**  `arXiv:2611.00941`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Li, Zezhi Shao, Jingbo Zhou, Yongjun Xu, Xueqi Cheng, Fei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Across heterogeneous real-world domains, time-series generation supports simulation, model development, and data augmentation by generating sequences that preserve target-domain statistics and temporal dependencies when real observations are scarce or costly. However, existing methods predominantly address in-domain generation, limiting their reuse in unseen domains. We study reference-conditioned zero-shot time series generation: target domains are unseen during pretraining, generation uses a single target-domain reference sequence, and no model parameters are updated. MarkovTS characterizes sequence evolution through local morphologies and their ordered transitions at multiple temporal scales. Its pretrained encoder quantizes normalized time-series patches into multiscale morphological states with a state codebook, while a transition codebook encodes contextual transitions between adjacent intervals. These transferable state-transition representations condition a pretrained flow-matching model. At generation, the frozen model combines a reference-derived condition with independent Gaussian noise to produce stochastic samples that retain the reference's temporal organization. Across 12 unseen target datasets, MarkovTS outperforms strong baselines in most distributional comparisons and generates diverse, reference-consistent sequences. As downstream augmentation, its samples yield overall gains across forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection benchmarks and diverse model families. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Selecting LLM-Generated Hypotheses Raises Truth Beyond What an LLM Panel Expects: A Pre-registered Study on NAS-Bench-201 and HPOBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00643)**  `arXiv:2611.00643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shanglin Yang, Zhan Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM research-ideation systems aim for hypotheses that are both true and non-obvious, yet neither property can normally be checked. We build a testbed in which both can. Generators propose conditional interaction claims about two fully tabulated benchmarks, NAS-Bench-201 and HPOBench; every claim is verified exactly against the tables, and its non-obviousness is scored against a calibrated blind prior elicited from other LLMs. Excess truth measures how much more often a run's claims hold than this panel expects. Across a series of pre-registered stages with Claude and GPT generators, our main positive result is a simple two-stage step found by a harness optimizer: draft a large pool of claims, then select a subset in a separate call. For Sonnet on NAS-Bench-201, this step raised excess truth in every pre-registered test, including against a same-pool ablation that keeps only likely-true claims, without lowering precision. The gain comes both from credit for true claims and from avoiding near-miss false ones. In post hoc checks it held under every calibration, panel and truth rule we tried, and it exceeded random and type-filter selection from the same pools. Because kept claims recur across runs, the evidence weakens when runs and claims are resampled jointly, and it is not significant against Sonnet's own prior. Two secondary findings are also pre-registered. GPT makes real use of the results of cheap probes, beating shuffled and withheld controls, though not more efficiently than asking for more claims. Optimizing the harness for an LLM judge raised judge scores while sharply cutting precision for every generator.
  </details>

- **[On the Limits of Test-Time Compute for Self-Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00350)**  `arXiv:2611.00350`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Anirudh Buvanesh, Khaled Rouissi, Sujin Yun, Johan Obando-Ceron, Morgane M Moss, Samuel Lavoie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-verification and aggregation have emerged as popular approaches for bridging the gap between pass@1 and pass@N. However, increasing the number of candidates does not consistently narrow the gap between final-answer accuracy and pass@N. To understand this limitation, we use ranking error to measure how reliably models distinguish their correct generations from incorrect ones. We show that ranking error admits a bias-variance decomposition, with both components shaped by algorithmic design choices and inference compute. Across our experiments, additional compute reduces variance, while bias initially decreases before approaching a plateau. Guided by this analysis, we introduce Scorpio, a simple and general method that jointly scores randomly grouped candidates. Across math, code, and puzzle benchmarks, Scorpio improves verification, tree search, and aggregation. For verification, Scorpio improves accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 7 percentage points while matching or exceeding that baseline's peak accuracy with 17-42% lower inference cost. For tree search, using Scorpio to score partial solutions and select the final answer improves accuracy by up to 10 percentage points over prior methods. Finally, Scorpio improves the scaling of aggregation-based methods by 11 percentage points on coding tasks.
  </details>

- **[CIS-Transolver: A Cross-layer Incremental-State Neural Operator for Fine-Scale Automotive Aerodynamic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26337)**  `arXiv:2610.26337`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianglong Liu, Zhicheng Zhu, Jia Hao, Liangyue Jia, Wenxuan He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators offer an efficient data-driven approach to predicting complex automotive aerodynamic flows. However, spectral bias limits their ability to reconstruct fine-scale flow variations around geometric features such as side mirrors and wheel arches. Reliable assessment of local geometric changes therefore requires models that capture fine spatial detail together with global flow interactions. Inspired by iterative refinement and state evolution, we propose CIS-Transolver, a cross-layer incremental-state neural operator that integrates local multiscale information into global flow modeling. The method extracts local feature variations and encodes them as zeroth- to second-order geometric moments. These encodings are combined with information from earlier layers to form a cross-layer memory. Memory-derived corrections enter the value branch of Physics-Attention, allowing local details to inform global physical-state interactions throughout the network. We systematically evaluate CIS-Transolver on three public automotive aerodynamics benchmarks, ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, covering surface pressure fields and volumetric pressure and velocity fields. CIS-Transolver achieves the lowest mean relative L2 error among the evaluated methods for all five prediction metrics across the three benchmarks. Relative error reductions average 8.72% compared with the best baseline for each metric. Also achieves the lowest high-frequency spectral errors across all five metrics, with an average relative reduction of 16.06% compared with the best baseline. These results support cross-layer reuse of local information for fine-scale automotive flow prediction.
  </details>

- **[Drafting Agents, Not Just Tokens: Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16491)**  `arXiv:2610.16491`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xin Xu, Ke Cheng, Hao Wang, Yangru Huang, Yanghao Xiao, Zhichao Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving an agent through successive revisions requires balancing exploration and evaluation under a limited budget. A narrow search can miss useful sequences of edits, while fully evaluating every intermediate version often exhausts the budget before those sequences yield a verified improvement. Inspired by the draft--verify principle of speculative decoding, we propose Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement (SpecRSI), a two-level framework coupling inner-loop agent search with outer-loop improver learning. SpecRSI drafts successors before their predecessors are fully evaluated, while adopting only fully evaluated, admissible versions. In the inner loop, Verification-Aware Tree Search (VATS) allocates the budget between further edits and outstanding checks using the predicted verified return of a continuation. In the outer loop, Group Verification Optimization (GVO) updates the improver from completed searches while keeping the real evaluator fixed. We evaluate SpecRSI on scientific optimization (including active queue management (AQM) for network infrastructure), coding, and general agent-system benchmarks. Under matched evaluation-call budgets, SpecRSI improves scientific terminal gain over Janus by 60.0%--96.3% and area under the budget curve by 32.1%--91.6%, while achieving higher means across all 50 scientific backbone--task pairs and outperforming MetaRSI-v1 by 1.30--2.70 percentage points (pp) in system score across twenty backbones. On instance-level comparisons, it further improves PyBaMM terminal gain by 142.6% and SWE-Pro success by 6.0 pp at 7.0% and 9.2% lower token cost, respectively. These results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed approach.
  </details>

- **[Towards Continual Motion-Language Agents: LoRA Variants for Incremental Motion Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30266)**  `arXiv:2606.30266`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bertram Taetz, Hugo Albuquerque Cosme da Silva, Gabriele Bleser-Taetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motion-language agents must possess the bidirectional capability to both understand human movement (motion-to-text, M2T) and generate it from natural language (text-to-motion, T2M). While foundational models have achieved strong performance in static settings, autonomous agents operating in dynamic environments must continuously incorporate new motion concepts -- such as novel athletic styles or specialized gestures -- without catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired skills. We investigate the stability-plasticity trade-off in bidirectional motion-language learning under sequential task exposure. Building on a frozen large language model backbone, we introduce low-rank adaptation (LoRA) variants designed to mitigate inter-task interference. We specifically propose mixture-of-experts architectures that utilize an autoencoder-based router to select task-specific experts at inference time, so that no task-label is needed. To evaluate these methods, we establish a reproducible five-task benchmark derived from HumanML3D through semantic clustering of motion descriptions. Our experimental results demonstrate near-zero forgetting across both M2T and T2M directions while maintaining high generation and captioning quality. Furthermore, we show that hard expert selection via routing significantly outperforms soft expert blending in quality metrics, indicating that preserving expert isolation is critical for maintaining performance in our continual learning setting. Finally, we observe that a divergence between token-level accuracy and downstream generation quality may occur, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation protocols in future research on lifelong motion-language agents.
  </details>

- **[BESplit: Bias-Compensated Split Federated Learning with Evidential Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17508)**  `arXiv:2605.17508`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhan Xie, Chen Lyu, Jingrong Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Split Federated Learning (SFL) enables privacy-preserving collaborative training by partitioning models between clients and a server. However, under non-IID data distributions, SFL often suffers from biased optimization and unstable convergence, while existing solutions largely adapt techniques from conventional federated learning. In this work, we observe that the split architecture of SFL inherently alters how client information is represented and coordinated, opening opportunities for bias compensation beyond parameter-level aggregation. Based on this insight, we propose BESplit, an architecture-aware framework that exploits the intrinsic structure of SFL to mitigate non-IID effects. First, to prevent biased local data from dominating global updates, we introduce Evidential Aggregation (EA) to perform fine-grained reweighting of client contributions based on evidential uncertainty. Second, to further reduce distributional skew, we develop Bias-Compensated Collaboration (BCC) to align split-layer representations by pairing complementary clients. Finally, Dual-Teacher Distillation (DTD) is incorporated to synchronize knowledge between decoupled client and server models, enabling independent local inference. Extensive experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that BESplit consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in accuracy, convergence stability, and computational efficiency under diverse non-IID settings.
  </details>

- **[LEAD: Length-Efficient Adaptive and Dynamic Reasoning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09806)**  `arXiv:2605.09806`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Songtao Wei, Yi Li, Zhikai Li, Xu Hu, Yuede Ji, Guanpeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models, such as OpenAI o1 and DeepSeek-R1, tend to become increasingly verbose as their reasoning capabilities improve. These inflated Chain-of-Thought (CoT) trajectories often exceed what the underlying problems require, wasting compute, latency, and context budgets. While introducing length-based efficiency rewards during reinforcement learning offers a natural remedy, existing methods struggle with two fundamental challenges: the optimal balance between correctness and efficiency is non-stationary throughout training, and intrinsic reasoning budgets vary drastically across problems. Relying on static reward weights and global length constraints inevitably forces a compromise between degraded accuracy and unrealized compression. To overcome these limitations, we propose LEAD (Length-Efficient Adaptive and Dynamic reasoning), a method that replaces static heuristics with online, self-adaptive mechanisms. LEAD dynamically calibrates the correctness--efficiency trade-off at each step using a Potential-Scaled Instability, directing optimization capacity to the most informative learning signal. Furthermore, it estimates an adaptive per-problem target length online based on the model's own correct rollouts, applying a symmetric efficiency reward that penalizes both overthinking and over-compression. Evaluated on five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, LEAD achieves the highest accuracy and Accuracy-Efficiency Score among RL-trained efficient-reasoning methods in our matched comparisons while producing substantially shorter outputs than the base model.
  </details>

- **[Fooling Reward Models with Gibberish: Adversarial Examples Beyond the Semantic Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02686)**  `arXiv:2604.02686`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuheng Zhang, Mingyue Huo, Minghao Zhu, Mengxue Zhang, Nan Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models (RMs) are widely used as optimization targets in reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). We show that leading RMs can assign higher scores to gibberish than to meaningful reference answers. To systematically discover such inputs, we introduce Token Mapping Perturbation Attack (TOMPA), a framework that uses a perturbed token mapping to extend an attack LLM's search beyond natural language. The mapping changes the linguistic meaning of the attack LLM's natural language responses, producing non-linguistic text for the target RM to score. We then use these scalar scores as rewards to train the attack LLM through reinforcement learning to discover non-linguistic adversarial inputs. Experiments against Llama and Qwen RMs demonstrate such attacks using two instantiations: identity token mapping across different tokenizers and random permutation with a shared tokenizer. Against Skywork-Reward-V2-Llama-3.1-8B, ranked first on RewardBench 2, the two mappings achieve mean rewards of +33.64 and +43.78, respectively, compared with +17.48 for GPT-5 reference answers. Their attack success rates are 97.6% and 100%, measured as the percentages of individual responses scoring above their corresponding GPT-5 references. These results show that strong benchmark performance does not ensure robustness to non-linguistic adversarial inputs, exposing a fundamental vulnerability in using RMs as optimization targets.
  </details>

- **[StretchTime: Adaptive Time Series Forecasting via Symplectic Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08983)**  `arXiv:2602.08983`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yubin Kim, Viresh Pati, Jevon Twitty, Vinh Pham, Shihao Yang, Jiecheng Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer architectures have established strong baselines in time series forecasting, yet they typically rely on positional encodings that assume uniform, index-based temporal progression. However, real-world systems, from shifting financial cycles to elastic biological rhythms, frequently exhibit "time-warped" dynamics where the effective flow of time decouples from the sampling index. In this work, we first formalize this misalignment and prove that rotary position embedding (RoPE) is mathematically incapable of representing non-affine temporal warping. To address this, we propose Symplectic Positional Embeddings (SyPE), a learnable encoding framework derived from Hamiltonian mechanics. SyPE strictly generalizes RoPE by extending the rotation group $\mathrm{SO}(2)$ to the symplectic group $\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})$, modulated by a novel input-dependent adaptive warp module. By allowing the attention mechanism to adaptively dilate or contract temporal coordinates end-to-end, our approach captures locally varying periodicities without requiring pre-defined warping functions. We implement this mechanism in StretchTime, a multivariate forecasting architecture that achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks, demonstrating superior robustness on datasets exhibiting non-stationary temporal dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Learning Molecular Chirality via Chiral Determinant Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07415)**  `arXiv:2602.07415`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Runhan Shi, Zhicheng Zhang, Letian Chen, Gufeng Yu, Yang Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chirality is a fundamental molecular property that governs stereospecific behavior in chemistry and biology. Capturing chirality in machine learning models remains challenging due to the geometric complexity of stereochemical relationships and the limitations of traditional molecular representations that often lack explicit stereochemical encoding. Existing approaches to chiral molecular representation primarily focus on central chirality, relying on handcrafted stereochemical tags or limited 3D encodings, and thus fail to generalize to more complex forms such as axial chirality. In this work, we introduce ChiDeK (Chiral Determinant Kernels), a framework that systematically integrates stereogenic information into molecular representation learning. We propose the chiral determinant kernel to encode the SE(3)-invariant chirality product and employ cross-attention to integrate stereochemical information from local chiral centers into the global molecular representation. This design enables explicit modeling of chiral-related features within a unified architecture, capable of jointly encoding central and axial chirality. To support the evaluation of axial chirality, we construct a new benchmark for electronic circular dichroism (ECD) and optical rotation (OR) prediction. Across four tasks, including R/S configuration classification, enantiomer ranking, ECD spectrum prediction, and OR prediction, ChiDeK achieves substantial improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, most notably yielding over 7% higher accuracy on axially chiral tasks on average.
  </details>

- **[InTAct: Interval-based Task Activation Consolidation for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17439)**  `arXiv:2511.17439`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Patryk Krukowski, Jan Miksa, Piotr Helm, Jacek Tabor, Paweł Wawrzyński, Przemysław Spurek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning seeks to acquire new knowledge while preserving previously learned representations. Despite the success of prompt-based methods, they remain fragile in domain-incremental learning scenarios, where shifts in the input distribution cause representation drift in shared layers and lead to forgetting. We introduce InTAct, a method that preserves functional behavior in shared representations without freezing parameters or retaining past data. InTAct constrains updates within activation regions associated with prior domains, while allowing flexible adaptation elsewhere, thereby stabilizing neuron functionality rather than directly restricting parameter values. The approach is architecture-agnostic, integrates seamlessly with existing prompt-based frameworks, and matches or outperforms state-of-the-art methods on domain-incremental benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[DELTA: Robustly Training Label-Conditional Diffusion Models with Weak Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03016)**  `arXiv:2510.03016`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Dong-Dong Wu, Jiacheng Cui, Wei Wang, Zhiqiang Shen, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While label-conditional diffusion models have recently exhibited remarkable generative capabilities, their success heavily relies on massive, cleanly labeled datasets. In practice, categorical supervision is rarely perfect: it is often corrupted by noise, clouded by ambiguity, or partially missing. Training directly on such weak annotations severely degrades generation quality, and existing robust methods offer only fragmented solutions that depend on scarce auxiliary priors. To overcome these limitations, we propose DELTA, a unified framework that robustly trains Diffusion modELs across all three weak annoTAtion types. By treating the unknown true label as a latent variable, DELTA optimizes a principled variational objective that jointly recovers the clean data distribution and infers the true label posteriors. To make this joint training computationally tractable, we further introduce a median-centered timestep sampling strategy that efficiently concentrates estimation where the diffusion process is most informative. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that DELTA produces high-fidelity, class-consistent samples across diverse weak supervision scenarios, outperforming specialized baselines while demanding strictly less prior knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Towards General Continuous Memory for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17670)**  `arXiv:2505.17670`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenyi Wu, Zixuan Song, Kun Zhou, Yifei Shao, Zhiting Hu, Biwei Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models (LMs) and their extension, vision-language models (VLMs), have achieved remarkable performance across various tasks. However, they still struggle with complex reasoning tasks that require multimodal or multilingual real-world knowledge. To support such capabilities, an external memory system that can efficiently provide relevant multimodal information is essential. Existing approaches generally concatenate image and text tokens into a long sequence as memory, which, however, may drastically increase context length and even degrade performance. In contrast, we propose using continuous memory, a compact set of dense embeddings to more effectively and efficiently represent multimodal and multilingual knowledge. Our key insight is that a VLM can serve as its own continuous memory encoder. We empirically show that this design improves performance on complex multimodal reasoning tasks. Building on this, we introduce a data-efficient and parameter-efficient method to fine-tune the VLM into a memory encoder, requiring only 1.2% of the model's parameters and a small corpus of 15.6K self-synthesized samples. Our approach CoMEM utilizes VLM's original capabilities to encode arbitrary multimodal and multilingual knowledge into just 8 continuous embeddings. Since the inference-time VLM remains frozen, our memory module is plug-and-play and can be flexibly integrated as needed. Extensive experiments across eight multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[FADE: Factored Ability--Difficulty Filtering for Online RLVR Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03625)**  `arXiv:2611.03625`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tomas Duncun, Jonah Ellison, Mateo Ruiz, Hanae Fujita, Ingrid Solberg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) learns only from prompts whose sampled completions disagree: all-pass and all-fail groups produce zero relative advantage and burn generation budget without moving the policy. Adaptive schedulers try to steer rollouts toward informative prompts, yet they rely on per-prompt pass-rate trackers that entangle two quantities evolving at very different speeds---the policy's shifting competence across reasoning skills and each prompt's intrinsic difficulty. When a single exponential average or Beta counter tracks both at once, short memory scrambles the relative ordering of prompts whereas long memory reports stale pass rates. We introduce FADE (Factored Ability--Difficulty Estimation), a bilinear state-space filter that separates the two timescales. FADE represents each prompt's success logit as the inner product of a whitened $K$-dimensional skill loading with a fast-drifting policy ability vector, minus a slow-shrinking scalar item intercept. Using Pólya--Gamma augmentation, each minibatch of binary rollout outcomes updates the $K$-dimensional ability posterior in closed form via a rank-$K$ Woodbury step, instantly transferring competence shifts to all unobserved prompts that share those skills while leaving item intercepts stable. From the resulting posterior mean and variance we derive a closed-form utility for the probability of drawing a non-degenerate rollout group. Across five reasoning benchmarks, two base models, and four scheduling paradigms under a matched rollout budget, FADE consistently improves downstream accuracy and calibration while adding less than $0.6$\,ms per step.
  </details>

- **[DynaSolver: A Neural Operator for Autoregressive 4D Flow Prediction with Dynamic Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03595)**  `arXiv:2611.03595`  `cs.LG`  
  _Rui Qian, Shibo Dai, Mengjun Lin, Yuk-Kit Hou, Saining Zhang, Bohan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators provide efficient surrogates for computational fluid dynamics (CFD), but most are designed for static geometries and are not equipped to model unsteady flows induced by continuously moving boundaries. We study dynamic-boundary 4D CFD prediction for articulated moving geometries and introduce RoboCFD-4D, a temporally resolved robotic CFD benchmark spanning diverse morphologies and flow conditions. To address the computational and autoregressive challenges of this setting, we propose DynaSolver, a neural operator that explicitly conditions on boundary evolution and causal flow history. DynaSolver employs an efficient physical-token formulation that amortizes point-to-token projection across the operator stack, substantially reducing repeated point-space spatio-temporal computation while retaining accurate point-level reconstruction. We further introduce Replay-Augmented Parallel Training, which exposes the model to autoregressively generated histories while preserving parallel dense supervision, narrowing the gap between ground-truth-conditioned training and autoregressive rollout. Experiments on RoboCFD-4D show that DynaSolver achieves the best full-rollout accuracy among a broad range of learned CFD surrogates, with its advantage increasing as rollout proceeds, indicating substantially reduced autoregressive error accumulation. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Living Leaderboard for Graph Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03433)**  `arXiv:2611.03433`  `cs.LG`  
  _Martin Ritzert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph machine learning benchmarking results are scattered across papers that use quite different sets of datasets and baseline methods, making it difficult to tell whether a reported gain reflects architectural progress or merely an outdated comparison or weakly tuned baseline. We introduce a living, graph-learning-specific leaderboard that organizes reported results by dataset, putting new results into context. As of 15 October 2026 our LLM-based extraction pipeline extracted 27,723 eligible results across 111 datasets from 885 peer-reviewed papers. Our analysis of these results quantifies concerns about graph-learning benchmarking: legacy datasets are still actively used and many result tables contain baseline results that are drastically lower than those already available in the literature, e.g. 45% Amazon-ratings tables and 37% for Peptides-struct. The leaderboard allows to quickly compare new results against compatible prior results, aiming to improve the overall quality of graph learning benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Island Sparse Autoencoders for Interpreting Clinical Time-Series Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03420)**  `arXiv:2611.03420`  `cs.LG`  
  _Muhan Yeo, Bong Gyun Kang, HyunGi Kim, Sungroh Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for electronic health records achieve strong predictive performance but provide little insight into what clinical concepts they represent internally. Post-hoc attribution methods produce per-cell importance scores that lack temporal coherence, cross-variable structure, and reusability across patients. We introduce the Temporal Island Sparse Autoencoder (TI-SAE), which learns a backbone-specific, patient-shared clinical concept dictionary from a frozen predictive model's representations. Each dictionary entry (latent) is defined by learned temporal Gaussian islands (specifying when the concept is active) and a sparse variable-composition vector (specifying which variables are involved). A decoupled selector then identifies which dictionary entries drive each patient's prediction, separating representation learning from attribution. We evaluate TI-SAE across five backbone architectures spanning different representation strategies on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV (three prediction tasks each). TI-SAE produces structured clinical concepts that go beyond transient attribution heatmaps while achieving strong performance on standard faithfulness benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Low-Rank Clebsch--Gordan Coupling for Equivariant Spherical Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03411)**  `arXiv:2611.03411`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yaojun Li, Zhixuan Zhao, Dongzhe Zheng, Christine Allen-Blanchette_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spherical-harmonic neural operators provide an efficient representation for global forecasting, but their discretized pointwise nonlinearities can break rotational equivariance through aliasing. Clebsch-Gordan (CG) tensor products provide exactly equivariant interactions between spherical-harmonic representations, but their computational cost grows rapidly with spectral bandwidth and has limited their use at forecasting scale. We introduce LCG-SFNO, which combines low-rank CG interactions within a controlled low-degree band with coefficient-space residual propagation and an exactly projected quadratic nonlinearity, preserving SO(3) equivariance throughout the learned operator. To make the CG interaction practical, we further develop SSHCG, a sparse GPU implementation with a factorized alternative for wider coupled bands. On spherical shallow-water dynamics and the ERA5-based WeatherBench benchmark, LCG-SFNO reduces rotational error to numerical precision (more than three orders of magnitude below SFNO on shallow water and five orders of magnitude below it on WeatherBench), while matching SFNO on shallow-water forecasting and substantially improving forecast skill relative to SFNO on WeatherBench. Matched ablations show that CG coupling accelerates convergence, while SSHCG keeps the coupling to about 10 percent of a training step at the operating bandwidth, making exact equivariant coupling practical at forecasting scale.
  </details>

- **[Certifying Graph Neural Networks Against Label and Structure Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03350)**  `arXiv:2611.03350`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lukas Gosch, Xichuan Chen, Yan Scholten, Stephan Günnemann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust machine learning for graph-structured data has made significant progress against test-time attacks, yet certified robustness to poisoning - where adversaries manipulate the training data - remains largely underexplored. For image data, state-of-the-art poisoning certificates rely on partitioning-and-aggregation schemes. However, we show that these methods fail when applied in the graph domain due to the inherent label and structure sparsity found in common graph datasets, making effective graph-partitioning difficult. To address this challenge, we propose a novel semi-supervised learning framework called deep Self-Training Graph Partition Aggregation (ST-GPA), which enriches each graph partition with informative pseudo-labels and synthetic edges, enabling effective certification against node-label and graph-structure poisoning under sparse conditions. Our method is architecture-agnostic, scales to large numbers of partitions, and consistently and significantly improves robustness guarantees against both label and structure poisoning across multiple benchmarks, while maintaining strong clean accuracy. Overall, our results establish a promising direction for certifiably robust learning on graph-structured data against poisoning under sparse conditions.
  </details>

- **[ENGINE: Endogenous Variational MultiScale Optimization for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03293)**  `arXiv:2611.03293`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuoli Ouyang, Changxi Chi, Siyuan Li, Tailin Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient large language model (LLM) fine-tuning, and recent methods incorporate curvature-aware strategies. However, in the high-dimensional parameter space of LLMs, these methods inherently suffer from exploding gradient variance and prohibitive costs. Conversely, confining updates to a low-dimensional subspace reduces variance but discards critical high-dimensional information, degrading performance. This creates a fundamental dilemma: full-space optimization is informative but has high variance, while subspace optimization has low variance but loses information. To break this trade-off, we propose ENGINE (Endogenous Variational MultiScale). Inspired by computational mechanics, we adapt static Variational MultiScale (VMS) theory to dynamic optimization, treating low- and high-dimensional spaces as resolved coarse and unresolved fine scales. Through a multigrid-style V-cycle, ENGINE alternates between spaces, dynamically probing fine-grained information in the high-dimensional space and injecting it to correct the efficient low-dimensional sampling process. Under appropriate assumptions, statistical analysis characterizes variance reduction and correction of curvature-induced bias, and convergence analysis establishes a stationarity bound for the corrected coarse updates. Evaluations on standard LLM benchmarks show that ENGINE achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than existing ZO methods, achieving up to a $2.61\times$ speed-up in forward passes with zero additional peak memory overhead. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Cost of Mismatch: Noise Amplification in Zeroth-Order Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03260)**  `arXiv:2611.03260`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lianmin Chen, Junbin Qiu, Chenxing Wei, Yao Shu, Kun He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization has emerged as a promising approach for memory-efficient large language model (LLM) post-training, yet its reliable application to reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) remains largely unexplored. Motivated by the distinct data regimes of on-policy RL and fixed-data optimization, we develop a generic variance-theoretic framework to investigate what limits the reliability of ZO gradient estimation. Our analysis reveals that estimation reliability is structurally determined by whether the two finite-difference evaluations are performed on matched data. On-policy reinforcement learning (RL) under the mismatched-data regime produces a $1/\mu^2$ oracle-noise amplification that sets an irreducible noise floor as the perturbation scale $\mu$ decreases, whereas Boltzmann Targeted SFT (BOLT) under the matched-data regime eliminates this amplification via noise cancellation. We further derive convergence guarantees with explicit constants that cleanly separate the two regimes. These results naturally lead to ZO-BOLT, which applies ZO optimization to BOLT's RLVR-aligned weighted-SFT objective. Controlled perturbation-scale sweeps and matched-versus-mismatched end-to-end comparisons validate the predicted effect of data matching. Experiments across three model variants and two mathematical reasoning benchmarks further show that ZO-BOLT consistently improves over the pretrained baseline while retaining a near-inference memory footprint. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CISA: Retrofitting Dense Attention Models with Chunk-Indexed Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03200)**  `arXiv:2611.03200`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammed Dabbah, Izhak Golan, Oren Tropp, Daniel Afrimi, Vladimir Anisimov, Tomer Asida, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context LLM inference is costly because attention repeatedly reads a growing key-value (KV) history. We introduce Chunk-Indexed Sparse Attention (CISA), which retrofits pretrained dense attention models without gradient updates. CISA selects fixed-size KV chunks using compact summaries of existing keys, avoiding token-level indexing and a separate full-length index cache. It uses one indexing query per KV group and shares selected chunks across queries in that group. During prefill, QShare reuses selections across adjacent queries to reduce indexing cost and improve KV reuse and GPU utilization. We evaluate CISA on NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Nano and Ultra, and Inkling-Small. In one-layer BF16 benchmarks at batch size 32 using Ultra's per-device attention dimensions, core attention is 11.7x faster for prefill queries near the end of a 1M-token prompt and 13.0x faster during decode. Including chunk scoring, selection, and all other measured GPU work, speedups are 8.6x and 3.0x. On Ultra NVFP4 with a 4,096-chunk budget, aggregate scores stay within 1.5 percentage points of dense attention on MRCR, LongBench v2, and GraphWalks. RULER sweeps on Nano and Ultra BF16 up to one million tokens show scores within 2.05 percentage points of dense attention with 8-16x fewer KV reads. Serving million-token prompts with 32 concurrent requests and 4K output tokens per request, CISA achieves 2.29x the end-to-end throughput of dense attention using Nemotron 3 Ultra NVFP4 on four NVIDIA B300 GPUs. Selecting fewer chunks increases throughput to 2.61x; in separate GraphWalks evaluations, the smaller budget widens the aggregate score gap from 1.49 to 3.59 percentage points. These results expose a practical quality-speed tradeoff without sparse training.
  </details>

- **[Optimal Subgroup Discovery at Every Support Threshold](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03053)**  `arXiv:2611.03053`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lincen Yang, Qi Huang, Niki van Stein, Thomas Bäck, Matthijs van Leeuwen, Zhong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Subgroup discovery aims to identify regions of the feature space where a target variable deviates from its marginal distribution. A fundamental challenge in this problem is the trade-off between a subgroup's deviation and its support: small subgroups can be highly deviating but statistically meaningless, while large ones cannot deviate much by construction. Existing methods tackle this challenge by collapsing the two into a single scalar quality measure, implicitly committing to one particular trade-off and making it difficult to recover the most deviating subgroup under a user-specified support constraint. We instead provide the first theoretical and structural characterization of optimal subgroups with maximum Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence under arbitrary support constraints. Under a partition-based generative model, where the feature space decomposes into regions of homogeneous conditional distributions of the target variable called atoms, we prove that every point on the deviation-support Pareto front is a union of whole atoms together with at most one fractional atom. This reduces an uncountable search to a finite combinatorial problem. Guided by this theory, we propose a simple two-step recipe: learn a homogeneous partition, then select atoms greedily. Across 19 benchmark datasets, it finds the most deviating subgroups at a range of support thresholds, and reliably improves subgroups returned by existing methods when applied as a post-processing step. These results empirically validate the sufficiency and necessity of our theoretical contributions.
  </details>

- **[TSB-SEG: A Systematic Time-Series Segmentation Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03039)**  `arXiv:2611.03039`  `cs.LG`  
  _Félix Chavelli, Arik Ermshaus, Fan Yang, Patrick Schäfer, John Paparrizos, Paul Boniol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series segmentation, studied as either Change Point Detection (CPD) or State Detection (SD), underpins a broad range of monitoring and diagnostic applications. However, the field suffers from a persistent divide between the statistical community (focusing on CPD) and the machine learning and data mining communities (focusing on SD). Consequently, the literature remains fragmented: evaluations typically cover narrow methodological families, datasets are often small or homogeneous, and CPD and SD are rarely evaluated together. We address these gaps with TSB-SEG, a unified benchmark comprising 592 univariate and multivariate time series from 8 heterogeneous datasets and surveying 26 algorithms that span five decades of progress. To ensure a fair comparison, we introduce a two-pool design: a scalable main pool of tractable detectors used for the aggregate ranking, and an extended-scope pool for computationally intensive methods. All methods are unified under tsseg, an open-source library to ensure full reproducibility. Our systematic evaluation yields three insights: (a) no single detector dominates across domains, and per-dataset hyperparameter tuning remains decisive for performance, (b) CPD accuracy is a useful, though imperfect, proxy for SD accuracy, and (c) data characteristics should guide method selection. We provide actionable guidance based on data properties, error tolerances, and tuning budgets, identify impactful hyperparameters, and characterize algorithm robustness to these choices.
  </details>

- **[ILR-SMO: Iterative Latent Refinement for Robust Spatial Multi-Omics Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02932)**  `arXiv:2611.02932`  `cs.LG`  
  _Anqi Yu, Xudong Xu, Jianzhi Lu, Yuqi Sun, Bingguo Chen, Chenxi Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial multi-omics technologies jointly profile diverse molecular modalities with spatial context, providing a comprehensive view of cellular heterogeneity and tissue organization. To integrate spatial multi-omics data and identify spatial domains, a wide range of unsupervised methods has been proposed. However, recent approaches rely on single-step fusion, directly aggregating heterogeneous modalities into a shared representation, making embeddings sensitive to modality imbalance and measurement noise that are inherent to spatial multi-omics data, as well as unstable optimization under weak supervision. Here, we propose ILR-SMO (Iterative Latent Refinement for Spatial Multi-Omics), a unified self-supervised framework that reformulates multimodal integration as a stability-aware refinement process over a shared latent representation. Instead of single-step plain fusion, ILR-SMO progressively integrates modality-specific information through sequential updates, allowing information to be injected in a controlled manner. This incremental refinement enables later updates to correct or compensate for earlier deviations, resulting in more stable and robust representation learning under heterogeneous and noisy modalities. ILR-SMO further incorporates reliability-aware modality gating for adaptive modulation of modality contributions, and employs a joint objective to enforce spatial coherence while preventing representation collapse. Extensive experiments on five spatial multi-omics benchmarks demonstrate that ILR-SMO consistently outperforms seven state-of-the-art methods and exhibits strong robustness across diverse settings.
  </details>

- **[Identifiable Signed Causal Learning via Latent Stochastic Differential Equations for Inferring Brain Dynamic Effective Connectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02876)**  `arXiv:2611.02876`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yiding Wang, Chen Qiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
