  Understanding how brain regions interact to support cognitive tasks requires characterizing directed causal influences among them, known as effective connectivity (EC). As inter-regional coupling changes over time, a dynamic EC (dEC) estimate is required to track this temporal variation. Existing dEC learning methods typically lack a causal rationality guarantee or an identifiability analysis, and output unsigned connectivity that cannot distinguish excitatory from inhibitory influences, which limits further study of brain mechanisms. We propose ISCL, a latent stochastic differential equation framework that derives dEC from latent dynamics through a causal influence operator defined in observation space. As established by a rationality theorem, the operator captures a directed interventional response at each brain state, and under invariance assumptions the operator is identifiable from data, with its signed output separating excitatory from inhibitory influences. On synthetic benchmarks, \model recovers the causal structure on Lorenz96 and achieves the lowest mean structural Hamming distance among compared methods on NetSim, while preserving signed influence structures that distinguish excitatory from inhibitory interactions. Applied to HCP Social Cognition task-fMRI, ISCL reveals a signed architecture whose stability across seeds supports the identifiability guarantee, identifies dominant source and target networks consistent with social-cognition systems, and shows that condition-specific training strengthens causal outflow from the default-mode and frontoparietal networks during theory-of-mind processing. In this task, the recovered signed structure reveals network-level excitatory and inhibitory organization, with the dorsal-attention network as the only consistently positive out-strength source.
  </details>

- **[NIR: Approximate Neural Instance Retrieval for ALCIQ](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02842)**  `arXiv:2611.02842`  `cs.LG`  
  _N'Dah Jean Kouagou, Pritilata Saha, Caglar Demir, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instance retrieval is the main bottleneck of concept-learning systems based on refinement operators. We present NIR, a family of neural instance retrievers for ALCIQ that output graded membership scores from knowledge graph embeddings. We compare tokenization-based encoders (GRU, LSTM, Transformer) with a Compositional encoder that mirrors DL operators via primitive sub-networks. On eight benchmarks, all variants approximate Pellet closely on complete knowledge bases, while the Transformer model is most robust under incompleteness. The source code of our approach is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SpecPilot: Autonomous Algorithm Discovery and Systems Optimization for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02501)**  `arXiv:2611.02501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kunjun Li, Wenhao Chai, Yihao Liang, Yuezhou Hu, Shuyao Xu, Yi Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates memory-bound LLM decoding with a lightweight drafter, and parallel drafters extend this speedup by drafting a whole block in one forward pass. However, it remains unclear whether their released systems (stock) attain the full speedup that parallel drafting permits. We identify three challenges. First, with one request, each step accepts few tokens but reads every target weight. Second, with batching, per-request verification work roughly halves the speedup over autoregressive (AR) decoding. Third, speculative decoding is deployment-specific, and no universal optimization fits every serving regime, drafter, target model and GPU. To address these challenges, we present SpecPilot, an autonomous agentic loop that discovers algorithms and systems optimizations for each deployment. In each round, a cost model locates the largest gaps between the current system and its ceiling. Parallel agents explore directions and integrate the successful ones. Token forcing checks every output token against stock to detect reward hacking. A structured memory carries validated mechanisms to new rounds and deployments. SpecPilot discovered a general lossless 12-bit weight format, tree verification for hybrid recurrent targets, two-level verification and three batching mechanisms. We evaluate it across four target models, three drafters, two GPU platforms and five benchmarks. Without training or quality loss, it raises the speedup over AR from stock's 4.46$\times$ to 10.87$\times$ with one request, and from 1.94$\times$ to 3.93$\times$ with batching.
  </details>

- **[Architecture-Aware Mixed Precision for Replica-Exchange Training of Geometry-Aware Neural Operators with Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02427)**  `arXiv:2611.02427`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ujin Jeon, Michael Penwarden, Wenqian Chen, Guang Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry-aware neural operators face substantial computational and memory demands when learning across complex physical domains. We introduce Architecture-Aware Mixed Precision (AA-MP), a replica-exchange training strategy that allocates numerical precision according to replica roles and architecture-specific operations. By retaining FP32 for refinement while selectively employing BF16 for exploration, AA-MP preserves predictive accuracy while improving training efficiency. Across fluid dynamics and three-dimensional structural mechanics benchmarks, AA-MP achieves comparable accuracy to full-precision training over an ensemble of runs, while reducing hot-replica peak GPU memory by 46.9% and training time by up to 25.8%. It also outperforms naive Automatic Mixed Precision (AMP) in memory efficiency and computational cost, with increasing memory advantages at larger model and geometry scales. Ablation studies show that retaining FP32 in the cold-replica is important for predictive accuracy, while additional FP32 operations in the exploratory replica provide limited benefits relative to their computational cost. Finally, conformal prediction for uncertainty calibration shows that AA-MP achieves near-nominal coverage with narrower prediction intervals than the alternatives. These results advance the scalability of geometry-aware scientific machine learning by reducing computational costs without compromising predictive accuracy or uncertainty quantification.
  </details>

- **[Behavior-Discriminative Reward Shaping for Reward-Robust Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02415)**  `arXiv:2611.02415`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zixuan Liu, Fangzheng Wu, Brian Summa, Zizhan Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward-robust RL typically models misspecification by specifying an uncertainty set that constrains the discrepancy of plausible rewards from a base reward. However, such reward space discrepancies can be behaviorally irrelevant: under potential-based reward shaping (PBRS), many distinct rewards preserve policy ordering. This can introduce substantial redundancy into standard uncertainty sets and degrade optimization performance. We propose Shaping-Aware Reward-Robust RL, which constructs uncertainty sets over PBRS equivalence classes by projecting each reward to a canonical representative, ensuring that the resulting set contains only rewards that induce behaviorally distinct policy rankings. We prove that this projection preserves the optimal robust value while shrinking the uncertainty set and improving empirical performance. Using the connection between robustness and regularization, we obtain a practical algorithm to solve the shaping-aware reward-robust RL problem and enjoy convergence guarantees under standard assumptions. Experiments on benchmarks spanning diverse task domains and levels of complexity show consistent improvements over representative robust RL baselines and exhibit improved robustness to reward perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Agent Memory as Rate-Limited Filtering: Explicit State Outlasts Long Context Beyond a Reader-Dependent Horizon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02361)**  `arXiv:2611.02361`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianhao Guo, Chongyao Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a language model that reads the full history of an access log say whether a service may act now? On an executable benchmark of grants, withdrawals, reinstatements, expiries, groups and change windows under a known policy, scored on pairs of histories that differ only in the order or one field of the deciding events, open models of 7 to 32 billion parameters fall from 72 to 86 percent after eight or sixteen events to 48 to 55 percent after 256, while reasoning-trained 32B models reading the current state reach 96 and 98 percent. A reasoning model of 120 billion parameters trails the state by four and seven points at 256 and 512 events and by about ten at 1,024, so the length beyond which explicit state outlasts long context grows with the reader. We treat agent memory as recursive filtering. The decision depends on the history only through a state whose size is set by the number of tracked keys. A store holding fewer component records than the state forgets on a time scale set by its capacity, and a reader model predicts its accuracy within 0.02 on average in a registered test. Retaining log lines without regard to time order errs with probability approaching one half, a bound that is attained. A filter in which a model parses each line and an explicit rule applies the updates answers 80 to 89 percent at 256 events and beats relevance retrieval, model-kept ledgers, a reimplementation of StateMem and the Mem0 library. A retriever that selects at query time the lines the queried state depends on matches it at 256 events on the first domain, so what decides is selection by the state's dependencies, which the filter performs when writing, without the query. Applying parsed updates themselves, the models fall to 58 and 59 percent. A frozen replication on a second domain with fresh wording reproduces the horizon.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Unit-Circle Eigenvalues: Invariant Bases for Stable State Space Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02174)**  `arXiv:2611.02174`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiaqian Zhu, Yang Zhang, Junhua Ding, Xiaowei Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diagonal state space models process long sequences at linear cost, yet the same transition is applied at every step of a rollout, so an unconstrained recurrence holds no quantity fixed and the state decays or grows without bound. Current designs constrain one quantity, and preserving one does not guarantee preservation of the others, so a model can hold its norm to machine precision while momentum drifts by hundreds of percent. We claim that the conserved quantities of a diagonal recurrence are layered, that each layer requires a strictly stronger condition on the operator, and that a unit-modulus spectrum reaches only the outermost. Acting on the claim, we enforce the conditions required for every layer by construction. The generator of the Kronecker-Cayley transition (KC) is a graph Laplacian over object subspaces, $\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{L}\otimes I_m$, advanced by a Cayley transform, and the step stays diagonal at $O(N^3+N^2m)$ cost. The conserved set is exactly a $2m^2$-dimensional family of quadratic forms, held at every parameter value, so the quantities and the dynamics the construction reaches are fixed before training. On four conservation benchmarks, KC holds total mass at or near the reference floor while every diagonal baseline loses it by orders of magnitude. We further compare against mass correction and find that two correctors fail once the underlying prediction diverges, which shows that assembly rather than correction is necessary for a guarantee that holds across the rollout. A free modal basis trained under a conservation penalty conserves only by freezing its spectrum, whereas the assembled operator conserves with every mode but one free to rotate. Beyond these benchmarks, KC holds the linear momentum of three $N$-body systems and, at the operator level, the conserved quantity in molecular dynamics, chemical kinetics, pharmacokinetics and power-grid swing dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Fragility Is Not Reducible to Hardness: Conditional Causal Difficulty for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02065)**  `arXiv:2611.02065`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Reza Rezaei, Dingxian Wang, Nikhil Verma, Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Andrew Rabinovich, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models show large and seemingly arbitrary performance drops when a task is rewritten in ways that leave its requirements untouched. We recast task difficulty as a causal quantity: the treatment effect of meaning-preserving perturbations on a model. A meaning-preserving rewrite can only be observed to hurt a task the model can already solve, so we place the estimand on the principal stratum where the degradation occurs: the conditional causal difficulty (CCD), the probability that a randomly drawn twin breaks a solvable task. Because harder tasks tend to be more phrasing-sensitive, complexity enters as an effect modifier rather than a confounder, and we residualize it out to obtain complexity-residualized difficulty (CRD); zero for a task exactly as fragile as its complexity predicts, positive for anomalously fragile ones. Current benchmarks do not supply the data CCD requires, so we introduce GlassGSM and GlassMATH, augmenting GSM8K and MATH with twins built from perturbation families, raising fragility on GlassGSM by $\approx 12\times$ over GSM-IC-style distractors on the same problems ($3.7\%\to44.9\%$) while keeping the answer key unchanged. Our predictor Frail learns the per-perturbation break probability and reaches $0.613{\pm}0.023$ of the suite reliability ceiling on GlassGSM and $0.557{\pm}0.023$ on GlassMATH, against $0.171$/$0.401$ for a surface-feature baseline and $0.444$/$0.463$ for supervised baselines such as VAP3, and, at a $5\%$ curation budget on GlassGSM, doubles random recall of fragile items, matching an oracle ranking. Additionally, cross-fitted residualization leaves CRD uncorrelated with the declared proxy in practice.Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[MIRA: Mutual Information guided calibration for Reliable Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02003)**  `arXiv:2611.02003`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hyeongyu Kim, Mingyeong Jang, Youngjun Song, GeonHui Han, Dosik Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time adaptation (TTA) updates a deployed model using only unlabeled target data, but standard entropy-based methods often rely on deterministic confidence and can reinforce overconfident errors under distribution shift. We propose MIRA, Mutual Information guided calibration for Reliable Test-Time Adaptation, a source-free online adaptation method that uses lightweight last-layer Gaussian perturbations to probe prediction reliability. For each target batch, MIRA reuses a single feature extraction pass and samples perturbed classifiers around the source classifier, forming an efficient local predictive distribution. Rather than fitting a Bayesian posterior, MIRA treats the perturbation scale as a local probing radius and selects a batch-specific scale using mutual information (MI) and prediction agreement. The resulting perturbation-sensitivity signal identifies predictions that are confident and locally stable under classifier perturbations, while suppressing brittle predictions with high stochastic disagreement. MIRA then performs adaptation with an MI-confidence weighted entropy loss, which down-weights perturbation-sensitive or low-confidence samples before they drive entropy minimization. This weighted loss reduces the influence of brittle predictions that can otherwise cause harmful adaptation under distribution shift. The method requires no source data, retraining, model ensembles, or offline posterior fitting, and can be applied as a plug-and-play modification to entropy-minimization TTA. Experiments on distribution-shift benchmarks show improvements in accuracy, calibration, and negative log-likelihood, suggesting that mutual-information-guided perturbation probing and weighted adaptation yield more robust online TTA than deterministic confidence.
  </details>

- **[ReFrame: Silence-Aware Compute Reclamation for Full-Duplex Speech Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01835)**  `arXiv:2611.01835`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zheng Zhou, Guanyi Chen, Jing Wei, Haoyuan Liang, Jian Zhang, Haohuan Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-duplex speech models execute dense model inference for every audio frame throughout a live interaction. This uniform framewise schedule creates sustained GPU load across dialogue states whose computation has strong, reusable temporal structure. ReFrame formulates full-duplex inference as a silence-aware computation problem. Mutual Silence Speculation (MSS) operates when both participants are silent: it evaluates multi-frame Temporal continuations, verifies text and hidden-state stability, and applies batched Mimi audio-token correction. Listening Frame Folding (LFF) operates while the user speaks and the agent listens: it processes already-arrived user frames through parallel Temporal execution and frame-batched audio-token generation while accepting only the verified prefix. Frame-Budget Reinvestment (FBR) assigns the released frame intervals to resumable external retrieval and language-model workers, then injects timely references through Moshi's conditioning interface. We instantiate ReFrame on five full-duplex models spanning Moshi-family, phase-structured, and flattened autoregressive generation. Across the complete 1,225-conversation FDBench, ReFrame reduces measured GPU execution time by 18.80-31.01% and retains 96.92-100% non-PAD text agreement on five different models. On HaluEval tests, FBR increases the number of correct generated spoken answers from 86 to 205 under the same auxiliary-service budget. ReFrame turns recurring dialogue structure into lower inference cost and higher end-to-end service capability.
  </details>

- **[SparseLift: Automatically Optimizing Opaque Sparse Paths in Industrial-Scale Recommendation Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01702)**  `arXiv:2611.01702`  `cs.LG`  
  _Changqing Shi, Anan Liu, Genbao Chen, Junchao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The sparse path, comprising sparse operations and collective communication, is a critical component of large-scale recommendation models. However, these operations are often represented as opaque nodes in the training computation graph, hindering whole-graph optimization. We present SparseLift, a low-overhead compiler for the sparse path of large-scale recommendation training. It exposes sparse computation and communication to whole-graph analysis to expand the optimization space and improve training efficiency. By analyzing model structure and runtime information, SparseLift reconstructs opaque nodes as analyzable subgraphs, recovering the resource semantics of sparse computation and communication within the graph. Building on the recovered semantics, we design incremental cost models and a constraint-aware planner to automatically select and organize subgraph optimizations into an execution plan that is reused during subsequent steady-state training. We evaluate SparseLift on an industrial video-platform recommendation task with 100B sparse parameters and the public DLRM benchmark. SparseLift improves steady-state throughput for ordinary industrial training steps by 16.5\% over the native baseline. On DLRM, it achieves $1.60\times$ and $1.26\times$ model-execution speedups over DLRM-Baseline and TorchRec, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Alignment Flywheel-Guided Suppression Patching for Interactionless Inverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01322)**  `arXiv:2611.01322`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elias Malomgré, Pieter Simoens_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned reward models allow reinforcement-learning agents to use demonstrations, implicit feedback, safety constraints, social norms, or other sources that do not provide a complete hand-designed scalar reward. However, a learned reward model can assign high reward to unwanted behavior. A policy can exploit these errors during training. Later requirements can also identify additional regions where the deployed reward should change. We introduce \emph{flywheel-guided patching}, a mechanism for making such localized corrections without retraining the learned evaluator. An Alignment Flywheel identifies overgeneralized or constraint-violating regions and compiles verified findings into suppression patches. We instantiate the mechanism for Interactionless Inverse Reinforcement Learning using MoE-GUIDE, placing patches in the model's latent space and retrieving only a small local subset during online reward evaluation. In the main MuJoCo benchmark, we evaluate patching without expert-state reset warm starts. We also test whether operator-specified norms absent from the demonstrations can be compiled into the same reward interface and compare this with enforcing the corresponding rules online. The results show that localized corrective information can modify an existing learned reward without providing a complete replacement reward. Compilation can reuse judgments that are expensive or unavailable during control. Cheap deterministic rules may be simpler to check directly at each step. Code for MoE-GUIDE, Alignment Flywheel, and flywheel patching is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AgentCBO: Causal Belief Routing for Bayesian Optimization under Unknown Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01317)**  `arXiv:2611.01317`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qiyu Wei, Xin Zhang, Richard Allmendinger, Mauricio A Álvarez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal Bayesian optimization (CBO) typically relies on the unrealistic assumption of a perfectly known causal graph. Recent methods relax this constraint by coupling optimization with structure learning, but they often waste sample budgets on structural uncertainties that are irrelevant to the optimizer. In this paper, we formalize routing-equivalence to shift the objective from full causal-graph recovery to causal belief routing, where structural differences only matter when they alter optimizer-facing objects such as admissible exploration sets and do-priors. Furthermore, we propose agentcbo, a graph-belief routing framework that incorporates LLM causal priors through guarded proposal and commit mechanisms. Specifically, a Language-Prior Agent combines LLM beliefs with constraint-based discovery to extract a protected causal backbone alongside residual uncertain edges. A Calibration Agent and a Residual Repair Agent then refine only these uncertain structures through score-gated bounded local edits. Finally, a deterministic commit gate evaluates candidate updates using routing-proxy checks before allowing them to influence intervention selection. Across ten benchmarks, agentcbo performs comparably to known-graph CBO, demonstrating that guarded LLM priors can improve fixed-budget CBO performance.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Ask and What to Believe: Distilling a Variational Bayesian Agent into Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01245)**  `arXiv:2611.01245`  `cs.LG`  
  _Baichuan Li, Yanling Chang, Alfredo Garcia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive tasks such as eliciting a user's preferences or diagnosing a hidden mechanism require an agent to track its uncertainty over a latent state, ask informative questions and act on the answers. Language models have no explicit mechanism for this, whereas Bayesian information seeking provides one through posterior inference and expected information gain (EIG), and we ask whether it can be distilled into an 8B language model. Our teacher, a lightweight Bayesian agent with a sequential variational belief and EIG query selection, matches or approaches exact and particle references on three task families. We generate synthetic interactions with the teacher on episodes disjoint from the benchmark and fine-tune \Student{} to reproduce its queries and its belief state as a structured scratchpad. On three Multi-Turn Puzzles tasks the student raises Circuit Decoding from 0.00 to 0.93 success, lowers the normalized rank of the recommended film from 0.50 to 0.10 and cuts word guessing from 32 to 3.4 attempts, exceeding the strongest reported language models on the two comparable tasks. Exposing the belief state closes much of the gap where the decision is an implicit computation rather than a read-out of the interaction; in matched teacher contrasts, EIG supervision consistently outperforms random querying, whereas greater inference fidelity does not uniformly improve the distilled student; a 70B language-model teacher does not match the Bayesian teacher in downstream student performance, and the learned skill does not transfer to an unseen task family. Variational Bayesian information seeking can thus be amortized into a language model by transferring the belief state that drives its questions and decisions.
  </details>

- **[TRIAGE: Reliability- and Load-Conditioned Escalation for Two-Tier LLM Serving with a Semantic Evidence Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01174)**  `arXiv:2611.01174`  `cs.LG`  
  _Basavaraj R M, Aryan Bharat Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two-tier LLM serving escalates selected requests from a small model to a large one, but prompt-based routers cannot see whether retrieved evidence supports an answer, and they treat the GPU cost of an escalation as constant. We study TRIAGE, which conditions escalation on a post-generation reliability state and on a load-dependent GPU price, and we report both what held and what did not on one A40 serving Qwen2.5-7B and 32B (AWQ) under VLLM. Escalation cost rose 21.9x from 1 to 10 requests/s but showed no monotone dependence on KV-cache headroom, and a pre-registered claim that the reliability state detects corrupted evidence (AUROC>=0.70) was falsified at 0.571, although any prompt-only router scores exactly 0.500 by construction. Post hoc, a semantic evidence judge raised detection to 0.846 (0.926 on never-served items) and predicted wrong small-model answers at AUROC 0.90-0.96 on RGB and three RouterBench model pairs; the resulting cascade stayed within about 3 pp of GPT-4 at 79.5% lower API cost for GPT-3.5->GPT-4, yet fell below random routing for open-model pairs with 2-3x price ratios. End to end, the 7B->32B pair showed only a +2.88 pp held-out gap, so the allocator rightly declined to escalate and the headline is a null that a small pre-launch gate failed to expose.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Asynchronous Federated Learning over Space-Air-Ground Integrated Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01025)**  `arXiv:2611.01025`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kai Wang, Chee Wei Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the emerging 6G paradigm where AI services operate over decentralized and resource-constrained edge infrastructure, performance of distributed learning algorithms remains a significant challenge. This paper proposes HAFL, a Low Earth Orbit (LEO) satellite-assisted hierarchical asynchronous Federated Learning (FL) framework, enabling resource-limited terrestrial clients to collaborate via Ground-Satellite Links (GSLs). Within this framework, LEO satellites serve multiple roles: as edge servers aggregating and broadcasting models, as clients accelerating training, and as high-throughput relays managing inter-satellite routing through Inter-Satellite Links (ISLs). Unlike conventional synchronous Jacobian-type updates, HAFL employs asynchronous Gauss-Seidel-type updates to enhance convergence speed. We provide theoretical convergences for convex, strongly convex, and nonconvex objectives, respectively, and formulate optimization problems to jointly determine optimal hyperparameters to minimize global training latency. Evaluations demonstrate that HAFL achieves faster initial convergence and comparable final performance for benchmark DNNs and datasets, offering promising FL systems across remote areas with 6G technologies, but much less global rounds and communication cost.
  </details>

- **[Jet-QSparse: Accelerating Agentic LLM Inference with 4-Bit Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01022)**  `arXiv:2611.01022`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhan Tang, Hao Kang, Meng Lu, Yecheng Wu, Zerui Wang, Seokho Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon reasoning and agentic workloads strain KV-cache capacity and decode attention throughput. We present Jet-QSparse, a training-free method that combines compact NVFP4 cache storage with native FP4 query-key computation on Blackwell tensor cores. A dense scan scores the full key history and selects 128-token blocks for sparse FP16 value aggregation with FP32 accumulation, preserving higher precision in the output-forming step while enabling contiguous tile loads. Calibrated key centering and at most two BF16 pages per sequence and layer address quantization error; FP8 prefill uses a temporary arena while the persistent cache is packed directly from BF16 activations. Across four hybrid LLMs, a budget of 128 blocks per KV head shows no detected accuracy loss on the evaluated mathematical reasoning, coding, and agentic benchmarks, although some smaller budgets incur losses. With 64 blocks, Qwen3.8-27B achieves a $1.94\times$ end-to-end decode speedup over dense BF16 at 128K context and batch size 16 on one GB300, and the measured Qwen KV pools provide $2.58$-$2.76\times$ BF16's token capacity.
  </details>

- **[Generative Forecasting from Coarse-Grained Observations: When Does Cascading Help?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00686)**  `arXiv:2611.00686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alexandra Spitzer, Giovanni Abati, Lorenzo Baldassari, Till Muser, Cheng Shi, Ivan Dokmanić_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study probabilistic forecasting of dynamical systems such as fluids and waves from coarse observations. Since the unseen fine scales influence the dynamics, the same coarse initial state can evolve into a whole distribution of futures. Generative forecasters may produce visually plausible samples, but it is hard to tell whether they sample the right conditional distribution, since trajectory data contain a single future for each initial state. We address this problem by generating reference data in a hierarchical way: for each coarse initial state, we generate many different fine-scale components and numerically evolve the full states. Using these conditional ensembles, we show that standard diffusion models and stochastic interpolants make substantial distributional errors. We then show that cascading shorter forecasts greatly reduces these errors for the turbulent flows and waves we study, but we also find and characterize cases in which it hurts. Since the coarse process is not Markov, cascading discards information about the unresolved fine scales even with exact transitions. To understand this, we propose a solvable analytic model which shows that the optimal cascade depth decreases as the coupling between the unresolved and observed scales gets stronger, and increases as the dependence of the forecast on the observation becomes more oscillatory. Consistent with our experiments, cascading is detrimental when the direct forecast is ``easy'' to learn, for example Gaussian as in the passive scalar case. We release our datasets as a first benchmark for probabilistic forecasting with ground-truth conditional ensembles.
  </details>

- **[Dataset Mismatch Matters in Group Relative Policy Optimization for Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00586)**  `arXiv:2611.00586`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhen-Yu Zhang, Jiandong Zhang, Huaxiu Yao, Gang Niu, Masashi Sugiyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is practically very important, where the leading approach is group relative policy optimization (GRPO) that typically faces a trade-off between rollout efficiency and downstream performance. To improve efficiency while preserving performance, existing selective rollout methods mainly exploit only source-side training dynamics to optimize the discrete distribution over source prompts. However, in practice, the source training dataset is rarely identically distributed to the target test dataset. When such a mismatch exists, due to legal or privacy constraints, it is usually not acceptable to share target prompts themselves with the RLVR trainer. Nevertheless, it is still possible for the target data owner to provide limited aggregated feedback from a small held-out target set without revealing target prompts or rollout traces. In this paper, we propose learning-to-reweight rollout (L2RR) that explicitly incorporates target-side performance feedback into GRPO. Specifically, L2RR learns a discrete sampling distribution over source prompts by optimizing a bi-level objective that maximizes accuracy on a held-out target dataset, while simultaneously penalizing rollouts that provide little incremental learning information. Empirical studies on six math reasoning benchmarks and three model scales show that L2RR improves target-domain performance and increases rollout efficiency.
  </details>

- **[TabAbax: A Tabular Foundation Model Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00562)**  `arXiv:2611.00562`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhixiong Cheng, Xiaoyun Gao, Shiyuan Han, Jingjing Suo, Zhengli Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) enable in-context prediction on unseen tabular datasets without task-specific training. Mechanistic work has shown that increasing the depth of the ICL Transformer yields weak gains, leaving the main headroom for gains in the synthetic pretraining prior and column-row encoder upstream of the ICL backbone. We introduce TabAbax, a TFM built on TabICLv2 that focuses all modifications on these two upstream components. On the prior side, we further improve the quality and complexity of the synthetic pretraining prior. On the encoder side, a two-pass column-row stack strengthens cross-feature interactions before row compression, using learned Fourier scalar encoding from TabFM and Causilo. Evaluations on BeyondArena and TabArena show strong regression-centric gains: TabAbax reaches 1,238 Elo on the 142-dataset BeyondArena suite (10th of 41 evaluated methods), 1,676 Elo on TabArena's overall 51-dataset suite (11th of 99 methods, +115 over TabICLv2), and 2,024 Elo on TabArena's regression benchmark (7th of 99 methods, +336 over TabICLv2, +21 over TabFM). A prior-only ablation confirms the attribution: the improved prior alone yields +228 Elo for regression, demonstrating that prior quality is the dominant knob for improving regression performance.
  </details>

- **[TileWeave: Heterogeneous Tiling for Sparse Prefill on Mobile SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00408)**  `arXiv:2611.00408`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wentao Ni, Hong Cai, Mingu Lee, Chihao Yu, Zhongming Yu, Hejia Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Smartphone applications increasingly require large language models to process long contexts, whereas high prefill latency and memory demands constrain local inference. Block-sparse attention reduces computation and memory traffic, but its irregular selections conflict with mobile NPUs' preference for large, regular tiles. Efficient execution must exploit cross-query reuse while avoiding redundant computation and preserving selections needed for accuracy. In this paper, we present TileWeave, which partitions sparse attention by this cross-query reuse: highly shared key blocks run in coarse NPU tiles, while low-reuse blocks run on the GPU only for the query blocks that selected them. In addition, we design an NPU-native pooled-key selector that keeps selection inexpensive, and a runtime that publishes GPU partials in the order the NPU consumes them and merges them in its existing output store. On RULER, HeteroSparse matches XAttention's accuracy with Qwen3-1.7B and improves it with Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct; its mean LongBench scores match XAttention as well. On Snapdragon SM8750 and SM8850 phones, it achieves 1.35--3.23$\times$ faster full-model prefill than XAttention over 1k--16k tokens and up to 1.58$\times$ faster than dense attention.
  </details>

- **[DEPLOY-RL: Active Boundary Discovery and Conservative Certification for Deployable Reinforcement Learning in Safety-Critical Continuous Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00342)**  `arXiv:2611.00342`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yeojin Jang, Minu Baek, Gihun Gil, Minsung Jung, Beomdo Park, Woohyeon Kwon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) policies often outperform classical controllers in simulation, yet rarely reach production in safety-critical processes. The barrier is that there is no principled way to answer "Is this policy safe to deploy?" with statistical guarantees. We introduce DEPLOY-RL, a post-training certification framework built on one key insight: deployment certification requires discovering where failures occur (boundary discovery), not measuring how much everywhere (uniform reconstruction). Our contributions: (1) a contract-coupled acquisition function that concentrates sampling on certification-critical boundaries, achieving approximately 2x sample efficiency with a semi-empirical ambiguity reduction bound (domain-calibrated convergence guarantee); (2) conformal risk control providing finite-sample false-go guarantees (<= alpha) under explicit deployment contracts; (3) a three-way decision framework (Deploy/No-Deploy/Abstain) with fail-safe PID/MPC fallback. In simulations on papermaking (industrial digital twin) and Tennessee Eastman (public benchmark), DEPLOY-RL achieves 4.4% false-go rate (vs. 8.6% for the best baseline) while retaining 88.6% policy coverage, the only method achieving <5% false-go with >85% coverage among 14 baselines under our evaluation protocol.
  </details>

- **[RaMMOBO: Random Max-Margin Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00312)**  `arXiv:2611.00312`  `cs.LG`  
  _Thomas Wedenig, Paul Plantosar, Robert Peharz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-objective Bayesian optimization (MOBO) maximizes $M$ expensive black-box objectives with the goal of approximating the Pareto set, whose objective vectors cannot be improved in one component without sacrificing another. A common quality measure is hypervolume, the volume in objective space dominated by these vectors relative to a reference point. Expected hypervolume improvement (EHVI) directly targets this metric and is one-step Bayes-optimal, but its standard computation scales exponentially with $M$, making it prohibitively expensive for many-objective problems. We introduce random max-margin multi-objective Bayesian optimization (RaMMOBO), whose acquisition function lower-bounds EHVI while requiring only linear cost in $M$ to evaluate. RaMMOBO measures the volume of a max-margin box contained in a candidate's hypervolume-improvement region and averages over random reweightings of the objective space, yielding a utility that is strictly Pareto-monotone on hypervolume-improving outcomes. It naturally handles noisy observations and different probabilistic surrogates and supports efficient gradient-based optimization. Across synthetic and real-world benchmarks, RaMMOBO matches or outperforms state-of-the-art methods, with largest gains at higher $M$, with acquisition optimization up to two orders of magnitude faster than qLogNEHVI. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Quantization of Large Models with Weight Outliers and Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00286)**  `arXiv:2611.00286`  `cs.LG`  
  _Leonidas Tsigkounakis, Abraham Jaeger Mountain, Yilang Zhang, Georgios B. Giannakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces the memory burden of large model deployment by casting the high precision trained weights to fewer bits. While this promotes memory-efficient inference, it also incurs quantization error that impairs model performance. To compensate for quantization error, the present contribution decomposes the original trained weights into a quantized matrix, a low-rank component, and a sparse matrix that accounts for outlier rows in the weights. Due to analytical intractability and the discrete structure of the decomposition, a simulated annealing search is employed to explore candidate decompositions while avoiding poor local minima. Numerical tests demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach across a broad suite of large language models, including Llama 3.2 1B, Llama 3.2 3B, Llama 3.1 8B, and Qwen2.5 7B, evaluated on natural language generation benchmarks using 4-bit NF4 and FP4 quantizers. The novel method can markedly outperform direct quantization while incurring minimal memory overhead.
  </details>

- **[OCELOT: Planetary-Scale Species Distribution Modeling with Satellite Foundation Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00284)**  `arXiv:2611.00284`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bart van Merriënboer, Lauren Harrell, Keith Anderson, Burcu Karagol Ayan, Elise Kleeman, Tom Denton, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale repositories of species observations now contain billions of occurrence records from around the world, but these sightings cluster heavily in human-accessible areas. Existing models use only a small fraction of these records and are not evaluated on extrapolating to remote, unvisited regions--where species distribution models can be used as geographic priors to help classify data from camera traps and acoustic recorders. We scale relative occurrence species distribution models by training a multimodal implicit neural representation network called OCELOT that fuses spatial coordinates with satellite foundation embeddings on two orders of magnitude more observations than previous work: 2 billion GBIF records across 100,000 taxa. Scaling to this regime requires co-designing the data pipeline, taxonomic representation, loss formulation, and model architecture: spatiotemporal thinning and sub-linear cell sampling correct for spatial redundancy and observer bias, while an additive taxonomic readout shares representations across species, genera, and families to improve predictions for rare taxa. To evaluate performance in remote areas, we benchmark geographic extrapolation using the NDCG metric on spatial block holdouts up to 600 km. Satellite foundation embeddings improve extrapolation by 51% over coordinate-only models and outperform classical bioclimatic rasters. The resulting model also matches or outperforms existing baselines on established interpolation benchmarks, demonstrating that optimizing for geographic extrapolation does not compromise in-distribution accuracy.
  </details>

- **[NAGO: Noise-Aware Generative Operator via Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38273)**  `arXiv:2610.38273`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guanyu Chen, Shengze Xu, Pengwei Liu, Xingyu Ren, Pengkai Wang, Antonios Armaou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce NAGO, a noise-aware generative operator for efficient sampling from invariant measures of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) in function space. Our central idea is to treat the source measure as a design variable rather than a fixed Gaussian default. We establish admissibility conditions for well-defined infinite-dimensional flows and show that the finite-band source--target covariance ratio controls the spectral stiffness of the marginal velocity field. This analysis yields an admissibility-constrained spectral-matching rule: choose a source that is sufficiently rough for well-posedness, then minimize covariance mismatch over the resolved Fourier modes. On three representative stochastic-field benchmarks, this source design improves few-step spectral accuracy and several complementary distributional and physical statistics. In a controlled Matern-field ablation, it reduces the accurate RK4 budget from about 30 steps for Gaussian functional flow matching to 3--5 steps, a $6$--$10\times$ reduction in neural ODE evaluations. These gains are strongest for stationary multiscale fields and depend on the metric, resolution, and guidance protocol.
  </details>

- **[RadonBench: A Benchmark of Classical and Learning-Guided Search Techniques for Problems in Combinatorial Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33259)**  `arXiv:2610.33259`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoan Xu, Jesus A. De Loera, Ethan X. Fang, Junwei Lu, Luze Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical optimization and learning-guided search offer complementary approaches to open problems in discrete mathematics, but their outputs are difficult to compare when search spaces, validity checks, and stopping rules differ. We present \emph{RadonBench}, a benchmark for constructing maximum-cardinality Radon-free lattice configurations: finite subsets of $\mathbb{Z}^d$ admitting no integral Radon partition. It remains open whether Radon-free configurations with $11$ points in $\mathbb{Z}^3$ or $21$ points in $\mathbb{Z}^4$ are possible. If they exist, they would improve the corresponding lower bounds on the integer Radon numbers. We compare exhaustive extension, greedy local search, PatternBoost, AlphaZero, and AlphaEvolve. All outputs use one success predicate: a convex target-sized configuration succeeds exactly when its number of integral Radon points is zero. PatternBoost and AlphaZero construct verified Radon-free configurations of sizes $10$ and $20$, one point short of the respective targets; AlphaZero's best convex target terminals have two integral Radon points in both dimensions. Exhaustive extension over fixed seeds and declared finite search boxes likewise reaches a minimum of two integral Radon points. Greedy local search and AlphaEvolve each produce target-sized near misses with one integral Radon point. No audited run reaches zero. These results establish reproducible near-miss baselines over explicitly scoped search domains.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Design SR Solvers: Harness Optimization for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16966)**  `arXiv:2610.16966`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qian Li, Yuxiao Hu, Qihao Duan, Jinyue Yan, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) seeks explicit equations that explain observed data, and recent methods increasingly employ large language models (LLMs) and agents for equation discovery. However, these systems typically rely on manually designed workflows and prompts. We argue that the SR agent harness should itself be an optimization target: its design shapes search priors and how numerical feedback guides subsequent equation refinement. We therefore propose HarnessSR, a framework for jointly optimizing prompts and workflows to construct reusable SR agents. During both harness optimization and agent execution, HarnessSR supports either PySR or an LLM-based equation generator through tool-specific interfaces, allowing the framework to optimize search guidance for different equation-discovery mechanisms. HarnessSR first optimizes role prompts through equation-discovery feedback in basic workflows, then jointly searches prompt assignments and workflows with brief adaptation to align the two. Finally, it refines role instructions through reflection on residual patterns, candidate equation structures, and search outcomes, improving how these signals guide subsequent equation search. On LSR-Synth in LLM-SRBench, HarnessSR achieves a mean ID $\mathrm{Acc}_{0.01}$ of 61.33% across three backbones, a 45.0% relative improvement over the next-best baseline.
  </details>

- **[DASS: A Solver-Adaptable Dynamic Auxiliary Search Strategy for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16812)**  `arXiv:2610.16812`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxiao Hu, Qian Li, Ding Wang, Yekun Zheng, Jinyue Yan, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) aims to discover interpretable mathematical expressions from data and plays a key role in scientific discovery. However, existing methods face a common bottleneck: the enormous search space and severe combinatorial explosion make it difficult to achieve a favorable trade-off between accuracy and algorithmic efficiency. We argue that, in addition to designing increasingly complex solver-specific SR strategies, an equally promising direction is to equip them with a shareable auxiliary strategy that can provide reliable search guidance. To this end, we propose DASS, a solver-adaptable Dynamic Auxiliary Search Strategy that improves SR by constructing and refining a high-potential auxiliary search subspace. DASS represents the auxiliary search space as a functional subspace spanned by a set of high-potential basis function terms. It estimates term utility through multi-environment evaluation, filters unstable guidance, and updates term potential scores with a Gibbs posterior, thereby calibrating the subspace to provide more reliable guidance for downstream solvers. The high-quality solutions produced by the solver are used to update the set of basis terms. This forms a virtuous closed-loop optimization process: the subspace guides the downstream solver, and the solver feedback reshapes the subspace for subsequent exploration. DASS can be easily integrated with various types of SR solvers. Extensive experiments on LLM-SRBench show that DASS significantly improves the accuracy of original solvers with a manageable increase in runtime.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization: Unified Learning from Offline Demonstrations and On- and Off-Policy Rollouts via a Shared Regression Target](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12830)**  `arXiv:2610.12830`  `cs.LG`  
  _Du Cheng, Junyu Lu, Jingfan Zhang, Yuzhuo Zhang, Haoxuan Jia, Shichao Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning post-training draws on offline demonstrations and policy-generated rollouts, yet conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) separates their objectives and limits continued reuse of collected responses. We propose \textbf{Cross-Behavior Policy Optimization} (CBPO), which makes these sources compatible with one regression loss through a fixed-reference target. Because the generating policy does not enter this target, positive and negative offline completions, reference responses, and current and historical rollouts can share a common reward-labeled record format and replay buffer. As new responses enter the buffer, optimization retains the same regression objective without coordinating separate SFT and RL objectives or requiring stored behavior-policy probabilities, importance weighting, or a learned critic. Restricting the available sources gives offline, on-policy, and off-policy variants, while CBPO-Unified combines them. Across two model scales and four mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CBPO delivers leading \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} performance across benchmarks and data regimes. On Qwen3-1.7B, CBPO-Unified improves benchmark-averaged \texttt{avg@32} and \texttt{pass@32} over SFT DAPO by 2.33 and 4.44 percentage points, respectively. In the replay ablation, retaining historical rollouts improves these metrics by 1.87 and 3.72 percentage points over the no-history configuration.
  </details>

- **[SPIN: Shadow Predictive Indexer for Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09025)**  `arXiv:2610.09025`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yao Fu, Jiahan Chang, Yueying Li, Ritchie Zhao, Bryce Long, Mohammad Mahdi Kamani, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indexer-based sparse attention reduces the cost of core attention by passing only a fixed, small number of important tokens to it. However, the indexer must still score the entire KV cache at every decoding step. This scoring overhead becomes a major bottleneck as the context length grows. We propose SPIN (Shadow Predictive Indexer) to reduce this indexer overhead. SPIN uses lightweight, history-based prediction to identify important KV blocks, avoiding the need to score the full KV cache at every decoding step. SPIN keeps the score history fresh with random exploration and allocates the block budget across layers by their measured miss rates. SPIN treats KV blocks and speculative decoding as first-class design and implementation considerations. Across extensive evaluations on long-context and agentic benchmarks, SPIN achieves 30-40% sparsity while preserving task quality. With larger exploration shares and per-layer block budgets, SPIN keeps task quality on the especially difficult MRCRv2 benchmark close to baseline at 50-57% effective block sparsity. In end-to-end vLLM serving, SPIN improves output throughput by up to 13.9% and reduces median inter-token latency by up to 13.6%.
  </details>

- **[Learning Risk Scores Robust to Unobserved Confounders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27144)**  `arXiv:2609.27144`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ryan Edmonds, Yingxiao Ye, Sina Aghaei, Andrés Gómez, Çağıl Koçyiğit, Phebe Vayanos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We consider the problem of learning risk scores to prioritize individuals for scarce resources or interventions, from historical observational data affected by unobserved confounding. Decisions about who receives scarce resources are often guided by risk scores based on recorded characteristics, such as responses to a survey. These risk scores are increasingly being learned directly from observational data: historical records of individuals' characteristics, allocation decisions, and outcomes. Standard methods such as inverse propensity weighting (IPW), which corrects for the bias introduced by the historical allocation policy, can be used to learn accurate risk scores if the historical decision process is fully explained by the recorded characteristics. In practice, however, historical decisions often depend on unrecorded information, causing learned risk scores to systematically under-prioritize exactly the individuals whose unrecorded circumstances drove past prioritization. We propose a method for learning risk scores that are robust to this kind of unobserved confounding, building on IPW. Since propensity weights cannot be reliably estimated under unobserved confounding, we instead treat them as belonging to an uncertainty set determined by the observable data and domain-informed estimates of the degree of confounding, combining sensitivity analysis from causal inference with Wasserstein distributionally robust optimization. The resulting robust risk score learning problem admits a sample-based approximation that we reformulate as an exponential cone program compatible with off-the-shelf solvers. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on semi-synthetic data derived from datasets in the UCI Machine Learning Repository. Our method improves calibration by up to 29.2% over traditional benchmarks and up to 11.1% over the state of the art, without compromising other metrics.
  </details>

- **[NashDreamer: Model-Based Reinforcement Learning for Zero-Sum Imperfect-Information Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01549)**  `arXiv:2609.01549`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tomáš Holeček, Viliam Lisý_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) has achieved remarkable results in single-agent domains, yet its extension to competitive imperfect information games (IIGs) remains underexplored. In multi-agent settings, opponent-induced non-stationarity complicates the learning process, and decentralized model learning faces severe identifiability barriers, which we argue make centralized model learning a mathematical necessity. Building on this analysis, we propose NashDreamer, a principled MBRL framework for two-player zero-sum IIGs. It introduces a centralized Multi-Agent Recurrent State-Space Model (MARSSM) that decouples environment dynamics from the effect of players' strategies on their individual observations. NashDreamer is designed to use arbitrary policy gradient algorithms and inherits their convergence guarantees towards Nash equilibria under an idealized model. Empirical evaluations across five benchmark games demonstrate that NashDreamer substantially improves sample efficiency over model-free baselines early in the training. Finally, we theoretically analyze the architecture's optimization landscape, identifying the vulnerability of the Dreamer family of algorithms to posterior collapse in stochastic environments. We leave it as an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[Advancing Open and Reproducible Relational Learning: RelArena-$α$, TabPFN-Rel and RPI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16319)**  `arXiv:2608.16319`  `cs.LG`  
  _Adrian Hayler, Klemens Flöge, Alan Arazi, Rishabh Ranjan, Jure Leskovec, Felix Birkel, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This first release of Prior Labs in relational learning shows our continued commitment to open science. We open-source three pieces of software that we expect to accelerate research in the field towards meaningful real-world impact. We aim to steer further development based on feedback from, and in collaboration with, the community. Given the early stage of development, our $\alpha$-release targets researchers and early-adopting practitioners. Over the past years, a variety of datasets and tasks for relational learning have emerged, but the community has not converged on a reliable, reproducible way to compare different methods on these tasks. Our $\alpha$-release, RelArena-$\alpha$, provides a unified framework for running and comparing baselines on RelBench v1 by standardizing data loading, evaluation protocols, tuning regimes, and support for systems with custom tuning, inspired by established tabular benchmarks such as TabArena. We plan to work with the research community to further develop RelArena-$\alpha$ into a catalyst for progress in the relational learning community. We release the initial version of TabPFN-Rel, a purpose-built relational harness for TabPFN-3. Currently ranked first among models on RelArena-$\alpha$, TabPFN-Rel makes key improvements upon RDBLearn. Beyond its ranking, TabPFN-Rel serves as a strong baseline, adding to the growing evidence that flattening a relational database into a single table remains competitive with specialized relational architectures on real-world tasks.To facilitate adoption of relational learning methods in research and industry, we release an initial $\alpha$-version of our Relational Predictive Interface, RPI, an open-source, model-agnostic interface that enables early adopters to easily define problems on new databases and apply any model implemented in RelArena-$\alpha$, including TabPFN-Rel, to these problems.
  </details>

- **[DACRI: Decision-Aware Causal Intervention Ranking for Critical Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11154)**  `arXiv:2608.11154`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shiqi Huang, Jiani He, Dingyan Shang, Yihua Xu, Jize Li, Yan Lyu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting or attributing a supply-chain disruption is not the same as selecting the intervention that maximizes recoverable net value. We present CriticalSCM-Bench v1, a controlled synthetic benchmark with causal ground truth, paired factual/counterfactual rollouts, and an explicit net-value objective. Relative to a full-information train-selected static benchmark, LambdaMART improves median normalized net value by 5.7--16.2\%, with paired statistical support on the semiconductor and critical-material archetypes but not on digital infrastructure. On digital infrastructure, a domain-informed constant-buffer policy remains stronger, showing that greater model complexity is not uniformly justified. Across partial and delayed settings, LambdaMART retains 33--75\% of full-clamp value. Stress tests further show that intervention fidelity, timing, cost, and held-out disruptions can alter policy ordering. Critical materials show the weakest out-of-distribution retention. Separately, a guarded explanation study over 540 generations preserves every fixed intervention decision after deterministic validation and template fallback, although exact wording remains unstable. Within this controlled setting, the results identify regimes in which adaptive ranking adds value and those in which simpler structural policies remain preferable.
  </details>

- **[GRIMIP: A General Framework for Instance-Specific Configuration of MIP Solvers Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23299)**  `arXiv:2606.23299`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yidong Luo, Chenguang Wang, Fangzhou Zhu, Tao Zhong, Tianshu Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Configuring the hyperparameters of Mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers is a high-dimensional, instance-dependent optimization problem where suboptimal settings can degrade solving time by orders of magnitude. Default configurations are often suboptimal, while traditional tuning methods either suffer from the ``cold-start'' problem and inefficient search or heavily rely on expert experience. This paper introduces \textbf{GRIMIP} (\textbf{\underline{G}}eneral \textbf{\underline{R}}easoning for \textbf{\underline{I}}nstance-specific \textbf{\underline{MIP}} configuration), a novel hybrid intelligence framework that synergistically integrates the semantic reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) with the sample-efficient search of Bayesian Optimization (BO). GRIMIP enables the LLM to function as a complete probabilistic surrogate within the BO loop, significantly improving performance and reducing sampling and evaluation costs. On seven benchmarks including MIPLIB, GRIMIP achieves over 40\% reduction in Primal-Dual Integral on hard instances, outperforming SMAC and other LLM-assisted BO methods. By granting LLMs sufficient autonomy, GRIMIP combines the expert-level reasoning of LLMs with the efficient search of BO, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[HERALD: High-Throughput Block Diffusion LLM Inference via CPU-GPU Cooperative KV Cache Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21633)**  `arXiv:2606.21633`  `cs.LG`  
  _Omin Kwon, Doyeon Kim, Jongseok Park, Seung Yul Lee, Sehoon Kim, Ion Stoica, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block diffusion LLMs (block dLLMs) improve decode throughput by generating tokens in parallel, and are emerging as a next-generation alternative to autoregressive LLMs. At long contexts, however, the KV cache remains a major throughput bottleneck in both memory bandwidth and capacity. For autoregressive LLMs, dynamic sparse attention is often combined with KV offloading to alleviate both bottlenecks, keeping the full KV cache in CPU DRAM and fetching only a critical subset to the GPU. However, KV subset selection and retrieval over bandwidth-limited PCIe incur substantial offloading overheads that often stall the GPU. We present HERALD, the first dynamic sparse attention method for block dLLMs that supports KV offloading. HERALD exploits a property of block dLLMs: a critical KV subset selected once per block is reused across all of its denoising steps, so the next block's selection can be hidden behind the current block's denoising. HERALD realizes this with a two-stage selection algorithm co-designed with its execution system. While the GPU denoises the current block, the CPU speculatively searches the full KV cache for the next block with a single query merged from the current block's B queries, cutting the selection compute by Bx; the GPU then refines the candidates into the final top-K subset with the next block's real queries. At K=1024, HERALD achieves near-lossless accuracy relative to Dense on LongBench and reasoning tasks across four block dLLMs, and improves decode throughput by up to 3.70x over Dense GPU-only inference, 2.59x over MAGE, a state-of-the-art block-dLLM sparse attention method, and 8.38x over MAGE-Offload, its direct KV-offloading extension.
  </details>

- **[Chess-World-Model: A 10M-Game Benchmark for Exact State Tracking from Chess Move Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30100)**  `arXiv:2605.30100`  `cs.LG`  
  _Benjamin Walker, Terry Lyons_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models require state tracking, which is the ability to maintain a correct latent state across action sequences. Existing benchmarks are often synthetic or language-based, limiting their value as tests of structured state updates in realistic domains. We introduce Chess-World-Model, a large-scale state-tracking benchmark built from 10 million real chess games, where models predict the exact board state reached after a sequence of legal moves. Alongside a held-out real-game split, we include an out-of-distribution test set of uniformly random legal play both at its original trajectory lengths and after matching the human-game length distribution. This tests whether models learn the transition rules rather than shortcuts from common human positions. Prior theoretical and empirical work has shown that Transformers struggle to state-track, while input-dependent linear RNNs require expressive state-transition matrices to do so. We therefore benchmark a causal Transformer, block-diagonal SLiCE, Mamba-3, and Gated DeltaNet with negative eigenvalues under a matched interface and training protocol. The recurrent models strongly outperform the Transformer at approximately 3 and 8 million parameters. Real-game performance largely saturates by the 18 million parameter scale, but length-matched random evaluation continues to distinguish architectures through the largest tested scale of approximately 43 million parameters. Additionally, ablations show that less expressive state-transition mechanisms generally reduce performance on the out-of-distribution splits. Together, these results establish Chess-World-Model as a practical large-scale benchmark for state tracking that exposes failures model scale would otherwise conceal.
  </details>

- **[MSAlign: Aligning Molecule and Mass Spectra Representations for Metabolite Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19752)**  `arXiv:2605.19752`  `cs.LG`  
  _Paul Krzakala, Gabriel Melo, Camille Lançon, Charlotte Laclau, Rémi Flamary, Etienne Thévenot, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately identifying metabolites i.e. small molecules from mass spectrometry data remains a core challenge in metabolomics, with broad applications in drug discovery, environmental analysis, and clinical research. We address the Molecule Retrieval task, which consists in recovering the chemical structure of a metabolite from its MS/MS spectrum given a set of candidate molecules. We make three contributions. First, we propose a unified framework encompassing recent approaches based on representation alignment and contrastive learning. Second, we introduce MSAlign, a lightweight model that achieves state-of-the art performances by aligning two frozen foundation models (DreaMS for mass spectra and MolDeBERTa for molecules) and demonstrate that a score fusion strategy further improves the performance for a very small computational cost. Third, we investigate a long-standing evaluation problem: data splitting strategies in molecule retrieval implicitly trade off data leakage against domain shift. We formalize this tension by introducing a quantitative measure of distribution shift, and use it to evaluate splitting strategies in existing benchmarks. All datasets, splits, candidate sets, and a unified implementation of MSAlign and baselines are publicly released to support reproducible research.
  </details>

- **[Dystruct: Dynamically Structured Diffusion Language Model Decoding via Bayesian Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09820)**  `arXiv:2605.09820`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bian Sun, Kevin Zhai, Mubarak Shah, Zhenyi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive models, primarily due to their ability to enable parallel decoding. Despite this advantage, most existing DLMs rely on a fixed generation length specified prior to decoding, which restricts their flexibility in real-world applications. While a few recent works attempt to support flexible-length generation, they typically suffer from notable limitations: some require costly retraining to accommodate variable-length outputs, while others depend solely on local confidence signals during decoding. Such local criteria fail to capture the evolving structure of the sequence, often resulting in suboptimal generation quality. In this paper, we propose a training-free, Bayesian structured decoding framework that formulates flexible-length generation as a dynamic structural inference problem. Our approach formulates flexible-length generation as a dynamic structural inference problem, jointly computing the expansion length, the block boundaries, and the decoding schedule. At each window expansion step, the method integrates local uncertainty with structural signals via a unified mechanism that supports dynamic structured generation, including both flexible block expansion and block organization, while maintaining coherence. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our approach significantly improves generation quality and flexibility over existing fixed-length and flexible-length baselines. These results highlight the advantage of Bayesian structured decoding for diffusion language model, providing a principled and efficient solution for structured text generation.
  </details>

- **[Elastic Spectral State Space Models for Train-Once Budgeted Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22488)**  `arXiv:2601.22488`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dachuan Song, Junyu Yin, Zechen Hu, Xuan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern sequence models are typically trained at a fixed computational capacity, while real-world applications require deployment across platforms with different resource constraints. Existing approaches either train or distill separate compact models for selected budgets, or use elastic architectures that shrink dimensions such as width, depth, Feed-Forward Network (FFN) size, or SSM state dimension. In this paper, we propose Elastic Spectral State Space Models (ES-SSM), a train-once, export-many sequence modeling framework that gains elasticity through spectral approximation of the SSM sequence operator. ES-SSM builds on Hankel spectral filtering for state space models, where long-range token mixing is represented through fixed Hankel spectral channels that define an operator-level approximation resolution. To make this spectral elasticity reliably deployable, ES-SSM combines input-adaptive channel-wise gates with budget dropout, training the same spectral prefixes that are used by compact models at inference time. This encourages low-index channels to become predictive on their own, while higher-index channels provide refinements for larger budgets. We evaluate ES-SSM across byte-level language modeling, Long Range Arena, Speech Commands V2, and offline reinforcement learning benchmarks. Across these settings, a single trained ES-SSM can be truncated to competitive compact models compared with modern Transformer and SSM baselines at similar parameter scales. Furthermore, by testing under various runtime budgets, we observe smooth and stable quality-cost curves over a wide range of truncation levels. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Generalist and Specialist Time-Series Foundation Models for PPG-Based Health Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14254)**  `arXiv:2510.14254`  `cs.LG`  
  _Saurabh Kataria, Yi Wu, Zhaoliang Chen, Hyunjung Gloria Kwak, Yuhao Xu, Lovely Yeswanth Panchumarthi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series Foundation Models (FMs) are pretrained on either heterogeneous corpora or specific signal domains. Using Photoplethysmography (PPG) as a representative modality, we compare generalist time-series FMs, PPG-specialist FMs, and traditional machine learning (ML) baselines for PPG-based health assessment. We conduct a two-stage benchmark. First, we evaluate multiple FM families across 23 downstream evaluations (e.g., atrial fibrillation detection, vital-sign estimation). Second, we conduct a focused four-FM comparison (MOMENT, Chronos-2, GPT-PPG, MOMENT-PPG) at multiple model sizes on an expanded 51-task suite, under both frozen-backbone and full-model tuning, to study tuning strategy, model scaling, and cross-dataset transfer. The multi-model benchmark shows that PPG-specialist FMs achieve the strongest aggregate performance, but their advantage is not uniform: generalist FMs remain competitive on several tasks, and traditional ML baselines outperform FMs on selected clinical regression tasks. The focused analysis shows that the advantage of PPG-specific pretraining appears mainly with frozen backbones and on targets that the waveform directly reflects, such as atrial fibrillation and vital signs. Full-model tuning narrows this advantage, model rankings change with the tuning strategy, and the largest model variant is the best choice in only about one-third of cases. Additionally, controlled experiments with two generalist models show that initializing PPG pretraining from generalist checkpoints provides an early advantage over training from scratch, even when the pretrained features offer little downstream benefit. Together, these findings suggest that PPG-specific pretraining is useful but task-dependent, and that FMs should be evaluated against simpler statistical, traditional ML, and clinical history-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[Black-Box Combinatorial Optimization with Order-Invariant Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01824)**  `arXiv:2510.01824`  `cs.LG`  
  _Olivier Goudet, Quentin Suire, Adrien Goëffon, Frédéric Saubion, Sylvain Lamprier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce an order-invariant reinforcement learning framework for black-box combinatorial optimization. Classical estimation-of-distribution algorithms (EDAs) often rely on learning explicit variable dependency graphs, which can be costly and fail to capture complex interactions efficiently. In contrast, we parameterize a multivariate autoregressive generative model trained without a fixed variable ordering. By sampling random generation orders during training, a form of information-preserving dropout, the model is encouraged to be invariant to variable order, promoting search-space diversity, and shaping the model to focus on the most relevant variable dependencies, improving sample efficiency. We adapt Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to this setting, providing stable policy-gradient updates from scale-invariant advantages. Across a wide range of benchmark algorithms and problem instances of varying sizes, our method frequently achieves the best performance and consistently avoids catastrophic failures.
  </details>

- **[Improving Sampling Efficiency in RLVR through Adaptive Rollout and Response Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25808)**  `arXiv:2509.25808`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuheng Zhang, Wenlin Yao, Changlong Yu, Yao Liu, Qingyu Yin, Bing Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) trains large language models with verifiable rewards by normalizing rewards within response groups. When every response in a group receives the same reward, the advantages vanish and the group provides no policy gradient signal. We propose Adaptive Rollout and Response Reuse Policy Optimization (AR3PO) to obtain informative groups with fewer generations. Adaptive rollout samples responses to each prompt in stages and stops sampling once the model generates a correct response. If all sampled responses are incorrect, response reuse replaces one of them with a past correct response to the same prompt, when available, and recomputes the group advantages. Policy-recomputed reuse trains on the updated groups using probabilities recomputed under the rollout policy; advantage-only reuse uses historical responses only to compute advantages. Across four mathematical benchmarks with 7B and 8B models, AR3PO consistently improves over GRPO and achieves performance comparable to DAPO while requiring up to $4.2\times$ fewer generated responses. Experiments with a 32B model also show comparable performance to DAPO at a similar training stage with substantially fewer generated responses.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Layer-wise Model Merging through Chain of Merges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21421)**  `arXiv:2508.21421`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pietro Buzzega, Riccardo Salami, Angelo Porrello, Simone Calderara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging has emerged as a simple yet effective method to fuse models without re-training. Existing techniques operate at the level of individual layers, thereby overlooking the inter-layer dependencies inherent in deep networks. We show that this simplification induces distributional mismatches in intermediate activations during merging, as changes applied to early layers fail to propagate to downstream ones. We identify these mismatches as a form of covariate shift that compounds across the network. To address this issue, we propose Chain of Merges (CoM), a novel procedure that sequentially merges weights across layers while accounting for inter-layer interactions. In particular, CoM mitigates covariate shift through a series of regression problems, where input activations are recomputed at each step to reflect the updated representations. Additionally, we introduce a dynamic weighting mechanism that prioritizes task-layer pairs most susceptible to performance degradation, improving robustness during merging. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that CoM achieves state-of-the-art performance across heterogeneous tasks.
  </details>

- **[HGCN(O): A Self-Tuning GCN HyperModel Toolkit for Outcome Prediction in Event-Sequence Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22524)**  `arXiv:2507.22524`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fang Wang, Paolo Ceravolo, Ernesto Damiani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Outcome prediction from event sequence data requires effective use of information at two distinct semantic levels: attributes of individual events and attributes of the complete sequence. Although graph models can explicitly represent temporal relations between events, existing approaches commonly rely on predefined representations and manually configured architectures, with limited consideration of how event and sequence information should be incorporated at their corresponding GNN levels or how GNN models should be fitted to different datasets without manual tuning. We propose HGCN(O), an end to end self tuning graph toolkit for hierarchical information representation and automated model configuration. Its core T-GCN architecture processes event information at the node level and sequence information at the graph level, while O-GCN provides a one level reference for early integration of sequence attributes. TP-GCN and TE-GCN extend T-GCN with a pseudo informative duration embedding and a learned activity embedding, respectively. HGCN(O) integrates these architecture specific designs with hypermodel optimization to jointly determine model structure and training configuration.
