The core T-GCN achieves F1 scores of 1.0000 on two balanced benchmarks and a weighted F1 of 0.8639 on a highly imbalanced dataset, compared with 0.8318 for GAT and 0.8453 for TransformerConv. Prefix and ablation analyses further demonstrate its effectiveness for outcome prediction from incomplete sequences and quantify the contributions of temporal edge weights and graph level sequence attributes.
  </details>

- **[GC4NC: A Benchmark Framework for Graph Condensation on Node Classification with New Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16715)**  `arXiv:2406.16715`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shengbo Gong, Juntong Ni, Noveen Sachdeva, Carl Yang, Wei Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph condensation (GC) is an emerging technique designed to learn a significantly smaller graph that retains the essential information of the original graph. This condensed graph has shown promise in accelerating graph neural networks while preserving performance comparable to those achieved with the original, larger graphs. Additionally, this technique facilitates downstream applications like neural architecture search and deepens our understanding of redundancies in large graphs. Despite the rapid development of GC methods, particularly for node classification, a unified evaluation framework is still lacking to systematically compare different GC methods or clarify key design choices for improving their effectiveness. To bridge these gaps, we introduce \textbf{GC4NC}, a comprehensive framework for evaluating diverse GC methods on node classification across multiple dimensions including performance, efficiency, privacy preservation, denoising ability, NAS effectiveness, and transferability. Our systematic evaluation offers novel insights into how condensed graphs behave and the critical design choices that drive their success. These findings pave the way for future advancements in GC methods, enhancing both performance and expanding their real-world applications. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[UA-Depth: Uncertainty-Aware Depth Restoration for Material-Agnostic Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01645)**  `arXiv:2611.01645`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Rui Cao, Jia-Hui Pan, Yin Zhang, Qiwei Meng, Jiapeng Li, Gang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation requires accurate metric depth, yet commodity RGB-D cameras often fail on non-Lambertian objects such as transparent, reflective, and translucent materials, producing holes, structured noise, and biased depth. Most restoration methods produce only a single depth map, treating noisy and reliable regions uniformly for downstream tasks while ignoring regional uncertainty. We present UA-Depth, an uncertainty-aware depth restoration framework that iteratively co-refines depth and pixel-wise uncertainty, and uses uncertainty-gated features to suppress unreliable cues for stable updates. To strengthen zero-shot behavior, UA-Depth leverages a frozen depth foundation model as a prior to initialize missing or unreliable depth, and applies two-stage MAD-weighted log-depth scale normalization to reduce outlier bias in relative-to-metric alignment. We introduce an eight-dataset zero-shot benchmark across diverse scenes, materials, and sensors. Across these benchmarks, UA-Depth achieves the best mean rank among representative baselines, with particularly strong gains on datasets dominated by transparent and reflective objects. Beyond depth metrics, we demonstrate downstream improvements in category-level 6D pose estimation and 6D grasp synthesis by exploiting the predicted uncertainty. \emph{Code, models, and data for training and evaluation will be released upon publication.}
  </details>

- **[A Straight Line Is Not a Camera Pose: Auditing Short-Clip Pose Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00482)**  `arXiv:2611.00482`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Xinyi Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The absolute trajectory error (ATE) is the standard measure of camera-motion accuracy, from SLAM to feed-forward 3D reconstruction. On the common Sintel protocol, a constant-velocity straight line using no images has a lower mean ATE than 10 of 11 recent models re-run with official code. Two properties of ATE's alignment explain this. First, it maps any constant-velocity prediction onto the best-fitting line through the true path, so the line scores the path's regression residual. A residual signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) criterion predicts when moving a prediction towards its constant-velocity fit lowers ATE. It gives the outcome for 95% of 1056 model-clip pairs, 98% of 792 held-out pairs and 97% of 1034 ScanNet pairs, and it separates Sintel (median SNR 0.86) from TUM, ScanNet and full-length Bonn and Wild-SLAM (2.7 to 27), where straightening raises every model's mean ATE. Second, position-only alignment ignores where cameras look: rotating a trajectory by 30 degrees against its orientations leaves ATE unchanged, yet worsens the reprojection of static points for every model on Sintel, TUM and ScanNet in the main setting, and the line, second by mean ATE on Sintel, places them worst by geometric mean: a lower ATE need not mean better geometry. Fitting the alignment's rotation to the predicted orientations gives pose-anchored ATE, a one-function change in ATE's units costing 0.17 ms per clip (ATE: 0.11 ms). It ranks every image-free reference below every model on Sintel, tracks the reprojection error under rotation and straightening, where ATE does not (Spearman 0.61 vs. 0.32), largely preserves the models' order, and ties 10-frame translational relative pose error (RPE) and the discernible trajectory error (DTE) on our rotation and reference tests. Code, predictions and results will be released.
  </details>

- **[SV-WAM: An Efficient Surround-View World-Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03602)**  `arXiv:2609.03602`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Jinyang Wang, Shiwei Li, Junjian Wang, Zhiqiang Deng, Jianbin Gao, Yihang Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models (WMs) have demonstrated strong potential for end-to-end autonomous driving by learning predictive representations of future scene dynamics. However, generating future videos during inference introduces substantial computational overhead, leading many recent driving WMs to adopt a single front camera as input for efficient deployment. This design restricts spatial coverage in safety-critical maneuvers such as lane changes, merges, and turns. To address this limitation, we propose SV-WAM, a surround-view world-action model (WAM) that preserves full six-camera observations while maintaining efficient inference. SV-WAM leverages future-video prediction as dense training supervision for action learning within a shared generative model, rather than as an inference-time output. At the core of this design is an action-centered causal mask that prevents action tokens from attending to future-video tokens during joint action-video denoising. Consequently, the video branch can be discarded at deployment, enabling efficient action-only planning. Furthermore, we introduce a differentiable drivable-area compliance regularizer that penalizes vehicle-footprint corners approaching or crossing drivable boundaries, improving planning safety and boundary awareness. Extensive experiments on the closed-loop NAVSIMv2 benchmark and the open-loop nuScenes benchmark demonstrate that SV-WAM achieves state-of-the-art planning performance with low inference latency and competitive zero-shot transfer capability.
  </details>

- **[LangMap: A Human-Verified Benchmark for Hierarchical Open-Vocabulary Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02220)**  `arXiv:2602.02220`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Bo Miao, Weijia Liu, Jun Luo, Lachlan Shinnick, Jian Liu, Thomas Hamilton-Smith, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-conditioned goal navigation requires embodied agents to locate user-specified targets without step-by-step guidance. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on category-level goals or rely on instance descriptions generated by vision-language models, which often contain ambiguities and semantic errors, limiting systematic and reliable evaluation. We introduce HieraNav, an open-vocabulary navigation task with goals at four hierarchical semantic levels: scene, room, region, and instance. We present Language as a Map (LangMap), the first goal navigation benchmark to enrich real-world indoor 3D scans with human-verified semantic annotations supporting tasks across all four goal levels. Built on HM3D using a contrastive annotation protocol that compares same-scene regions and instances, LangMap provides region labels and discriminative region and instance descriptions covering 414 object categories and contains over 18K tasks. Each target has concise and detailed descriptions, enabling evaluation across instruction styles. Automated and human evaluations validate our annotation quality: our descriptions improve text-to-view matching accuracy over GOAT-Bench's by 23 points on all shared annotated instances, and an independent human audit yields 92.5% unique-and-correct matches. We also propose PlaNaVid, an RGB-only baseline that combines Bounded Diverse Memory with high-level planning to prime a reactive policy for multi-goal navigation, achieving top-tier success rates without depth, 3D scene representations, or object masks. Further analyses reveal that exploration and hierarchical disambiguation failures become more prominent at finer goal levels, while long-tail categories, small objects, distant targets, timely stopping, and multi-goal completion remain challenging. Benchmark and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Disen-Forcing: Disentangling Semantic Anchoring from Motion for Autoregressive Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02822)**  `arXiv:2611.02822`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengyang Yu, Akio Hayakawa, Masato Ishii, Qingtao Yu, Xuesong Li, Takashi Shibuya, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive video diffusion models (AR-VDMs) enable real-time, long-horizon video generation but degrade rapidly once the inference horizon outruns the training horizon, due to compounding exposure bias. To stabilize long rollouts, recent methods heuristically adopt frame sink, keeping initial frames as a persistent model context, inspired by attention sinks in LLMs. We revisit this design and find that the mechanism by which initial frames stabilize long-range consistency in AR-VDMs is not an LLM-style attention sink; instead, long-range consistency is anchored through a sparse subset of attention heads, while the majority of heads predominantly capture local temporal dynamics. This observation reveals an over-conditioning pathology in existing frame-sink strategies: by universally exposing every head to the pinned initial-frame context, the model becomes prone to collapsing motion toward the low-dynamic modes of the teacher during model distillation. We propose Disen-Forcing, a distillation-time plug-in for AR-VDMs that disentangles and reinforces this head specialization through disentangled context routing and motion-disentangling regularization. On long-horizon video generation benchmarks, Disen-Forcing simultaneously improves long-range consistency and motion dynamics of base AR-VDMs, a trade-off that sink-based methods fail to balance.
  </details>

- **[Subspace Representations for Synthetic-to-Real Transfer in 3-D Surface Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02744)**  `arXiv:2611.02744`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Abdulla Nasir Chowdhury, Naish Ibrahim, Gulpi Qorik Oktagalu Pratamasunu, Kazuhiro Fukui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies what subspace representations contribute to synthetic-to-real transfer in 3-D surface inspection, where models learn from synthetic defects and the only real data before deployment are scans of good parts. We use three classical constructions as instruments: the nearest-subspace classifier, one-class template subspaces and the generalized difference subspace (GDS). On 1,197 MVTec 3D-AD scans without target annotation we find that every decision rule ranks real defects at a similar window AUC, that image-level detection stays at chance, and that no rule keeps its in-domain threshold. We show that the margin of a nearest-subspace rule moves by at most the part of the feature shift outside its class subspaces, and that this leakage, measured from unlabeled good scans, bounds the displacement observed on unseen scans. On 597 Real3D-AD objects, a one-class subspace of registered templates evaluated at every location on frozen features reaches point AUROC 0.957 on pre-registered scans and a category-mean object AUROC of 78 to 81 with no training on the benchmark. Our results suggest that a subspace helps under shift in proportion to the shift it spans, and that in reference-based inspection the reference and its registration matter more than the subspace.
  </details>

- **[CNN-Based Retinal OCT Classification: Research Gap Analysis and a From-Scratch Baseline, ED126-A](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02114)**  `arXiv:2611.02114`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Shkelqim Sherifi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated classification of retinal pathologies from Optical Coherence Tomography (OCT) images remains challenging because diagnostically relevant morphological patterns may exhibit substantial inter-class similarity. Although high classification accuracies have been reported on benchmark OCT datasets, cross-study comparison is limited by heterogeneous evaluation protocols and by the limited characterization of compact convolutional neural network (CNN) baselines trained from random initialization. This study addresses this gap through two contributions. First, it provides a focused review of CNN-based retinal OCT classification and identifies methodological considerations relevant to controlled benchmarking. Second, it presents ED126-A, a purpose-built four-class CNN baseline trained from scratch under a documented experimental protocol on the Kermany labeled OCT dataset. The model contains 1,241,380 total parameters, including 1,239,460 trainable parameters, and is evaluated using training-dynamics analysis, class-wise performance metrics, confusion-matrix analysis, confidence analysis, t-SNE representation-space visualization, Grad-CAM, hierarchical feature-map analysis, and one-vs-rest ROC/Precision-Recall analysis. On the image-level held-out test set, ED126-A achieved 95.03% accuracy, with a macro-F1 of 0.9205 and a weighted-F1 of 0.9521. The principal class-conditional limitation occurred for Drusen, which attained a recall of 0.9605 but a precision of 0.7493 and an F1-score of 0.8419. The confusion matrix identified Normal to Drusen and CNV to Drusen as major error channels. These results support the use of ED126-A as a documented from-scratch reference baseline and provide an empirical basis for controlled evaluation of transfer learning, increased depth, attention, multi-scale feature fusion, and calibration in subsequent studies.
  </details>

- **[When Does Latent Prediction Error Support Physical Surprise Claims in Video World Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01815)**  `arXiv:2611.01815`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Anton Hristov, Aleksandar Slavov, Vasil Vasilev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prediction errors from video world models are often treated as physical surprise, but they can also reflect appearance, static content, motion, or ordinary prediction difficulty. We present an evaluation framework that separates what frozen representations contain, how they organize appearance and physical differences, and what changes a model's own prediction error. On IntPhys2, a benchmark of physically possible and impossible events, we compare representations from latent prediction, masked pixel reconstruction, and simple visual statistics. Appearance remains easy to decode, while V-JEPA2 exposes modest information about whether events are possible or impossible. Looking at the latent geometry, appearance changes become smaller compared with differences between videos, their directions line up more consistently across scenes, and their overlap with physical changes decreases in deeper layers. Across action-conditioned models, incorrect actions raise prediction error, while appearance changes also produce substantial responses. In Physion++ video clips, repeating a single frame can produce higher error than the original clip, while changing color throughout the clip can lower it. Using Kubric, a simulation and rendering framework, we find that impossible events can produce lower error than possible events. A fixed error threshold also incorrectly flags many possible events as impossible. A model can therefore represent physical information and respond to incorrect actions without its native prediction error reliably measuring physical surprise.
  </details>

- **[SEALECT: Scene-Conditioned Pose Priors for Plausible Maritime Vessel Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01688)**  `arXiv:2611.01688`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Faisal Mohammad, Mohamed Younis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We address scene-conditioned object retrieval for compositional augmentation in maritime imagery, where strong geometric constraints make appearance-only retrieval produce physically implausible vessel insertions. We propose SEALECT, a retrieval framework that jointly models visual compatibility, vessel pose, scale, and scene geometry through a unified score combining neural similarity, scene-conditioned pose priors, and observed-pose consistency. We construct a maritime scene-pose library and model the pose and scale priors nonparametrically, as a kernel that conditions each prior continuously on the scene with no learned parameters. Across seven maritime benchmarks, our trained scene-conditioned retriever exceeds the strongest appearance encoder (SigLIP 2) in recall (+0.14), and the training-free prior raises geometric plausibility further at negligible recall cost, more than halving the constraint violations of appearance retrieval and outperforming learned foreground-search baselines (GALA-style, DiscoFOS). In a blind study of end-to-end composites (BootPlace placement, DiffHarmony harmonization), SEALECT ranks first among six methods, ahead of random and GALA-style selection and statistically tied with CLIP, DiscoFOS, and DINO.
  </details>

- **[Leveraging 2D Foundation Priors for Generalized Category Discovery under Test-Time Adaptation in 3D LiDAR Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01682)**  `arXiv:2611.01682`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Hanqi Chen, Chaoqi Chen, Tian Ji, Hui Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In autonomous driving, 3D LiDAR semantic segmentation is expected to adapt to evolving environments and recognize previously unseen categories, making generalized category discovery under test-time adaptation (GCD-TTA) an important problem. However, existing works treat GCD and TTA separately, while the limited semantic information in 3D point clouds makes GCD-TTA under domain shift more prone to feature collapse when combining GCD with TTA. To address this issue, we propose a two-stage framework for GCD-TTA in 3D LiDAR semantic segmentation. Specifically, in the first stage, we adaptively aggregate local 3D features according to geometric density variations and obtain reliable pseudo labels under a global class-wise prototype consistency constraint. In the second stage, we cast category discovery as an optimal transport assignment process for globally consistent grouping of novel categories. Meanwhile, semantic features from 2D vision foundation models are fused with 3D point cloud features to enrich 3D semantic information and promote a more separable feature space. Extensive experiments on Synth4D to SemanticKITTI, SynLiDAR to SemanticKITTI, and Synth4D to nuScenes demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework. Under a unified evaluation protocol, our method achieves the best overall mIoU on 9 out of 10 benchmark splits and improves the overall mIoU by up to 12.21 points over the strongest re-implemented baseline.
  </details>

- **[Reproducing INP-Former: Reconstructing Intrinsic Normal Prototypes for Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01230)**  `arXiv:2611.01230`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yunfei Liang, Juan Pablo Osorio Rivera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We reproduce the central claims of INP-Former, a reconstruction-based method for multi-class industrial anomaly detection. INP-Former extracts Intrinsic Normal Prototypes (INPs) from each test image and uses them to guide a decoder that reconstructs normal features, without a stored memory bank. Using the authors' released code, we reproduce the reported performance on MVTec AD and VisA and extend the evaluation to MVTec AD 2, where image AUROC with the default settings is 63.4, compared with 99.7 on MVTec AD. On MVTec AD, a five-seed ablation shows that most of the performance comes from the INP mechanism, while the two auxiliary losses add small changes whose direction depends on the metric. A paired comparison with both losses disabled confirms the benefit of INP on MVTec AD 2. The default of six prototypes transfers less well: the plateau beyond four prototypes reproduces on MVTec AD and VisA, but on MVTec AD 2 a single prototype outperforms six in AU-PRO on the public split and on both hidden test splits. We also measure practical trade-offs in resolution, backbone size, training length, and LoRA adaptation, and examine performance by defect size, lighting condition, and a stricter AU-PRO limit. Several conclusions from the original benchmarks, including the default prototype count, do not carry over to harder data. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Regularized Wasserstein Distance for Discrete Generative Modeling and Clustering Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00653)**  `arXiv:2611.00653`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Senmao Ye, Fei Liu, Dawei Zhang, Yuqian Zheng, Jun Zhang, Zhonglong zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we formulate clustering analysis as discrete generative modeling. Specifically, when the generator is restricted to a finite candidate space, the generated samples naturally emerge as the most representative prototypes of the underlying data distribution. To model such a discrete candidate space, we employ a Discrete Distribution Network (DDN). However, DDN suffers from unstable training due to its hard nearest-neighbor selection. To stabilize training and encourage geometric consistency, we replace DDN's l2 loss between images with regularized Wasserstein distance between the empirical distributions of real and generated images. The resulting framework, termed Discrete Generative Clustering (DGC), offers four notable advantages: 1) the learned cluster centers are directly interpretable, as they are realistic synthetic samples; 2) the inference cost scales logarithmically with the number of clusters, i.e., O(log K), making the method scalable to extremely large K; 3) the regularized Wasserstein distance provides a smooth and well-behaved optimization objective that facilitates stable convergence, whereas DDN's l2 loss tends to converge to poor local optima; and 4) DGC replaces DDN's nearest-neighbor selection with random-neighbor selection, which avoids the density shift problem during multi-step forward. Extensive experiments on several benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and desirable properties of DGC. The code is publicly available at GitHub.
  </details>

- **[Auditing GeoFM Evaluation for Field-Extent Segmentation: Label Proxies, Baselines, and When Frozen Features Match Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00054)**  `arXiv:2611.00054`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Santosh Pant_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geospatial foundation-model (GeoFM) benchmarks for agricultural field-extent segmentation often rely on cropland land-cover proxies and compare against per-pixel spectral baselines. We measure how these choices affect conclusions using identical single-date Sentinel-2 pixels from six countries. Replacing polygon-derived field labels with ESA WorldCover cropland raises random-forest AUROC from 0.55-0.82 to 0.79-0.96, showing that the proxy creates a more spectrally separable task. On the actual field-extent labels, a from-scratch U-Net reaches 0.89-0.98 AUROC and has higher point AUROC than a Prithvi frozen linear probe in five of six regions. We then compare a frozen backbone and full fine-tuning using the same decoder for Prithvi and TerraMind, with ten matched seeds. At $\varepsilon=0.02$ AUROC, 9 of 12 region$\times$model cells are TOST-equivalent. Kenya is the common failure case: positives cover roughly 0.1% of pixels, and full fine-tuning performs better for both models. Cross-region results are less favorable to frozen models. On AUROC and average precision (AP), the U-Net leads the full 30-transfer matrix, while an exploratory analysis excluding Kenya finds that both frozen decoders outperform their fine-tuned counterparts; no AUROC difference is detected between frozen TerraMind and the U-Net in that subset. For Prithvi, a frozen decoder trains about $110\times$ fewer parameters than full fine-tuning. These results argue for auditing labels and baselines before interpreting GeoFM rankings, and for testing a frozen decoder before assuming that full fine-tuning will help. Our conclusions are limited to single-date, per-pixel field-extent segmentation, not parcel-boundary or instance delineation.
  </details>

- **[SIMIT: Self-Improving Vision-Language Models via Imagination at Test-Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00053)**  `arXiv:2611.00053`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Mehmet Onurcan Kaya, Desmond Elliott, Dim P. Papadopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce SIMIT, a test-time self-improvement framework in which a vision-language model creates its own query-specific training data before answering an unlabeled test query. Our goal is to improve performance on a given target test set without annotations or external models. To pursue this goal, some test-time methods improve answers through longer reasoning or repeated sampling but leave the model unchanged. Others update the model using rewards based on majority agreement among multiple candidate answers or the model's own judgments of correctness, but such rewards can reinforce existing mistakes. SIMIT instead imagines similar problems whose solutions it controls: a model need not correctly solve the target query to construct useful practice examples. It synthesizes diverse (question, answer, image) triplets by specifying each answer before realizing its image. To support diverse multimodal tasks, SIMIT combines native image generation with an extensible skill library for structured visuals, including documents, charts, and diagrams. Within this pipeline, multi-stage verification checks that images support their assigned answers, while difficulty filtering retains informative examples. Adaptive budgeting further targets this practice by allocating more samples to queries the model is less confident in answering. The resulting data can improve the model in-context (SIMIT-ICL) or in-weight (SIMIT-FT). Across 17 diverse benchmarks, SIMIT outperforms existing self-improvement methods. Its best configuration achieves a +7.20% mean relative gain over BAGEL-7B, while training-free SIMIT-ICL achieves +6.98%.
  </details>

- **[Background Bias Assessment and Mitigation in Plant Disease Detection Using EfficientNet-B0 with Image Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00049)**  `arXiv:2611.00049`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Majed Molhi, Nishi Gupta, Ali Algumaei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plant disease detection using deep learning has achieved remarkable accuracies exceeding 99% on benchmark datasets. However, these metrics may mask a critical vulnerability: models may be taking advantage of false background correlations instead of learning real pathological features. This study presents a systematic investigation of background bias in plant disease detection through a two-stage training framework with quantitative validation. We propose a three-condition evaluation protocol testing models on original, leaf-only, and background-only images to directly measure background dependency. Our approach combines standard transfer learning using EfficientNet-B0 and manipulation of background information during optimization. Experimental results on the PlantVillage dataset (20,638 images, 15 disease classes) show that while our model achieves 98.97% accuracy on the original images and maintains 96.62% accuracy on leaf-only images, the performance drops to 23.58% on background-only images. The calculated background bias (BBC) coefficient of 0.762 indicates that a large part of the model's predictive performance depends on leaf-related features rather than background distortions. This research offers clear evidence that even with the most advanced models, environmental shortcuts can be leveraged, and this highlights the importance of bias evaluation. The approach that has been used has laid a foundation for the development of agricultural AI that is ready for deployment. The implementation, models, and evaluation process are all publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing Zero-Shot Forgetting in CLIP Fine-Tuning: Attention Drift Characterizes Adaptation, Representation Drift Predicts Transfer Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00042)**  `arXiv:2611.00042`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruize Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning CLIP on a downstream task typically reduces its zero-shot accuracy on other tasks, and quantifying this loss ordinarily requires a labeled transfer benchmark. We study whether the loss can be anticipated from unlabeled target images that are already available during fine-tuning. Four signals are compared: the entropy of CLS token attention, linear centered kernel alignment (CKA) with the pretrained model, embedding drift, and relative weight change. ViT-B/32 is adapted by full fine-tuning (Full FT) and by LoRA on EuroSAT and Oxford-IIIT Pets over four learning rates and three seeds, with an additional ViT-B/16 grid; DTD and GTSRB are held out as confirmation targets, and forgetting is measured as zero-shot retention on CIFAR-100, Food-101, and ImageNet-R. Within a fixed configuration, the change in attention entropy is strongly associated with forgetting (mean $|\rho|=0.860$). Its sign, however, depends on the dataset, the adaptation method, and the backbone, and the pooled correlation falls to 0.361. A block decomposition shows that this sign is determined almost entirely by blocks 7 to 12. CKA ranks runs by retention at $\rho=0.960$, and the ranking is already established after the first of six epochs ($\rho=0.959$). On the held-out targets, CKA reaches $\rho=0.95$, and the block account correctly predicts the sign of the attention change for both datasets. These results support a simple decision rule: CKA is suited to comparisons across configurations, whereas attention entropy is informative only within a single configuration. The evidence is limited to one model family and short training schedules.
  </details>

- **[Covariance-aware Scoring: A Geometric Correction for Vision-Language Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00024)**  `arXiv:2611.00024`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Lakpa Tamang, Saman Forouzandeh, Kamal Berahmand, Wei Shao, Hamid Beigy, Mahdi Jalili_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time adaptation (TTA) enables vision--language models such as CLIP to adapt to distribution shifts without labels or access to source data. Existing methods adapt through feature caching, prototype estimation, or the online accumulation of feature statistics, but most ultimately score a test feature against a class representative in the ambient embedding space, typically through cosine similarity. We identify this shared decision rule as a limitation under anisotropic feature distributions, which means when a small number of directions account for a large fraction of feature variance, the score can be dominated by high-variance directions that are not necessarily discriminative. In light of this, we propose CARe, a covariance-aware scoring for TTA that replaces the ambient geometry with a Mahalanobis geometry induced by the target feature second moment. Theoretically, we show that under a shared-covariance Gaussian model the covariance-aware decision direction coincides with that of the Bayes-optimal linear discriminant, with ambient-space scoring as the isotropic special case, and establish that the potential benefit of covariance-aware scoring grows with feature anisotropy. Empirically, we show that CARe improves adaptation accuracy over its base cosine scoring on both standard and fine-grained distribution-shift benchmarks across two backbones. Consistent with our analysis, the gains are largest on datasets whose features are most anisotropic and vanish as the features approach isotropy.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Quadtree Tokenization and Transformer for Adaptive Mesh PDE Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04044)**  `arXiv:2610.04044`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yilin Zhuang, Noah Zambrano, Karthik Duraisamy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quadratic attention cost of Vision Transformers (ViTs) forces a trade-off between spatial resolution and rollout horizon, particularly for fine-scale PDEs where shocks, reaction fronts, and material interfaces occupy small, evolving regions of the domain. Conventional neural surrogates also lack mechanisms to adapt resolution dynamically. We propose WAMRViT, a ViT that tokenizes inputs as balanced quadtrees using a wavelet-inspired refinement criterion, jointly encodes position and refinement level with 3D rotary positional embeddings, and regrids in cell space during inference for stable long-horizon rollouts. A multi-scale variant retains each leaf at its native source resolution and lets the model learn across resolution levels. Unlike fixed-budget adaptive-tokenization methods, WAMRViT imposes no predetermined token count and supports fully adaptive topology throughout autoregressive rollout. To our knowledge, it is the first machine-learning surrogate to natively tokenize multi-level Adaptive Mesh Refinement (AMR) data. On uniform-grid benchmarks, uniform-patch WAMRViT improves finest-level region-of-interest VRMSE over a finest-patch uniform ViT while using substantially fewer tokens. The multi-scale variant achieves the lowest first-step full-field RMSE and VRMSE on both benchmarks and improves rollout-averaged full-field and refined-region accuracy at long horizons. With parallelized regridding, its end-to-end rollout cost lies between finest-patch and approximately token-matched coarser-patch ViTs. On a complex AMR combustion problem whose finest features cannot be represented natively by the evaluated uniform-grid baselines, WAMRViT operates on adaptive cells built from the simulator hierarchy and substantially reduces finest-level error at matched transformer capacity. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Learning Steadily: Accumulating Relative Point Margin Scores for Face Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31662)**  `arXiv:2609.31662`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Guray Ozgur, Tahar Chettaoui, Eduarda Caldeira, Marco Huber, Jan Niklas Kolf, Naser Damer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Face Image Quality Assessment determines the suitability of captured face images for automated face recognition (FR), a critical capability for reliable biometric systems. Existing state-of-the-art FR-integrated FIQA methods suffer from temporal instability: as the feature space evolves during training, single-epoch quality estimates fluctuate, creating a moving target that undermines reliable quality prediction. We introduce CARPM-FIQA, a stabilization strategy for FR-integrated FIQA that accumulates relative point margin measurements, the ratio between intra-class compactness and inter-class separation, across the entire training trajectory rather than relying on single-epoch estimates. This cumulative averaging approach provides theoretically grounded advantages: reduced variance in quality estimates, improved mean squared error, and enhanced ranking stability with convergence guarantees as training progresses. Through controlled experiments on the SynFIQA dataset with labeled quality groups, we demonstrate that cumulative averaging achieves superior discriminative ability, and ablation studies across different training configurations confirm consistent improvements. Evaluated against twelve FIQA methods on eight challenging benchmarks with four FR models at two FMR thresholds, CARPM-FIQA places 4th (CARPM-FIQA(L)) and 6th (CARPM-FIQA(S)) of 17 compared methods by pAUC-EDC and AUC-EDC averaged across FR models and, after per-benchmark normalization, across benchmarks, staying within a few percent of the best method's normalized average for every FR model, providing a principled solution to training instability while maintaining the performance benefits of FR integration. More broadly, our work demonstrates that temporal aggregation strategies can stabilize training objectives in deep learning systems where target values inherently fluctuate due to evolving feature representations.
  </details>

- **[MedFL-Stress: A Systematic Robustness Evaluation of Federated Brain Tumor Segmentation under Cross-Hospital MRI Appearance Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09025)**  `arXiv:2605.09025`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Kiran Naseer, Naveed Anwer Butt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning lets hospitals train segmentation models without sharing patient data, but standard evaluation reports one number: mean Dice across all sites. That number can look competitive while one hospital runs far behind the rest. Our results suggest this gap is not primarily a federation problem. Under controlled MRI appearance shift on BraTS 2020, even a centralised model trained on all data (single seed) shows a 0.055 best-worst client gap. Under FedAvg, federation adds a further 0.030 on top of that. In our setup, appearance shift accounts for most of the gap and federation widens it. We test five methods - centralised, FedAvg, FedProx, FedBN, and q-FedAvg - under this protocol. q-FedAvg achieves the best worst-client Dice in both full-data and subsampled regimes while also maintaining the highest mean Dice among FL methods: no accuracy-fairness tradeoff in our setting, though its margin over FedAvg is not significant at five seeds (p=0.436). FedProx significantly lowers the worst client across five seeds (p=0.019). Worst-client rankings are identical across both evaluation regimes, but FedBN's worst-client spread across seeds is nearly double FedAvg's - a reliability gap that single-seed evaluation cannot see. MedFL-Stress packages this stress test as a reproducible protocol. The main practical message: report worst-client Dice and confirm rankings across evaluation regimes before drawing conclusions from any FL benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Validity-Gated Evaluation of Multimodal Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02206)**  `arXiv:2605.02206`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Abdullah Ahmad Khan, Hamid Laga, Ferdous Sohel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of training data without retraining a model from scratch. Evaluation of vision-language models relies on forget accuracy (FA), retain accuracy (RA), membership inference, and similarity to a retain-only reference, but evaluation pipelines are rarely tested. We propose a validity-gated unlearning audit (VGUA) with three checks: the forget set must be memorized without duplicate leakage into the retain set, the scorer must measure correctness, and the training code must implement the intended procedure. Applying these checks reveals that random MMBench splits leak duplicate questions, a lenient scorer hides memorization, and two coding errors remove the signal while training completes. After correcting these problems, we evaluate 234 unlearned models using six methods, three learning rates, and three backbones. Each model is compared with a retain-only reference trained using low-rank adaptation (LoRA). The results depend on update strength. Among models with RA at or above 0.70, lower FA generally indicates closer agreement with the retrained FA-RA operating point. Aggressive updates overshoot this point, collapse RA, and reverse method rankings. Relative to the original model, 78% of healthy models move closer to the retrained model's scores, whereas 94% move farther from it in activation space. Membership-inference advantage does not reliably track retraining alignment. Under option-shuffled training, unlearned models answer 25% of reordered forgotten ScienceQA questions, compared with 6% for the retain-only reference. These results show that multimodal unlearning conclusions depend on pipeline validity, update strength, and the comparison criterion.
  </details>

- **[Tune-Up Open-Weight CLIP: Optimization Framework for Self-Supervised Fine-tuning of CLIP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09859)**  `arXiv:2601.09859`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Anant Mehta, Xingyu Chen, Xiyuan Wei, Tianbao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CLIP has become a cornerstone of multimodal representation learning, yet improving its performance typically requires a prohibitively costly process of training from scratch on billions of samples. We ask a different question: Can we improve the performance of open-weight CLIP models across various downstream tasks using only existing self-supervised datasets? Unlike supervised fine-tuning, which adapts a pretrained model to a single downstream task, our setting seeks to improve general performance across various tasks. However, as both our experiments and prior studies reveal, simply applying standard training protocols starting from an open-weight CLIP model often fails, leading to performance degradation. In this paper, we introduce TuneCLIP, a self-supervised fine-tuning framework that overcomes the performance degradation. TuneCLIP has two key components: (1) a warm-up stage of recovering optimization statistics to reduce cold-start bias, inspired by theoretical analysis, and (2) a fine-tuning stage of optimizing a new contrastive loss to mitigate the penalization on false negative pairs. Our extensive experiments show that TuneCLIP consistently improves performance across model architectures and scales. Notably, it elevates leading open-weight models like SigLIP (ViT-B/16), achieving gains of up to +2.5% on ImageNet and related out-of-distribution benchmarks, and +1.2% on the highly competitive DataComp benchmark, setting a new strong baseline for efficient post-pretraining adaptation. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OpenSR-SRGAN: A Flexible Super-Resolution Framework for Multispectral Earth Observation Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10461)**  `arXiv:2511.10461`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Simon Donike, Cesar Aybar, Julio Contreras, Luis Gómez-Chova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Satellite images often contain useful measurements beyond visible red, green and blue light, but at a spatial resolution that is too coarse for some applications. Super-resolution methods estimate a finer-resolution image from these coarse observations. OpenSR-SRGAN is a modular, configuration-driven framework for training and benchmarking such models on multispectral Earth-observation data. The software enables users to train and evaluate configurable generator-discriminator architectures on arbitrary sensor band setups using concise YAML configurations, without modifying model code. All components, such as generators, discriminators, loss functions, training schedules, normalizations, and stability heuristics, are exposed through a common YAML interface. OpenSR-SRGAN makes it straightforward to reproduce experiments, compare architectures, and deploy super-resolution pipelines across diverse remote-sensing datasets.OpenSR-SRGAN supports complete end-to-end workflows with minimal setup: selecting architectures, scaling factors, band combinations, and training strategies entirely from configuration files. Although designed for remote-sensing super-resolution, its tensor-based model components can be adapted to other paired imaging data when suitable preprocessing and validation are provided.
  </details>

- **[PixelBench: Can LLMs Understand and Generate Images Pixel by Pixel?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02540)**  `arXiv:2611.02540`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Haoquan Zhang, Feiyang Liu, Juanxi Tian, Wanhua Li, Haodong Duan, Weiyang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate visual assets through text-based formats such as SVG and CAD, but these symbolic representations describe images using high-level primitives rather than explicit raster values. We ask whether LLMs can instead generate pixel images directly through their existing vocabulary, without a learned image tokenizer or decoder. We introduce PixelBench, a benchmark for text-to-pixel (T2P) and image-to-pixel (I2P) generation across multiple resolutions, together with a complementary pixel-understanding evaluation. Models generate an RGB color palette followed by a grid of indices specifying the palette color of each output pixel. In T2P, the strongest models produce recognizable images directly from text. In I2P, the strongest models outperform palette-matched downsampling and specialized pixelization algorithms at low resolutions. Pixel-understanding performance correlates strongly with generation performance. A resolution-scaling study shows that pixel-generation performance often improves initially before declining at high resolutions. Finally, we introduce Pixel Reconstruction Tuning (PRT), which augments visual instruction tuning with caption-palette-grid trajectories. We post-train Molmo2-4B from its pretraining checkpoint by mixing general instruction data with our curated PRT data, improving performance across 16 visual-centric benchmarks, indicating that explicit pixel reconstruction can provide useful auxiliary supervision.
  </details>

- **[ODC: Orthogonal Drift Correction for Improved Text-to-Image Semantic Alignment at Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00819)**  `arXiv:2611.00819`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kasra Kamali, Basim Azam, Tuan Duong Trinh, Jean Honorio, Naveed Akhtar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image models have achieved remarkable success in generating high-quality images from textual descriptions. However, they often struggle with "semantic drift," where the generated output fails to precisely align with complex or nuanced text prompts. While recent approaches have attempted to address semantic errors regarding attribute binding or object presence, there remains a gap for a more holistic method that addresses these issues by directly refining the text embeddings of the initial user prompt. In this work, we introduce Orthogonal Drift Correction (ODC), an inference-time guidance method designed to mitigate semantic drift without requiring model retraining or additional user inputs. ODC guides image generation through a two-stage process. In the first stage, it identifies the semantic drift by evaluating the initially generated image against the user prompt in a shared vision-language embedding space. It then isolates the component of this drift vector that is orthogonal to the prompt's direction and translates it back into text via a vocabulary-based surrogate mechanism. In the second stage, it produces refined text conditioning for a second generation pass by feeding both the initial text embedding and the re-embedded drift representation into an adaptive rank-reduced concept removal module. Our experiments demonstrate that ODC consistently improves prompt-image alignment, increasing CLIP scores by up to 5.4% over the baseline on the COALBench-300 and PartiPrompts benchmarks. Furthermore, on the T2I-CompBench complex subset, ODC improves the average 3-in-1 score by 4.5% over the competing methods. As a plug-and-play module, ODC offers a practical and efficient method for improving the reliability of text-to-image models. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Edge-Optimized YOLOv11n Architecture for Offline Recognition of Nigerian Sign Language Alphanumeric Gestures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00025)**  `arXiv:2611.00025`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Adeleke Adeniyan, Abimbola H. Afolayan, Olanrewaju V. Johnson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deaf inclusion across sub-Saharan society faces regional hurdles, with Nigeria as the core empirical focus. Existing recognition models demand heavy computational infrastructure. However, standard mobile phones used locally rarely exceed 1 GB to 4 GB of RAM. This study addresses constraints through a lightweight YOLO11n object-detection framework. The system provides real-time Nigerian Sign Language recognition for low-resource hardware. A benchmark dataset containing 11,208 raw gesture images was established. The data spans 33 static classes collected from five signers. Partitioning isolates signers S1 through S3 for training. Signer S4 provides validation data. Signer S5 is reserved for testing. Spatial augmentations are restricted strictly to the training set. This yields 23,509 training images. The validation and test sets each maintain 1,681 images across varied environments. The framework integrates a quantization-aware compression objective. Loss functions merge with squared Frobenius-norm penalties to govern weight distortion. A Spatial-Density Occlusion Index quantifies background complexity and self-occlusion. A latency-profiling equation relates floating-point operations and memory bandwidth to chipset clock speed. Performance testing on physical Android hardware utilizes unquantized FP32 weights. Mean inference latencies span 405.5 ms to 1,759.4 ms. Measured against test data, the algorithm attains a Precision of 0.9975. Recall reaches 0.9991. Mean Average Precision is 0.9942. Outcomes support offline functionality on constrained mobile devices. Practical deployment operates through a mobile utility using CameraX. The application captures gestures in real time. Recognized signs display as text instantly.
  </details>

- **[Parsed Text Understates Vision-Language Models: From Below to Above Expert Agreement on Fine-Grained Facial Emotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08162)**  `arXiv:2610.08162`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Tobias Hallmen, Fabian Deuser, Robin-Nico Kampa, Norbert Oswald, Elisabeth André_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EmoNet-Face-HQ is a benchmark of 2,500 generated portraits, each rated by four psychology experts on 40 emotion categories. It concludes that vision-language models (VLMs) on average offer no dependable improvement over random guessing and that a fine-tuned model is required. The benchmark reads each model's answer from generated text, and its zero-shot prompt demands a fixed number of emotions per face. Its own results already show that this protocol can decide the outcome: one VLM moves from chance to close to expert agreement when only the prompt changes. We keep the images, expert ratings and metric and change only how the answer is asked for and read: one yes/no question per category, scored by the probability of "yes". Scored from parsed text on the five categories experts agree on most, eleven open-weight VLMs average a quadratic-weighted $\kappa$ of 0.425 against individual experts, below the 0.468 at which two experts agree. Scored from yes-probabilities, the same models average 0.553, and all eleven agree with an individual expert better than a second expert does. The VLMs' mean is level with the benchmark's fine-tuned model (0.555), and three of them significantly exceed it without any training. Only one exceeds a stricter reference, the mean of three experts scored against the fourth (0.563). Verdicts that VLMs fail at graded facial affect ratings may therefore describe the readout more than the model. A fine-tuned model should have to beat a pretrained VLM read this way before it is called necessary, and the benchmark's own does not clearly do so.
  </details>

- **[Scene-Adaptive Readout and Decoding in Diffusion Driving VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02076)**  `arXiv:2611.02076`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Seungjun Yu, Jiho Choi, Inha Kang, Hojun Choi, Seungwook Kim, Hyunjung Shim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion driving vision-language-action models repeatedly execute the backbone at every denoising stage, incurring substantial inference cost, while it remains unclear whether all scenes require the same decoding computation. To distinguish when a trajectory becomes readable from how much further refinement it requires, we analyze trajectory readout across network depth and decoding progress. We identify a depth-decoding dissociation in which late-layer representations already support useful trajectory readout after the first forward sweep, whereas early-layer representations benefit more from continued decoding. We further find that both the preferred readout layer and decoding depth vary across scenes. Based on these observations, we propose a scene-adaptive readout and decoding framework. A scene-conditioned readout MoE adaptively weights and combines trajectory predictions from layer-specific late-layer representations after a single backbone sweep, while an adaptive decoding policy applies additional native refinement only when required by the scene. We evaluate our framework on two diffusion driving VLAs across the Waymo E2E and nuScenes benchmarks. Our method improves validation RFS by up to 0.56 while also improving test performance, and achieves an end-to-end speedup of up to $6.0\times$ on Waymo E2E, while consistently reducing trajectory error on nuScenes. These results demonstrate that scene-level readout and decoding allocation improves planning quality while avoiding unnecessary decoding. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[What Does a Safety Label Measure? Auditing Situational-Safety Benchmarks for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01125)**  `arXiv:2611.01125`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Wayner Barrios, Kenneth Barrios_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Situational-safety benchmarks label each scene SAFE or UNSAFE and score a multimodal large language model (MLLM) against that label, usually by judging its free-form answer, and these scores guide deployment. We ask what such a label measures. A label score can come from the scene, the request or the benchmark's own files. We propose the Benchmark Card, seven measured fields that tell them apart, and fill it for six benchmarks. Only MSSBench chat leaks the label through its image files: image size alone reaches 82.3% accuracy, matching or beating six of seven commercial models. Elsewhere the signal outside the scene comes from design. In MOSSBench the item partition fixes the label, and on HoliSafe the request alone ranks pairs about as well as the scene. On our corrected chat split, the size rule falls to chance. We then introduce the Spot-Stop decomposition, which compares ranking with labelling on pairs of a safe and an unsafe scene. It splits the gap above a label-swap null into three parts: a threshold part, overlap between safe and unsafe scores, and order that holds only within a pair. Most of fourteen open MLLMs rank better than they label, beyond what their AUROC predicts. On chat, one shared threshold repairs a third to a half of the excess, and label-free recentering recovers it on balanced batches. Recentering on unlabelled scenes of the same situation improves ranking on VSCBench for 13 of 14 models.
  </details>

- **[MoviEdit: Long-form Multi-Shot Video Editing Made Easy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02737)**  `arXiv:2611.02737`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Suhyeon Lee, Zhengang Li, Soo Ye Kim, Peihao Zhu, Jong Chul Ye, Chun-Hao Paul Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form multi-shot video editing requires an intended edit to remain coherent across abrupt changes in scene, viewpoint, and subject appearance, while processing substantially longer visual context than conventional single-shot editors. We introduce MoviEdit, an end-to-end framework for scalable long-form multi-shot video editing that jointly addresses the challenges of data, modeling, and evaluation. To provide cross-shot supervision data at scale, we develop automatic data-generation pipelines spanning synchronized multi-view footage, real-world multi-shot videos, and generated multi-shot content, yielding 87K paired editing videos without manual annotation. Architecturally, MoviEdit employs a cascaded bidirectional-autoregressive design: a lightweight bidirectional stage first establishes a globally coherent editing draft over a spatiotemporally compressed representation, which is then autoregressively propagated and refined over the full video. We further introduce MoviEdit-Bench, a dedicated benchmark for systematic evaluation of long-form multi-shot video editing. Extensive experiments show that MoviEdit substantially outperforms existing open-source models and performs on par with industry frontier models across text-guided and image-guided editing. Visit our project page for complete results and to access the benchmark:this https URL
  </details>

- **[DTP-OPSD: Reinforcement Learning with Distractor-Aware Temporal-Privilege On-Policy Self-Distillation for Temporal Video Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00120)**  `arXiv:2611.00120`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaoyu Chen, Yongwei Nie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal Video Grounding (TVG) aims to localize the start and end times of an event described by a language query in a video. The task becomes particularly challenging when the same video contains multiple repeated, similar, or opposite events. On these hard examples, all responses sampled by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) may miss the target and receive zero temporal Intersection-over-Union (tIoU). Their group-relative advantages are then all zero, leaving no useful policy gradient signal. To address this, we propose DTP-OPSD, a reinforcement learning framework with distractor-aware temporal-privilege on-policy self-distillation. The student observes the clean video and generates responses on-policy, while a shared-weight teacher observes a privileged video in which the target window is marked by red full-frame borders and same-video distractor windows by blue full-frame borders. For the distillation loss, the teacher evaluates student-generated prefixes and provides dense token-level Jensen-Shannon divergence supervision. This supervision remains informative even when GRPO provides no policy gradient signal. We suppress distillation when the teacher does not outperform the student and strengthen it for groups where the teacher performs better but all student rollouts receive zero tIoU. We also construct a compact hard-interference training set through VLM screening and human verification. With only 300 additional post-training examples, DTP-OPSD achieves the highest mIoU among the compared open-source 8B models on Charades-TimeLens, ActivityNet-TimeLens, and QVHighlights-TimeLens, reaching 55.93, 54.77, and 66.71, respectively. Compared with Pure GRPO trained on the same data, DTP-OPSD improves all 12 reported metrics, with R1@0.7 gains of 1.28, 1.62, and 1.78 points on the three benchmarks. Code will be released atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[A Trustworthy Clinical Decision Support Framework for Brain Tumor MRI Classification: Integrating Attention-Guided Learning, Explainable AI, and Out-of-Distribution Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00034)**  `arXiv:2611.00034`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Arjun Kumar Gupta, Harminder Pal Monga_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for brain tumor diagnosis need to do more than achieve high classification accuracy. For practical use, they should also provide understandable predictions, recognize unsuitable inputs, and work within a usable clinical system. This paper presents a framework for classifying brain MRI into four categories: glioma, meningioma, pituitary tumor, and no tumor. The model uses InceptionResNetV2 with a Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM), while Grad-CAM provides visual explanations of its predictions. A two-stage out-of-distribution (OOD) mechanism screens unsuitable inputs using an initial grayscale check followed by Mahalanobis distance, softmax confidence, and predictive entropy. These components are brought together in NeuroScan AI, a real-time web application. Evaluated on a Mendeley brain tumor MRI dataset of 12,064 images, the framework achieved 98.70% validation accuracy, 96.73% test accuracy, and a macro-average AUC of 0.9965. The OOD pipeline achieved 99% detection accuracy on a preliminary 100-image benchmark. Grad-CAM visualizations showed qualitative correspondence with clinically established tumor locations across all four classes. Overall, the work brings classification, explainability, input validation, and deployment into one framework, while recognizing that broader clinical and OOD validation is still required.
  </details>

- **[ReLATE: Learning Relative Representations for Post-Hoc Multimodal Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16834)**  `arXiv:2605.16834`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shiwon Kim, JaeSeong Hong, Yu Rang Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-hoc multimodal alignment connects independently pretrained vision and language encoders while keeping them frozen, offering an alternative to joint pretraining when paired data are scarce. A central design challenge is to construct comparable representations across heterogeneous feature spaces while retaining local information relevant to distinct semantic concepts within each input. We propose Relative Alignment with Learnable Anchors and Token Evidence (ReLATE), which learns modality-specific anchors that jointly define relative representations and determine token contributions to each relative coordinate. Image patches and text tokens are represented by their similarities to anchors within their respective frozen feature spaces. Anchor-wise Similarity Pooling (ASP) then weights and aggregates these similarities separately for each anchor to construct the corresponding relative coordinate. A sample-level symmetric contrastive objective aligns the resulting image and text representations by optimizing only the anchors, without updating the encoders. Experiments on 13 general-domain benchmarks across four vision-language tasks demonstrate improvements over the compared post-hoc alignment methods under matched paired-data budgets, with further evaluations across data scales and encoder combinations. Beyond the general domain, we evaluate ReLATE in pathology as a specialized-domain application. By aligning frozen pathology image and biomedical language encoders, ReLATE matches or exceeds the evaluated pathology-specific vision-language models in balanced accuracy on three zero-shot classification benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01467)**  `arXiv:2611.01467`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.
  </details>

- **[LF-VideoQA: Evaluating Long-Video Understanding with Long-Form Theory-of-Mind Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00383)**  `arXiv:2611.00383`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shaden Shaar, Bradon Thymes, Majd Aldaye, Claire Cardie, Bharath Hariharan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The hardest part of understanding a long video is not pinpointing any one moment but synthesizing many, e.g., answering a question that requires tracking what develops across hours of content and articulating how the pieces fit together. To date, most long-video QA datasets require only a short answer or a multiple-choice selection, oversimplifying the long-video understanding task to retrieval. We introduce LF-VideoQA, a dataset for long-form, open-ended question answering over long narrative videos built on theory-of-mind questions: a character's belief depends on everything they have seen or been told, so each question demands assembling and reasoning from evidence distributed across the video. The dataset contains more than 6,300 questions on 94 recent films; each requires reasoning over scenes spanning an average of 23 minutes and comes with a question-specific rubric for recall and precision scoring. The dataset is created entirely automatically by annotating screenplays with scene events and each character's first-order beliefs and meta-beliefs, then generating (question, answer, rubric) triples through LLM-based question generation and verification; human studies validate their quality. We evaluate four families of state-of-the-art open-source VLMs across three settings and find the task highly challenging: (i) even an oracle with access to the screenplay falls well short of complete answers; (ii) the best non-oracle model attains barely half of the recall level of the oracle; (iii) unlike on prior long-video benchmarks, captions and subtitles underperform the video itself, indicating that the questions need visual evidence; and (iv) recall is the predominant bottleneck, with answers omitting many required rubric claims.
  </details>

- **[ClinFusion: A Vision-Centric Multimodal LLM System for Holistic Medical Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24743)**  `arXiv:2607.24743`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hangjie Yuan, Yichen Qian, Zhiwei Tang, Xianzhe Xu, Sicheng Yang, Lirong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) hold immense potential to revolutionize clinical practice, yet deploying them in the medical domain is fundamentally a vision-centric challenge: models must absorb knowledge from heterogeneous 2D and 3D medical images, and evaluation protocols must align with radiologists' clinical practice and provide an accurate, fine-grained and factualness-driven assessment. In this paper, we introduce ClinFusion, a vision-centric MLLM designed for holistic medical understanding that systematically addresses these limitations. We propose a compositional and cascaded vision encoder architecture featuring a Cascade Spatial-Aware Locality Fusion operator that unifies diverse 2D and native 3D medical image understanding within a fused encoder. We further introduce a vision-grounded evaluation framework, including MedIF-Bench for instruction-following assessment and a region-of-interest-grounded method for clinically aligned and factualness-driven report generation evaluation. We show that ClinFusion sets a new state-of-the-art across a comprehensive suite of 2D and 3D multimodal medical benchmarks---spanning visual question answering, report generation, and instruction following---as well as textual medical tasks, outperforming leading open-source medical MLLMs (\textit{e.g.}, Hulu-Med, Lingshu) on 20 out of 24 benchmarks and demonstrating multimodal capabilities better than powerful proprietary models such as GPT-5.2 and Gemini-3-Flash on 13 out of 16 benchmarks, and can be further augmented with agentic tool use for retrieval-augmented and tool-assisted clinical workflows. A blinded evaluation by board-certified radiologists confirms that ClinFusion produces the highest-ranked reports, and validates our RoI-grounded metric as achieving the strongest correlation with expert judgment among all automatic evaluation metrics examined.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchy-Aware Multimodal Unlearning for Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09867)**  `arXiv:2512.09867`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fengli Wu, Vaidehi Patil, Jaehong Yoon, Yue Zhang, Mohit Bansal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used in sensitive domains such as medical AI, where privacy regulations, including HIPAA and GDPR, require the removal of specific individuals' or institutions' data. This motivates machine unlearning, which aims to remove the influence of target data from a trained model. However, existing unlearning benchmarks fail to reflect the hierarchical and multimodal structure of real-world medical data, limiting their ability to properly evaluate unlearning in practice. Therefore, we introduce MedForget, a hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning benchmark that models data from medical institutions as a nested structure, enabling fine-grained evaluation across retain and forget splits. Experiments show that current unlearning methods struggle to achieve effective hierarchy-aware forgetting without degrading retained utility, measured by performance on clinically relevant prediction tasks. To address this limitation, we propose Cross-modal Hierarchy-Informed Projection for unlearning (CHIP), a training-free, hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning method that deletes information by selectively removing target-specific weight subspaces while preserving sibling-shared information. Our results show that CHIP achieves the highest forget-retain performance gap across all hierarchy levels while maintaining competitive downstream utility compared to existing methods. Overall, MedForget provides a practical benchmark for evaluating hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning for medical data, while CHIP offers an effective solution that generalizes across architectures and balances deletion with utility.
  </details>

- **[MIRA: Reinforcing Multimodal Reasoning via Deceptive Contextual Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03087)**  `arXiv:2611.03087`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhihan Yin, Jianxin Liang, Yifeng Yao, Nonghai Zhang, and Bingyue Peng, Chunguang Qie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has significantly advanced multimodal reasoning, existing frameworks suffer from a critical "perception-reasoning gap." Due to an over-reliance on seemingly relevant visual tokens, even minor perceptual perturbations can propagate through the reasoning process, leading to compounding hallucinations. To address this issue, we propose MIRA, a training framework that improves robustness to erroneous visual contexts while enhancing logical reasoning ability. By injecting filtered, deceptive visual contexts during training, we construct challenging "hard traps" that stress-test the model's robustness to misleading information. Furthermore, we introduce a group-wise reflection-triggering mechanism that enables the model to autonomously detect and rectify inconsistencies between visual cues and logical constraints, which is ultimately internalized as an intrinsic policy behavior. Importantly, MIRA does not rely on human annotations. Experimental results across multiple reasoning and perception benchmarks show that MIRA yields an average 7% improvement on in-domain benchmarks, with consistent improvements on vision-centric benchmarks. These findings highlight that active error recovery is essential for robust multimodal reasoning. Comprehensive ablation studies and analyses further provide insights into why MIRA is effective.
  </details>

- **[Training-Time Domain Transfer for Generator-Free Diagnostics on Novel Modalities in Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02931)**  `arXiv:2611.02931`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Julia A. Mielcarz, Andrea Camilloni, Nita Mulliqi, Jessica A. Frank, Anders Blilie, Emilius A.M. Janssen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Novel imaging modalities promise improved diagnostics, but AI development is constrained by scarce training data. Inference-time domain transfer can address this by mapping novel modalities to established ones, enabling reuse of existing diagnostic models. However, this introduces hallucination risks, computational overhead, and regulatory complexity. Inspired by sim-to-real transfer, we propose training-time domain transfer, generating synthetic target-domain training data from archived routine samples to enable direct diagnosis on real inputs without inference-time generation. Using unstained prostate histopathology as a proof-of-concept, we compare this approach with inference-time virtual staining and conventional H&E-based cancer detection. Training-time transfer achieved higher concordance with the conventional benchmark than virtual staining for both raw (Cohen's $\kappa$=0.59 vs. 0.37; Gwet's AC1=0.73 vs. 0.29) and deparaffinized tissue ($\kappa$=0.66 vs. 0.61; AC1=0.73 vs. 0.64), supporting its potential for emerging diagnostic modalities.
  </details>

- **[Risk-Constrained Evaluation of Financial Document Recognition: A Formal Framework for Evidence, Critical Errors, and Selective Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02775)**  `arXiv:2611.02775`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Subodh Lonkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial document recognition is commonly evaluated using character and word error rates, which do not adequately represent the consequences of errors in financial workflows. This paper formalizes financial document recognition as a risk-constrained evidence-recovery problem. It defines typed, evidence-linked financial facts and a multi-layer quality model spanning text, layout, tables, fields, semantic relations, calibration, robustness, and operational performance. It introduces non-compensatory acceptance gates based on upper confidence bounds for critical-field errors, and formulates selective automation as a constrained risk-coverage problem with review-capacity limits. The paper also specifies annotation requirements, leakage-resistant benchmark splits, statistical estimands, and a reproducibility card. Formal propositions establish why aggregate scores cannot certify critical-field reliability and why zero observed errors do not imply zero risk. A synthetic example illustrates the decision rule. The work is theoretical; no empirical benchmark or model performance claim is presented.
  </details>

- **[Probe-Guided Gradient Balancing for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02651)**  `arXiv:2611.02651`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Vu Vo, Haytham M. Fayek, Thuy Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jointly trained multimodal networks frequently underutilize weaker modalities, as faster-learning modalities dominate the shared fusion objective and suppress gradients reaching slower ones. Most existing approaches throttle dominant modalities, whereas directly boosting weaker modalities often fails because monitoring signals derived from the joint loss remain dominated by stronger modalities. We propose Probe-Guided Gradient Boosting (PGGB), a gradient modulation approach that boosts weaker modalities while preserving the strength of dominant ones. PGGB attaches lightweight linear classifiers to stop-gradient features from each modality to estimate representation quality independently. The gap between these scores, termed the utilization gap, provides an imbalance signal used to adaptively scale weaker-modality gradients through bounded and smoothed modulation. PGGB is composable with throttling methods, as it leaves the loss function and fusion architecture unchanged. Experiments across eight benchmarks spanning four domains and 2-4 modalities demonstrate that PGGB outperforms state-of-the-art methods on highly imbalanced multimodal datasets while remaining competitive under low or negligible imbalance. We further establish bounded scaling, self-attenuation, and a descent bound for standard SGD under smoothness and bounded-variance assumptions. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AgiWorld: An Agentic Video World Model for Building Persistent Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02341)**  `arXiv:2611.02341`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shaoheng Fang, Yiqun Mei, Hanwen Jiang, Xin Sun, Haitian Zheng, Hao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent interactive video world models generate visually compelling videos from a single image, following user-provided camera motion and text prompts. However, such low-level control demands substantial user effort to realize a high-level intent, and the generated world struggles to remain persistent as the exploration proceeds. In this work, we present AgiWorld, an agentic video world model that explores and builds a persistent world from a single image and a free-form instruction. For high-level planning, our agent AgiWorld-Agent designs the world and the journey through it in a world state and directs the exploration. For low-level realization, our video world model AgiWorld-WM generates the video chunk by chunk and stores it with its geometry in a spatial memory, from which the agent updates its world state. This closed loop keeps the world faithful to the user's intent and keeps the agent's world state consistent with the generated content throughout the exploration. We co-design the two levels around the shared spatial memory and an interface of metric camera trajectories and structured prompts. In AgiWorld-WM, to handle occlusion and accumulated geometric errors in the spatial memory over long rollouts, we propose occlusion-aware history selection, which retrieves past views by surface visibility, and reliability-based memory maintenance, which prefers reliable geometry and earlier observations. AgiWorld-WM achieves state-of-the-art overall performance on world revisiting and exploration benchmarks, and qualitative results and ablations show that AgiWorld builds persistent worlds from free-form instructions over minute-long journeys.
