Persistence thus arises not from the video model alone but from its closed loop with the agent.
  </details>

- **[Forecasting Earth Surface Change in Embedding Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02211)**  `arXiv:2611.02211`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jingtao Li, Sadiq Jaffer, Anil Madhavapeddy, Pedro Sousa, Anthony Ortiz, Kyle Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated satellite imagery permits understanding of how our planet changes over time, letting us predict its future and plan mitigating actions. Most existing studies that rely on a time series of satellite observations forecast the spectral values at each pixel for a future target date. However, clouds, shadows, and sensor noise often corrupt these observations, so that spectral forecasters waste much of their capacity on low-level features or noise rather than real change. With the advent of Earth foundation models, we argue that forecasting in embedding space is a feasible and more effective alternative. To this end, we design a token space that handles irregular histories from different sensors and foundation models and supports forecasts at any query date. Tokens from this space are inputs to our proposed forecaster, E$^2$Forecast, which is a transformer with two branches: a Gaussian branch that directly predicts a mean point forecast and per-dimension standard deviations, and a rectified-flow branch that generates forecast ensembles. On Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 benchmarks, forecasting embeddings instead of spectral values halves the cosine distance (from 0.20 to 0.10) and greatly reduces overconfidence, lowering the z-score standard deviation from 2.63 to 1.22. By comparing new observations with its forecasts, E$^2$Forecast can also detect anomalous changes without labels or retraining, raising F$_1$ from 41.58 to 48.70 on a global benchmark. This consistent superiority is validated on nine remote sensing foundation models. Beyond change detection, we believe our approach advances the field by permitting effective reuse of diverse pretrained task heads. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reconstruction as Spatial Evaluation: Diagnosing Cross-View Spatial Intelligence in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02026)**  `arXiv:2611.02026`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junfei Wu, Qiuyue Wang, Xuhong Huang, Qihang Peng, Mingsheng Li, Jian Guan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The spatial intelligence of large vision-language models (LVLMs) requires the ability to combine local visual observations into a geometrically consistent representation of the physical world. Many static benchmarks assess isolated spatial judgments, such as relative direction or distance, while interactive embodied evaluations combine perception with exploration, planning, control, and simulator-specific execution. To bridge this gap, we introduce RECONSPATIAL, a benchmark centered on multi-view 3D scene reconstruction across indoor, tabletop, and outdoor environments. Given real multi-view images and target-object queries, LVLMs are required to jointly predict oriented 3D bounding boxes in a shared, camera-anchored metric coordinate frame. Reconstructions are evaluated along two complementary axes: Structure for relative layout and Metric for physical scale and camera-anchored placement. Nine atomic spatial QA tasks, grouped into Geometric Perception and Spatial Relations, probe spatial capabilities involved in joint reconstruction. Offline navigation extends evaluation to path planning. Evaluation of 18 frontier LVLMs reveals a consistent gap between relative scene structure and metric reconstruction; even GPT-6-Astra scores 81.4% on Structure but only 58.7% on Metric. Tracing this gap, diagnostic analyses link atomic spatial capabilities to reconstruction quality and show that relational consistency alone does not ensure metric accuracy. Experiments with 3D map injection further separate scene construction from path planning, revealing how these geometric errors limit navigation success. Finally, spatial fine-tuning yields broad transfer across both spatial and general vision-language benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SurgStream: A Workflow-Anchored Proactive Streaming Framework and Benchmark for Real-Time Surgical Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01982)**  `arXiv:2611.01982`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruipeng Ma, Qing Xu, Jie Liang, Zhen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical video understanding is essential for intraoperative assistance such as workflow monitoring, instrument preparation and procedural documentation. Delivering such assistance in real time requires a model to jointly perceive the ongoing scene, remember past surgical events, and respond before clinically relevant moments pass. Existing approaches provide limited support for jointly retaining procedural evidence and deciding when to respond in streaming surgical video. In this work, we propose SurgStream, a workflow-anchored proactive streaming framework for real-time surgical video understanding. We first introduce Workflow-Anchored Memory (WAM) to record structured instrument-action-target observations and fold them into phase-level summaries, providing compact and queryable surgical memory. Deadline-aware Transition Learning (DTL) is then devised to supervise response states within clinical windows, enabling timely responses before procedural deadlines. We further introduce SurgStream-Bench, comprising 11.7K streaming interactions across various tasks covering real-time perception, backward memory queries, forward proactive QA, proactive alerts and caption memory. Experiments on SurgStream-Bench show gains over state-of-the-art methods, especially in memory and forward proactive QA. The source code will be available.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge-Guided Floodwater Level Reasoning for Cloud-Edge Collaborative Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01970)**  `arXiv:2611.01970`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Ziyi Geng, Lu Tao, Qian Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frequent urban floodwater poses a serious threat to the safety and efficiency of intelligent transportation systems. Existing vision-based methods for floodwater level detection face two main limitations. First, the lack of explicit reasoning leads to poor detection performance and little interpretability. Second, these methods struggle to generalize to the complex and varied real-world road scenes seen from different viewpoints. To address this, we reformulate the task as an explicit visual reasoning problem and propose HydroReasoner, a floodwater level reasoning framework designed for cloud-edge collaboration. HydroReasoner builds on Qwen2.5-VL and is trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization. To address the scarcity of reasoning process annotations, we build a floodwater domain knowledge graph HydroKG and design a novel KG path reward. Through deterministic graph traversal over HydroKG, this reward supervises the model's reasoning process at a fine granularity, which improves both reasoning and generalization. For edge deployment, we apply 4-bit quantization to the model, cutting GPU memory usage by 57% and inference latency by 43% while out-of-domain accuracy drops by only 0.2 percentage points. We also propose a confidence-based cloud-edge routing mechanism, which improves system safety and eases the computational load on the cloud. In addition, we build HydroLevel, a floodwater benchmark dataset of 3,000 images, for training and evaluating the model. Experimental results show that HydroReasoner achieves detection accuracy 4.0 and 13.6 percentage points higher than conventional vision baselines in the in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations respectively, with performance dropping by only 4.4 percentage points out of domain.
  </details>

- **[dKV Forcing: Separating Generation and Memory Representations through a Differentiable KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01648)**  `arXiv:2611.01648`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqi Li, Bo Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A causal video generator produces two key--value (KV) states for every segment: the KV it uses while denoising that segment, and the KV it writes into the cache for later segments to read. In a pretrained generator the two already differ substantially, later segments attend to them differently, and replacing the written KV with the generation KV degrades the continuation. We propose dKV Forcing (dKV denotes differentiable KV) to learn a dedicated memory representation from its future use: a writer-specific low-rank residual on the shared backbone separates memory writing from generation and is trained through the future reads of what it writes. Learning a write from its future use needs gradients through the cache, which the mutable cache in common use cannot provide. dKV Forcing replaces the training-time cache by persistent differentiable pages with local rematerialization and, in one temporal traversal, computes exactly the gradient that a two-pass reconstruction of the same objective computes. One traversal computes the full memory gradient at $7\%$ generator-update overhead over detached-write training, with $28\%$ less resident memory and $20.8\%$ shorter training cycles than the two-pass reconstruction. Over Self Gradient Forcing (SGF), the same objective trained with the same traversal, writer adaptation raises VBench-Long aesthetic quality by $6.6$ points at 60\,s and $8.5$ points at 240\,s with the highest subject and background consistency, and rollout dropout of the residual keeps most of this gain while restoring the motion of training without write gradients.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Urdu Nastaliq Handwriting Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01635)**  `arXiv:2611.01635`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haseeb Yaqoob, Fatima Azfar, Emaan Arif Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Handwritten Urdu is hard to recognize because the cursive Nastaliq script has no fixed baseline and no clear character boundaries. We benchmark six systems on the Urdu Nastaliq Handwritten Dataset (UNHD): Tesseract (default configuration), a script-specific recognizer trained on printed Urdu (UTRNet-Large), two open-source vision-language models (VLMs), and two commercial VLMs. All six systems are evaluated without fine-tuning on 2,465 single-line images (Part A), and the four VLMs receive the same fixed prompt (decoding uses temperature 0 where configurable, except for Claude Sonnet 5, whose decoding cannot be configured). Both commercial VLMs reach lower error than the printed-Urdu recognizer and the open-source VLMs. We do not compare against handwriting-trained recognizers, which would likely do better in-domain; we characterize zero-shot behavior, not state of the art. We also describe three distinct failure modes among the ranked systems. In one of them, a commercial model explicitly declines to transcribe a few very hard-to-read images instead of guessing. In a second study (Part B), VLMs correct the output of a separate OCR system using both the source image and the noisy text. Once separated from each model's own reading ability, the benefit is small on Tesseract's output (0.7% to 7.4%) and larger (17.9% and 21.9%) only for the two commercial correctors given UTRNet's text. It appears to depend on how legible the supplied text already is, and a weaker corrector can make a more legible text worse.
  </details>

- **[PhoneValue-Bench: A Multimodal Benchmark and Datasets for Consumer Smartphone Recommerce Valuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00791)**  `arXiv:2611.00791`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihan Deng, Jun Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As consumer smartphone recommerce scales, buyers must quote binding repurchase prices from seller-uploaded multi-view photos and diagnostic screenshots before devices reach the warehouse. Accurate remote valuation requires fusing noisy cosmetic evidence with identity, battery, lock, and channel risk from screenshots and staff forms, since an error in any modality changes both grade and payout. However, existing multimodal resources mainly target general VLM competence, screen reading, or factory defect detection, and largely overlook staff-aligned trade-in fields, dual appearance/staff ground truth, and executable valuation scorecards. This gap stems from two challenges: (1) the lack of large-scale real trade-in data with audited dual annotations and a compact ranking benchmark kept separate from training labels, and (2) the need to jointly reason over unordered multi-view cosmetics and diagnostic screens under operational deduction rules. To address these challenges, we propose PhoneValue-Bench, a dual-GT evaluation suite with tier-gated scorecards, together with PhoneValue-Corpus, a matched weakly labeled training set of over twenty thousand orders and hundreds of thousands of captioned images. We further present ValueAgent, a staged perception-fusion-valuation reference pipeline aligned with production inspection, plus an executable public scorer and submission schema. Experiments show that ValueAgent reaches a measured 84.1/100 on Bench-Full, with 97.0 appearance fill and 54.1 staff-field fill, establishing a practical dual-GT benchmark for multimodal recommerce valuation.
  </details>

- **[Words Before Pixels: Selective Modality Routing for Vision-Language Model Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00608)**  `arXiv:2611.00608`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Laura Yao, Haochen Zhang, Jinhao Duan, Sijia Liu, Tianlong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language model (VLM) unlearning is often treated as an objective-design problem: given multimodal forget data, the goal is to optimize a loss that removes undesirable behavior while preserving retained capabilities. We argue that this view overlooks an equally important data-centric question: how should each forget example be represented before optimization? Existing methods typically keep image-conditioned failures in their original image-text form, implicitly assuming that pixels are the right route for unlearning whenever pixels appear in the input. We challenge this assumption by constructing modality-decomposed versions of VLM benchmarks, enabling controlled comparisons among image-only, text-only, and multimodal representations of the same examples. Our modality-mixing experiments show that unsafe instruction-following behavior is more effectively suppressed through textual representations than through images alone, while purely text-only unlearning can still weaken visual grounding when the target behavior depends on visual evidence. Motivated by this trade-off, we introduce ModRoute, a gradient-guided per-sample modality routing strategy that selectively converts high visual-pressure examples to text-only form while keeping the remaining examples multimodal. Notably, on VLGuard with LLaVA-1.5-7B, ModRoute has the strongest composite unlearning-utility score of 0.847 at $\gamma=0.5$, a 2.5% improvement over the best random-switching score and $\geq$46% better than pure multimodal or text unlearning. Overall, our results show that effective VLM unlearning depends not only on the objective, but also on the composition and per-sample modality routing of the unlearning data.
  </details>

- **[A Novel Lightweight Multi-Scale Branching Encoder for Few-Shot Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00099)**  `arXiv:2611.00099`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Oriod Malo, Alexandre Xavier Falcão, Laurent Najman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-Shot Learning (FSL) addresses classification scenarios in which only a small number of labeled examples are available for each class. This paper presents the Sum12 Multi-Scale branching encoder, a lightweight convolutional architecture designed to improve the embedding stage of Prototypical Networks without substantially increasing computational cost. The encoder uses seven parallel convolutional branches whose kernel-size combinations sum to 12, allowing local and broader spatial structures to be represented within a unified Multi-Scale feature space. The proposed architecture was evaluated on Omniglot, CIFAR- FS, FC100, and miniImageNet under controlled few-shot learning protocols and compared with the original Prototypical Network encoder and Inception-inspired branch variants. Additional ablation studies have investigated fusion strategies, dilated convolutions, branch compositions, and receptive field budget. Beyond few-shot learning, the encoder was also tested on the MedMNIST 2D benchmark to assess its behavior in conventional supervised medical image classification. Across these experiments, Sum12 generally improved the low-shot classification performance with respect to the basic ProtoNet encoder while preserving a compact model size. These properties suggest that the proposed encoder may be suitable for low-data visual recognition and resource-constrained intelligent imaging applications. However, complete end-to- end validation on the target embedded platform, including resolution of the observed implementation discrepancies, remains future work.
  </details>

- **[ReactionBench: A Human-Anchored Benchmark for Modeling Reactions to Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00050)**  `arXiv:2611.00050`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yibo Zhao, Ao Qu, Xuan Jiang, Jiahao Xu, Keane Ong, Hang Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding what happens in a video is not sufficient for socially responsive multimodal systems: such systems also aim to relate what a person observes to how that person responds. Existing video benchmarks largely evaluate events, temporal reasoning, or people depicted in the video, while affective and viewer-response datasets often focus on emotion labels, aggregate responses, or prediction from the stimulus alone. This leaves an underexplored question: can current multimodal models ground an observed viewer reaction in the stimulus context associated with it? We introduce ReactionBench, a human-anchored benchmark for reaction-specific video understanding. Built from a candidate pool of 31,834 synchronized stimulus-reaction clips spanning 433 hours and eight domains, ReactionBench evaluates three complementary abilities: ranking the intensity of a viewer's reactions within a video, matching a reaction to its temporally aligned stimulus, and explaining the concrete stimulus cues linked to that reaction. Human annotation provides 14,894 ratings over 3,257 moments, and the frozen evaluation comprises 250 intensity profiles, 112 matching items, and 200 rationale items with 447 blind-written human references. Across seven multimodal models, reaction-specific grounding remains challenging: the best model reaches 50.9% accuracy on four-way stimulus-reaction matching, while GPT-5 shows no detectable loss under reaction substitution. Performance is also sensitive to evaluation protocol. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Think Before You Locate: Clinical Chain-of-Though Supervision for Sequential Medical Image Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00041)**  `arXiv:2611.00041`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Adinath Madhavrao Dukre, Govinda Kolli, Yifan Lu, Ziyun Zou, Azmine Toushik Wasi, Sara Atito, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinicians rarely localize a finding from one image alone. They compare current and prior scans, follow structures across slices, and match regions across views or modalities. Yet vision-language models for sequential medical grounding usually learn only from bounding boxes. Boxes show where the target is, but they do not explain why that region in that image answers the question. We study whether explicit, image-grounded reasoning can improve localization, and which tasks benefit from it. We introduce method, whose core is MedChain, a corpus of 182,573 filtered rationales spanning eight sequential grounding tasks and ten imaging modalities. A medical VLM (FlemingVL-38B) first drafts a caption-like rationale, and a multimodal refiner (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B) then re-examines the images, question and annotated box to write a box-conditioned explanation with explicit cross-image reasoning. We fine-tune Qwen3.5 on these rationales with deliberative chain-of-thought supervision (dCoT-SFT). Across 4B, 9B, and 27B models, dCoT-SFT sets a new state of the art on MedSG-Bench. The 27B model reaches 77.24 mIoU and 85.20 Acc@0.5, improving over the strongest prior model by 4.69 and 5.49 points. In a paired comparison using fixed 4B checkpoints, dCoT-SFT improves over answer-only SFT by 3.56 mIoU (95% CI 3.13--4.00). The gains mainly come from change detection and object tracking, while performance drops on three appearance-matching tasks. Replacing a model's own rationale with a rationale from another task reduces mIoU by 12.2 points, showing that localization depends strongly on the reasoning context. Finally, adding GRPO with an IoU reward after dCoT-SFT gives only small and task-dependent gains, which we analyze through controlled multi-seed development experiments.
  </details>

- **[Adapting Video Models for Robot Control via Motion-guided Asymmetric Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36624)**  `arXiv:2610.36624`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Taeyoung Kim, Junyoung Sung, Heeseung Kwon, Minseop Kim, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World Action Models (WAMs) leverage pretrained video generative models for robotic control by jointly predicting future visual observations and actions. The visual prediction objective plays a key role in adapting pretrained video representations to the target robot domain. Yet standard visual prediction inherited from video pretraining treats all visual regions uniformly, despite their differing relevance to robotic control. We therefore investigate the spatial allocation of visual supervision in WAMs and show that focusing supervision on dynamic regions can substantially improve policy performance. Building on this finding, we introduce Motion-Guided Asymmetric Prediction (MoGAP), which augments WAM training with motion-focused visual prediction. MoGAP allocates noise asymmetrically across regions, training the model to reconstruct more heavily corrupted dynamic regions using less corrupted static regions as context. MoGAP is trained alongside standard video-action prediction through a training-only auxiliary prediction path within the shared video diffusion backbone. It uses motion cues derived directly from training videos, requiring neither an external teacher nor additional modalities. Across robot manipulation benchmarks and WAM architectures, MoGAP consistently improves policy performance, increasing success rates on the GR-1 tabletop benchmark from 38.8% to 47.5% for Fast-WAM (Wan2.2-5B) and from 52.3% to 62.3% for Cosmos3-Nano.
  </details>

- **[ProtoSemImage: Image-Valued Prototypes with Deformable Row Alignment for Interpretable Document Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11460)**  `arXiv:2610.11460`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammad Zare, Pirooz Shamsinejadbabaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prototypes in classification models are almost always vectors, and a vector has no readable form. This paper asks what happens when a prototype is an image. Documents give the question a natural form, because a document can be rendered as a multi-channel image in which every token becomes a pixel, so a class representative can take the same shape and the same channel semantics as the inputs it stands for. ProtoSemImage represents each class by one or more visual archetypes: prototype images in a four-channel HSV space whose channels carry named linguistic factors. A Skip-Gram objective learns that color space end to end through a four-dimensional bottleneck, discourse boundary rows become differentiable typed difference rows, and classification reduces to 2D visual template matching: a deformable row alignment between a document image and the archetype bank, in the spirit of dynamic time warping. Because the match is a spatial pattern comparison rather than a linear readout, the model reports where an input departs from its archetype and along which channel, and a generative head decodes each archetype back into text. The image representation works: it beats an otherwise identical model with vector prototypes in all three paired seeds, by between 4.3 and 11.8 points on a ten-class task. The distance-based matching does not. A diagnostic that keeps the representation fixed and swaps only the classifier recovers the sequence baselines, which locates a 20.6-point shortfall in the matching rather than in the color compression, and a benchmark built so that a pair of documents shares a bag of words and differs only in arrangement confirms the layout-preservation it was designed for. We report both directions, because for a representation whose whole purpose is inspect ability, the failure modes are as informative as the gains.
  </details>

- **[Mixture of Layers: Dynamic Layer Routing for Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09440)**  `arXiv:2610.09440`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jeonghwan Kim, Sofia Stoica, Jiwan Chung, Ansel Blume, Hyeonjeong Ha, Zhenhailong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained vision encoders contain layer-wise visual representations that differ in spatial granularity, semantic abstraction, and sensitivity to local details. However, most Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) rely on only the final or penultimate vision encoder representations or fixed aggregation rules, making visual abstraction largely query-agnostic and limiting access to fine-grained cues such as small objects, spatial details, text, and subtle visual attributes. In this work, we propose Mixture of Layers (MoL), an instruction-conditioned layer routing approach at the visual patch level that dynamically aggregates query-relevant latent representations from intermediate vision encoder layers. Given a text query, MoL predicts routing probabilities over vision encoder layers and performs a top-k sparse aggregation over selected hidden states at either the image level, patch level, or through a hybrid routing mechanism. In doing so, MoL enables query-adaptive access to layer-specific visual features for fine-grained visual reasoning. Our experiments across 7 fine-grained visual reasoning tasks demonstrate substantial performance improvements, especially across fine-grained visual grounding and understanding tasks such as +18.9% improvement on V* in overall accuracy, +4.5% on HRBench4K, and +16.3% on CharXiv compared to the baseline MLLMs, without resorting to multi-resolution inputs, simple interleaving of multiple vision encoders, or increasing the number of patch tokens. We study vision encoders' receptive field scales across different layers and their sampling behaviors to provide an in-depth analysis of why layer-wise sampling is helpful, demonstrating that conditional visual representations are a key step towards better visual perception and reasoning in MLLMs. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Cost-efficient Active Learning for Referring Image Segmentation and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30621)**  `arXiv:2608.30621`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junbeom Hong, Seonghoon Yu, Hyung Rok Jung, Sundong Kim, Jeany Son_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collecting natural-language referring expressions along with region annotations, such as masks or boxes, is a major bottleneck in visual grounding (VG), as annotators must write descriptions that distinguish target regions from visually similar ones. We tackle this by formulating active learning (AL) for VG under the realistic setting where only raw images are available without accompanying text. Since ground-truth text is unavailable, sample selection must estimate which images contain ambiguous regions that would require discriminative referring expressions. To address this, we generate auxiliary region-text pairs using foundation models, and introduce Referred Region Ambiguity, a new acquisition function that measures whether the model's confidence collapses onto a single region or disperses across multiple candidates. It allows our method to prioritize images with strong cross-region competition, which are more informative due to their visual ambiguity. We also design a referring-expression annotation interface that helps annotators quickly focus on writing discriminative language with a few clicks. Experiments on RIS and REC benchmarks show that our AL framework consistently outperforms several AL baselines, while a user study shows up to 1.6X faster description labeling of ours.
  </details>

- **[MOPDA: Mixed-Trajectory On-Policy Distillation for Language-Guided Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18850)**  `arXiv:2607.18850`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuimu Chen, Jing Jin, Nan Su, Hongbo Xu, Zebang Cheng, Wenming Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) have shown strong potential for industrial anomaly detection (IAD) by providing image-level anomaly judgments and interpretable reasoning. However, reliably translating generated judgments into precise pixel-level localization remains challenging. We propose \textbf{M}ixed-Trajectory \textbf{O}n-\textbf{P}olicy \textbf{D}istillation for Language-Guided Industrial \textbf{A}nomaly Detection (MOPDA), the first framework to introduce on-policy self-distillation into LVLM-based IAD. For judgment learning, \method introduces \textbf{Mixed-Trajectory Supervision}, combining student-generated on-policy trajectories with evidence-conditioned teacher trajectories under a shared token-level distillation objective. Student trajectories preserve supervision on deployment-relevant response paths, while teacher trajectories provide complementary evidence-conditioned supervision. For dense localization, \textbf{Language-guided Visual Anchoring} uses the final judgment as a compact semantic condition to construct image-specific normal and abnormal anchors, which are contrasted with dense visual features to produce anomaly maps. This keeps language as semantic guidance while grounding pixel-level responses in visual evidence. Under a strict cross-dataset zero-shot protocol on five IAD benchmarks, \method outperforms the evaluated LVLM-based baselines on most detection, localization, and judgment metrics while remaining competitive with CLIP-based methods. Ablations further validate both mixed-trajectory supervision and final-judgment conditioning.
  </details>

- **[EV9V: An Echocardiographic Video Benchmark with Uncertainty Guided View Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17437)**  `arXiv:2606.17437`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Bo Gou, Jicheng Zhang, Jianlong Xiong, Tao He, Hai Wu, Bentian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated classification of standard echocardiographic views is crucial for efficient clinical workflow but faces three main challenges. First, publicly available datasets are scarce and limited in scale and view coverage. Second, the performance of some modern video-level architectures for echocardiographic view classification remains underexplored. Third, some view categories exhibit highly similar spatial appearances, making single-frame features insufficient for discrimination, while heterogeneous frame quality complicates robust temporal information fusion. To address these challenges, we release the Echocardiographic Videos of Nine Views (EV9V) dataset, comprising 5,138 videos, 910,579 frames, and 9 standard views, which is, to the best of our knowledge, the largest openly released echocardiography video dataset. Furthermore, we propose a Spatio-Temporal Fusion Model (STFM), an efficient dual-stream framework that jointly captures spatial anatomical structures and temporal cardiac dynamics. The STFM leverages uncertainty-aware learning to preferentially sample representative video segments during training and evidence-based fusion during inference, improving robustness to variations in frame quality across echocardiographic videos. Extensive experiments demonstrate that STFM achieves competitive performance and effectiveness compared with diverse video classification architectures, including two-dimensional and three-dimensional convolutional neural networks, recurrent neural networks, and Transformers. The code is released atthis https URLand the EV9V dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aes3D: Aesthetic Assessment in 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05155)**  `arXiv:2605.05155`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chuanzhi Xu, Boyu Wei, Haoxian Zhou, Xuanhua Yin, Zihan Deng, Haodong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) gains attention in immersive media and digital content creation, assessing the aesthetics of 3D scenes becomes important in helping creators build more visually compelling 3D content. However, existing evaluation methods for 3D scenes primarily emphasize reconstruction fidelity and perceptual realism, largely overlooking higher-level aesthetic attributes such as composition, harmony, and visual appeal. This limitation comes from two key challenges: (1) the absence of general 3DGS datasets with aesthetic annotations, and (2) the intrinsic nature of 3DGS as a low-level primitive representation, which makes it difficult to capture high-level aesthetic features. To address these challenges, we propose Aes3D, the first systematic framework for assessing the aesthetics of 3D neural rendering scenes. Aes3D includes Aesthetic3D, the first dataset dedicated to 3D scene aesthetic assessment, built on our proposed annotation strategy for 3D scene aesthetics. In addition, we present Aes3DGSNet, a lightweight model that directly predicts scene-level aesthetic scores from 3DGS representations. Notably, our model operates solely on 3D Gaussian primitives, eliminating the need for rendering multi-view images and thus reducing computational cost and hardware requirements. Through aesthetics-supervised learning on multi-view 3DGS scene representations, Aes3DGSNet effectively captures high-level aesthetic cues and accurately regresses aesthetic scores. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach achieves strong performance while maintaining a lightweight design, establishing a new benchmark for 3D scene aesthetic assessment.
  </details>

- **[DiffCap-Bench: A Comprehensive, Challenging, Robust Benchmark for Image Difference Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04503)**  `arXiv:2605.04503`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuancheng Wei, Haojie Zhang, Linli Yao, Lei Li, Jiali Chen, Tao Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image Difference Captioning (IDC) generates natural language descriptions that precisely identify differences between two images, serving as a key benchmark for fine-grained change perception, cross-modal reasoning, and image editing data construction. However, existing benchmarks lack diversity and compositional complexity, and standard lexical-overlap metrics (e.g., BLEU, METEOR) fail to capture semantic consistency or penalize hallucinations, which together prevent a comprehensive and robust evaluation of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on IDC. To address these gaps, we introduce DiffCap-Bench, a comprehensive IDC benchmark covering ten distinct difference categories to ensure diversity and compositional complexity. Furthermore, we propose an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation protocol grounded in human-validated Difference Lists, enabling a robust assessment of models' ability to both capture and describe visual changes. Through extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs, we reveal significant performance gaps between proprietary and open-source models, highlight the critical importance of reasoning capability, and identify clear limitations in model scaling. Our framework also demonstrates strong alignment with human expert judgments and strong correlation with downstream image editing data construction quality. These findings establish DiffCap-Bench as both a reliable IDC evaluation framework and a practical predictor of downstream utility. The benchmark and code will be made publicly available to support further research.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models for Crash Detection in Video: A Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02074)**  `arXiv:2507.02074`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sanjeda Akter, Ibne Farabi Shihab, Anuj Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crash detection from video feeds is a critical problem in intelligent transportation systems. Recent developments in large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) have transformed how we process, reason about, and summarize multimodal information. This paper surveys recent methods leveraging LLMs for crash detection from video data. We present a structured taxonomy of fusion strategies, summarize key datasets, analyze model architectures, compare performance benchmarks, and discuss ongoing challenges and opportunities. Our review provides a foundation for future research in this fast-growing intersection of video understanding and foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Smart Surveillance: Deep Learning and Hybrid Approaches for Dynamic Scene Analysis, Object Detection and Motion Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.05331)**  `arXiv:2412.05331`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shahran Rahman Alve, Shahnewaz Siddique_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Event-triggered recording reduces the footage a surveillance camera must store, but the trigger decides how much idle video is kept, how many events are missed, and how much computation is spent. We compare six motion triggers (frame differencing, dense optical flow, MOG2, KNN, CNT, GSOC), a CNN detector, strided detection, and three motion-gated hybrids under one recording policy, on a benchmark of seven static-camera scenes (5,800 frames, 27 annotated events) with thresholds transferred to unseen scenes by leave-one-video-out. Detector-based triggers stored 19-28% of idle frames, against 36-89% for motion triggers. Under simulated camera jitter, frame differencing, optical flow, MOG2 and KNN stored 97-100% of idle frames, while detector triggers stayed below 29%. Running the detector on every 8th frame kept 99.7% of event frames at 9.8 ms of CPU per frame, cheaper and more robust than gating it with background subtraction. On VisDrone, raising YOLO11n's input from 640 to 1280 px raised AP from 15.5 to 26.8, more than a larger model, and INT8 quantization cut latency by 17% for 0.3 AP. In tracking, longer track memory raised IDF1 from 70.5 to 76.7, but every tracker recovered only 39-44% of overlapped people, because detection rather than association failed.
  </details>

- **[EgoActionBench: Benchmarking Dense Action Captioning for Egocentric Human Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03646)**  `arXiv:2611.03646`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yujiao Shen, Jiahe Song, Haitian Li, Jingkang Yang, Ziwei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from human data offers a scalable path toward embodied intelligence, yet it requires models to accurately understand fine-grained human actions from egocentric observations. However, existing benchmarks remain limited in action atomicity, temporal precision, and hand-action association. We introduce EgoActionBench, a benchmark that represents continuous human manipulation as temporally localized atomic actions associated with the left hand, right hand, or both hands. EgoActionBench contains 1,407 episodes with 41,132 human-refined action annotations. To evaluate such dense predictions, we propose an evaluation protocol that retrieves temporally eligible candidates, selects a prediction by content agreement, and scores semantic fidelity and temporal overlap alongside action precision and recall, jointly capturing what happens, when it happens, and which hand performs it. We benchmark 20 baseline models and systems on EgoActionBench and further adapt two backbones, Qwen3-VL-4B and LLaVA-OneVision-2-8B, through supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning on automatically annotated training episodes. EgoAction improves EgoActionScore by 15.9 and 23.2 points on the two backbones, and both adapted models outperform all open-source baselines. Our experiments reveal substantial limitations of current models in action atomicity, temporal precision, and hand-action association, highlighting the gap between general video understanding and the action understanding required for embodied intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Reviso: Not All Visual Evidence Deserves Equal Attention in Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03602)**  `arXiv:2611.03602`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tengfei Liu, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Yuqi Tang, Yizhuo Jia, Zhuoran Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate detailed video captions, yet they may overemphasize minor details while overlooking essential context and key events. We argue that not all visual evidence deserves equal attention, as the importance of visual information depends on the content of each video. Motivated by this observation, we propose Reviso, an agentic video captioning framework that learns what to observe and how to gather relevant visual evidence. From a low-frame-rate global preview, Reviso constructs a hierarchical Context--Focus--Points (CFP) observation plan that identifies the video context, prioritizes key aspects, and defines concrete observation targets and corresponding tools. Guided by this plan, Reviso performs goal-directed active perception by selectively revisiting temporal segments and combining tools for fine-grained inspection. It interprets the acquired evidence, updates its plan as needed, and integrates global and local information to generate the final caption. To learn this observation strategy, we develop a verification-guided pipeline that constructs high-quality training trajectories through factual verification and teacher-guided refinement. We further train Reviso using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), incorporating a key-fact reward for caption quality, a Plan Alignment Reward (PAR) for identifying important observation targets, and an Unproductive Tool-Call Penalty (UTCP) that adjusts observation-cost penalties based on caption quality. Experiments across four video captioning benchmarks show that Reviso outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines, while its generated captions better support downstream video question answering. These findings highlight the effectiveness and potential of priority-guided active observation for video captioning.
  </details>

- **[VidDub: Replacement-Aware Distillation for Two-Step Video Dubbing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03404)**  `arXiv:2611.03404`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yexin Liu, Yunlong Lin, Zehan Wang, Guangxin He, Xinli Xu, Jiajun Zha, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video dubbing aims to synthesize lip movements that match target speech while preserving the speaker identity, head pose, and background of a source video. Existing editors are predominantly trained via self-reconstruction under original paired audio, even though the source mouth becomes a conflicting target when driving speech is replaced. We introduce replacement-aware cross-audio supervision: paired-audio samples use full-frame reconstruction, whereas unpaired replacement-audio samples mask out source-mouth reconstruction losses and supervise the mouth solely through audio-visual synchronization while preserving unmasked facial regions and background. Because offline dubbing provides the complete source video and target speech beforehand, we present VidDub, a full-context diffusion editor distilled into two denoising steps (2 NFE). Our replacement-aware teacher first learns accurate lip synchronization without regressing toward conflicting source mouth movements. Matched trajectory regression then transfers the teacher's CFG-guided trajectories to a two-step student, and distribution matching distillation recovers visual quality after step compression. We will release the curated training dataset, VidDub-Bench, fixed paired- and cross-audio evaluation manifests, preprocessing outputs, and unified evaluation code. Across three training seeds, masking out conflicting source-mouth reconstruction improves cross-audio Sync-C by 0.833; the final two-step model combines strong lip synchronization with superior visual quality, identity preservation, and visual speech recognition across four benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Image Guided Depth Upsampling using Anisotropic Total Generalized Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03399)**  `arXiv:2611.03399`  `cs.CV`  
  _David Ferstl, Christian Reinbacher, Rene Ranftl, Matthias Rüther, Horst Bischof_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work we present a novel method for the challenging problem of depth image upsampling. Modern depth cameras such as Kinect or Time of Flight cameras deliver dense, high quality depth measurements but are limited in their lateral resolution. To overcome this limitation we formulate a convex optimization problem using higher order regularization for depth image upsampling. In this optimization an anisotropic diffusion tensor, calculated from a high resolution intensity image, is used to guide the upsampling. We derive a numerical algorithm based on a primaldual formulation that is efficiently parallelized and runs at multiple frames per second. We show that this novel upsampling clearly outperforms state of the art approaches in terms of speed and accuracy on the widely used Middlebury 2007 datasets. Furthermore, we introduce novel datasets with highly accurate groundtruth, which, for the first time, enable to benchmark depth upsampling methods using real sensor data.
  </details>

- **[TaoMate-FVSR:Audio-Guided Facial Video Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03393)**  `arXiv:2611.03393`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinyang Li, Zeran Xu, Qijun Gan, Chenwei Zhang, Meiguang Jin, Junfeng Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated videos often depict people within detailed scenes while leaving their small facial regions poorly resolved. Visual super-resolution can sharpen these regions without resolving whether the recovered lips and teeth agree with the spoken audio. We present an audio-guided facial video restoration framework that addresses this ambiguity within a reference-guided video flow model. Source--noise mixing initializes reconstruction from a partially preserved observation, and structured reference injection separates appearance anchors from continuous motion context. Training combines restoration of degraded images and videos with correction of degraded dubbed sources under target audio. Appearance and synchronization supervision allow the model to recover facial detail and support visual dubbing when the audio is replaced. On talking-head restoration benchmarks, our method achieves the best paired perceptual similarity and synchronization scores among the compared restoration methods. Component comparisons examine audio conditioning, the reconstruction formulation, and reference injection. Native AIGC examples further illustrate the recovery of lip contours, dental structure, and surrounding facial texture in scene-level footage. injection. Native AIGC examples further illustrate the recovery of lip contours, dental structure, and surrounding facial texture in scene-level footage.
  </details>

- **[ASCoR: Asymmetric Semantic-Spatial Learning for Remote Sensing Domain Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03382)**  `arXiv:2611.03382`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zitong Wang, Shuchang Lyu, Meng Li, Qiang Wang, Guangliang Cheng, Zhenwei Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Source-only domain generalization (DG) for remote sensing semantic segmentation seeks reliable predictions in unseen domains without target-domain data during training. Although vision foundation models offer transferable knowledge, category recognition remains sensitive to source-specific visual cues, while task-specific adaptation may weaken pretrained transferability. We propose ASCoR, an asymmetric semantic-spatial framework built on SAM3. Its text-conditioned semantic branch uses class-name prompts to provide explicit category guidance. Probe-Once Dynamic Adaptive LoRA distributes a fixed adaptation budget across encoder blocks to limit source-specific specialization. A complementary task-specific dense branch learns multi-scale spatial representations using a separate frozen SAM3 image encoder. A lightweight reliability router integrates the two branches through bounded spatial correction based on local prediction reliability. All components are trained using source-domain supervision only. Experiments on the ISPRS, LoveDA, and OpenEarthMap benchmarks cover nine cross-domain transfers. ASCoR achieves a state-of-the-art mean IoU of 68.61% across six ISPRS transfers, outperforming the previous best method by 2.69 percentage points. It also achieves the best results on all three LoveDA/OpenEarthMap transfers, with 59.07%, 47.95%, and 49.38% mIoU on Rural-to-Urban, Urban-to-Rural, and Urban-to-OpenEarthMap, respectively. Ablation studies, feature visualizations, and spatial reliability analyses support the effectiveness and complementarity of the proposed components. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EO-Jev: Decision Model for Earth Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03371)**  `arXiv:2611.03371`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mohamed Atef Abouelhadid, Diaa Addeen Abuhani, Muhammad Akhtar Munir, Fahad Shahbaz Khan, Salman Khan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Earth observation (EO) workflows require selecting among known outcomes, estimating confidence, and processing large volumes of imagery. Generative vision-language models can address these tasks, but autoregressive decoding is computationally costly and their confidence estimates are unreliable. Decision models offer an alternative, returning probabilities over candidate answers in a single forward pass. However, their accuracy, calibration, and transfer across EO tasks remain insufficiently understood. We investigate these properties through a diagnostic study of d3-flash (8.4B parameters) across 30 settings from 23 public EO benchmarks. We find that compositional reasoning transfers well and calibration improves over its Qwen3.5-9B backbone on 24 settings, while counting, change quantification, damage grading, and absent-object recognition remain challenging. To address these limitations, we adapt d3-flash through low-rank fine-tuning on public EO training splits, producing EO-Jev. EO-Jev improves significantly over d3-flash on 26 of 30 settings and matches or exceeds specialist references on 20 of 28. It also achieves an average gain of 8.0 points across 12 held-out settings and lower expected calibration error than specialists on 21 of 28 settings. These improvements come at a modest cost of 1.8 points in general decision performance. Our results demonstrate the potential of adapting general decision models into competitive EO generalists while highlighting persistent challenges in quantitative perception, option-order consistency, and calibration under distribution shift.
  </details>

- **[Fuse To Align: Multimodal Representation Learning for Retrieval via Hypergraph Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03370)**  `arXiv:2611.03370`  `cs.CV`  
  _Anindya Nag, Ambuj Mehrish, Sebastiano Vascon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal retrieval must integrate complementary evidence from views that may be missing, noisy, or unequally informative. When a sample provides limited reliable evidence, learning from its views alone can hinder the acquisition of useful retrieval representations. Semantically related training samples may provide complementary observations, yet existing volume-based objectives jointly align modalities within each sample without explicitly enabling information exchange across samples. We therefore propose a training-time hypergraph fusion framework that connects observed modalities both within samples and across semantically related samples, allowing representation learning to draw on evidence beyond an individual sample while leaving the retrieval pipeline unchanged at inference. The framework includes HyVol as a special case and introduces two configurations: wHyVol learns view contribution weights and symmetric semantic affinities, whereas wRHG additionally models typed interactions and participant roles through relational propagation. Missing modalities are omitted rather than imputed, and the hypergraph is discarded after training, incurring no additional inference cost. Experiments on eleven zero-shot video-text and audio-text retrieval benchmarks demonstrate broad improvements with both GRAM and HyperGRAM. Across 17 video benchmark-modality settings evaluated in both retrieval directions, wRHG outperforms GRAM in all 34 comparisons and HyperGRAM in 33 of 34. Mean text-to-video R@1 increases from 63.4 to 68.2 with GRAM and from 64.8 to 68.4 with HyperGRAM, while the largest video-to-text gain reaches 13.3 percentage points on ActivityNet. Under missing-modality evaluation, both weighted configurations retain positive text-to-video gains over GRAM across seven benchmarks at every evaluated missingness rate, even when 90% of clips lack one modality.
  </details>

- **[ArabGeoText: Tool-Augmented Vision-Language Models for Arabic Geospatial Text Spotting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03339)**  `arXiv:2611.03339`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yakoub Bazi, Sarah Alsaleh, Mohamad M. Al Rahhal, Mohamed A. Mekhtiche, Mansour Zuair_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geospatial text spotting involves locating and transcribing geographic labels, such as road names, district names, and landmarks, in raster maps. These labels are often small, densely arranged, and arbitrarily oriented. Although Arabic text recognition has been studied extensively in documents and natural scenes, Arabic geospatial text spotting remains underexplored. We introduce ARABGEOTEXT, an Arabic-centric benchmark covering 970 geographic locations in Riyadh and Jeddah, with 17,875 manually annotated text instances per view type. For each location, we pair an OpenStreetMap (OSM) rendering with a composite created by overlaying that rendering on the corresponding remote sensing (RS) imagery. The paired views preserve label content and pixel geometry, allowing evaluation across different backgrounds. Geographic partitions support in-city and city-disjoint evaluation of detection, recognition, and end-to-end spotting. Experiments with direct Qwen3.5-4B spotting show difficulties in recovering all labels within a tile. We therefore propose a tool-augmented vision-language framework that combines YOLO26 localization and crop rectification with Qwen3.5-4B recognition adapted through low-rank adaptation (LoRA). The framework outperforms direct LoRA-adapted Qwen3.5-4B spotting across both image views and test cities. With localization held fixed, reader adaptation further improves recognition over the pretrained zero-shot reader. The dataset and code will be made publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReVA-HPE: Relative 6DoF Head Pose Estimation with Synthetic Pose Supervision and Personalized Virtual Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03283)**  `arXiv:2611.03283`  `cs.CV`  
  _Vasiliki Vasileiou, Panagiotis P. Filntisis, Petros Maragos, Kostas Daniilidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular head pose estimation is usually formulated as absolute regression from a single face crop, yet many applications observe the same person over time and can supply an explicit anchor image. Recently, relative head pose estimation was proposed, which casts the problem as estimation of the relative geometric change between an anchor and a query image. However, existing methods still require the anchor image pose to be known at inference, significantly limiting their practical use. We first extend relative head pose estimation to full 6DoF by additionally predicting a depth-normalized relative translation, which composes with the anchor to recover metric query pose in the camera frame. To eliminate the need for a ground-truth anchor pose, we reconstruct a personalized avatar from a single unlabeled enrollment image and render a bank of virtual anchors with known poses. The bank provides robust rotation hypotheses through multi-anchor consensus, while the real enrollment image serves as the real anchor in the translation branch via landmark PnP. We also introduce an efficient variant that amortizes the bank cost once at enrollment, after which each query requires a single real-to-real relative prediction, with limited accuracy sacrifice. The relative pose estimator is trained exclusively on synthetic face renderings, without real pose supervision, pseudo-labels, domain adaptation, or fine-tuning on the evaluation sets. On real 6DoF benchmarks, ReVA-HPE achieves state-of-the-art rotation accuracy on BIWI and, given a near-frontal enrollment image, on FaMoS, while remaining competitive in metric translation despite relying solely on synthetic pose supervision. Project page and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[R$^2$-Omni: Reducing Structural and Precision Redundancy in Omni-LLMs for Long-Form Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03247)**  `arXiv:2611.03247`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bolong Feng, Pengfei Zhou, Zhenglin Wan, Wei Wang, Wangbo Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) typically process large numbers of multimodal tokens from audio-visual inputs, incurring substantial computational costs and a large memory footprint for the key-value (KV) cache. Aggressive token pruning can alleviate this problem while maintaining competitive performance on multiple-choice tasks, which require only a single option as output. However, we observe substantial quality degradation when generating long-form, open-ended responses. We attribute this gap to query-guided selection, which can retain sufficient evidence for answer selection while discarding details needed for comprehensive generation. This motivates reducing overlapping information across tokens, termed structural redundancy, while preserving diverse content. However, the retained representations may still contain precision redundancy due to unnecessarily high numerical precision in their cached values. To address these two levels of redundancy in token representations, we introduce a training-free framework named R$^2$-Omni. First, Structure-Aware Token Consolidation (STC) consolidates redundant representations using spatiotemporal similarity in video and local continuity in audio. Then, Semantic-Sensitivity Guided Precision Allocation (SSPA) allocates value precision to the retained representations according to the estimated reduction in attention-output error under quantized keys, with query attention and audio-visual alignment providing a semantic prior. Extensive experiments on long-form generation benchmarks demonstrate an average 5.0$\times$ reduction in the memory footprint while preserving 97.98% of baseline generation quality and achieving a 1.48$\times$ end-to-end speedup, highlighting the benefits of jointly reducing structural and precision redundancy.
  </details>

- **[CL3DOD: Understanding the Stability-Plasticity Dilemma in Outdoor Class-Incremental 3D Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03229)**  `arXiv:2611.03229`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Ahmed Ullah Khan, Khalil Ahmed, Didier Stricker, Muhammad Zeshan Afzal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class-incremental learning (CIL) for outdoor 3D object detection remains largely underexplored. Existing outdoor studies are limited to single-benchmark evaluations, with SDCoT++ considering KITTI and CL2OD considering nuScenes under different incremental settings. This makes it difficult to assess whether observed continual-learning behavior generalizes across autonomous-driving benchmarks. To the best of our knowledge, we provide the first unified cross-benchmark analysis of outdoor class-incremental 3D object detection across multiple autonomous-driving benchmarks, covering both two-task and multi-stage settings. We investigate: (1) how severely existing 3D detectors forget previously learned classes, (2) how incremental training exposes the stability-plasticity dilemma between knowledge retention and new-class acquisition, and (3) which forms of supervision are most effective for balancing these objectives. We introduce CL3DOD, a class-incremental 3D detection framework that restores missing supervision for previous classes using predictions from the previous-stage detector, preserves old-class responses through query-wise knowledge distillation, and employs EMA-based temporal consistency regularization. Experiments on KITTI and nuScenes reveal a consistent stability-plasticity dilemma across both benchmarks. CL3DOD generally achieves a more favorable stability-plasticity balance than controlled incremental baselines and improves upon published prior methods in the reported benchmark comparisons. This advantage persists across multiple incremental stages, demonstrating strong retention of previously learned knowledge while maintaining effective new-class acquisition. Our code, evaluation setup, and incremental task splits will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[SurgPace: Complexity-Aware Progressive Distillation for Surgical Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03125)**  `arXiv:2611.03125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhicheng He, Xiaohan Xing, Hongqiu Wang, Kun Yuan, Guankun Wang, Yanheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential in robotic surgery for question answering, scene understanding, and grounded assistance, but their high computational cost limits real clinical deployment. Knowledge distillation provides a practical way to build compact surgical MLLMs, yet direct distillation is not well suited to surgical data because surgical samples vary greatly in difficulty, visual ambiguity, and required clinical context, making uniform teacher-student transfer unstable. In addition, the role of supervision changes during training: early stages require stronger teacher guidance for multimodal alignment, while later stages rely more on task supervision for effective optimization. To address these challenges, we propose SurgPace, a complexity-aware continuous curriculum framework for progressive distillation of surgical vision-language models. SurgPace estimates sample complexity using predictive confidence and Lorentz geometric consistency, and uses the fused score to guide a continuous easy-to-hard training process. We further introduce hierarchical representation alignment, which aligns the student with the teacher at the feature, logit, and probability levels, while gradually shifting the training focus from teacher imitation to task supervision. This design separates sample-level curriculum from objective-level scheduling, leading to more stable and effective distillation. We evaluate SurgPace on Surg-396K, a benchmark built from four public surgical datasets and covering five dialogue paradigms. SurgPace consistently outperforms strong MLLM distillation baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness for building compact, memory-efficient surgical MLLMs with faster decoding under controlled benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[3D Gaussian Splatting within Self-Learned Neural View-Dependent Color Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03114)**  `arXiv:2611.03114`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yixiao Yang, Yu-Shen Liu, Zhizhong Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a vital tool in novel view synthesis. 3D Gaussian functions with attributes like color, opacity, and scale are stacked together to explicitly model a radiance field. However, discrete 3D Gaussians usually produce poor depth with few geometry details but lots of floaters, which hinders the effective modeling of smooth radiance fields. To address this issue, we propose to learn a radiance field within a continuous view-dependent color field parameterized by neural networks. With continuous constraints in color, the bias on low-frequency signals of neural networks can highly encourage the geometry modeling to contribute to high-frequency color variations on images, getting rid of solely relying on the color attribute itself. Moreover, we impose a constraint to improve the Lipschitz continuity of the neural color field, making large changes of each Gaussian's color also match with large changes of its position in terms of a ratio. To this end, we additionally introduce novel self-learning strategies to improve the learning of the neural view-dependent color field without any external knowledge or priors, aiming for better generalization on unseen scenes. Our numerical and visual comparisons on widely used benchmarks justify our idea and show better ability of high fidelity geometry recovery in 3DGS. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[VL-Dedup: Graded Multimodal Entity Resolution for Image-Text Data Deduplication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03080)**  `arXiv:2611.03080`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenxi Liu, Guanze Chen, Dehui Yu, Xingyu Wang, Bingxu Hou, Chengwei Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-text corpora contain copies of one image, captions that describe one image in different ways, captions reused for different images, and captions that do not match their images. Each case calls for a different curation action. Deduplication and multimodal entity resolution (ER) only decide whether records are the same, and they are evaluated on labeled pairs. Yet pair scores do not tell which records end up in the same cluster or which useful captions get deleted. We therefore formulate image-text deduplication as graded multimodal ER, which decides visual identity, caption handling, and mismatches for each pair, and we evaluate it at three levels: pairs, clusters, and the retained set. Our method, VL-Dedup, groups records of the same visual entity into clusters and caps the size of each cluster. Within each cluster, a vision-language model (VLM) and CLIPScore decide which captions to keep and which to flag. We also build VL-DedupBench, with 50,000 COCO and SOP records and 66,788 labeled pairs. On it, a classifier that selects no false edge among the labeled pairs still joins millions of record pairs from different entities. Spending VLM calls on the pairs inside clusters, rather than on pairs with uncertain scores, gives better caption decisions from the same model. Even when almost all clusters are correct, keeping one record per cluster loses facts in many deletions, while the deletions of VL-Dedup lose much less information.
  </details>

- **[VLM-as-a-Judge for Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03027)**  `arXiv:2611.03027`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bessie Dominguez-Dager, Miguel Cazorla, Vincent Lepetit_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cloth-changing person re-identification (CC-ReID) is still very challenging, and no method clearly dominates: methods that lead on one benchmark collapse on the others, because they rest on different identity cues. We show that a general-purpose vision-language model (VLM), used zero-shot as a judge for reranking a short candidate list, beats the expert retrieval models it reranks by a wide and consistent margin. On the four scenarios of CHIRLA, a long-term CC-ReID benchmark that no publicly released Re-ID model is trained on, reranking the candidates of two frozen experts raises rank-1 on every scenario, by 12.9 points on average over the best expert and 9.4 over the best naive ensemble of the two, with no component fine-tuned on the target data. Merging both experts' shortlists matches or beats reranking the stronger expert's shortlist alone, at lower cost. Introducing a VLM does, however, come at a cost: almost all of the pipeline's FLOPs are spent on it. We therefore borrow a criterion from ensembling and invoke the VLM judge only where the two experts disagree at rank 1. This threshold-free gate removes 40% of the calls, and a comparable share of total compute, with no significant drop in rank-1, trading 1.8 to 3.8 points of mAP. Judging remains the dominant cost, but on a benchmark where dedicated experts stall, we find a frozen general-purpose model to be the strongest judge of identity. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ILLRAW: Illumination-Aware RAW Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02995)**  `arXiv:2611.02995`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinzhe Wang, Xiao Liang, Xiaoli Zhang, Kang Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing RAW-based detectors typically introduce learnable ISP modules to convert RAW inputs into sRGB-like formats to fit off-the-shelf detectors. However, this RAW-to-sRGB paradigm decouples representation learning from the downstream detection task. Crucially, it forces the network to inherit human-centric visual priors while discarding intrinsic RAW advantages, such as linear sensor responses and high-bit-depth, minimally processed scene cues. In this paper, we propose \textbf{ILLRAW}, an \textbf{ILL}umination-aware end-to-end \textbf{RAW} object detection framework. Instead of reconstructing visually plausible sRGB images, ILLRAW directly learns detection-oriented RAW features through a lightweight feature adapter inserted at the input stage of existing detectors. ILLRAW estimates an illumination mask and introduces channel-sensitive embeddings to model Bayer-channel responses. These features are fused by an illumination attention fusion module. Experiments show clear improvements on the diverse AODRaw benchmark and competitive performance on LOD and PascalRAW, with particularly strong results under dark exposure.
  </details>

- **[Comprehensive Evaluation of Monocular and Multi-view 3D Human Pose Estimation Models for Fitness Exercise Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02929)**  `arXiv:2611.02929`  `cs.CV`  
  _Néstor Ortega, Agustín Salgado, Javier Sánchez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Three-dimensional human pose estimation has become a fundamental component of computer vision systems for sports analytics, rehabilitation, and motion understanding. Although recent deep learning approaches have achieved remarkable accuracy on standard benchmarks, their performance under realistic sports scenarios remains insufficiently studied. Furthermore, the trade-offs between recent monocular and multi-view approaches, as well as the benefits of post-processing techniques for improving temporal consistency, have not been systematically evaluated on fitness-oriented datasets. This paper evaluates recent state-of-the-art 3D human pose estimation methods on Fit3D, a dataset of synchronized multi-view recordings of a wide variety of fitness exercises with accurate ground-truth annotations. We study two recent monocular lifting approaches, TCPFormer and PoseMamba-L, and a multi-view model, MVGFormer, under a unified evaluation protocol. To investigate the influence of the input data, monocular methods are evaluated using ground-truth keypoints and detections generated by recent 2D pose estimators, RTMPose and YOLO-Pose. Additionally, we analyze the effectiveness of three temporal filters for reducing prediction jitter while preserving motion dynamics. Experimental results provide a detailed comparison of accuracy, temporal consistency, and robustness to imperfect 2D detections across the evaluated methods. Our analysis highlights the impact of detector quality on lifting-based approaches, compares transformer- and state-space-based temporal architectures, and discusses the practical trade-offs between monocular and multi-view systems for fitness motion analysis. Our results show that 2D detection quality, temporal modeling, and post-processing must be considered jointly in pose estimation pipelines for exercise analysis.
  </details>

- **[PhotoGuider: Composition-Aware Cropping and 360$^{\circ}$ View Framing with Explainable Photographic Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02897)**  `arXiv:2611.02897`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuan-Kang Lee, Kuan-Lin Chen, Chia-Che Chang, Yu-Lun Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional image cropping assumes the photograph already contains the composition worth keeping, and asks only where to crop. A full 360$^{\circ}$ scene offers no such guarantee: the viewing direction in a scene has to be discovered before anything can be framed. We present PhotoGuider, a measure-then-frame method that answers both questions with compact learned scorers over one shared set of composition-aware features. The designed features are extracted using foundation and task-specific vision models and structured into four photographic tokens: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics, and Integrity. Our MLLM explainer is fine-tuned to generate composition-related explanations conditioned on images and measured token assessments. To our knowledge, PhotoGuider is the first method to combine explicit composition-based direction selection in 360$^{\circ}$ panoramas with refined framing, camera-parameter output for native re-rendering, and measurement-conditioned explanations. On two standard 2D cropping benchmarks, PhotoGuider achieves the highest average mIoU performance against ground-truth crops. In a blind ranking study, 120 participants ranked three cropping methods over 100 panoramic scenes from the public Poly Haven HDRI library, placing our framings first in 53.2% of cases, against 27.3% and 19.5% for two strong alternatives. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ProtoFusion: Protocol-Guided Prompt Refinement for Generative Infrared and Visible Image Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02877)**  `arXiv:2611.02877`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengwei Liang, Junjun Jiang, Junhui Hou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Infrared and visible image fusion integrates complementary information from both modalities to provide a more comprehensive representation of the scene. We propose ProtoFusion, a protocol-guided generative framework for infrared and visible image fusion. Instead of relying on fixed fusion operators, hand-crafted objectives, or manually specified one-shot prompts, ProtoFusion formulates fusion as protocol-constrained generation. It represents fusion intent with a structured protocol, consisting of environment state cards, grounding principles, and a style profile, which explicitly specifies which evidence should be preserved or suppressed and which properties the fused result should satisfy. To optimize this protocol, we introduce protocol-guided prompt refinement, a closed-loop learning framework that iteratively performs generation, evaluation, and revision under multimodal evidence. Once learned, the optimized protocol is fixed at inference time and directly guides fusion generation. To address residual source inconsistencies, we further incorporate structured belief construction, which organizes modality reliability, scene evidence, and cross-modal conflicts into a structured representation for conservative correction after generation. Extensive experiments on standard benchmarks show that ProtoFusion improves quantitative performance, achieves favorable results in aesthetic evaluation, and produces visually coherent fusion outputs. Project Page: {this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Action-Free Video Latent Prediction for Transferable Representations in End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02853)**  `arXiv:2611.02853`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xingtai Gui, Xubin Zhou, Yucheng Zhou, Dongqian Guo, Jiahao Gong, Feiyang Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end autonomous driving policies require effective visual representations that capture both spatial scene structure and temporal dynamics. Video latent prediction has emerged as an effective paradigm for learning spatiotemporal visual representations, yet it remains unclear whether scaling this objective with action-free driving videos can yield representations that benefit end-to-end planning. We study action-free video latent prediction as a scalable pretraining objective and introduce ScaleDrive, a driving-oriented framework pretrained exclusively on large-scale internet open-world driving videos, without action annotations or benchmark-specific data. We keep the pretrained video encoder frozen during downstream end-to-end planning training to isolate the transferability of representations learned from internet driving videos. Ego-motion and depth probes show that scaling action-free video pretraining progressively strengthens the temporal and geometric information encoded in the frozen representation. We systematically study the visual representation transfer across different end-to-end driving policies and closed-loop benchmarks, where ScaleDrive consistently improves downstream performance and demonstrates that scaling action-free video latent prediction yields representations that generalize across both planning policy architectures and evaluation scenarios and protocols.
  </details>

- **[History Forcing: Distilling History-Dependent Continuations for Long-Horizon Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02782)**  `arXiv:2611.02782`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hangyi Kuang, Chengzhi Wang, Yefei He, Weijie Wang, Yuke Zhao, Zhuokun Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables efficient generation, yet maintaining consistency over long rollouts remains challenging, especially when subjects or scenes reappear after going out of sight. Despite progress in fidelity and rollout robustness, existing distillation methods do not fully exploit richer historical information to constrain the student's current prediction. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{History Forcing}, a unified on-policy distillation framework that strengthens historical supervision for few-step autoregressive video generators. The framework uses the residual between teacher predictions with and without informative historical context to decouple this supervision. Such a historical context can be obtained using earlier generated chunks with less accumulated drift or a fixed-view teacher rollout that keeps the scene visible while the student looks away. The residual is then weighted and added to the score or velocity prediction used for distillation, forming a corrected target. By aligning the student with this corrected target through distillation, History Forcing incorporates long-range historical constraints into autoregressive generation. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance among 1.3B models, with a VBench quality score of 85.07 for 15s video generation and a VBench-Long aggregate score of 79.82 for 60s generation. For out-of-sight world modeling, evaluations further demonstrate more plausible scene evolution and more consistent states when the camera returns, with Evolution Quality improving from 2.35 to 2.70 on PlayWorld and Object Consistency from 58.43 to 62.18 on MemoBench.
  </details>
