  Multi-image vision-language models (VLMs) incur substantial computation when processing long visual sequences. Compressing these sequences must preserve complementary evidence across images and keep tokens from different images separate. Global selection can underrepresent question-relevant images, and pruning in early decoder layers risks removing relevant content before sufficient multimodal processing, yet existing methods spend most of the budget on these early layers. We propose SIEVE, a training-free, single-pass framework that jointly allocates visual tokens across images and decoder depth. SIEVE first pools each image into a compact spatial grid to reduce early processing cost. It then progressively selects representatives using prompt attention under equal per-image quotas and aggregates unselected tokens into similar representatives from the same image. These operations keep every image represented and its tokens separate from other images, while refining the retained representations. A closed-form schedule sets retention counts to target a prescribed average token budget across decoder layers. Across four VLM backbones, SIEVE achieves the highest average at a 6.25% budget on five multi-image benchmarks and four single-image benchmarks, and the highest mean on two video benchmarks. On LLaVA-OneVision-7B, it outperforms the strongest baseline by 9.96 and 9.03 points at 6.25% and 12.5% budgets, and at 12.5% it exceeds the uncompressed model by 2.01 points with 6.6x fewer prefill FLOPs.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Less in a Sparse World: Cross-View Geolocalization from Limited Field-of-View Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00288)**  `arXiv:2611.00288`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fahimul Aleem, Shruti Vyas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-view geolocalization aims to determine the geographic location of a ground-level image by retrieving its corresponding satellite image, offering a promising alternative for localization in Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS)-denied or degraded environments such as urban canyons, dense forests, and disaster scenarios. Despite recent progress, existing approaches remain limited by the lack of globally diverse training datasets and benchmarks that are often geographically restricted. Furthermore, most benchmarks rely on panoramic ground views, which do not reflect the limited field-of-view imagery captured by real world cameras.To address these challenges, we introduce Cross-View World 500k (CVW500k), a large-scale globally distributed dataset containing over 500,000 ground-satellite image pairs collected across diverse geographic regions and environments. Unlike previous datasets, CVW500k uses limited field-of-view ground images, providing a more realistic and challenging setting for cross-view geolocalization. In addition, we propose GeoQueryNet, a query-based cross-view fusion transformer framework designed to bridge the viewpoint gap between ground and satellite images. The model leverages a CLIP-based visual encoder with parameter-efficient LoRA adaptation and introduces a Cross-View Alignment Module that uses learnable query tokens to extract geographically consistent features across viewpoints. Our code can be found here:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[OCRia-1 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00092)**  `arXiv:2611.00092`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aman Rangapur, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Daniel Bustamante, Nutchapol Dendumrongsup, Sebastián G. Acosta, Pierre-Loïc Doulcet, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce OCRia-1, a 4B vision-language model (VLM) for document parsing that excels at preserving table structure, extracting chart data and retaining text formatting. OCRia-1 handles content that has traditionally been challenging in document parsing: merged cells and spanning headers, tables with thousands of cells, chart data, and meaningful text formatting such as bold, italics, subscripts and superscripts, preserving details needed for business-critical document analysis. At the time of this report, it achieves the highest scores on tables, charts and text formatting among the systems evaluated, including a 35B model and Mistral OCR 4.1. Its mean ParseBench score over the four dimensions we evaluate is 0.87, against 0.75 for the next-best system.We emphasize data quality and reward design in training OCRia-1. Synthetic pages provide exact ground truth for long tables and rich formatting, while real pages are transcribed using a pipeline grounded in the PDF text layer. We attribute OCRia-1's performance primarily to careful data curation that prioritizes quality and diversity over quantity. Dimension-specific rewards evaluate table reconstruction, chart-data extraction and text-formatting fidelity. We train the model on the curated data using supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then refine it through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR).To our knowledge, OCRia-1 is the fastest published document-parsing VLM in its class, decoding at about 4,700 tokens per second with a single request on one B200 GPU, about nine times faster than the base model on stock vLLM. This is achieved through a block-diffusion speculator, quantization and custom kernels.
  </details>

- **[Sequential Transfer Learning for Plant Disease Recognition Across Perennial Crop Domains Using MobileNetV2-CBAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00080)**  `arXiv:2611.00080`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ropkathaa Choudhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: This study investigates whether a learned disease-recognition representation can be progressively reused across distinct perennial crop domains and how its predictive behaviour changes as target-domain heterogeneity increases.Design/methodology/approach: A lightweight MobileNetV2 framework enhanced with the Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) was evaluated through sequential transfer from Walnut to Cashew, Coffee and Mango, followed by validation on the heterogeneous ABCGMP six-crop benchmark. Each stage used the preceding model representation and was evaluated on an independent test set using complementary classification measures.Findings: Test accuracy increased from 75.76% for the Walnut baseline to 84.85% after CBAM integration, followed by 90.67% on Cashew, 100.00% on Coffee and 99.50% on Mango. The final ABCGMP evaluation achieved 88.44% accuracy, 81.49% Balanced Accuracy and 0.9949 ROC-AUC. The results show that substantial predictive capability was retained through successive crop transfers, while broader multi-crop heterogeneity introduced a measurable performance constraint.Originality: The study provides an empirical characterisation of sequential knowledge reuse across multiple perennial crop domains using a common lightweight attention-enhanced representation, extending evaluation beyond isolated source-to-target transfer.
  </details>

- **[Faithful Where You Look: Anchor-Based Evaluation Hides Text Fabrication in Image-Conditioned Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00079)**  `arXiv:2611.00079`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yujia Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-conditioned video models are used to show real places, so whether their output stays faithful to the place they were shown is a practical question. The text benchmarks we found that check fidelity against a reference do so at an anchor - an annotated region, typically the sign the task is about. We show that this choice hides most of the failure. We conditioned four models from three vendors on 48 street-level references of Tokyo storefronts and measured text fidelity at the annotated sign and in the periphery, the other text the reference covers. At the anchor, all four models are close to a no-model control (anchor text fidelity 0.90-0.94 against 0.996) and are not reliably separated from one another. In the periphery, 36-54% of generated text regions have no counterpart in the reference, against 1.6% for the control, although every prompt forbade new readable text. Two blinded raters agree on which regions are fabricated (kappa = 0.83-0.96), the result held in preregistered rounds on two vendors whose videos the measure had never seen (42% and 56%), and VBench-I2V's reference-based consistency scores show no evidence of tracking it in our sample. Most fabricated text is legible-looking text where the reference shows none or cannot be read - a form that string-matching metrics discard, which is why our own first measure missed it. A preregistered Latin-script replication in London missed its bar in a first round and met it in an independent second round. We release code, annotations, per-video scores, ratings and every preregistration. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AegisBench: Auditing Aerial Search-and-Rescue Person Detection Under the Conditions It Is Deployed In](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00077)**  `arXiv:2611.00077`  `cs.CV`  
  _Amogh Vinaykumar, Prem Kamasani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drone-based person detection is being adopted by emergency-management agencies to find missing people faster than ground teams can. The benchmarks behind that adoption were collected in calm, clear daylight, while the missions that trigger deployment happen in floods, wildfires, storms, and at night. AegisBench measures that gap. It applies nine physically motivated corruptions from four disaster families, each at three severities monotone in a declared image statistic or physical quantity, to two aerial search-and-rescue datasets, HERIDAL and SARD. Three architecturally distinct detectors (Faster R-CNN, YOLOv11, RT-DETR) are scored across 168 conditions by one shared evaluator, at confidence thresholds frozen on clean validation data as a fielded system would run. Robustness is highly uneven. Low light is the extreme case: a 4.5-stop exposure loss drives recall to exactly zero for all three detectors on both datasets, a result that holds under threshold-free mAP and across three training seeds, and is mirrored on 300 real night drone images, where 2 of 759 people are found. Surviving detections largely stay on target, so the failure is silent: an empty frame looks the same as one whose occupants the system cannot see. Fine-tuning on milder low-light levels recovers 0.06 to 0.19 recall at the held-out severity, at an architecture-dependent cost on other conditions, and does not transfer to real night imagery. Spatial corruptions rank differently on the two datasets, which we cannot attribute to altitude alone because their parameters scale with frame size rather than person size. We release the corruption engine, evaluation pipeline, and all per-condition results as an audit instrument for anyone evaluating these systems.
  </details>

- **[From Clips to Streams: A Unified Framework for Streaming Sign Language Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00055)**  `arXiv:2611.00055`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hoang Quan Dang, Xin Shen, Yanbin Liu, Xin Yu, Ling Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sign Language Translation (SLT) research has typically focused on pre-segmented clips, a constraint that disconnects models from the continuous reality of real-world communication. To bridge the gap from clips to streams, we formally define Streaming SLT, a realistic task demanding the joint discovery and translation of linguistic events from continuous, untrimmed video inputs. To address this challenge, we propose StreamSLST, the first end-to-end framework for Streaming Sign Language Segmentation and Translation via Deformable Transformer architecture. Unlike cascaded approaches that suffer from error propagation, StreamSLST employs a parallel decoding strategy to jointly optimize temporal segmentation and translation in a single pass. To enable scalable training on long-form videos, we incorporate a Sentence-aware Sliding Window Sampler that crops continuous streams into trainable units. We further design a Tri-modal Visual-Language Pretraining stage that aligns pose dynamics with textual semantics before joint optimization. To support comprehensive evaluation, we conduct experiments on two real-world streaming datasets, BOBSL and How2Sign, together with two newly constructed synthetic streaming benchmarks, Streaming-Phoenix-2014T and Streaming-CSL-Daily. The results demonstrate that StreamSLST consistently outperforms a matched cascaded segmentation-then-translation pipeline. Interestingly, our analysis reveals that joint optimization learns wider, translation-effective temporal support that simple expansion or rescaling of the annotated spans fails to reproduce. Our work establishes the first robust benchmark for continuous sign language understanding and paves the way for accessible real-world communication interfaces. The source code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TopoMix: Continuous Spatial Mixing for Long-Tailed Data Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00047)**  `arXiv:2611.00047`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yefan Wang, Yusen Wu, Zihui Ling, Jianyuan Ni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most mixing-based augmentation methods improve generalization through a simple idea: combine two training samples with a fixed global ratio or a hard spatial replacement. While effective, this design leaves little room for local variation, which becomes a more visible limitation in long-tailed recognition, where minority classes are especially sensitive to the quality of augmented samples. We propose TopoMix, a training-free augmentation method that replaces fixed mixing with continuous spatial mixing. TopoMix constructs a smooth spatially varying mask from a low-resolution random control grid, bicubic interpolation, and stochastic non-linear shaping, and uses this mask for element-wise image mixing and label reweighting. Without introducing trainable parameters or auxiliary networks, TopoMix offers a simple way to make mixing more flexible in the input space. Experiments on standard long-tailed benchmarks show that it consistently improves over representative mixing-based baselines, indicating that smooth spatial mixing is an effective augmentation strategy for long-tailed recognition.
  </details>

- **[DiA: Directional Adapter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00029)**  `arXiv:2611.00029`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ashish Kumar Singh, Prakash Chandra Chhipa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained action recognition often depends on how an action unfolds over time rather than on appearance alone. Visually similar classes may differ in preparation, execution, or completion phases, making uniformly aggregated temporal context suboptimal. We study this problem under a practical objective: i) improving recognition performance with few tunable parameters, ii) being compute efficient, and iii) using only the readily available video modality. Motivated by this missing temporal observation and the accuracy--compute efficiency--unimodality objective, we propose Directional Adapter (DiA), a parameter-efficient adapter on top of CLIP. DiA formulates temporal specialization into causal and anti-causal directions, allowing past-to-present and future-to-present cues to be modeled differently. To keep this directional modeling parameter- and compute-efficient, both directions use depth-wise temporal convolutions in a compact bottleneck space and are combined through a learnable fusion. DiA achieves state-of-the-art performance among methods using a single video input modality across seven public benchmarks, with only $\sim$3.6M tunable parameters, while also delivering higher throughput and lower inference latency than prior methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131)**  `arXiv:2610.40131`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengdong Hu, Chao Wang, Kaituo Feng, Huanjin Yao, Mingfei Lu, Hehe Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. Existing video QA benchmarks leave this ability unmeasured: their questions are mutually independent, never reference one another, and each is answerable from the video alone, so nothing ever requires carrying information across turns. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Among over 20 evaluated open- and closed-source MLLMs, Claude Opus 5 performs the best with an overall score of only 45.7%, highlighting the challenge of in-context video memory. Our dataset and evaluation are released.
  </details>

- **[BiHashFormer: Hash-Driven Dual-Branch Transformer for Efficient Object Detection in HRW Shots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39937)**  `arXiv:2610.39937`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingchen Huang, Wenxi Li, Chenyang Lyu, Moran Liu, Haozhe Lin, Yuchen Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in gigapixel-level imaging have brought High-Resolution Wide shots to the forefront of research. However, these images present significant challenges: extreme sparsity of foreground, gigapixel-level resolutions and interleaving of foreground and background. This causes traditional detectors and attention mechanisms to be hindered by background, resulting in inefficiency and inaccuracy. To tackle this problem, we propose BiHashFormer, a dual sparsified branch transformer built on hashing. RoI Selector will predict the foreground proportion for top-k selecting target-containing windows. The compression branch processes windows selected and refines attention into HashAttention by discarding half of the key-value pairs, reducing cost and improving efficiency. The compensation branch uses HashMiner to perform low-cost hash searches in the remaining regions, recovering features of missed objects. Experiments confirm that the selection relationship in HashAttention is one-way and reveal the preference of different queries for keys. In experiments on the gigapixel benchmark PANDA, BiHashFormer reduces 37.5% backbone FLOPs while improving AP50 to 81.0%. Moreover, this parallel dual-branch structure allows the model to avoid inference delays caused by inter-branch dependencies, while achieving much greater stability than single-branch models, specially with low rates of RoI selection.
  </details>

- **[Venus-DefakerOne2: A Vision-Forensics Foundation Model with Broader Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37918)**  `arXiv:2610.37918`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chenfan Qu, Yachao Liang, Mingqi Fang, Jiangwei Xie, Zhenming Wang, Baoyue Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world forgery is inherently heterogeneous: it manifests not only as digital manipulation but also as physical spoofing, and is carried not only by images but also by videos. Despite substantial progress, a unified anti-forgery foundation model covering such a broad threat spectrum remains absent. In this paper, we present Venus-DefakerOne2, which substantially extends the forensic capability boundary along both the forgery-form and media-type dimensions. Trained on tens of millions of open-source and in-house samples, it incorporates two key designs. Tide-Style-Tuning alternates LoRA and full-parameter fine-tuning to balance computational efficiency, generalization, and hard-sample fitting while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Entropy-based Test Adaptation determines the optimal prediction modality by analyzing prediction entropy. Venus-DefakerOne2 achieves new state-of-the-art performance across a comprehensive suite of benchmarks, including DeepFake faces (96.7%), AI-generated images (93.3%), tampered documents (87.6%), manipulated natural images (89.6%), physical spoofs (93.1%), and AI-generated videos (96.0%). It significantly outperforms both specialized expert models and its predecessor, DefakerOne, establishing itself as a powerful and versatile foundation model for binary-decision forensic tasks.
  </details>

- **[TurboWAM: Learning What Matters for Fast World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33941)**  `arXiv:2610.33941`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guile Wu, Shuangzhi Li, Xingxin Chen, Chengjie Huang, Sam Hosseini, Tongtong Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models have demonstrated promising performance in robot policy learning, yet their high inference latency remains a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. Recent approaches have sought to improve inference efficiency by eliminating test-time future-frame imagination or accelerating action prediction. However, aggregating representations from all video world model layers can introduce redundant computation, while standard backpropagation may distribute action-supervision gradients unevenly across layers, limiting the effectiveness of world representation learning. In this work, we propose TurboWAM, a fast world action model that learns to efficiently aggregate and optimize world representations for action prediction. Specifically, TurboWAM introduces an efficient world representation aggregation mechanism that restricts aggregation to a bounded range of relevant world model layers identified through layer relevance analysis. Within this bounded range, TurboWAM estimates aggregation weights for different world model layers based on the current action denoising state and combines them with learnable layer-wise weights to fuse world representations. Moreover, TurboWAM employs a world model gradient modulation mechanism that decomposes action-supervision gradients into a layer-shared component and layer-specific residual components, enabling more balanced gradient propagation across world model layers. This mechanism operates exclusively during training and introduces no additional inference computation. Together, these designs enable more efficient world representation learning and low-latency action prediction. Extensive experiments on multiple robot manipulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks show that TurboWAM achieves low inference latency while maintaining competitive task success rates compared with state-of-the-art methods.
  </details>

- **[VoxProg: 3D Spatial Reasoning through Voxel Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30127)**  `arXiv:2610.30127`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhichao Zhai, Menglei Zhao, Guikun Chen, Zihao Yan, Kanzhi Wu, Wenguan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models' abilities to interpret tasks and generate programs offer a promising basis for 3D spatial reasoning. Yet following spatial instructions in 3D scenes requires programs to construct geometric references that language leaves implicit. In this work, we introduce VoxProg, which formulates 3D object placement through voxel programming: a language model translates task interpretations into executable programs over local voxel views of scene surfaces. Because these views index surface geometry on regular grids with explicit coordinates and neighborhood relations, an evidence program can isolate task-relevant regions and boundaries directly from scene meshes, and a downstream pose program can measure the resulting geometry to compute a target $SE(3)$ pose. Visual and numerical feedback from execution lets the model inspect intermediate selections and candidate placements to iteratively revise its code. To evaluate open-ended placement across functional use, geometric fit, and contextual arrangements, we introduce the 71-task VoxProg-Bench benchmark along with an evaluation suite pairing perceptual Intent Alignment and 3D Physical Validity. Across VoxProg-Bench and external benchmarks, VoxProg consistently outperforms relation-based, keypoint-based, and mesh-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[HiQGS: Hierarchical Spatiotemporal Querying for Feed-Forward Gaussian Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27604)**  `arXiv:2610.27604`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiaowei Miao, Qifeng Cheng, Yijing Guo, Yuru Xiao, Ziqian Ni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward Gaussian reconstruction from sparse posed monocular frames requires reasoning over observations that vary jointly in viewpoint and time. Existing methods either predict Gaussian primitives from observation-aligned features or aggregate multi-frame information through compact latent queries. However, the former tightly couples Gaussian allocation to individual frames, while the latter does not explicitly structure observed evidence according to its temporal relation to the requested timestamp. Consequently, evidence across spatial scales, viewpoints, and timestamps can be difficult to align and integrate. We present \textbf{HiQGS}, a query-based hierarchical framework for feed-forward Gaussian scene reconstruction from sparse monocular frames. HiQGS introduces a \textbf{Hierarchical Spatiotemporal Encoder} that organizes multi-resolution geometry-appearance visual features according to image location, observation time, and camera pose, thereby preserving complementary appearance and structural cues. Our \textbf{Time-Adaptive Decoder} use target timestamp queries the resulting hierarchical representation to retrieve relevant spatiotemporal evidence and predicts the corresponding Gaussian scene state. Experiments on static and dynamic scene reconstruction benchmarks demonstrate strong rendering quality and geometric accuracy, validating the effectiveness of hierarchical visual encoding and target-time-aware Gaussian querying.
  </details>

- **[From Label Priors to Task Evidence: Efficient Long-Video Frame Selection via Bayesian GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25418)**  `arXiv:2610.25418`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Bingjie Gao, Qingyang Liu, Qianli Ma, Yibo Miao, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face significant scalability challenges in long-video understanding due to quadratic attention complexity and context window constraints. Existing retrieval-based approaches typically rely on supervised learning from static keyframe annotations. However, these label priors often fail to align with the actual information required by downstream MLLMs for complex reasoning, creating a misalignment between the training target and inference needs. To bridge this gap, we propose a probabilistic framework that reformulates frame selection as latent-variable inference. We first initialize a lightweight policy using supervised learning to capture general visual saliency. Then, we treat a frozen MLLM as a task environment and use its answer likelihood as dense task evidence to refine the policy via Bayesian Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Within this formulation, the negative log-likelihood (NLL) over ground-truth answer tokens provides a principled dense reward, while a prior-matching KL term preserves semantic consistency with the elicited label prior. This process transitions the selection principle from static label priors to task evidence. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench demonstrate that our selector achieves competitive accuracy with MLLM-based scorers while retaining the inference efficiency of lightweight methods. The selector is trained once with a frozen MLLM environment and transfers directly to multiple downstream MLLMs without retraining. Code is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[MaskSense: Confronting the Visual Exploration Trap in Masked Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17009)**  `arXiv:2610.17009`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yawen Shao, Jie Xiao, Kai Zhu, Yu Liu, Hongchen Luo, Xueyang Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown strong potential for aligning generative models with human intent, but adapting RL to masked generative models (MGMs) remains largely underexplored. Existing approaches that directly adapt Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to MGMs leave a foundational conflict unaddressed: MGMs' exploitative decoding nature is inherently at odds with the exploratory diversity that GRPO presupposes. In this work, we reveal a structural decoding bias termed the visual exploration trap: confidence-based sampling trades the exploration of diverse subject realizations for the greedy resolution of low-uncertainty background regions, causing premature collapse of the generative exploration space and starving GRPO of the rollout diversity it depends on. To this end, we propose MaskSense, a novel RL framework for MGMs that confronts the visual exploration trap at both the sampling and optimization stages. Specifically, we introduce a semantic anchored routing sampling mechanism that leverages semantic priors to preserve high-entropy subject token exploration while amplifying intra-group reward variance through exploratory-exploitative routing to yield more discriminative advantage estimates. Furthermore, we design a global entropy transition anchoring strategy to identify the most consequential decoding steps and concentrate gradient updates on them. Extensive experiments on multiple text-to-image benchmarks demonstrate that MaskSense substantially improves upon the base model, with GenEval accuracy improving from 54% to 86% and HPS from 28.89 to 36.39, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[ExeWorldBench: Benchmarking Code-Based Executable 3D World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15166)**  `arXiv:2610.15166`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinge Peng, Zhen Fang, Ruibing Wang, Yongxuan Lv, Haodong Li, Guohui Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents are increasingly evolving beyond reproducing what a world looks like toward constructing executable worlds that capture how a world works. This shift calls for executable world modeling, which integrates two core capabilities: world understanding, reasoning about the underlying structural, mechanical, and causal principles of a world, and executable realization, translating that understanding into a code-based system whose behavior emerges from physical interactions. However, existing benchmarks provide only fragmented evaluations centered on physical reasoning, visual fidelity, or behavioral correctness, leaving the integration of these capabilities insufficiently assessed. To bridge this gap, we introduce ExeWorldBench, to our knowledge the first benchmark to comprehensively evaluate world understanding and executable realization through code-based 3D world construction. ExeWorldBench comprises three progressively challenging tasks: Mechanism Executability, which evaluates the realization of individual mechanisms from visual observations; Compositional Executability, which extends to multi-stage systems with spatial, mechanical, and causal dependencies; and Intent-Driven Executable World Synthesis, which requires constructing executable worlds from high-level goals and physical requirements. Extensive experiments on frontier models reveal substantial limitations in executable world modeling, offering insights into the development of more physically grounded and causally coherent world construction systems.
  </details>

- **[TouchScale: 500 Hours of Human Vision and Touch for Visual-Tactile Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10288)**  `arXiv:2610.10288`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dayou Li, Hao Wang, Qianqian Yang, Zihao Zhu, Haoquan Fang, Ziyao Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale egocentric human interaction data is becoming an important source of physical supervision for embodied learning, yet video alone leaves the contact and pressure that characterize physical interaction unrecorded. Recent visual-tactile datasets provide this missing supervision, but their synchronized tactile data remain far smaller in volume than human video. Moreover, the largest resources often merge recordings from different sensors or annotation procedures, which makes the effect of data scale difficult to isolate. We therefore introduce TouchScale, a 500-hour dataset of contact-rich human interaction recorded with a single unified wearable setup. Its approximately 2K predefined task descriptions span everyday activities and structured manipulation, and each recording temporally aligns egocentric RGB-D video with wrist RGB video and dense full-hand bimanual tactile measurements. Compared with prior tactile data, training on the full TouchScale raises zero-shot contact IoU on data from an unseen tactile sensor from 0.134 to 0.383. Pretraining a visual encoder on TouchScale also yields the highest action recognition accuracy on three benchmarks among the compared visual-tactile datasets. Used for visual-tactile mid-training of a robot policy, TouchScale improves the average real-world success rate across four contact-rich manipulation tasks from 22.5% to 57.5%. With the sensor and collection protocol held fixed, both zero-shot tactile prediction and robot success show an overall upward trend as more TouchScale data is used. These results suggest that human visual-tactile data collected at scale with consistent sensing benefits both perception and robot manipulation. We will publicly release TouchScale, including all synchronized visual-tactile recordings and reconstructed object models, to support future research on scalable visual-tactile learning.
  </details>

- **[RSPDBench: Benchmarking Vision Foundation Models on Earth Observation Tasks Under Physically Grounded Remote-Sensing Product Degradations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23427)**  `arXiv:2609.23427`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tanjim Bin Faruk, Khondaker Masfiq Reza, Shrideep Pallickara, Sangmi Lee Pallickara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision foundation models for Earth observation (EO) are commonly evaluated on clean benchmarks, but operational EO products can already contain spatial, radiometric, alignment, noise, and harmonization defects before reaching the model. Generic image corruptions and broad domain shifts do not isolate these product-level failures. We introduce \textbf{RSPDBench}, a physically grounded \textbf{r}emote-\textbf{s}ensing-\textbf{p}roduct \textbf{d}egradation \textbf{b}enchmark that evaluates seven foundation-model entries and two supervised baselines on five EO datasets under primitive degradations and compound product chains. Its degradation parameters follow published Sentinel-2 and Landsat~8 specifications and are checked against Sentinel-2's paired $10\,\mathrm{m}$ and $20\,\mathrm{m}$ near-infrared bands. We evaluate each model under its own native protocol and measure robustness as the drop from its own clean baseline. Our analysis reveals that degradation sensitivity is strongly structured: (i) the resolution-conditioned encoder is the most robust to resolution loss yet the most sensitive to radiometric gain on satellite products, (ii) the same physical defect can hurt one model while helping another, and (iii) model rankings under resolution loss and radiometric gain are largely reversed. Compound chains add up to $38$ percentage points of drop beyond their strongest component. On m-BigEarthNet, retraining the probe on degraded views removes about 75\% of the noise and harmonization losses, but resolution loss still costs about 20\% of the clean score. EO robustness must therefore be measured against the structured defects of remote-sensing products, not by clean accuracy or generic perturbations alone. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Not Another Text Benchmark: Putting the "Visual" Back in Visual Question Answering for Large Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17112)**  `arXiv:2609.17112`  `cs.CV`  
  _Rwiddhi Chakraborty, Yinong Oliver Wang, Cheng Zhang, Fan Bai, Zhuoran Yu, Michael Kampffmeyer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large video models have exhibited impressive performance on a wide range of visual question answering tasks, owing to the rise of powerful, pretrained text and vision encoders. The usefulness of such models have also been demonstrated on a wide range of benchmarks, with an important caveat - the dominant approach in these benchmarks evaluates multiple choice reasoning via text options. This is a natural way to test text-based reasoning in these models, and has led to significant insights regarding model behavior in the community. In this work, we ask a different question - what happens when the evaluation modality is visual, rather than text? We introduce three new vision-centric evaluation benchmarks in temporal frame retrieval, video future prediction, and causal memory distortion, all designed around evaluating visual understanding capabilities in large video models. Our approach complements the existing approaches to evaluate video understanding in frontier models. We show that current frontier models exhibit significant weakness when attempting to reason through visual queries, rather than text. We conclude with an extended analysis section that provides pointers for future improvements in visual understanding for large video models.
  </details>

- **[Matching in Context: Temporal Fusion and Query Adaptation for Training-Free Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08850)**  `arXiv:2609.08850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuo Cao, Bingqing Zhang, Sen Wang, Xue Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-guided Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) localizes moments in an untrimmed video that correspond to a text query. Supervised approaches require costly temporal annotations and task-specific training, yet can generalize poorly under distribution shift. To reduce this dependence on task-specific supervision, training-free approaches use pretrained vision-language models to localize moments by measuring the similarity between video and query features. However, independently encoded frames provide limited temporal context, while a fixed query embedding can assign similar scores to different candidate moments within the video. To address these limitations, we propose DSE-VTG, a \underline{D}ual-\underline{S}ide \underline{E}nhancement framework that combines visual evidence across temporal granularities with video-specific query adaptation, without task-specific training. On the visual side, Multi-scale Similarity Fusion (MSF) combines frame- and clip-level similarities into a unified, temporally aware similarity profile. On the query side, Query-level Test-Time Adaptation (Q-TTA) uses pseudo-labels to optimize a lightweight additive offset for more discriminative matching within the video, without fine-tuning the backbone or calling external large language models. Experiments on three standard and two out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks show gains over the prior training-free methods. On Charades-STA, DSE-VTG improves mIoU by 5.89 percentage points over the strongest compared baseline under the same backbone. Under distribution shift, DSE-VTG reaches 50.86 mIoU on Charades-CG Novel-Word, surpassing the strongest supervised baseline by 2.76 mIoU. Our code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[FreeTransformSR: Efficient Lightweight Image Super-Resolution via Free Low-Rank Learnable Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05912)**  `arXiv:2609.05912`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongji Li, Yunhui Li, Jianing Wang, Xiaodong Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single image super-resolution aims to reconstruct high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs. This paper proposes FreeTransformSR, a novel lightweight super-resolution network based on a channel-wise free low-rank learnable transform. The transform learns task-adaptive basis functions in a data-driven manner, enabling adaptive feature modulation with minimal parameter overhead. To further enhance high-frequency detail recovery, we in-troduce a local feature modulation branch that complements transform-domain processing with depthwise convolu-tion. In addition, a soft complexity adaptive module dynamically fuses the outputs of local convolution and window self-attention branches through a lightweight gating network, adaptively adjusting the fusion ratio based on regional texture characteristics. An adaptive intensity modulation strategy is also incorporated to adjust transform-domain response strength at the sample level, enabling the network to dynamically adjust processing intensity according to input features. Extensive experiments on five benchmark datasets demonstrate that FreeTransformSR achieves competitive PSNR/SSIM performance with significantly fewer parameters and FLOPs. Specifically, FreeTransformSR achieves 32.41 dB on BSD100 (X2, 634K parameters) and 27.00 dB on Urban100 (X4, 653K parameters), while delivering faster inference speed than competing methods, making it well-suited for deployment in resource-constrained scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Progressive Pseudo-Label Optimization for Point-Supervised Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02171)**  `arXiv:2609.02171`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hailong Ning, Hao Wang, Yimeng Wang, Tao Lei, Renwei Dian, Asoke K. Nandi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Point-supervised change detection (PS-CD) aims to identify pixel-level changes between bi-temporal images using only sparsely annotated points. Although point annotations substantially reduce labeling costs, their limited spatial coverage often results in incomplete and noisy pseudo-labels. To address this issue, we propose a two-stage framework that introduces SAM2 priors into PS-CD and progressively adapts them to the target task. In Stage I, SAM2 generates object-aware candidate masks from point annotations on the bi-temporal images, and a bi-temporal mask selection strategy is designed to convert generic segmentation responses into more reliable change pseudo-labels. Subsequently, a lightweight CNN refinement module with an uncertainty-aware loss is employed to improve boundary quality and local structural consistency. In Stage II, we construct a teacher-student self-training framework in which the teacher is updated by exponential moving average and periodically refreshes the pseudo-labels. This design establishes a closed-loop optimization process that alternates between pseudo-label refinement and model re-optimization. Experiments on three benchmark datasets, including WHU-CD, LEVIR-CD, and SYSU-CD, demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms previous weakly supervised approaches on most benchmarks and remains competitive with several fully supervised methods.
  </details>

- **[Understand Before Detect: Vision--Language Learning for Omni-Domain Infrared Small Target Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07015)**  `arXiv:2608.07015`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyang Yuan, Boyang Li, Yingqian Wang, Yimian Dai, Nuo Chen, Xinfei Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-domain infrared small target (IRST) detection is crucial for infrared surveillance, yet remains challenging due to heterogeneous imaging domains and inconsistent target characteristics. Previous deep learning-based methods have been developed for visual-only paradigms and achieved promising performance on domain-specific tasks. However, existing methods follow the task-specific supervised learning paradigm. This paradigm simplifies the full-scene infrared observations to sparse target supervision, discarding the semantics that remain invariant across heterogeneous domains. Consequently, detection performance suffers substantially under domain shifts. To handle this issue, we introduce \textbf{``understand before detect''}, a paradigm that formulates omni-domain IRST detection as an understanding-driven process, where holistic infrared target understanding precedes precise detection. Building on this paradigm, we propose \textbf{JinSight}, which first develops holistic IRST understanding through language supervision and then transfers the learned cross-domain representations to precise small-target detection. By grounding infrared representations in language semantics, JinSight enables a single model to generalize across heterogeneous infrared domains. We then introduce Latent Semantic Interaction (LSI), which exchanges language-aligned global semantics with fine-grained spatial features in a compact low-rank space. To address the lack of multimodal omni-domain IRST benchmarks, we build \textbf{OmniIRST-VL}, the first large-scale, highly diverse vision--language dataset for omni-domain IRST detection. It comprises over 39k annotations across six complementary instruction tasks covering both scene-level understanding and target-centric reasoning.
  </details>

- **[UAV-DualCog: A Dual-Cognition Benchmark for Grounded UAV Agent-World Reasoning with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16193)**  `arXiv:2607.16193`  `cs.CV`  
  _Like Liu, Zhengzheng Xu, Hongzhe Li, Lin Wang, Dian Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) perform strongly across vision-language tasks, yet remain insufficiently explored in UAV scenarios. Recent UAV-oriented benchmarks have begun to evaluate MLLMs in aerial scenarios, but typically focus on scene understanding, event recognition, or navigation completion, rather than jointly assessing the dual-cognition capability required for UAV agents: reasoning about the UAV's own position, behavior, and observation state alongside external landmark states under continuously changing viewpoints, supported by explicit spatio-temporal grounding. To address this gap, we present UAV-DualCog, the first benchmark to formulate UAV dual cognition as the coordinated evaluation of Agent-State Reasoning and World-State Reasoning across multi-view images and egocentric videos, with explicit spatial or temporal grounding. The State-aware UAV Simulation Platform (SUSP) and the DualCog-aware Capture System (DCCS) construct 4,096 image and 2,048 video samples from 12 scenes, pairing complementary state questions within shared Agent-Landmark contexts. Extensive evaluations on 45 models show that current MLLMs remain far from reliable in UAV dual cognition: Agent-State Reasoning is generally weaker than World-State Reasoning, semantic answers do not reliably coincide with spatial or temporal grounding, and coordinated abilities remain limited. Thinking-mode comparisons, open-ended analyses, and sim-to-real validation show that these limitations persist across inference settings, formats, and environments. We further construct UAV-DualCog-Train and improve UAV dual-cognition performance through a lightweight optimization probe. Supplementary website, code, and dataset:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Readout: Unlocking Video Backbones for AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15321)**  `arXiv:2607.15321`  `cs.CV`  
  _Manni Cui, Ziheng Qin, ZiAn Wang, Ruiqi Liu, Dianyuan Zou, Jianglan Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated videos (AIGVs) typically contain subtle temporal artifacts that arise from inter-frame inconsistencies rather than within individual frames. A detector that captures such artifacts should therefore benefit from video pretrained backbones over image only ones. In practice, however, video backbones with standard global readouts often fail to outperform strong image pretrained probes on AIGV benchmarks. We attribute this gap to excessive spatiotemporal aggregation in the readout. Video pretrained backbones tend to compress each frame into a single global descriptor. This compression suppresses local patch level temporal dynamics and discards inter patch relations, which are precisely the cues that AIGV detection most reliably depends on. Based on this, we propose Velocity Gated Patch Velocity Profiling (V-PVP), a lightweight readout that replaces only the aggregation layer with two parallel streams over the patch velocity field, adding only about $0.5$M trainable parameters. V-PVP serves as a general plug-and-play module that consistently improves performance across diverse video backbones under both end-to-end fine-tuning and linear probing settings. Our method reaches 95.28 AUC on AIGVDBench while keeping the backbone fully frozen. The results show that simply replacing the aggregation layer reactivates the temporal potential of frozen video backbones, restoring their advantage on AIGV detection. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Why Domain Matters: Domain-Aware Benchmarking of Underwater Object Detection and Annotation Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10575)**  `arXiv:2607.10575`  `cs.CV`  
  _Melanie Wille, Dimity Miller, Tobias Fischer, Scarlett Raine_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underwater object detection is strongly affected by domain shift, where performance can vary significantly across different locations, habitats, and deployment conditions. However, detector performance is typically evaluated using aggregate metrics that hide failures in specific environments, while existing domain generalization benchmarks often rely on synthetic variations that do not reflect real-world conditions. To address these limitations we introduce a framework that characterizes underwater images by appearance, scene composition, and acquisition geometry to assign domain labels. Using this framework, we perform the first systematic study of how domain factors influence both human annotation quality in underwater object detection datasets and deep learning-based detector performance, revealing substantial domain-dependent discrepancies. By incorporating physically meaningful domain labels, domain shift becomes something we can characterize, measure, benchmark, and act on. We highlight how our framework can be used to guide data collection and annotation, design more informative benchmarks, and assess detector robustness across diverse underwater environments.
  </details>

- **[A Large Scale Open-Source Image and Video Dataset for Robust Wildfire Detection and Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10174)**  `arXiv:2606.10174`  `cs.CV`  
  _Emadeldeen Hamdan, Yingyi Luo, B. Ugur Toreyin, Erdem Koyuncu, Adam J. Watts, Ugur Gudukbay, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wildfire detection and monitoring are critical for mitigating fire spread and reducing environmental and infrastructural damage. In this work, we introduce GWFP (Global Wildfire Prevention Dataset), a large-scale, open-source dataset of wildfire images and videos designed to support early fire and smoke detection research. GWFP contains geographically diverse wildfire scenes, including flames, smoke, Waterdog/Fog environmental conditions, Near Infrared (NIR) imagery, Ember, and challenging negative samples collected from real-world scenarios worldwide. To evaluate dataset robustness and cross-domain generalization, we benchmark multiple convolutional and transformer-based architectures across both in-domain and cross-dataset settings. Additionally, we explore lightweight frequency--spatial feature interaction using Hadamard-enhanced residual connections (HTE-ResNet) to analyze representation robustness under domain-shift conditions. Experimental results demonstrate strong cross-dataset generalization and practical utility for real-world wildfire monitoring applications. The dataset is available on Kaggle:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Every9D-22M: Large-Scale Real-World 9D Canonicalization of Everyday Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28270)**  `arXiv:2605.28270`  `cs.CV`  
  _Leonhard Sommer, Emil Akopyan, Adam Kortylewski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the 9D pose of everyday objects from a single real-world image remains challenging. This is largely due to the lack of large-scale supervision. Most existing datasets either rely heavily on synthetic renderings or provide limited coverage of real-world objects. We address this gap with Every9D-22M, a dataset of 9D pose annotations for 22.8M real-world images from 114K object-centric videos spanning 630 everyday object categories, making it nearly two orders of magnitude larger than any prior real-world 9D pose dataset in at least one of category, object, or image count. To achieve this scale, we leverage object-centric videos by reconstructing object-level point clouds via multi-view geometry and aligning similar instances into a shared canonical coordinate frame. Canonical poses are manually annotated for only a small set of reference objects (fewer than 0.01% of all images) and propagated to the remaining instances via cross-instance alignment. All propagated canonical poses are then verified from multiple viewpoints. We further introduce cross-category orientation rules that induce category-level symmetries, enabling symmetry-aware evaluation. Beyond establishing dedicated training and evaluation splits as a benchmark for 9D pose foundation models, we show that training on Every9D-22M generalizes better to other datasets than training on existing datasets, and outperforms prior foundation models in rotation accuracy on all benchmarks except the viewpoint-biased ImageNet3D, where joint training with ImageNet3D surpasses them as well. Data and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GateMOT: Q-Gated Attention for Dense Object Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26353)**  `arXiv:2604.26353`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingjin Lv, Zelin Liu, Feifei Shao, Yi-Ping Phoebe Chen, Junqing Yu, Wei Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While large models demonstrate the strong representational power of vanilla attention, this core mechanism cannot be directly applied to Dense Object Tracking: its quadratic all-to-all interactions are computationally prohibitive for dense motion estimation on high-resolution features. This mismatch prevents Dense Object Tracking from fully leveraging attention-based modeling in crowded and occlusion-heavy scenes. To address this challenge, we introduce GateMOT, an online tracking framework centered on Q-Gated Attention (Q-Attention), an efficient and spatially aware attention variant. Our key idea is to repurpose the Query from a similarity-conditioning term into a learnable gating unit. This Gating-Query (Gating-Q) produces a probabilistic gate that modulates Key features in an element-wise manner, enabling explicit relevance selection instead of costly global aggregation. Built on this mechanism, parallel Q-Attention heads transform one shared feature map into task-specific yet consistent representations for detection, motion, and re-identification, yielding a tightly coupled multi-task decoder with linear-complexity gating operations. GateMOT achieves state-of-the-art HOTA of 48.4, MOTA of 67.8, and IDF1 of 64.5 on BEE24, and demonstrates strong performance on additional Dense Object Tracking benchmarks. These results show that Q-Attention is a simple, effective, and transferable building block for attention-based tracking in dense tracking scenarios.
  </details>

- **[LPLCv2: An Expanded Dataset for Fine-Grained License Plate Legibility Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08741)**  `arXiv:2604.08741`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lucas Wojcik, Eduardo A. F. Machoski, Eduil Nascimento Jr., Rayson Laroca, David Menotti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern Automatic License Plate Recognition (ALPR) systems achieve outstanding performance in controlled, well-defined scenarios. However, large-scale real-world usage remains challenging due to low-quality imaging devices, compression artifacts, and suboptimal camera installation. Identifying illegible license plates (LPs) has recently become feasible through a dedicated benchmark; however, its impact has been limited by its small size and annotation errors. In this work, we expand the original benchmark to over three times the size with two extra capture days, revise its annotations and introduce novel labels. LP-level annotations include bounding boxes, text, and legibility level, while vehicle-level annotations comprise make, model, type, and color. Image-level annotations feature camera identity, capture conditions (e.g., rain and faulty cameras), acquisition time, and day ID. We present a novel training procedure featuring an Exponential Moving Average-based loss function and a refined learning rate scheduler, addressing common mistakes in testing. These improvements enable a baseline model to achieve an 89.5% F1-score on the test set, considerably surpassing the previous state of the art. We further introduce a novel protocol to explicitly addresses camera contamination between training and evaluation splits, where results show a small impact. Dataset and code are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Simplifying, Scaling, and Saturating Flow Matching for Cell Microscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26790)**  `arXiv:2603.26790`  `cs.CV`  
  _Charles Jones, Emmanuel Noutahi, Zeinab Navidi, Alessandro Palma, Kian Kenyon-Dean, Jason Hartford, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching generative models are increasingly used to simulate cell responses to biological perturbations. However, the design space for building such models is large and underexplored. We systematically analyse the design space of flow matching models for cell microscopy images, finding that many popular techniques can add unnecessary complexity and may even hurt performance. We develop a simple, stable, and scalable recipe which we use to train our generative microscopy model. We scale training compute to an order of magnitude larger than prior methods, saturating existing public benchmarks with approximately two-fold FID and ten-fold KID improvements over prior methods. We report an extended battery of metrics, finding that improved evaluation encoders can alleviate the saturation problem. Our methods represent progress towards high-fidelity virtual microscopy assays for tasks such as virtual screening, batch effect correction, and data enrichment.
  </details>

- **[Early Failure Detection and Intervention in Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14320)**  `arXiv:2603.14320`  `cs.CV`  
  _Byungki Kwon, Sohwi Lim, Nam Hyeon-Woo, Moon Ye-Bin, Tae-Hyun Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models have rapidly advanced, yet generations still occasionally fail in practice, such as low text-video alignment or low perceptual quality. Since diffusion sampling is non-deterministic, it is difficult to know during inference whether a generation will succeed or fail, incurring high computational cost due to trial-and-error regeneration. To address this, we propose an early failure detection and diagnostic intervention pipeline for latent T2V diffusion models. For detection, we design a Real-time Inspection (RI) module that converts latents into intermediate video previews, enabling the use of established text-video alignment scorers for inspection in the RGB space. The RI module completes the conversion and inspection process in just 39.2ms. This is highly efficient considering that CogVideoX-5B requires 4.3s per denoising step when generating a 480p, 49-frame video on an NVIDIA A100 GPU. Subsequently, we trigger a hierarchical and early-exit intervention pipeline only when failure is predicted. Experiments on CogVideoX-5B and Wan2.1-1.3B demonstrate consistency gains on VBench with up to 2.64 times less time overhead compared to post-hoc regeneration. Our method also generalizes to a higher-capacity setting, remaining effective on Wan2.1-14B with 720p resolution and 81-frame generation. Furthermore, our pipeline is plug-and-play and orthogonal to existing techniques, showing seamless compatibility with prompt refinement and sampling guidance methods. We also provide evidence that failure signals emerge early in the denoising process and are detectable within intermediate video previews using standard vision-language evaluators.
  </details>

- **[Towards Visual Query Segmentation in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08898)**  `arXiv:2603.08898`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bing Fan, Minghao Li, Hanzhi Zhang, Shaohua Dong, Naga Prudhvi Mareedu, Xiaoqiong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we introduce visual query segmentation (VQS), a new paradigm of visual query localization (VQL) that aims to segment all pixel-level occurrences of an object of interest in an untrimmed video, given a visual query. Compared with existing VQL locating only the last appearance of a target using bounding boxes, VQS enables more comprehensive (i.e., all object occurrences) and precise (i.e., pixel-level masks) localization, making it more practical for real-world scenarios. To foster research on this task, we introduce VQS-4K, a large-scale benchmark dedicated to VQS. Specifically, VQS-4K consists of 4,111 videos with more than 1.3 million frames, and covers a diverse set of 222 object categories. Each video is paired with a visual query defined by a frame outside search video and its target mask, and annotated with spatial-temporal masklets corresponding to the queried target. To ensure high quality, all videos in VQS-4K are manually labeled with meticulous inspection and iterative refinement. To our best knowledge, VQS-4K is the first benchmark specifically designed for VQS. Furthermore, to stimulate future research, we present a simple yet effective method, named VQ-SAM, which extends SAM 2 by leveraging target-specific and background distractor cues from the video to progressively evolve the memory through a novel multi-stage framework with an adaptive memory generation (AMG) module for VQS, significantly improving the performance. In our experiments on VQS-4K, VQ-SAM achieves promising results and surpasses existing methods, evidencing its effectiveness. With VQS-4K and VQ-SAM, we expect to inspire more future research and applications on VQS. Our benchmark and code are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Evaluation of Visual Processing Should Be Human-Centered, Not Metric-Centered](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00643)**  `arXiv:2603.00643`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fanghua Yu, Jinfan Hu, Zhiyuan You, Xiang Yin, Hongyu An, Xinqi Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This position paper argues that the evaluation of modern visual processing systems should no longer be driven primarily by single-metric image quality assessment benchmarks, particularly in the era of generative and perception-oriented methods. Image restoration exemplifies this divergence: while objective IQA metrics enable reproducible, scalable evaluation, they have increasingly drifted apart from human perception and user preferences. We contend that this mismatch risks constraining innovation and misguiding research progress across visual processing tasks. Rather than rejecting metrics altogether, this paper calls for a rebalancing of evaluation paradigms, advocating a more human-centered, context-aware, and fine-grained approach to assessing the visual models' outcomes.
  </details>

- **[FSDAM: Few-Shot Driving Attention Modeling via Vision-Language Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.12708)**  `arXiv:2511.12708`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaiser Hamid, Can Cui, Khandakar Ashrafi Akbar, Ziran Wang, Nade Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding not only where drivers look but also why their attention shifts is essential for interpretable human-AI collaboration in autonomous driving. Driver attention is not purely perceptual but semantically structured. Thus attention shifts can be learned through minimal semantic supervision rather than dense large-scale annotation. We present FSDAM (Few-Shot Driver Attention Modeling), a framework that achieves joint spatial attention prediction and structured explanation generation using 90 annotated examples. Our key insight is to decompose attention into an explicit reasoning representation, including scene context, current focus, anticipated next focus, and causal explanation, and to learn next-focus anticipation through minimal-pair supervision. To address task conflict and large sample requirements of existing models, and to mitigate task interference under limited data, we introduce a novel dual-pathway architecture in which separate modules handle spatial prediction and caption generation. In addition, we use a training-only vision-language alignment mechanism that injects semantic priors into spatial learning without increasing inference complexity, mitigating task interference under few-shot training. Despite extreme data scarcity, FSDAM achieves competitive performance in gaze prediction, and generates coherent, context-aware structural reasoning for improved interpretability. The model further demonstrates strong zero-shot generalization across multiple driving benchmarks. These results suggest that semantically grounded attention modeling enables data-efficient learning and provides a scalable path toward explainable driver attention systems in data-constrained environments.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Perceive and Describe Medical Image Quality Beyond Scores?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01691)**  `arXiv:2510.01691`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiyao Liu, Jinjie Wei, Wanying Qu, Chenglong Ma, Junzhi Ning, Yunheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical Image Quality Assessment (IQA) serves as a critical safety gate for clinical AI, yet existing approaches rely on scalar metrics that fail to capture the descriptive, expert-like evaluation process central to clinical practice. We introduce MedQ-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for language-based medical IQA evaluation with MLLMs, establishing a perception--description paradigm across 5 imaging modalities and over 40 quality attributes. The benchmark comprises two complementary tasks: MedQ-Perception probes low-level visual attribute discrimination via 2,600 expert-annotated perceptual queries; MedQ-Description covers both no-reference and comparison description sub-tasks with 708 expert-annotated samples, evaluated through a four-dimensional judging protocol. Comprehensive evaluation of 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals that current models exhibit only preliminary and unstable capabilities, and highlights the urgent need for targeted optimization of MLLMs in medical IQA. Project & Dataset:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Fieldable Low-Light Multi-Task Perception via Foundation-Model-Assisted Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23626)**  `arXiv:2509.23626`  `cs.CV`  
  _Beomseok Kang, Niluthpol Chowdhury Mithun, Mikhail Sizintsev, Han-Pang Chiu, Supun Samarasekera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fieldable low-light perception systems require both semantic and geometric scene understanding under severe domain shift and embedded compute constraints. We present FAMDA, a foundation-model-assisted self-training framework for unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) in multi-task dense prediction. The framework integrates segmentation and depth foundation models into a self-training paradigm to generate high-quality pseudo-labels for the target domain, distilling their generalization capabilities into a single lightweight student network. To connect the method to deployment, we instantiate the adapted student on a compact handheld low-light prototype with DomiNite imaging and Jetson Orin Nano embedded compute. We evaluate on standard synthetic-to-real multi-task UDA benchmarks for comparison with prior work and on our field-collected low-light dataset to validate daytime-to-low-light adaptation in the target application setting. Across these evaluations, the proposed approach achieves strong segmentation and depth accuracy with substantially lower memory use and inference latency than direct foundation-model inference, demonstrating a practical path toward embedded multi-task perception for low-light field systems.
  </details>

- **[Every Camera Effect, Every Time, All at Once: 4D Gaussian Ray Tracing for Physics-based Camera Effect Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10759)**  `arXiv:2509.10759`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yi-Ruei Liu, You-Zhe Xie, Yu-Hsiang Hsu, Jing-En Huang, I-Sheng Fang, Yu-Lun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current video diffusion models can generate recognizable camera-effect appearances but do not reliably follow specified physical parameters such as field of view, aperture, and readout time, partly because real dynamic videos with diverse and accurate camera-effect annotations are scarce. Physics-based simulation provides exact labels but suffers from a sim-to-real gap. Reconstructing real dynamic scenes offers an alternative, but Dynamic NeRF methods render too slowly, whereas rasterization-based Dynamic Gaussian Splatting cannot model the light transport required by these effects. We propose 4D Gaussian Ray Tracing (4D-GRT), a two-stage data-generation pipeline that reconstructs dynamic scenes as 4D Gaussians from multi-view pinhole videos and re-renders them with physically based camera models under specified parameters. On CamVFX-4D, our controlled benchmark of eight dynamic indoor scenes under four camera effects, 4D-GRT achieves up to 3.4 dB higher PSNR and 6 times faster rendering than MSTH. Finally, we demonstrate two downstream applications: fine-tuning a video generation model on our data to improve camera-effect control and extending our pipeline to monocular input. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[FDSG: Forecasting Dynamic Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01487)**  `arXiv:2506.01487`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yi Yang, Yuren Cong, Hao Cheng, Bodo Rosenhahn, Michael Ying Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic scene graph generation extends scene graph generation from images to videos by modeling entity relationships and their temporal evolution. However, existing methods either generate scene graphs from observed frames without explicitly modeling temporal dynamics or predict only relationships while assuming static entity labels and locations. These limitations hinder effective extrapolation of both entity and relationship dynamics, restricting video scene understanding. We propose Forecasting Dynamic Scene Graphs (FDSG), a novel framework that predicts future entity labels, bounding boxes, and relationships, for unobserved frames while also generating scene graphs for observed frames. Our scene graph forecast module leverages query decomposition and neural stochastic differential equations to model entity and relationship dynamics. A temporal aggregation module further refines predictions by integrating observed and forecasted information via cross-attention. To benchmark FDSG, we introduce Scene Graph Forecasting, a new task for full future scene graph prediction. Extensive experiments on Action Genome and VidOR show that FDSG outperforms state-of-the-art methods on dynamic scene graph generation, scene graph anticipation, and scene graph forecasting. Codes will be released upon publication.
  </details>

- **[GatedV: Self-Gated Values for Transformer Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02145)**  `arXiv:2611.02145`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Li-Ming Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard attention forms query (Q), key (K), and value (V) matrices through learned linear projections of input hidden states. Architectures such as GAU and Mega incorporate nonlinear transformations into attention. In this paper, we propose GatedV, a simple attention variant that applies SiLU only to V before aggregation without introducing additional trainable parameters. We evaluate GatedV against a standard decoder-only Transformer using matched parameter counts, paired initializations, and equal training-token budgets. At 2.33M parameters, GatedV reduces mean held-out perplexity from 59.1 to 57.9 (2.0%), with improvements across all five paired seeds. At 26.16M parameters, following an equal-budget learning-rate search, mean held-out perplexity falls from 20.2 to 19.7 (2.4%), with improvements across all three paired seeds. Small-model ablations show that the tested affine rescaling of values does not fully reproduce GatedV's gains. A short interleaved benchmark on NVIDIA DGX Spark measures approximately 0.7% higher full-context forward latency for the larger model, based on synchronized wall-clock timings. These findings suggest that value-only self-gating can improve language modeling performance without increasing parameter count or redesigning the attention-weighting mechanism.
  </details>

- **[FinForesight: A Diagnostic Backtesting Benchmark for Deep Research Systems in Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01621)**  `arXiv:2611.01621`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fengyuan Liu, Mukai Li, Jieshun You, Yilin Zheng, Chenjia Min, Yizhou Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anticipating financial outcomes requires synthesizing evolving evidence and reasoning under uncertainty, making deep research systems promising tools for forecasting. Yet evaluating forecasting ability is challenging because historical backtests may reward memorized outcomes or information retrieved after the forecast date. Live forecasting avoids direct access to realized outcomes, but feedback arrives only after events resolve. To obtain timely feedback and assess historical scores, we introduce FinForesight, a diagnostic backtesting benchmark for financial deep research systems. Across the Chinese, U.S., and Hong Kong markets, the benchmark evaluates four forecasting tasks: valuation, return ranking, event direction, and future financial fundamentals. Two capability diagnostic tasks assess counterfactual reasoning and financial calculation, scored separately from forecasting performance. Our harness-compatible framework restricts retrieved evidence to information available at the forecast date. We compare pretraining-era, resolved post-cutoff, and live evaluations to test whether controlled backtests approximate future performance. Pretraining-era scores exceed post-cutoff scores in most temporal comparisons, and stronger retrieval controls narrow the gap between post-cutoff backtests and live results. Forecasting attribution and robustness analyses reveal how scores respond to retrieval policy and how predictions respond to prompt rewrites and numerical perturbations. The code and benchmark data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Write Facts the Way Models Read Them: Lifting the Two-Hop Curse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01519)**  `arXiv:2611.01519`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyun Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model that learns a new fact by fine-tuning can repeat it but cannot reason with it: taught that a person is a citizen of a new country, it still cannot say, without chain-of-thought, who the head of state of that person's country is. This two-hop curse is widely treated as a reasoning deficit, and the strongest knowledge editors answer it with synthesized reasoning data. We show that the reasoning is intact and the writing is wrong. Fine-tuning writes a new fact as an answer to be said, whereas the second hop reads the bridge ("that person's country") as an entity, from the middle layers on: patching the model's own representation of the entity into the bridge at those layers largely restores the second hop, yet after fine-tuning the bridge there is no closer to the new entity than before the edit. We therefore propose read-equivalent writing (Legible): one loss term makes the bridge phrase, at the layer where the second hop finishes reading it, match the model's own reading of the new entity, and keeps the same relation's other subjects unchanged. Phrases and targets come from the edited model itself, and no multi-hop training data is used. Across Qwen2.5, Llama-3 and Qwen3, Legible (I) composes learned facts more often than the model composes its own knowledge, on MQuAKE's two-hop-curse questions; (II) reaches 1.8-2.6x the bridge accuracy of CaKE, whose reasoning data composes no better than plain fine-tuning, and has the best average over composition, accuracy and locality against four families of writers; and (III) lifts the curse on RippleEdits, on the benchmark of Balesni et al., who named the curse, and at 14B.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive-Policy Distillation: Knowledge Injection under Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00852)**  `arXiv:2611.00852`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hojun Cho, Kang Eun Jeon, Jaegul Choo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD), which uses reverse-KL supervision on student rollouts to transfer a context-conditioned model's behavior into student model parameters for closed-book inference, has shown strong results. However, we find that this success does not translate to knowledge injection: across two document-QA benchmarks, OPD injects document knowledge far less efficiently than simple off-policy distillation. We trace this reversal to two failures that are structural, yet sparse across token positions. Reverse KL weights its gradient by the student distribution and therefore barely lifts document-specific tokens the student considers unlikely; after forward KL restores this signal, student rollouts expose later positions on the target trajectory only after generating the preceding tokens correctly, so coverage deteriorates with target length. Motivated by the concentration of teacher-student divergence at these hard positions, we propose Adaptive-Policy Distillation (APD), whose per-token gate jointly shifts the rollout toward the teacher and the objective toward forward KL where needed, withdrawing both interventions as the student learns. Without labels or a verifier, APD improves over OPD by up to 54.6%, achieving the best scores of 0.558 F1 on QASPER and 0.486 MRR on BioASQ.
  </details>

- **[An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093)**  `arXiv:2611.03093`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lakshmi Narayanan P, Narenkarthik K, Naren Kumar C_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support.
