In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.
  </details>

- **[MemShift: Separating Detection from Repair in LLM-Derived Personal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02338)**  `arXiv:2611.02338`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zichuan Xu, Haochen Zhang, Yuyang Dong, Makoto Onizuka, Chuan Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI assistants store values that a language model derived from a person's records under the person's own definitions, such as their important contacts. Keeping such a value correct takes two steps: detection, noticing that an input changed, and repair, producing the correct new value. Benchmarks that score only the final answer cannot tell which step failed, and they state every change as new text. Seeing this limitation, we present MemShift, a benchmark of five shifts: source revision, deletion, redefinition, writer switch, and export. The first three fork a synthetic world into branches that differ only in the shift. Gold values and dependencies are fixed before any text, and a deterministic scorer replaces an LLM judge. For a memory that reports its invalidations, MemShift shows for each changed item whether the memory noticed the change and whether its repair was right. Moreover, we build a reference memory with known detection and repair to validate the measure. By evaluating four open-weight LLMs, we find that detection helps only as much as repair allows: the LLMs re-derive few values computed by the person's definitions correctly, and programs that the LLM writes over typed records close most of the gap to exact recomputation. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Halal-NeSyNet: Clause-Scoped Neuro-Symbolic Cross-Attention for Automated Food Ingredient Auditing and Risk-Aware Halal Logistics Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01612)**  `arXiv:2611.01612`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Mochamad Rizal Fauzan, Naila Nursyifa, Irgi Surya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifying that a packaged food is Halal requires reading ingredient declarations and E-number additives before goods enter certified warehouses. Dual-origin additives such as E471, E422, E441 and E920 are Mushbooh (doubtful) unless a botanical, microbial or certified-Halal qualifier appears in the same ingredient clause, so a plant-based qualifier on one clause must not clear an unqualified additive in another. We call a failure to respect this scope cross-clause modifier confounding and build a benchmark of 3,199 items (277 Open Food Facts records and 2,922 template-generated clause-swap items, labelled by an explicit 50-code rule base rather than by a certification authority) with a 540-item counterfactual suite. We propose Halal-NeSyNet, which couples a DistilBERT encoder with a 16-dimensional rule-derived symbolic vector through cross-attention and gated fusion, trained with an asymmetric false-Halal risk loss. Over five seeds, text-only TF-IDF baselines reach 86.25-86.71 % accuracy with 13.42-21.73 % false-Halal leakage and 6.33-26.00 % counterfactual triad consistency, whereas Halal-NeSyNet reaches 97.04+/-0.34 % and 99.67+/-0.50 %. Gradient boosting given the same symbolic vector reaches 96.25+/-1.24 % (+0.59 points from Halal-NeSyNet, 95 % CI [-1.11, +2.28]), so most of the gain comes from clause-scoped symbolic features and not from the network, and the risk loss did not help. Holding out whole template families, text-only models fall to 61.91-78.35 %, Halal-NeSyNet to 90.87+/-11.76 % and logistic regression with the symbolic vector to 91.11+/-2.88 %, with far more spread for Halal-NeSyNet. A three-lane receiving simulation auto-clears 49.33 % of cargo at 0.76 % green-lane contamination. Because labels and features come from one rule engine and 91.3 % of the items are templated, the results measure fidelity to a rule policy, not religious certification.
  </details>

- **[T2D: Target-to-Draft Model Pruning & Adaptation for Efficient Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34103)**  `arXiv:2610.34103`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shahriar Kabir Nahin, Xiao Ye, Muhao Chen, Ben Zhou, Anshuman Chhabra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding significantly accelerates autoregressive text generation in Large Language Models (LLMs), but its efficacy depends largely on two key factors: the distributional alignment between the draft and the target model, and the draft model's latency during inference. However, existing approaches typically utilize independently pretrained small draft models or seek to prune the target model to a manually chosen architecture, thereby failing to explicitly balance the trade-off between draft model alignment and token generation efficiency. To address this gap, we formulate draft model design as an optimization problem and propose T2D, a framework that constructs draft models directly from the target model using latency-aware structured pruning and knowledge distillation. In this manner, T2D obtains draft models that are jointly optimized for both measured inference latency and cross-entropy loss on the target model's responses, resulting in superlative speculative decoding performance. Through extensive experiments on multi-domain speculative decoding benchmarks and across several target model families of differing scale, we demonstrate how T2D prunes up to 92\% of the target model to obtain highly performant draft models compared to competitive baselines. Moreover, draft models obtained via T2D can also be utilized by other speculative decoding token-alignment and knowledge distillation methods in a plug-and-play fashion, leading to further gains. Our findings underscore the importance of jointly navigating the draft model architecture-alignment frontier to unlock the full potential of speculative decoding for LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Consensus: Structural Ensembling of LLM Reasoning via Weighted DAG Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27783)**  `arXiv:2607.27783`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Amruta Parulekar, Jinu Lee, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Hari Sundaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) reason in free-form chain-of-thought (CoT) without revealing which steps were well supported and which alternatives were rejected. In high-stakes domains, users then cannot judge if an answer rests on sound reasoning. Majority voting and multi-agent debate improve answers using model agreement, but do not expose agreement on individual steps. Reasoning Consensus (RC) extracts a typed reasoning graph from each CoT, merges equivalent steps across graphs from diverse models, and weights every step by how many chains attest to it. RC reveals this step-wise support and the varied justifications behind each answer. On 7 benchmarks across law, science, medicine, narrative reasoning and logic, RC matches majority voting and debate in accuracy (69.4% vs. 69.0-70.3%). An LLM judge prefers RC's best-supported graph to other graphs for the same answer. In a user study, participants read RC graphs faster than debate transcripts or vote tallies, and 14 of 18 preferred them for future use.
  </details>

- **[What Transfers from Text to Vision? Capability Scaling Laws and Transfer Dynamics for VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00013)**  `arXiv:2608.00013`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Ziran Li, Qiang Wang, Zhengyu Chen, Shanglin Lei, Borun Chen, Jingang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Choosing the right large language model (LLM) backbone is the most consequential decision when building a vision-language model (VLM), yet it remains fundamentally unprincipled: compute-based scaling laws fail to generalize across model families, and no framework exists for directly predicting VLM performance before training begins. We propose the Capability-Driven Multimodal Scaling Law, the first cross-family framework that predicts VLM benchmark accuracy from directly observable textual capability. Given a low-dimensional capability score $S$ extracted from LLM textual benchmarks via PCA, we model VLM performance as a function of $S$, with a per-backbone transfer rate and an absorption rate that quantifies data-scaling efficiency. To fit and validate the framework, we train over 150 VLMs on 34 LLMs spanning 7 model families under a strictly controlled recipe. Evaluations on more than 200 textual and 50 multimodal benchmarks show that the law accurately extrapolates transfer rate from models up to 8B parameters to 72B-scale backbones, predicts full VLM training trajectories with high fidelity, and generalizes to entirely held-out model families. Beyond the scaling law, our analysis surfaces actionable insights: certain textual benchmarks negatively correlate with multimodal performance, exposing latent benchmark-gaming behavior; base LLMs outperform instruction-tuned counterparts as VLM backbones due to higher absorption rates and lower data-scaling decay; and different model families occupy distinct positions in the transfer--absorption space. The framework turns backbone selection from costly empirical sweeps into a principled, quantitative decision. Project website:this https URL; Code:this https URL; Model weights:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551)**  `arXiv:2611.03551`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Marianne Menglin Liu, Rongguang Wang, Zheng Wang, Yewen Wang, Leonid Boytsov, Tao Sheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.
  </details>

- **[Maswao: An automatically collected, prepared, and benchmarked dataset for the South African Sign language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02903)**  `arXiv:2611.02903`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Thapelo Sindane, Unarine Netshifhefhe, Vukosi Marivate, Abiodun Modupe, Herkulaas Combrink, Ketro Sithole_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in sign language processing can be attributed to developments in both large-scale dataset collection and algorithmic complexity in areas such as pose estimation techniques, signal processing through Transformer models, and model optimization. However, only a handful of sign languages, such as the American, British, and German, from over 200 globally recognized sign languages, reap the benefits of such advancements, while the rest lag behind. In this study, we aim to address this technological disparity by proposing a framework for the automatic collection and preparation of sign language datasets, with very minimal human-labour intervention, using the South African Sign Language (SASL) as a testbed. Specifically, we automatically collect SASL video content using public resources such as news broadcasts. Additionally, we convert their accompanying audio streams into text using available Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) tools. Furthermore, we crop the sign language area in the videos and use gradient descent to locate valleys of non-motion to determine end-of-sentence points within long videos to create our weakly sentence-aligned parallel corpora -- the Maswao (Signs) dataset. Finally, we benchmark the weakly aligned dataset on the task of Sign-to-text Translation using Recurrent Neural Networks and Transformer models to compare their individual intricacies. Our results show that Transformer models proposed in the literature outperform recurrent baselines, with the best-performing Transformer achieving BLEU-1 to BLEU-4 scores of 13.78, 5.21, 2.52, and 1.54, respectively, as well as ChrF = 18.09 and ROUGE-L = 10.51. These results indicate that the task remains promising and warrants further investigations.
  </details>

- **[Scalaris: A Scalable Enterprise Memory Architecture for Conversational AI Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02612)**  `arXiv:2611.02612`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Devarsh Gandhi, Sarthak Pawar, Hemprasad Badgujar, Vaibhav Rathore, Siddharth Sureka, Neha Bhargava_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise conversational systems require memory to personalize interactions, retain information across sessions, and maintain conversational coherence. At enterprise scale, however, memory must not only retrieve relevant information accurately but also provide it efficiently to the answer-generation model. Existing memory systems face a trade-off between retrieval accuracy and context efficiency. Retrieving more context can improve recall but increases inference cost and latency. We present Scalaris, a scalable enterprise memory architecture that addresses this trade-off through a unified long-term and short-term memory design. We propose that user interactions and assistant responses possess distinct characteristics and roles in a conversation: user interactions capture evolving knowledge about the user and provide value across sessions, whereas assistant responses must be stored efficiently so that only information relevant to the current query is retrieved. We therefore treat them separately and support each with specialized representations and ingestion/retrieval pipelines. Scalaris reaches 92.2 % accuracy on LongMemEval-S and 90.77 % on LoCoMo using Qwen3-32B, state-of-the-art results on both benchmarks, while supplying the answer-generation model with approximately 10 times fewer retrieved context tokens than Hindsight, a leading memory system for conversational AI. When integrated with our proprietary closed-source AI assistants, Scalaris improved overall response quality by 36 %. Together, these results establish Scalaris as a cost-effective framework for building scalable, personalized conversational AI systems.
  </details>

- **[They Know Where It Is, But Not What It Says: A Bilingual Hallucination Benchmark of Hadith Recall in Open LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02587)**  `arXiv:2611.02587`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Dayam Nadeem, Safdar Mustafa, Ameen Ur Rehman, Shahab Saquib Sohail, Mohammad Nadeem, Usman Naseem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly consulted for religious knowledge, including the recall of specific Hadith (recorded sayings and actions of the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him). Unlike open-ended generation, Hadith recall is a high-stakes, verifiable task: a reference (collection, chapter, and Hadith number) has exactly one correct text, and any deviation from it constitutes a fabrication that can be mistaken for authentic scripture. This study introduces a bilingual (English/Arabic) benchmark that probes five open-weight, locally-deployable language models (Mistral-7B, Gemma3-4B, Jais-7B, ALLaM-7B, and Phi4-mini-3.8B) across all six canonical Sunni Hadith collections (Sahih al-Bukhari, Sahih Muslim, Sunan Abi Da'ud, Jami' al-Tirmidhi, Sunan an-Nasa'i, Sunan Ibn Majah), sampling 500 Hadith per collection (3,000 per model per language, 30,000 responses total). Each model is given only the bibliographic reference and asked to reproduce the exact Hadith text, with no retrieval augmentation, and is scored on lexical fidelity (BLEU) and semantic fidelity (multilingual cosine similarity), with refusals flagged separately. Across all five models and both languages, 0% of 30,000 responses achieve both high lexical and high semantic fidelity, i.e., an exact, verifiable match to the source text. Arabic responses fare uniformly worse than English. These findings suggest that current open-weight LLMs, used without retrieval grounding, are unreliable and potentially dangerous sources for exact religious-text recall, and that deployment for Islamic scholarship should mandate retrieval-augmented generation over a verified corpus rather than parametric memory. The complete code, prompts, evaluation pipeline, and scored outputs are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Accuracy: How Quantization Affects Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02368)**  `arXiv:2611.02368`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Areej Mehboob, Muhammad Ali Shafique, Muhammad Usman Qadeer, Hamza Farooq_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents are increasingly used for coding tasks, but the inference cost of large language models (LLMs) limits where they can run. Quantization reduces model size, but its effects on coding agents that execute and revise programs remain underexplored. We compare BF16 with 4-bit AWQ and GPTQ for Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B, 14B, and 32B) and Devstral-Small-2507 (24B) on OJBench coding problems across three seeds. We detect no statistically significant accuracy loss for any of the coding agents. However, final accuracy can conceal substantial behavioral changes: AWQ reduces Qwen-7B one-shot solves from 17 to 0 while increasing loop-based solves from 10 to 25. All Qwen-32B successes involve tool use, whereas Devstral rarely makes a valid executor call. Failure profiles also differ: Qwen-32B produces far more compilation errors than the smaller Qwen models, rising slightly under quantization, while Devstral's failures shift significantly from runtime errors toward wrong answers and timeouts. These findings indicate that quantization robustness in coding agents should be assessed through solution trajectories, tool use, and failure modes, in addition to final accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Consolidating Reasoning with Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02104)**  `arXiv:2611.02104`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Wu, Zeming Chen, Hao Zhao, Antoine Bosselut_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multi-agent collaboration requires finalizing solutions from reasoning that is distributed across the individual attempts of many agents. However, useful intermediate reasoning can remain unexploited when collaboration is limited to voting on conclusions reached among individual agents, or aggregating long, noisy collections of reasoning trajectories in context. In this work, we propose TTT-Consolidate, a framework that uses multi-agent reasoning trajectories as adaptation data to improve a model's subsequent reasoning. TTT-Consolidate jointly consolidates trajectories into parametric representations through test-time training, then generates a new solution by conditioning on the consolidated information. We meta-learn how to consolidate using bi-level optimization at training time, where an inner loop encodes reasoning trajectories into a lightweight LoRA adapter, and an outer loop learns meta-parameters for the inner loop such that the consolidation is helpful for the model to synthesize a final solution.As no large-scale corpus of diverse reasoning trajectories exists for training aggregation methods, we construct the OpenParallelThinking corpus composed of 12,810 math problems paired with a mixture of trajectories from six LLMs. Evaluated on challenging math and STEM benchmarks, TTT-Consolidate outperforms the best aggregation baselines by up to 20 percentage points, even under matched compute and runtime settings, with the gap widening at larger model scales. TTT-Consolidate also recovers from unsuccessful individual trajectories more robustly, an ability that improves as the number of available (even incorrect) trajectories increases. Finally, we demonstrate that TTT-Consolidate benefits from both SFT and RL-based outer loop objectives. We will release TTT-Consolidate and OpenParallelThinking for use in future work on reasoning aggregation.
  </details>

- **[TrackMem: Tracking Evolving Beliefs in Long-Horizon Financial Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02001)**  `arXiv:2611.02001`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Austin M.Y. Cheung, Harsh Vardhan Gupta, Go Nam Lui, Lok Kan Chan, Ginny Y. Wong, Simon See_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for LLM agents are evaluated mostly on social dialogue, where facts accumulate and rarely change, so remembering reduces largely to finding the turn in which something was said. Financial dialogue breaks this assumption: figures are revised, stances shift without being restated, rules stated once govern later questions, and a single company is discussed under several analytical lenses. Memory systems that retrieve the passages most similar to a question, or the most recent ones, therefore return superseded values, miss rules that share no wording with the question, and cannot recover a stance spread across sessions. We present TrackMem, a per-speaker graph memory that tracks the history of figures, separates perspectives by entity and lens, and preserves each speaker's actions in chronological order. To evaluate it, we introduce FinLoCoMo, a benchmark of 15 long-horizon investment conversations with 765 questions in two splits: one following the LoCoMo categories and one targeting four failure modes. With two readers, TrackMem outperforms existing memory systems on both splits. On the failure-mode split it reaches 73.9% macro accuracy against 69.4% for the strongest baseline, with the largest gains on questions about revised figures (86.5% against 73.4%).
  </details>

- **[LegoEnv: Self-Evolving Agentic Environments via Modular Recombination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01834)**  `arXiv:2611.01834`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuosong Jiang, Chengxu Wu, Junqi Liu, Yujie Yi, Siqin Wang, Zhixuan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents learn by interacting with environments, yet existing environments are designed for specific tasks and fail to generalize to new ones. Recent methods automate environment construction with LLMs. However, they generate each environment from scratch, neither reusing parts shared across environments nor carrying a fix from one environment to the rest. To address this, we propose a new paradigm of evolving environment atoms, which composes environments from reusable atoms (intent, rule, data and server) and evolves the atoms when an environment fails to give reliable feedback (e.g., an application lacks a tool the task requires). To instantiate this paradigm, we introduce LegoEnv, which builds 5,465 verified environments over 223 applications, and one round of evolution raises the share of solvable environments from 71.6% to 88.3%. On AutomationBench, SFT on these environments lifts Qwen3.6-27B from 16.0% to 32.0%, and RL with their verifiers lifts Qwen3.8-27B from 37.6% to 40.3%. Further analyses show steady gains with corpus size, transfer to unseen applications, and failures concentrated in a few shared root causes.
  </details>

- **[AStockArena: Beyond Returns, Can We Entrust Real Capital to the Decisions of Financial Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00515)**  `arXiv:2611.00515`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyu Liu, Sing Kwong Lai, Xuanzhi Feng, HaoYang Shang, Yang Li, Zihao Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in dynamic financial trading, yet real-time trading benchmarks mainly evaluate agents by cumulative returns and risk-adjusted performance. Such outcome metrics cannot show whether trades are faithfully reported, supported by available evidence, and consistent with account states. We introduce AStockArena, a real-time decision-auditing benchmark for continuous trading in China's A-share market. Under shared market conditions and execution constraints, we evaluate 13 open- and closed-source models over 3,198 sequential decisions. AStockArena reconstructsthe actions actually posted to accounts by aligning reports, trade requests, execution responses, and account states, and audits them with eight checks spanning execution traceability (O), evidence sufficiency (RK), and financial meta-awareness (B), combining record verification with consensus expert annotation. Of 1,070 trading points, 72.99% exhibit at least one audit gap; model-level rates range from52.2% to 91.2%, with evidence gaps most prevalent. Historical trade-filtering replay further shows thatportfolio outcomes are sensitive to these flagged trades. Decision-process auditing thus offers a verifiable complement to return-based evaluation of financial agents.
  </details>

- **[Poolwise: Evaluating Reward Models for Selection across Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31090)**  `arXiv:2610.31090`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models are used to select training data, but common benchmarks compare responses only within the same prompt. Selecting data across prompts also depends on how scores compare between prompts. We introduce Poolwise, a protocol that selects a fixed fraction of a response pool by score, measures the selected set's quality using external labels, and reports within-prompt ranking separately. In matched experiments on two rating sets and two model sizes, training each prompt's mean score to match its average response rating improves selection over permuted or omitted mean targets in every seed at 30 percent retention. In grouped HelpSteer experiments, the model with the highest within-prompt accuracy selects a lower-rated set than the correct-mean model. Subtracting prompt means estimated from separate reference responses preserves local rankings but lowers selected helpfulness for six open reward models and two closed judges on HelpSteer. Poolwise provides a direct way to compare reward models and score transformations for data selection.
  </details>

- **[A Proposed Rubric for Evaluating Expressed Clinical Reasoning in Large Language Model Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37788)**  `arXiv:2609.37788`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhangshu Joshua Jiang, Zina Ibrahim, James T. Teo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a rubric for assessing expressed clinical reasoning in model responses, drawing on three bodies of work: medical education assessment frameworks (ART, SCT, Key Feature Problems and OSCE); clinical LLM benchmarks (MedR-Bench, HealthBench, TIMER-Bench, DR. BENCH, PrIME-LLM and PatientSafeBench); and general LLM reasoning evaluation research, including the Factuality-Validity-Coherence-Utility taxonomy, FaithCoT-Bench and C2-Faith. We use groundedness as a clinically oriented adaptation of the taxonomy's factuality category. The rubric brings these concepts together in a multidimensional framework for scoring free-text responses to gold-standard clinical vignettes. It includes provisional behavioural anchors, applicability rules and a separate flag for case-specific safety-critical errors. General-domain frameworks inform its design but are not treated as validated clinical instruments. The rubric does not replace case-specific reference criteria or the task-specific metrics of existing benchmarks. It has not yet been tested for inter-rater reliability, construct validity or clinical utility. Its immediate purpose is to make evaluation decisions explicit and open to scrutiny before empirical testing.
  </details>

- **[AgentHop: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Agentic Multi-Hop Question Answering over Scientific Citation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34428)**  `arXiv:2609.34428`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chanhee Park, Jeongho Yoon, Sungbin Han, Hyeonseok Moon, Heuiseok Lim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic tasks require a large language model to interact with the world, navigating information and gathering evidence across multiple steps with restricted resources. Due to this complexity, agentic task failures arise from various sources, and pinpointing these failure causes is essential to diagnose and improve agentic systems. Existing benchmarks, however, tend to focus on a single leaderboard score, leaving the underlying failure modes opaque. To fill this gap, we introduce AgentHop, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,011 multiple-choice questions paired with a controlled seven-tool sandbox under fixed token, turn, and tool-call constraints. AgentHop reveals model vulnerabilities by dissecting a single accuracy score along four axes of agent operation: retrieval, synthesis, tool-call, and resource management. Across 19 models, we find that behavior clusters by model family, with tool-call signatures revealing distinct family fingerprints: GPT models commit early, Anthropic and GLM checkpoints verify before committing, DeepSeek and Kimi over-search, and Gemini-3 Pro stays balanced. Decomposed axes further expose within-family structure: Claude Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 land within one accuracy point yet diverge on retrieval-versus-synthesis emphasis, with Opus retrieving more and Sonnet synthesizing better. We release the full benchmark set and the harness to support diagnostic agent benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[Sledgehammer or Scalpel? A Fine-grained Adaptive Framework for Implicit Hate Speech Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27462)**  `arXiv:2608.27462`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Han Wang, Hanqing Tao, Xuesong Wang, Yi Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike explicit attacks with obvious profanity, implicit hate speech hides malice within seemingly compliant expressions through metaphors and contextual hints, making its detection in online content review challenging. Many PLM- or LLM-based methods apply the same processing pipeline across samples, which may overlook differences in information requirements and introduce unnecessary computation. We observe that online hate speech is not monolithic but manifests in varied forms. We therefore define three fine-grained categories: Shallow, Targeted, and Context-Dependent. Accordingly, we propose Fine-grained Adaptive Implicit Hate speech Detection (FAID), a novel framework that first performs fine-grained classification and then adapts to specific categories. Specifically, for Shallow samples with surface-identifiable intents, the framework adopts lightweight Prompt-Tuning for rapid classification; for Targeted comments that bind malicious intent to concealed targets, we design knowledge augmentation to iteratively refine the model and reveal hidden targets; for Context-Dependent comments lacking background information, we utilize an agentic framework that automatically generates prompts to evolve context, infer missing background information and identify ambiguous malicious intents. This adaptive architecture focuses computational resources on complex implicit samples while avoiding redundant reasoning for shallow samples. Experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that FAID significantly outperforms SOTA baselines.
  </details>

- **[When Answer Evaluation Misleads Confidence Selection: Fact-Grounded Form Grading for Open-Ended QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25654)**  `arXiv:2608.25654`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhexi Feng, Bingrui Zhang, Wuxi Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many benchmarks now prefer open-ended questions for testing LLM and NLP methods. We find that the choice of a calibration rule can flip when only the correctness labels change: on 141 pilot answers, rules chosen with exact-match labels look 0.239 Brier points better than raw confidence but are 0.103 worse under human labels. How such flips arise remains unclear. We look deeply into their mechanism: reference matching rejects many correct answers (exact match misses 42%), and an exact Brier identity shows these errors favour rules giving such answers lower confidence, which abstain on them at common thresholds. Guided by this, we propose FactForm, a grader that trusts reference matches and otherwise consults evidence retrieved from the question alone. One LLM call per answer fills a fixed-order closed form that a program scores. On 998 fresh EVOUNA answers with 400 fact-based human labels, FactForm reaches a balanced error of 0.077 and an AUROC of 0.985, against 0.112 and 0.930 for the best of eight traditional graders. Its same-model stability is no worse than that of LLM judges (mean absolute run-to-run difference 0.0136 against 0.0159 to 0.0206). We hope this helps benchmarks grade open-ended answers more accurately, with repeatable and discriminative scores.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Single-shot Writing: Deep Research Agents are Unreliable at Multi-turn Report Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13217)**  `arXiv:2601.13217`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bingsen Chen, Boyan Li, Ping Nie, Yuyu Zhang, Xi Ye, Chen Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing benchmarks for Deep Research Agents (DRAs) treat report generation as a single-shot writing task, which fundamentally diverges from how human researchers iteratively draft and revise reports via self-reflection or peer feedback. Whether DRAs can reliably revise reports with user feedback remains unexplored. We introduce Mr Dre, an evaluation suite that establishes multi-turn report revision as a new evaluation axis for DRAs. Mr Dre consists of (1) a unified long-form report evaluation protocol spanning comprehensiveness, factuality, and presentation, and (2) a human-verified feedback simulation pipeline for multi-turn revision. Our analysis of five diverse DRAs reveals a critical limitation: while agents can address most user feedback, they also regress on 16-28% of previously covered content and citation quality. Over multiple revision turns, even the best-performing agents leave significant headroom, as they continue to disrupt content outside the feedback's scope and fail to preserve earlier edits. We further show that these issues are not easily resolvable through inference-time fixes such as prompt engineering and a dedicated subagent for report revision.
  </details>

- **[NitiBench+: A Temporal Thai Legal Question Answering Benchmark Across 17 Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03576)**  `arXiv:2611.03576`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pawitsapak Akarajaradwong, Sarana Nutanong, Chompakorn Chaksangchaichot_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Law changes over time. A court decides a case under the law in force on that day, and legal QA benchmarks grade answers against today's law. We checked 156,650 decisions of four Thai courts. Up to 67% of them cite law that has since been amended or repealed. We introduce NitiBench+, the sequel of our NitiBench, a Thai legal QA benchmark with 1,670 items across 17 domains, built from decisions of three courts. For each cited section it holds both the text in force on the decision date and the current text, and its retrieval corpus holds every dated version of every Thai statute section. Answers are graded on the Thai Bar Council rubric, the Bar-standard score, and on the overlap between the cited sections and the court's. The report has two parts. Part I measures the cost of the wrong text on 13 open-weight models. Reading the current text of amended law drops the Bar-standard score by 0.25 and missing repealed law drops it by 0.79. The penalty comes from rule changes, 0.45 on the 428 rule-changed items and none on the 331 wording-only items, and Citation F1 does not see it. The text in force is the fair setting. Part II benchmarks today's models on that setting. A public leaderboard ranks 26 models on a held-out test split of 500 items stratified by domain, with a validation split of the same shape released for public use, from memory alone, with the court's sections provided, and as a retrieval agent over the law in force, with the cost per item and the hallucinated citations. The best model scores 7.78 out of 10 and the best open-weight model 6.39. Without a search tool only five open-weight models recall any Thai law, and with it twenty of the 26 models gain and six lose. Leaderboard will take new models as they are released. Legal benchmarks must track dates, and on the right text today's models still leave a wide gap on Thai law.
  </details>

- **[IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424)**  `arXiv:2611.03424`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shanshan Zhong, Chen Xu, Jingjie Ning, Zhengzhong Liu, Aviral Kumar, Chenyan Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. We introduce IdeaTaste, a benchmark that scores an LLM by how closely the research proposals it writes from a literature snapshot frozen at 2025 match the work the same communities published in 2026. Our experiments span six contemporary language models and four research areas from established to emerging fields, covering more than 90{,}000 proposals in total. LLM agents fall well short of human communities in both the literature they cite and the ideas they state, reaching about 26\% of the human level on citation matching and only 18\% on idea matching. Their populations can cite as diversely as a community and take up its tasks and methods, yet rarely arrive at the concrete approaches and research plans it pursues. Neither richer field context nor larger populations substantially close this gap. In blinded expert review, AI proposals trail the 2026 papers least in presentation and most in expected success, echoing their gap in concrete approaches, and those whose ideas no human paper shares fare worst, suggesting weaker directions rather than overlooked opportunities. These results indicate that the main bottleneck in AI research taste lies less in finding the directions a community works on than in turning them into the concrete approaches it goes on to pursue. We release IdeaTaste as an open, continuously updated leaderboard (this https URL) that benchmarks contemporary AI proposals against future publication cycles.
  </details>

- **[MultiInpaintCOCO: Fine-Grained Visual Understanding in Multiple Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03309)**  `arXiv:2611.03309`  `cs.CL`  
  _Andrei-Alexandru Manea, Jindřich Libovický, Philipp J. Rösch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Previous research shows that vision-language (VL) models struggle to understand fine-grained image details and tend to focus on the overall layout. Moreover, any weakness in English tends to be further amplified in the multilingual setting. To study that, we extend InpaintCOCO, an English dataset focused on fine-grained visual details, to 13 typologically diverse languages across five language families, creating MultiInpaintCOCO - a new multilingual benchmark of 953 minimal-pair examples. We further test several state-of-the-art models in multilingual multimodal retrieval, considering both light VL CLIP-like encoders and large language models. Our results show that performance degrades substantially for lower-resource languages, with the strongest predictor being the data resources available for the languages rather than typological similarity to English. In cross-lingual retrieval, most models exhibit a systematic bias toward English: when given a correct caption in a non-English language and an incorrect one in English, models preferentially select the English caption. This bias varies considerably across architectures. Among the tested models, Gemma 3 shows the strongest English preference while Qwen 3 is the least biased, despite both reaching similar monolingual accuracy across languages. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Meet Students Where They Are: Student-Prefix Teacher Adaptation for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03133)**  `arXiv:2611.03133`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xue Zhang, Songming Zhang, Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) mitigates the student's exposure bias by optimizing on student-generated trajectories. However, student-generated prefixes may contain reasoning errors or follow patterns unfamiliar to the teacher, undermining the reliability of its token-level supervision. This degradation becomes more pronounced as prefixes grow longer. To address this teacher-side distribution mismatch, we propose Student-Prefix Teacher Adaptation (SPTA), which adapts the teacher to the states on which it is queried during distillation. Specifically, before OPD starts, we collect student rollout prefixes with random lengths and continually train the teacher to continue from the prefixes using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). By treating each prefix as context and rewarding correct final answers, the teacher learns to recover from student-induced states rather than imitate the student's reasoning. Then, the adapted teacher is used for standard OPD, with no changes to the distillation objective. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that our SPTA improves average student performance over standard OPD and OPD with task-level RLVR-adapted teachers. These results highlight the importance of adapting teachers not only to solve problems independently, but also to provide reliable guidance on the states their students actually visit.
  </details>

- **[SAGE (Subjectivity-Aware Generation Estimator): A Linguistic Framework for AI-Generated Review Detection *](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03060)**  `arXiv:2611.03060`  `cs.CL`  
  _Gonzalo Freijedo Aduna, Charles Yaiche, Patrick Bas, Alda Mari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a simple, linguistically motivated approach to detecting AI-generated peer reviews based on the inherently human trait of subjectivity. We capture subjectivity at two levels: the semantic level of evaluative language and modality and the pragmatic level of speech acts. Using features derived from speech act annotations and model-predicted subjectivity scores, we train a lightweight classifier that achieves an AUROC of 0.9566 with only these linguistic features. Combining its output with the state-of-the-art detector Sem-Detect raises the TPR at 1% FPR of Sem-Detect on the APRDB benchmark from 0.874 to 0.985, outperforming the current state of the art. Our results show that linguistic features of subjectivity provide an explainable source of information for detecting AI-generated peer reviews and can be readily integrated into existing detection methods. The code and models are publicly available on GitHub (this https URL\_ text\_detection\_hub) and Hugging Face (this https URL), respectively.
  </details>

- **[Finding Errors Is Not Enough: Selective Model Escalation for Entity-Targeted Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02989)**  `arXiv:2611.02989`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nishan Chatterjee, Matthew Purver, Antoine Doucet, Senja Pollak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving a sentiment classifier with limited resources involves more than simply deciding how much data to collect or which model to train. A labelled collection may lack examples of an important class. Once trained, a classifier may benefit from another model even when that model is weaker on its own. We study these decisions in document-level Entity-Level Sentiment Analysis (ELSA), primarily using South Slavic news. We first compare acquisition orders as collections grow. We then use uncertainty to choose which predictions to revisit, how many to process, and whether an alternative encoder or local LLM can correct them. Finally, we select complete routing policies on development data for held-out evaluation. Minority-prioritised acquisition helps at some very small training sizes, but not consistently thereafter. On SentiCoref, an oracle correcting all errors in the least-confident 10% without harming correct predictions would add 9.14 Macro-F1 points; however the best evaluated action gains only 0.90. Encoder averaging helps on AspectBench and NewsMTSC but harms SentiCoref, where selective LLM assistance is more promising. Both development-selected policies route 30% of records to the encoder-assisted LLM, yielding observed gains of 1.02 points on SentiCoref (interval includes zero) and 1.71 on NewsMTSC, at substantially different inference costs.
  </details>

- **[A State Change Is Local, Its Consequences Are Not: Dependency-Aware State Revision for Long-Term Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02912)**  `arXiv:2611.02912`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqi Wang, Tongyuan Zhao, Jiaqi Zhang, Yiqun Duan, Zhangkai Wu, Mingkai Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-lived agents rely on persistent memory of users, tasks and decisions, and this memory must stay consistent with a world that keeps changing. When an upstream fact changes, however, memory systems can revise the contradicted memory itself while the beliefs, plans and actions derived from it remain accepted, even though they have lost their support. We therefore formulate dependency-aware state revision, which asks not only what changed but also which dependent states should no longer be trusted. To address it, StateGraph maintains an evolving state dependency graph whose edges are recovered from interaction histories. Its core design is counterfactual verification: each candidate edge is tested by asking whether the dependent state would survive the loss of its premise, which separates support dependence from semantic relatedness, and revision then invalidates a dependent state only when a strict dependency fails and no independent support path survives. To test this design, we introduce StateChangeBench, a controlled benchmark of 50 author-constructed cases annotated with cascade depth, hard negatives and action consequences. On it, StateGraph's Impact-F1 margin over a CUPMem reimplementation is 6.9 percentage points at depth 1 and 26.0 at depth 3+. On public long-term memory benchmarks, its point estimates are comparable to or higher than those of the evaluated baselines on most datasets. These results provide initial evidence that explicit dependency tracking can improve selective memory revision under changing conditions.
  </details>

- **[Omni-Life: Benchmarking Omnimodal Real-Time AI Assistants in People's Daily Lives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02835)**  `arXiv:2611.02835`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mengze Hong, Ziwei Shang, Di Liang, Zijian Li, Liang Zhu, Hongyu Shan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time omnimodal assistants are expected to support people in everyday life, but audiovisual understanding alone does not establish whether they can provide timely and appropriate help. We introduce Omni-Life, a benchmark grounded in real-world user needs, deployment scenarios, and device-dependent observations. It combines controlled video synthesis for hazardous situations and edge cases with annotated real-world videos under a shared temporal protocol. The benchmark contains 400 videos: 290 requiring assistance, with 555 required intervention events, and 110 requiring silence throughout to test whether models can withhold unnecessary responses when no assistance is needed. We evaluate twelve configurations featuring mainstream real-time omnimodal models, jointly assessing event coverage, response content, timing, and unwanted output. At most 4.48% of videos requiring assistance are handled completely correctly. To examine practical deployment strategies, we study task reminders and response-latency tolerance. Reminders can improve recall by 17.12 percentage points but add approximately 4.6 unwanted responses per minute of reference silence. Increasing latency tolerance by 10s improves recall by at most 5.95 percentage points. These results show that existing models remain insufficient to deliver reliable assistance in real-life applications, calling for tailored model optimization in instruction following, timely proactive intervention, and withholding responses when assistance is not needed.
  </details>

- **[TRIO: Task Refinement with Individual Optimization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02810)**  `arXiv:2611.02810`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yingtao Ren, Xiao Luo, Yiwei Fu, Jinzhao Zhou, Yu-Cheng Chang, Chin-Teng Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), which leverages external knowledge to improve the performance of language models. Despite the remarkable progress, current RAG approaches are still far from satisfactory due to inadequate retrieval and potential hallucinations. Towards this end, we propose a novel approach named Task Refinement with Individual Optimization (TRIO) for RAG. The core idea of our TRIO is to equip three well-designed agents with an iterative refinement paradigm by aligning their individual optimization objectives. Specifically, we first expand ambiguous queries with a query extension agent and quantify the reward based on the improved answer quality, which can be utilized to refine our agent for higher expansion efficiency. To enhance the retrieval quality, we include a unanimity voting to annotate the expanded query as insufficient or sufficient, and their generative outcomes are utilized as ground truth to optimize the judge agent. To further improve evidence utilization and answer accuracy, an answer agent is optimized with dynamic matching-based rewards with curriculum learning for final outputs. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmark datasets validate the effectiveness of the proposed TRIO in comparison with state-of-the-art approaches. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PGuard: A Native, Streaming, and Plug-in Guardrail for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02791)**  `arXiv:2611.02791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junyu Lin, Mengjie Wu, Yonghao Dong, Yuchuan Fu, Jinfeng Li, Xiaodan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As streaming generation becomes the dominant serving paradigm for both text-only and multimodal large language models, ensuring safety throughout the generation process has become a critical challenge. We introduce PGuard, a native, streaming, and plug-in guardrail that transforms safety monitoring from an external serving dependency into an integrated capability of the backbone model. PGuard directly leverages the backbone's intermediate representations for token-level safety detection, without altering its original inference pathway or generation behavior. Specifically, dynamic layer gating selectively aggregates residual-stream representations across layers to extract safety-relevant signals during both prefill and autoregressive decoding. To balance detection accuracy and computational efficiency, PGuard supports multiple representation-modeling architectures, including a lightweight stateless variant and sequence-aware variants built on multi-scale causal EMA-attention and GRU-based recurrent modules. Its design also accounts for the execution characteristics of LLM serving frameworks, such as SGLang, optimizing computation and data access to minimize additional runtime and synchronization overhead. As a result, PGuard incurs only 0.82-2.26% end-to-end overhead on the hundred-billion-parameter backbone Qwen3.5-122B-A10B served by SGLang. Extensive evaluations across more than 30 benchmarks spanning text and multimodal safety, jailbreaks, cyber and CBRN risks, multilingual scenarios, and real-world traffic demonstrate state-of-the-art performance among native guardrails. On text queries with Qwen3.5-9B, PGuard achieves an average F1 score of 0.836, approaching dedicated external guardrails, while supporting deployment-time configuration of category-specific thresholds and enforcement actions.
  </details>

- **[MicroMouse: Masking Harmful Supervision in Long CoT via Relative Potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02710)**  `arXiv:2611.02710`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaowei Zhu, Hengzhu Tang, Zhenyu Zhang, Shuohuan Wang, Yubing Ren, Yanan Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Even successful long CoTs may contain harmful supervision, motivating the need for methods that can automatically identify and mask such signals during SFT. Inspired by the MicroMouse maze-solving process, we view a long CoT as a search trajectory from the input problem toward the target answer. This perspective motivates maintaining a goal-relative measure, analogous to a MicroMouse's spatial coordinates, to quantify how close the current reasoning state is to the target answer. Building on this perspective, we propose MicroMouse, an automated selective-SFT framework for masking harmful supervision in long CoTs. In MicroMouse we introduce a Relative Potential (REP) to measure how closely each reasoning state supports the gold answer. Unlike the perplexity of the gold answer alone, REP measures relative support by contrasting the gold answer with a matched foil. This contrast cancels likelihood variations shared by the pair, yielding a more stable signal of progress toward the target answer. By tracking the REP trajectory, MicroMouse identifies consecutive potential decreases as erroneous exploration and post-peak reasoning as redundant wandering, and masks these harmful supervision during SFT. Extensive experiments across multiple student model scales and reasoning benchmarks show that MicroMouse consistently outperforms Full-CoT SFT and existing selective-SFT baselines, yielding average relative gains of 5.5% in greedy accuracy and 2.6% in Pass@1 relative to Full-CoT SFT. Meanwhile, MicroMouse improves reasoning efficiency by reducing average output length by 8.8%, while identifying harmful supervision in only 1.7 seconds per long CoT on average.
  </details>

- **[From Language to Country to Valence: A Multilingual Audit of Stereotype Bias Across LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02585)**  `arXiv:2611.02585`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihao Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly considered for education, recruitment, workplace decision support, and other consequential applications, making pre-deployment social-bias detection essential. Yet major benchmarks are English-centred or translated, focus mainly on negative stereotypes, and may miss linguistic and cultural variation. We investigate gender, racial, and religious bias using 12,000 locally written Reddit posts in English, Spanish, French, and German. Only search terms are translated, preserving local context. The dataset is balanced by language and category; English gender is additionally balanced by stereotype valence. Three open-weight and three proprietary API-based models are evaluated with a two-blank design that reduces grammatical-gender cues. Religion has the highest absolute choice imbalance in every model-language condition. Spanish is highest for gender, whereas German is highest for race and religion. The evaluated API models score below the open-weight models throughout. Negative English-gender items exceed positive items; Australian and Mexican contexts exceed their Canadian and Spanish counterparts. These descriptive differences measure choice concentration, not stereotype endorsement or causal country effects. The crossed patterns show how aggregate scores can conceal category- and context-specific behaviour, motivating closer inspection before deployment.
  </details>

- **[Re$^3$Rank: Listwise Reranking with Reasoning-Conditioned Retrieval Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02550)**  `arXiv:2611.02550`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruiyi Yan, Zhuoyuan Mao, Yugo Murawaki, Yiwen Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention-based rerankers score each candidate by the attention mass that retrieval heads route to it from the question, but the question, serving as a fixed query, carries limited information for locating relevant evidence. We propose Re$^3$Rank, a listwise reranker built on reasoning-conditioned retrieval heads (RRHeads). A generator first writes a reasoning trace over the question and the candidates, and a scorer then uses the trace tokens as the attention query of a few selected RRHeads, reading relevance as continuous scores. We present a straight-through estimator to propagate the ranking loss through the discrete trace, so that the generator and the scorer can be trained jointly for ranking. Averaged over 9 benchmarks, Re$^3$Rank outperforms 11 baseline rerankers: our 4B variant exceeds the strongest baseline by 3.11 NDCG@10 points on average, and even our 0.6B variant surpasses rerankers with up to 32B parameters.
  </details>

- **[FindLink: Learning to Assess Scientific Connections from Source-Grounded Premises](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02545)**  `arXiv:2611.02545`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jingxuan Wei, Song Tan, Xiaohan Liu, Chao Sang, Changwei Wang, Linzhuang Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery from the literature requires assessing connections among findings obtained under different study conditions. Source attribution makes individual findings traceable, but does not determine what they jointly support. We introduce GI-TraceBench, a benchmark of 10,282 questions in gastrointestinal oncology that unifies provenance question answering, knowledge discovery, and hypothesis evaluation through shared Paper, Claim, and Evidence Span records. We further propose FindLink, a two-stage framework for provenance-grounded scientific reasoning. Premise Grounding constructs source-bound premises with explicit study context and support status. Structured Reasoning organizes these premises into support plans that represent both supported relations and unresolved conditions, then generates evidence-conditioned answers using deterministic compiler feedback. The compiler verifies structural and source-binding consistency, but does not assess scientific entailment. On the 1,545-question fixed-evidence test set, FindLink improves the balanced family macro score from 67.95 to 87.25 over direct supervised fine-tuning of the same backbone. A retrained variant without Structured Reasoning achieves slightly higher premise-identifier F1, but substantially lower relation and evidence-sufficiency F1. These results indicate that scientific reasoning requires not only identifying grounded premises, but also assessing what connections those premises support.
  </details>

- **[Same Sentence, Different Construct: Identity-Swapped Rationales Reveal Measurement Non-Invariance in LLM Hate Speech Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02491)**  `arXiv:2611.02491`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenchang Wang, Yue Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used as measurement instruments for socially consequential constructs such as hate speech, and are typically validated by their aggregate agreement with human labels. A valid instrument must also apply the same construct to every group it measures. We test this requirement using 421 \hc{} templates that are instantiated identically for seven protected groups, so that only the identity term varies. Four LLM classifiers each exceed 0.95 balanced accuracy, yet within identical templates two of them miss hateful statements about women at markedly higher rates than the same statements about other groups (excess false-negative rates of 8.3 and 4.4 percentage points), a gap first reported for fine-tuned classifiers in 2021. We show how the construct shifts. Comparing rationales within each template, we find that models invoke protected-group status for other groups but recast the identical statement about women as personal dislike or as aimed at an individual, in 22 of 30 missed templates for one model. Adding the benchmark's own definition, which lists sex as a protected characteristic, does not close the gap, and the contrast persists. Apparent false-positive disparities, by contrast, largely vanish under identity control and trace to reclaimed slurs present only for some groups. Errors shared by all models concentrate where model and benchmark definitions diverge, and pairwise counterfactual accuracy adds little beyond endpoint accuracy. We argue that validating LLM measurement requires identity-controlled tests of measurement invariance that examine both decisions and the criteria models state for them.
  </details>

- **[GigaSpeech 3: Scaling Multi-Dialect Arabic Speech Recognition with Weakly Labeled Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02476)**  `arXiv:2611.02476`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junzhe Liu, Yujie Tu, Yifan Yang, Peng Wang, Haoran Wang, Guodong Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dialectal Arabic speech recognition remains constrained by the scarcity of dialect-resolved transcribed speech despite the abundance of web audio. We introduce GigaSpeech 3, a corpus of 39,000 hours and 10 million weakly transcribed speech segments covering Algerian, Egyptian, Iraqi, Saudi, and Syrian Arabic. Starting from 60 hours of manual transcription per dialect, we train labeling models using speech-recognition-biased self-supervised learning and apply them to dialect-matched web audio. Data selection and four rounds of noisy-student refinement for Saudi and Syrian Arabic yield the final corpus. We validate its utility through dialect-specific and multi-dialect speech recognition training and cross-domain evaluation. On GigaSpeechBench, the final dialect-specific models outperform their labeling models across all five dialects and achieve a macro-average word error rate of 23.86%, a 16.6% relative reduction from the strongest third-party system covering all five dialects, while outperforming the best third-party result on three dialects. A multi-dialect model using self-predicted dialect identities achieves a macro-average word error rate of 25.51% while also supporting Modern Standard Arabic. Across eight conditions from three external corpora, a GigaSpeech 3-trained Zipformer achieves the lowest word error rate among six evaluated models in every condition, while adapting the Qwen3 speech recognizer on GigaSpeech 3 improves performance across all eight conditions. We release the corpus and recipes as a foundation for dialect-aware Arabic speech processing.
  </details>

- **[As Expected? Efficient LLM Planning via Expectation-Observation Consistency Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02438)**  `arXiv:2611.02438`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qingfu Zhu, Huanyang Zheng, Ting Liu, Wanxiang Che_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM planning often relies on reactive executors (e.g., ReAct) that generate a single action at each step to remain grounded in the latest observations, incurring substantial inference cost. Batched executors reduce this cost by predicting multiple actions in a single model invocation, yet they are prone to compounding errors, as intermediate observations are ignored. We propose an expectation-observation consistency verification (EOCV) mechanism for batched executors, preserving grounding while improving efficiency. The mechanism generates expectations of action outcomes and compares them with the resulting observations. Consistency permits continued execution, whereas mismatches trigger early termination and replanning from the current observation. Experiments on text-based and multimodal benchmarks across multiple backbones show broadly comparable average task performance between EOCV and reactive executors, with a 29-35% reduction in average weighted token usage. Moreover, the mechanism is model-agnostic and remains effective when integrated into state-of-the-art planners.
  </details>

- **[Does a Norwegian further-trained model give more outdated legal values than its base model? A preregistered paired comparison of Borealis 2 and Gemma 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02334)**  `arXiv:2611.02334`  `cs.CL`  
  _Eirik Botten Nicolaysen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We compare how often two released language models give outdated numeric values from Norwegian statutes and regulations: NbAiLab/borealis2-26b-a4b-preview (Borealis 2), which the National Library of Norway further trained from google/gemma-4-26B-A4B-it (Gemma 4), and Gemma 4 itself. The study was preregistered (doi:https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23858965) and uses the closed norregel benchmark, scored deterministically against a value history. On the 411 of 503 primary items where both models gave an interpretable answer, Borealis 2 alone was outdated on 10 items and Gemma 4 alone on 11 (d = -0.2 percentage points, 95% cluster bootstrap interval [-2.7, +2.5], exact one-sided McNemar p = 0.668). By the preregistered rule this is no effect of interest: the whole interval lies within the smallest effect of interest of +/-10 percentage points. Among the secondary measures, only the share of answers giving a value that took effect after the question date differs after Holm correction; Gemma 4 gave such answers more often. An exploratory comparison of two smaller Borealis models had no discordant pairs and is not informative. The design compares released models and does not isolate the effect of any part of their training.
  </details>

- **[GRIPS: An Empirical Study of Pre-SQL Grounding Information for SQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02314)**  `arXiv:2611.02314`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ekata Mitra, Yufei Tao, Kamalamalini Nagasundaram, Ameeta Agrawal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems answer natural language questions over structured data, but can fail before SQL generation by linking question mentions to the wrong table, column, value, or relation. Even valid SQL can then return an incorrect answer. This raises a central question: What pre-SQL information helps a SQL generator, and when does additional information cease to help? We investigate this question on TempTabQA-C and BIRD. Among the audited SQL Static failures, grounding errors form the largest category on both benchmarks. We introduce GRIPS (Grounding Representation and Input Processing for SQL generation), a three-stage method that derives structured pre-SQL information to make these decisions explicit before query generation. Across GPT-5-nano and Llama-70B, GRIPS-Adaptive improves over SQL Static in 18 of 20 settings, with an average accuracy gain of 11.8 percentage points, while reducing model-call token usage by 47.8%. Our controlled analysis shows which grounding information can improve SQL generation, whereas adding more pre-SQL information does not consistently help. By separating question-input grounding from SQL generation, GRIPS makes text-to-SQL more accurate, efficient, and easier to diagnose.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Collusion and the J-Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02153)**  `arXiv:2611.02153`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marco Molinari, Gabriele Bettineschi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly deployed in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), and they introduce the risk of coordination among agents that might escape human oversight. The J-space has been used to uncover deception in single LLMs, but its use for uncovering and steering collusion between agents remains unexplored. We show, with interpretability experiments on DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B) on the NARCBench collusion benchmark, that colluding agents' J-space holds the collusion itself: before replying, 86% of colluders represent misrepresentation, concealment or remorse. In a steganographic card-counting task, the receiver's J-space holds a hidden count, and causal interventions alter its count. We also introduce the Jacobian Pair Contrast (J-PACT), an unsupervised method for detecting collusion between agents. We evaluate J-PACT on four open-weight models: GPT-OSS-20B, Llama-3.3-70B-IT, Qwen3-32B, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B). J-PACT retains 85% of the above-chance AUROC of supervised NARCBench probes, matches them on steganographic scenarios, and names the colluding pair up to its complement in 99% of detected runs.
  </details>

- **[CULTURE-CMT: Benchmarking Contextualized Social Media Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02018)**  `arXiv:2611.02018`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linjuan Wu, Dingming Li, Yiwen Qiu, Xinze Lyu, Ye Guo, Daoxin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social-media comments are interactions, yet they are often translated and evaluated as isolated text. This mismatch is especially problematic when meaning depends on surrounding discussion, community-specific slang, or culturally situated expressions. We introduce CULTURE-CMT, a Chinese-English benchmark for contextualized social-media comment translation that restores the post and conversational context naturally available to users. Its 572 manually verified examples are organized into Slang, Context, and Both, enabling diagnostic analysis of distinct and interacting sources of translation difficulty. To support scalable evaluation, we further develop a retrieval-augmented cultural-effectiveness Judger that combines a calibrated scoring model with dynamically retrieved cultural knowledge. CULTURE-CMT reveals several non-trivial patterns. Providing interaction context improves most model configurations, but does not eliminate culturally ineffective translations. Examples requiring both contextual grounding and culturally situated language remain substantially more difficult than either source of difficulty alone. Moreover, larger models are not consistently better, and thinking produces highly model-dependent gains, including occasional degradation. In contrast, a 27B model adapted on benchmark-disjoint social-media data achieves the lowest observed invalidity rate among the evaluated configurations under thinking inference with context. Together, these results suggest that realistic social-media translation is not simply a matter of scaling models or reasoning longer, but of learning to translate meaning as it is constructed within evolving online interactions.
  </details>

- **[Switch-SWE: Do Coding Agents Hold Focus When Switching Between Unfinished Tasks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02017)**  `arXiv:2611.02017`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Qianli Ma, Yibo Miao, Qingyang Liu, Bingjie Gao, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Software development sessions often involve several unfinished tasks rather than one issue carried through to completion. As coding agents take on repository-level development, they need to recover an earlier task's requirements and partial progress after intervening work. Existing repository-level benchmarks typically score one issue-to-patch episode and do not directly measure whether an agent can resume a task after advancing other unfinished work in the same repository. We introduce \textbf{\ourmethod}, a benchmark for focus-task resumption in sessions that interleave unfinished tasks drawn from the same source repository, with a persistent checkout for each task. Each focus task is evaluated in matched uninterrupted and switched sessions with identical focus requirements and executable verifiers. The switched session interleaves ordered stages from other unfinished tasks in the same repository, while only the final focus patch is scored. \ourmethod~contains 200 focus tasks from 19 Python repositories, reconstructed from issue, pull-request, and review histories and rendered as 400 matched session specifications. Across 7 coding agents, interleaved unfinished work produces an average focus-task resolution gap of 11.5 percentage points, ranging from 6.0 to 15.0 percentage points. Trajectory analysis identifies cross-task requirement confusion and loss of task state as recurrent resumption behaviors. We introduce a plug-in context-management module that selects task-relevant history and maintains concise task-state summaries. On OpenCode, the module improves focus-task completion under same-repository task switching by 5.5 to 15.5 percentage points across the evaluated models. Code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[MedPsy: State-of-the-Art Small Medical Language Models for Efficient Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02011)**  `arXiv:2611.02011`  `cs.CL`  
  _Davide Vitabile, Alexandro Buffa, Akshay Nambiar, Amril Nazir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical language models promise expert clinical support, but the strongest open-source LLMs remain too large for private edge-device deployment, while compact medical models still trail substantially on knowledge-intensive and real-world healthcare tasks. Reasoning models that fit edge memory budgets can produce long reasoning traces, increasing sequential decoding, end-to-end latency, energy consumption, and KV-cache memory pressure. We present MedPsy, a family of text-only 1.7B and 4B medical language models designed for edge deployment, targeting this accuracy-generation-efficiency trade-off. Our recipe combines a synthetic medical data pipeline over biology, medicine, and health seeds with chain-of-thought targets from a 235B medical-focused LLM teacher; a four-stage post-training curriculum comprising two SFT stages followed by two RL stages with hard-sample mining; and an edge deployment evaluation spanning multiple GGUF quantization variants and real-device inference on laptop and smartphone hardware. On seven closed-ended medical benchmarks, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-1.5-4B by 19.34 points and matches MedGemma-27B while being 6.75x smaller; MedPsy-1.7B outperforms MedGemma-1.5-4B by 11.42 points despite being less than half its size. On HealthBench-Hard, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-27B by 15.33 points, while MedPsy-1.7B surpasses it by 11.66 points despite being ~16x smaller. Beyond accuracy, MedPsy reduces average response length by 1.7x (1.7B) and 3.2x (4B) versus its Qwen3 backbones, while Q4_K_M quantization preserves the aggregate score within 1 point of BF16 with ~69% lower disk footprint. In real-device inference, MedPsy-4B's shorter generations translate into 75% lower median end-to-end latency and estimated energy per answer than its Qwen3 backbone at matched quantization, while peak RAM remains below 5 GB across our tested laptop and smartphone configurations.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval-Enhanced Cultural Evaluation for Social Media Translation Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02009)**  `arXiv:2611.02009`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linjuan Wu, Yiwen Qiu, Xinze Lyu, Dingming Li, Hui Yao, Zhongyi Sang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social media translation requires preserving meaning while capturing the cultural nuances of user-generated content (UGC). However, gaps in the cultural knowledge of LLM-based evaluators can lead to inaccurate assessments and unreliable training rewards. To improve these signals, we use retrieval-enhanced cultural evaluation to train CUE-MT, a family of social media translation models. A calibrated, domain-specific Judger guides supervised data refinement and is augmented by an Agent for hard-example mining and reinforcement learning. The Agent retrieves relevant external evidence as needed and distills it into concise hints that the Judger uses to score translations and assign rewards. Across 9B, 27B, and 35B-A3B backbones, CUE-MT outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines of comparable size on CUE-MT-Bench and three public benchmarks. Human evaluation on CUE-MT-Bench shows cultural effectiveness gains of 7.44-15.12 percentage points over the strongest external baseline in each size group. Retrieval improves the Judger's four-way classification accuracy by 8.07 percentage points over a matched no-retrieval baseline that uses LLM-generated hints alone. Training ablations further support the combination of hard-example mining and Agent-enhanced rewards.
  </details>

- **[TAC-CoT: Tool-Empowered Implicit Chain-of-Thought via Interleaving Explicit Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01926)**  `arXiv:2611.01926`  `cs.CL`  
  _Juseon Do, Sangmin Bae, Jeesu Jung, Hwanjun Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agentic systems are increasingly combined with CoT reasoning to solve tasks that require accurate reasoning, external knowledge, and multi-step deliberation. Yet, explicit CoT incurs substantial generation overhead, while implicit CoT, which replaces textual rationales with compact continuous tokens, does not naturally support the explicit communication interface required for agentic tool-use. We propose TAC-CoT, a tool-empowered adaptive implicit CoT framework that replaces explicit reasoning traces with compact latent reasoning segments while retaining textual tokens only for tool calls, observations, and final answers. This interleaved design enables agentic tool-use without verbalizing every intermediate reasoning step. Experiments on agentic math and QA benchmarks show that TAC-CoT improves accuracy by up to 8.8 points while reducing model forward passes by up to 6.8x. Furthermore, analyses show that TAC-CoT improves tool-use quality in both single- and multi-tool settings.
  </details>

- **[Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878)**  `arXiv:2611.01878`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rongyu Zhang, Mutian Bao, Jinjian Zhang, Yuetian Du, He Xu, Wei Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question.
