To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.
  </details>

- **[Less but Sufficient: Relation Knowledge Selects Compact Knowledge-Graph Contexts, and What External Checks Can and Cannot Catch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01733)**  `arXiv:2611.01733`  `cs.CL`  
  _Seunghui Jwa, Minsu Oh, Sangwoo Hong, Yeo Chan Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) read knowledge-graph facts ranked by relevance, leaving open whether they suffice or conflict. Our checks use relation knowledge, written by hand or learned from training questions labelled with their relation paths. It selects a compact context of facts yielding an answer, and derives it; a derivability check tests whether an answer follows, a consistency check whether facts conflict, and a validation-chosen routing gate decides where the derived answer replaces the LLM's. Seven open LLMs read the compact context better in exact match than relevance-ranked facts; on ComplexWebQuestions (CWQ) they match SubgraphRAG's facts at a quarter of the prompt tokens, and stronger LLMs read SubgraphRAG's better. Where the routing gate passes, ours leads SubgraphRAG's in exact match in all 53 LLM, benchmark and prompt settings. A precision gate, returning the derived answer only when the check passes with exactly one derived entity, answered 18 and 5 percent of held-out WebQSP and CWQ questions in a pre-specified test, right for 90 and 96 percent. The consistency check judges facts by path evidence only where another path joins their entities, true of 2.5 percent of named-entity facts in our compact WebQSP and CWQ contexts.
  </details>

- **[EarlySelectKV: Anticipatory KV Selection via Cross-Layer Query Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01715)**  `arXiv:2611.01715`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Liu, Yuehao Li, Linghe Zhang, Gaobin Huang, Junran Li, Hongchang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-conditioned sparse KV selection reduces long-context inference cost by attending to a subset of historical KV entries at each decoding step. Existing selectors must wait for the target-layer query before scoring candidates and generating indices, placing routing on the immediate pre-attention path. We introduce EarlySelectKV, a training-free transformation that uses proxy queries from earlier hidden states to precompute selected KV indices while retaining the real target-layer query for final attention. Tests with RocketKV, Quest, and Loki show no consistent pattern of quality degradation across the evaluated settings. In the primary RocketKV evaluation, ES-RocketKV differs by +0.018 points on LongBench, approximately -0.01 points on RULER, and +0.25 percentage points on representative NIAH/Passkey settings. In a TensorRT-LLM deployment with CPU-offloaded routing, ES-RocketKV improves end-to-end output throughput by up to 12.4% and reduces GPU-resident memory by 11.1% of the combined KV/K-summary pool. This makes routing timing an explicit optimization target alongside KV subset selection in sparse long-context decoding. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EEG-HealthLLM: Hierarchical Adaptation of Large Language Models for Unified Heterogeneous EEG Health Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01652)**  `arXiv:2611.01652`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruidong Ma, Jisheng Dang, Ronghao Lin, Bimei Wang, Jingwen Zhao, Wenjun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) provides a non-invasive means for neurological and mental health assessment, yet existing EEG models are typically developed for individual tasks and are difficult to unify across heterogeneous signal configurations and assessment objectives. Large language models (LLMs) offer a common interface for multiple tasks, but adapting heterogeneous EEG signals to a shared LLM raises a key challenge: how to preserve task-specific signal characteristics while enabling effective language-side parameter sharing. To address this problem, we propose EEG-HealthLLM, a unified and parameter-efficient framework for heterogeneous EEG health assessment. We introduce an Asymmetric Hierarchical Adaptation Strategy: task-specific EEG adapters preserve heterogeneous spatio-temporal dynamics, while a unified shared LoRA is injected into the LLM backbone (e.g., Phi-3) to capture shared high-level assessment semantics across tasks. We further establish EEG-HealthBench by reorganizing five existing EEG resources into paradigms covering epileptic seizure detection, major depressive disorder assessment, anxiety risk assessment, depressive symptom risk assessment, and emotional state assessment under a unified subject-independent protocol. Extensive experiments show that EEG-HealthLLM achieves an average balanced accuracy (BAC) of 69.58%, ranking first among the evaluated methods in both average BAC and macro-F1. Compared with independent single-task LoRA adaptation, it improves average BAC from 67.70% to 69.58% while reducing the aggregate language-side adapter footprint by 80.0%. Further analyses reveal task-dependent cross-task interactions and trade-offs, supporting the proposed hierarchical adaptation strategy. The implementation is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Accounting for Irregular State Transitions through Belief Estimation in Online Medical Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01474)**  `arXiv:2611.01474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingzhe Lu, Xiangru Chen, Jian Qin, Yunpeng Li, Xihan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While online medical consultation broadens access, unprompted patient disclosures and topic shifts frequently disrupt clinician inquiry, leaving clinical states progressively revealed. We characterize these dynamics using both existing evaluation metrics and task-specific interaction metrics designed in this work. Through an empirical study across 16 medical dialogue datasets, we show that authentic online interactions exhibit substantially lower contextual continuity and higher unpredictability than offline and simulated benchmarks. We propose PRISM (Policy Reasoning with Inferred States for Medicine), a framework inspired by partially observable Markov decision processes that decouples latent state estimation from action selection to address drifting dialogue trajectories. Specifically, PRISM factorizes clinical triage into belief estimation, policy optimization, and response realization. By maintaining an evolving posterior belief over consultation states and conditioning action selection on the maximum a posteriori state, PRISM advances clinical goals while accommodating patient-initiated shifts. Extensive evaluations show that PRISM substantially outperforms frontier LLMs and clinical agents, achieving a 22.40 percentage point gain in clinical decision accuracy alongside decisive improvements in response generation, surpassing the strongest baseline by 5.51 points in BERTScore_F1. Furthermore, ablation studies show that providing oracle consultation states consistently boosts response quality across all evaluated baselines by guiding their action reasoning, yielding average gains of 5.58 points in BERTScore_F1.
  </details>

- **[Latent Harness: Compiling Agent Behaviors into Post-Block Activation Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01415)**  `arXiv:2611.01415`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ran Li, Shimin Di, Zeyu Ren, Ling Yue, Jiachuan Wang, Lei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness engineering and self-evolving agents improve language-model agents quickly, yet their gains live outside the model. The harness is re-sent and re-paid on every call, and the standard route to internalization is a weight update that is costly to iterate and fixed once merged. We propose compiling the harness instead. Latent Harness distills an external condition into a rank-8 activation shift. The shift is applied at the output of every decoder block of a frozen backbone, at 0.03-0.05% of parameters. The condition is either an explicit process-level behavior instruction or the behavioral contract of an agentic harness. At inference the compiled bundle carries no harness text and adds no tokens. It exposes three runtime controls for injection strength, injection phase, and shift-space mixing. Compiled behaviors recover the source-harness gain in 8 of 10 preregistered settings. Where the explicit source pays the heaviest per-call token tax, the compiled form exceeds it. The same bundles transfer zero-shot across tasks (5 of 7 pairs positive) and compose in shift space, while stacked text instructions interfere destructively. Used as a search space, they lift budget-matched harness search by 1.45 times. On three agentic benchmarks the operator aligns policy to the harness con- tract. Under matched data, capacity, and effective strength it outperforms same-capacity LoRA, which needs 11.1 times the parameters to reach the same band, and it regresses nothing where the base policy is already strong. Across 1B-to-8B models the method skeleton is invariant while gain locations move with the model-task pair, which delimits where compiled harnesses should be deployed.
  </details>

- **[XDecideBench: Evaluating Typed Decision Models and Confidence Policies Across Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01370)**  `arXiv:2611.01370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marek Šuppa, Jaroslav Kopčan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Applications use Typed Decision Models to route and moderate text in many languages, and they often act only on decisions above a confidence threshold. We introduce XDecideBench, a benchmark of nine suites in 122 languages with 88,425 texts and 153,875 typed questions. All suites share one scoring contract, and we report aligned, locally labelled and native items separately. Model rankings change from suite to suite. The model that is best in English, chosen in hindsight, loses significantly in about a quarter of the suite-language cells, and on Burmese Belebele it falls far behind the best model. Three open models fine-tuned for typed decisions beat the Qwen models they were built from in all twelve development languages. When the Qwen models are run locally, a pre-registered held-out cohort confirms that the accuracy gain is larger in the four languages with the least web text than in the six with the most. In both sets of languages, however, fine-tuning fixes a similar share of the base model's errors, so the larger gain may reflect weaker base models. A confidence threshold set on English data to allow 5% errors exceeds that error rate on XNLI, even in German and French.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking LLM Pruning via Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01133)**  `arXiv:2611.01133`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured pruning reduces the size of large language models, often at the cost of lower accuracy. Whether this decline reflects diminished reasoning capacity or greater difficulty in sampling correct solutions is less clear. We examine this question across expert, FFN and layer pruning on four mathematical and coding benchmarks, comparing original and pruned models through pass@k at matched sampling budgets. Even after substantial pruning, some models match or exceed the original model's reasoning coverage despite lower pass@1, and solve problems left unsolved in the original samples. Retaining broad coverage, however, does not mean that correct solutions remain easy to obtain. Under severe pruning, diverse generated chain-of-thought (CoT) traces can coexist with increased repetition and less reliable generation. As correct answers become harder to sample, the additional attempts can sharply increase total token use even when individual responses become shorter. This separation between coverage and sampling efficiency also clarifies expert-selection gains, as methods with markedly different pass@1 can solve the same problems at large budgets yet require different numbers of attempts to reach a common coverage target. Pruning should therefore be assessed by both the range of problems a model can still solve and the sampling cost of obtaining correct solutions.
  </details>

- **[Random Is Almost Enough? Stable and Low-Cost Benchmark Compression via BestRand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00883)**  `arXiv:2611.00883`  `cs.CL`  
  _George Ling, Shanshan Zhong, Lijin Yang, Hao Yang, Nanxiang Li, Zhongzhan Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark compression reduces the often prohibitive time and cost of model evaluation by evaluating models on a small benchmark subset that closely matches full-benchmark results. To quantify estimation error, we derive an accurate analytical approximation to the error distribution when estimating full-benchmark scores from a subset, showing that most subsets yield small errors, while a few produce much larger errors, resulting in a long-tailed distribution. This means random sampling alone can achieve surprisingly strong performance with high probability, but remains unstable due to rare large errors. We further show that this instability is inherent to modern foundation models and persists even as more models are evaluated. Based on these findings, we introduce BestRand, a stable and low-cost benchmark compression method that preserves the efficiency of random sampling while substantially reducing its high-error tail. We validate BestRand on 70+ text, multimodal, and agent benchmarks, and further extend it to VLA evaluation. Compared with competitive methods, BestRand reduces high-error risk by 50.2% and estimation error by 16.4% on average, while requiring only 51.3% of the construction cost. It further reduces OOD estimation error by 21.8% on average across score-range, model-family, and temporal shifts. Together, our theory provides practical guidance for understanding and predicting the accuracy and reliability of benchmark compression, while BestRand enables stable and accurate compression at low cost. The code and data are publicly available onhttps://github.com/George-Ling3/BestRand.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Language Model Reasoning with Structured Multi-Level Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00616)**  `arXiv:2611.00616`  `cs.CL`  
  _Siheng Xiong, Ali Payani, Faramarz Fekri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inference-time scaling enhances a model's reasoning by extending its chain-of-thought (CoT). However, existing approaches typically rely on a single policy trained with outcome-reward reinforcement learning (RL), which often suffers from long-horizon plan failures where the implicit plan drifts from valid strategies, especially for small LMs with limited capacity. To address this, we propose Multi-Level Reasoning (MLR), which reformulates long-CoT generation as a two-level stochastic process. A high-level planner generates structured step descriptors specifying both the reasoning mode and the semantic subgoal. The low-level executor then produces detailed reasoning conditioned on these descriptors, forming an alternating plan--execute loop. To maintain scalability, we adopt a minimal design where the base model serves as the low-level policy and a lightweight LoRA module implements the high-level policy. For training, we observe that outcome-reward RL provides sparse and delayed feedback for long trajectories (e.g., several thousand tokens), hindering credit assignment. We therefore introduce iterative Step-DPO, a process-level preference optimization scheme that leverages Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo (TSMC) to provide scalable stepwise supervision. This yields more effective training, improved stability, and higher accuracy. Extensive experiments on challenging math, science, and logical reasoning benchmarks show that, under the same reduced data budget (10% SFT and 5% preference relative to the DeepSeek-R1 distillation setup), MLR outperforms both SFT-based distillation and strong RL/preference-optimization baselines across multiple base models and tasks. Moreover, MLR exhibits slower performance degradation on long-horizon reasoning, demonstrating stronger robustness under extended CoT generation.
  </details>

- **[From Apparent Search Gains to Selection Failures: Auditing Test-Time Inference in Structured Workflow Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00579)**  `arXiv:2611.00579`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhitong Chen, Weifeng Zhang, Kai Yin, Zhiyuan Wang, Ali Mostafavi, James Caverlee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time search can improve structured planning, but when methods differ in prompts, post-processing, and selection, it is unclear what an observed gain should be attributed to. We study this question on DisasterBench, where models compose typed multi-tool workflows, by decomposing inference into three observable stages: whether a correct workflow is discovered, whether it survives structural validation, and whether it is finally selected. Comparing independent sampling, a nominal tree-search configuration, and multi-operator search under controlled inference budgets on two model families, we find that (i) the nominal tree-search configuration realizes non-adaptive complete-workflow sampling, and independent sampling with its expansion prompt reproduces its candidate discovery without constructing a tree; (ii) structural validation never removes the last correct workflow in our evaluated candidate pools, whereas majority voting leaves a discovered correct workflow unselected on 10-19% of tasks per pool, with an audit confirming substantive tool-choice or information-flow errors in 57 of 60 examined cases; and (iii) a tool-specification-aware verifier recovers some of these losses but overturns more correct decisions than it fixes. Our results motivate auditing realized rather than nominal inference pipelines, and reporting discovery, validity, and selection separately.
  </details>

- **[Distinguishing among reasoning levels of GPT 5.5 Pro via a grammar lens benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00555)**  `arXiv:2611.00555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Emma Graham, Richard Granger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does the level of test-time processing effort affect a language model's computational capability? A formal grammar-lens benchmark method, derived from the theory of computation, generates novel data at specified levels, each successively more computationally powerful than the levels below. Using this data, we tested GPT-5.5, and GPT-5.5-Pro-Medium, -High, and -XHigh, showing incrementally increased acceptance of test data: 16, 40, 43, and 52\%, respectively, illustrating the benchmark's ability to discern fine-grained differences among models' added test-time reasoning levels. Additionally, as test-time compute effort increases, model responses diverge from human responses to the same data. This increasing disagreement may turn out to be broadly characteristic of more capable language models, introducing the potential of a general capability-versus-alignment conflict.
  </details>

- **[GAVAGAI: Referential Alignment for Infant-Scale Vision-Language Models via Balanced Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00319)**  `arXiv:2611.00319`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rajdeep Pal, Jotiraditya Banerjee, Saptarshi Pani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A child learns which words name which things from roughly 100 million words of input. Vision-language models trained at a comparable scale bind words to referents far less reliably, and we argue that part of the gap is structural: the usual next-token captioning objective has no variable standing for "this word refers to that region," so training applies no direct pressure to ground words. We add such a variable by treating each scene as a small assignment problem between the content words heard and the regions seen, solved as entropic optimal transport under two constraints. First, a word may be assigned to a special NOTHING column instead of any region, which matters because most content words in caregiver speech do not name anything visible. Second, no region may receive more than a bounded share of the assignment mass, a capacity constraint that encodes mutual exclusivity. The alignment step costs about eight Sinkhorn iterations, small next to the vision encoder. In controlled simulation, the naive version of the idea, a row-wise softmax that forces every word onto some region, collapses to 0.000 word-to-picture accuracy under realistic non-reference rates, below the 0.058 of captioning alone, while our two constraints reach 0.075 in that regime and 0.700 on clean input. A one-parameter version of the model reproduces adult cross-situational learning accuracy across three ambiguity conditions (RMSE 0.024). On the SAYCam infant video corpus, adding the alignment loss to a next-token captioning model raises word-to-picture accuracy from 32.4% to 48.7% and localization IoU from 0.18 to 0.38. A contrastive model trained on the same child's data remains stronger on its own benchmark, so our claim is that the loss improves a captioning objective under high non-reference rates, not that it sets a new best.
  </details>

- **[Keepace.ai: A Technical Report on a Large Language Model for Sports and Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38249)**  `arXiv:2610.38249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Keepace.ai Team, Bowen Wu, Heng Wang, Jufeng Mi, Kang Liu, Kun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models hold promise for delivering safe, scientifically grounded, and personalized exercise guidance at scale, yet the sports-and-health domain still lacks curated training corpora and rigorous evaluation benchmarks. Keepace (this http URL) is a domain-specialized LLM built on Qwen3.5-27B through post-training, currently optimized for Chinese-language exercise guidance across three tasks: workout plan generation, sports-and-health knowledge QA, and personal sports data analysis. In internal rubric-based evaluations designed with domain experts, Keepace outperforms its base model and larger general-purpose baselines, with the most pronounced improvements in exercise safety, boundary compliance, structured output completeness, and scientific accuracy. This report summarizes the current design, data pipeline, safety-first evaluation framework, and evaluation results of Keepace.
  </details>

- **[Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Large Language Models to a Low-Resource Agglutinative Language: A Corpus, Datasets, and a Reproducible Benchmark for the Bashkir Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31397)**  `arXiv:2610.31397`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mullosharaf K. Arabov, Svetlana S. Khaybullina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bashkir is a low-resource agglutinative language written in Cyrillic and remains poorly represented in modern LLMs. We present an open resource suite and a systematic benchmark of adaptation methods: the Bashkir Web Corpus (71,567 documents, 46.9M tokens, 16 sources) and three labelled datasets for binary, multiclass and multilabel text classification; the benchmark covers the first two. Experiments use eight architectures (82M-8B: DistilGPT2, GPT-2 base/medium, Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B, YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B, mBERT, XLM-R) and three adaptation groups (full fine-tuning, QLoRA, in-context learning), plus continual pretraining (CPT). (1) QLoRA on 3.8-8B models outperforms encoder-only fine-tuning on binary classification: YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B with CPT reaches macro-F1 0.748 vs 0.653 (XLM-R) and 0.650 (mBERT); pairwise differences are significant after Holm correction (p<0.001). (2) YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B significantly outperforms Mistral-7B and Phi-3-mini on multiclass classification, BPB and native-speaker ratings (mean 3.28 vs 2.52 and 2.22; n=100; alpha=0.69-0.77; p<0.05); on binary classification its QLoRA advantage is not significant (p>=0.084). (3) CPT effects depend on model and task: perplexity improves for Phi-3-mini (+0.58) and YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B (+0.33) but worsens for Mistral-7B (-1.15, p<0.001); on binary classification CPT helps Mistral-7B most (+4.0 macro-F1). (4) Tokeniser fertility on Bashkir varies 2.67x (2.92-7.80) and tracks adaptation quality, while perplexity across tokenisers is not comparable. Cyrillic tokenisers with Russian pretraining are preferable; LLM judges reproduce native ratings for grammar and coherence (rho=0.33-0.46) but not language correctness (rho=0.16, p=0.11); native evaluation remains necessary. All artefacts are open-licensed.
  </details>

- **[norregel: a preregistered benchmark of dated numeric values in Norwegian law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29207)**  `arXiv:2610.29207`  `cs.CL`  
  _Eirik Botten Nicolaysen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  norregel asks language models for numeric values in Norwegian statutes, regulations and parliamentary decisions (NOK amounts, percentages, multiples of the National Insurance basic amount (G) and age limits), each at a stated date. The 916 items were extracted mechanically from Lovdata's open data, and each carries the dated history of its provision, so an answer can be classed as correct, outdated, wrong or uninterpretable. We report a first and a second preregistered run on the same items. In the first run (five models, listed publicly only after local analysis), H1, that outdated answers are more frequent for values that took effect after a model's cutoff, could not be tested in substance, and H2 and H3 were inconclusive; Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 4.6 answered 46.7% and 40.2% of the 880 primary items correctly. The second run was preregistered and published before any of its calls, and eight models were analysed. Its pooled primary test supported H1 under the preregistered decision rule (d = 17.8 percentage points, 95% interval [7.7, 28.9], p = 0.0001). In the preregistered sensitivity analysis S8, which counts as outdated only the most recent value that differs from the key, d was 3.9 [-3.6, 12.7] (p = 0.1754), an interval that contains 0. H1 was supported in every other preregistered sensitivity analysis, but S8 is the only one that gives both groups one target value, so we do not regard the support for H1 as robust. The share of outdated answers equal to that most recent value (H2) was 56.1% [46.6, 65.0], an interval that contains 0.5, and the pooled test of a lower share correct in Nynorsk than in Bokmål (H3) was inconclusive. Several models, among them Haiku 4.5, gave mostly uninterpretable answers. Items and keys are withheld; item IDs, per-answer classes, code and results are on Zenodo.
  </details>

- **[Empathy Is Not the Scapegoat for Declines in Factuality and Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18541)**  `arXiv:2610.18541`  `cs.CL`  
  _Letian Shi, Kanwal Mehreen, Deheng Zhang, Yuxia Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are increasingly expected to support users in stressful or uncertain situations, where warmth and empathy are desirable. While recent work associates warmer models with reduced reliability, we show that this conclusion is incomplete. Prior studies often train primarily on empathetic dialogue, making it difficult to separate tone effects from SFT-induced distribution shift. We revisit this issue at both the model and evaluation levels. We study prompting, SFT, and RL for inducing warm behavior, and comprehensively evaluate capability preservation using an 11-dataset benchmark suite and EMOFACT under emotional contexts. Across multiple model families, we find that warmth effects are task- and model-dependent, while task-aligned adaptive tone can improve empathy and targeted reliability, although some capability trade-offs remain. We further introduce human-validated empathy evaluators specifically developed to assess empathetic model responses. Our results suggest that models can be more supportive in social interactions while remaining reliable.
  </details>

- **[EvalMem: An Operation-Level Diagnostic Framework for Long-Term Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22231)**  `arXiv:2609.22231`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyu Liu, Jian Zhong, Rongduo Han, Ziyang Wu, Shunye Tang, Chenghao He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon interactions with LLM-based assistants require memory systems that preserve and update user states, preferences, and interaction histories. Existing evaluations report end-to-end QA accuracy and cannot determine whether errors arise from encoding, retrieval, or generation. We introduce EvalMem, an operation-level diagnostic framework with three parallel Examiners. For each query, the Encoding Examiner checks whether the target fact is stored, the Retrieval Examiner assesses whether the native retriever returns usable evidence, and the Generation Examiner tests whether the model can answer from oracle evidence. Their outputs form fine-grained multi-label defect codes. To improve store-level diagnosis, we adapt agentic RAG with a recall-first strategy that searches using both the query and source evidence, increasing recall of present evidence on LoCoMo from 70.2% to 95.6%. Evaluations of seven memory systems on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and dynamic DynaMem-Bench identify retrieval as the most frequently attributed failure layer; in default LoCoMo, retrieval defects reach 22.1%, compared with 7.7% for encoding and 6.5% for generation. Guided by this diagnosis, MemWiki, a search-friendly auxiliary structure built from each system's memory export, improves mean accuracy by 2.5 and 2.3 percentage points on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S, respectively.
  </details>

- **[PredActBench: Benchmarking Tool-Augmented Dialogue under Controlled Tool Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02372)**  `arXiv:2608.02372`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abdulrahman AlRabah, Xiaocheng Yang, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Abdussalam Alawini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to support decisions by calling external tools. Existing work on LLM tool-calling assumes these tools are perfectly reliable. However, in many applications, the tools are statistical predictors with non-trivial errors. We introduce PredActBench, a benchmark for evaluating dialogue agents that work with imperfect predictive tools, using education as a testbed. First, we build a benchmark in which LLMs work with noisy predictors to guide users' decisions. Second, we extend two prior metrics for trust calibration, Relative AI-Reliance and Relative Self-Reliance, to multi-turn dialogue. Third, we evaluate 13 LLMs on OULAD and our released PredAct-CS dataset of 60 courses with real final grades and synthetic weekly scores, alongside a human study involving 13 instructors and teaching assistants. We find that when tools are noisy, SOTA models rarely make predictor uncertainty visible to users, with our strongest baseline GPT-5.5 achieving an F1 of 57.2. As a result, users over-rely on incorrect predictions and unsupported claims, showing that task accuracy alone is not enough for AI decision support and agents must communicate when their tools may be wrong. We release PredActBench to support the development of LLM agents that help users calibrate their reliance.
  </details>

- **[BiCAA: Bidirectional Credit Assignment for Search-Augmented Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01321)**  `arXiv:2608.01321`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yibin Huang, Bin Xu, Hailong Cao, Conghui Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  By iteratively retrieving and integrating external evidence, multi-step search enables search-augmented agents to tackle complex question answering. However, vanilla GRPO assigns rewards based solely on final answers, providing limited guidance for intermediate search steps. To provide fine-grained process feedback, we evaluate the value of each search step: it should acquire evidence that advances problem solving while avoiding redundant retrieval. Building on these complementary criteria, we introduce BiCAA, which combines Forward Solvability Gain (FSG) and Subsequent Reasoning Gain (SRG) for bidirectional credit assignment. To reward progress in evidence acquisition, FSG quantifies changes in ground-truth answer likelihood after each interaction. SRG compares the subsequent action's likelihood with and without the current interaction to approximately measure its support for subsequent reasoning, using this support as a proxy signal for non-redundant search to encourage effective and efficient search. BiCAA calibrates local feedback through asymmetric fusion; for policy optimization, it integrates accumulated process rewards with terminal outcome supervision. Across seven open-domain QA benchmarks and two Qwen backbones, experiments show improvements in average F1, particularly on multi-hop tasks.
  </details>

- **[Why Struggle with Continuous Latents? Interpretable Discrete Latent Reasoning via Rendered Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29712)**  `arXiv:2606.29712`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Qingyang Liu, Shaobo Wang, Bingjie Gao, Qianli Ma, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models achieve high reasoning performance via explicit chain-of-thought and reinforcement learning, but require long output sequences and extended inference time. Latent reasoning reduces this cost by shifting computation into a latent space; however, continuous latent methods are hard to train, suffering from unstable and uninterpretable reasoning trajectories. We argue these issues stem from a misalignment between continuous-space reasoning and discrete symbolic supervision, as continuous states lack explicit anchors for step-by-step alignment. To resolve this, we propose \textbf{Discrete Latent Reasoning~(DLR)}, the first method that converts continuous latent states into explicit discrete tokens. Inspired by render-based compression, we render textual chains of thought into images, extract visual features, and construct a discrete latent vocabulary via clustering-based fine-tuning. Expanding the vocabulary and output head enables standard autoregressive modeling over both natural language and latent tokens, supporting pretraining alignment, SFT, and RL. Experiments on five reasoning benchmarks and two model series~(Qwen3-VL and LLaMA-3) confirm that \textbf{DLR} outperforms prior latent reasoning baselines with up to \textbf{20$\times$ compression}. Furthermore, the learned latent trajectories retain an interpretable semantic structure. Overall, discrete latent tokens provide a controllable and interpretable basis for efficient latent reasoning. Code is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Self-Reflection: An Execute-Distill-Verify Framework for Agentic Experience Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24428)**  `arXiv:2606.24428`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shiding Zhu, Yudi Qi, Yajie Wang, Jiaze Li, Chao Song, Yaorui Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Experience learning lets large language model agents reuse lessons through external memory without updating model parameters. However, reflection can endorse execution errors and store them as future guidance, a failure mode we call the Self-Confirmation Trap. We propose EDV, an Execute-Distill-Verify framework for offline experience construction. Heterogeneous agents independently execute each task; a third-party distiller compares their trajectories and environmental feedback to extract candidate lessons; and the executors separately review each candidate, retaining only unanimously approved lessons. Evaluation uses a single solver retrieving from the frozen memory bank. Experiments on $\tau^2$-bench, Mind2Web, and MMTB show improved revisited-task performance, with additional evidence of within-domain transfer to held-out tasks on $\tau^2$-bench. On an annotated Retail subset, EDV reduces the Self-Confirmation Trap Rate from 11.0% with RB-MaTTS to 5.0%, indicating less retention of erroneous guidance.
  </details>

- **[AutoSpecNER: A Fine-Grained Named Entity Recognition Dataset for Vehicle Specification Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24387)**  `arXiv:2606.24387`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jordan Lee, Filippos Ventirozos, Abdirahman Abdullahm, Ioanna Nteka, Peter Appleby, Matthew Shardlow_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vehicle advertisements contain rich specification information, but automotive NER resources remain limited. We introduce AutoSpecNER, an expert-annotated dataset for fine-grained entity recognition in vehicle listings. The dataset includes 659 advertisements from a popular car-selling website, with over 10,000 entities annotated across 15 categories, including MODEL, ENGINE_SPEC, and BATTERY_CAPACITY. Annotation quality was validated through inter-annotator agreement, achieving an average score of 91.5%. We benchmark rule-based extraction, fine-tuned transformer encoders, and large language models. DeBERTa achieves the best performance with a 90% micro-F1 score, outperforming the rule-based baseline (43%) and the strongest large language model (77.8%).
  </details>

- **[Tmax: A simple recipe for terminal agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23321)**  `arXiv:2606.23321`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hamish Ivison, Junjie Oscar Yin, Rulin Shao, Teng Xiao, Nathan Lambert, Hannaneh Hajishirzi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Terminal-using agents have quickly become a popular and important downstream application of language models (LMs). Despite their prevalence, relatively little academic work has examined RL-based training of these models, due to difficult benchmarks, a lack of data, and a lack of simple baseline recipes. We present TMax, an open RL recipe for terminal agents that brings small open models closer to the frontier. While simple, our recipe achieves 33% on Terminal-Bench 2.0 with only 9B parameters, outperforming much larger models from prior work. Our recipe first involves generating data using a novel taxonomy, combining difficulty control, personas, and verifier diversification, which allows us to cheaply generate large amounts of terminal environments for RL and SFT training. We then train open-weight models using RL with our data, using a simple, outcome-only recipe, with small adjustments to improve stability. Our recipe works for models from 2B to 27B and across model families, dominating the Pareto curve for open terminal-agent performance at smaller sizes. We release our data, models, and code as a strong baseline for future open academic work on terminal agents atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AtomMem: Building Simple and Effective Memory System for LLM Agents via Atomic Facts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19847)**  `arXiv:2606.19847`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanyu Yao, Shangze Li, Zhi Zheng, Hui Zheng, Qi Liu, Tong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong reasoning and generation abilities, but their fixed context windows limit long-term information accumulation and reuse across multi-session interactions. Existing memory-augmented systems often construct memory in a coarse and unstable manner, relying on inefficient memory representations or unstable unconstrained updates. To address these challenges, we propose AtomMem, a long-term memory system designed for value-dense storage and stable memory evolution. AtomMem introduces a Fact Executor, which selectively extracts high value atomic facts from long form interactions to serve as highly efficient memory representations. Subsequently, AtomMem organizes these facts into hierarchical event structures and temporal profiles, capturing coherent episodic contexts and tracking dynamically evolving user attributes over time. During retrieval, the system activates an associative memory graph to connect fragmented memories. Experiments on the LoCoMo benchmark confirm that AtomMem achieves state-of-the-art performance across various reasoning tasks, offering a scalable and economically viable solution for deploying intelligent personalized agents.
  </details>

- **[Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11435)**  `arXiv:2606.11435`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kexin Ding, Yang Zhou, Can Jin, Feng Tong, Mu Zhou, Dimitris N. Metaxas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growth of agent skills has transformed how agentic systems are built, evaluated, and deployed. As skill libraries continue to scale, rigorous evaluation becomes critical to ensuring their utility, quality, and safety in real-world applications. Consequently, the field is undergoing an emerging paradigm shift from isolated skill creation to automated, evaluation-driven skill evolution. In this survey, we systematically examine the landscape of skill evolution and evaluation beyond foundational skill creation. We categorize evolution into four distinct paradigms, spanning execution feedback, trajectory distillation, compression, and reinforcement learning, showing how each element contributes to improving skill utility and reliability. We also provide an analysis of six skill-centric benchmark categories, identifying structural gaps in benchmark coverage, trade-offs, and metric richness to advance skill research. Finally, we identify open directions for building skill ecosystems that are generalizable, efficient, and verifiably safe. The project URL isthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Stepwise Model Routing: A Cost-Efficient Table Reasoning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29319)**  `arXiv:2605.29319`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shenghao Ye, Yuxiang Wang, Yu Guo, Tao Zhang, Shuangwu Chen, Zijian Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve strong performance on table reasoning tasks but incur substantial inference cost due to long reasoning traces. Stepwise model routing mitigates this issue by dynamically assigning reasoning steps to smaller or larger models. However, stepwise model routing for table reasoning remains underexplored. Through empirical analysis, we find that reasoning steps involving tables contain two types of tokens with distinct uncertainty distributions: table tokens grounded in table structure, such as cell values and headers, and text tokens representing surrounding natural-language reasoning. The uncertainty of both token types is correlated with the risk that the model makes an error in the next reasoning step. However, existing methods fail to model them separately, leading to suboptimal routing decisions. To address this, we propose EcoTab, a table-aware stepwise routing framework for efficient table reasoning. At each reasoning step, EcoTab separately estimates the uncertainties of table tokens and text tokens, maps them to next-step failure risks for the small model, and combines the two risks for routing. Experiments on multiple table reasoning benchmarks show that EcoTab consistently outperforms strong baselines and achieves a better balance between accuracy and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Contextual Linear Activation Steering of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24693)**  `arXiv:2604.24693`  `cs.CL`  
  _Brandon Hsu, Daniel Beaglehole, Adityanarayanan Radhakrishnan, Mikhail Belkin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear activation steering is a powerful approach for controlling and specializing large language models by adding linear representations of concepts to their activations at inference. While effective, existing methods often steer with a fixed strength, independent of how much steering any given input requires. In this work, we introduce Contextual Linear Activation Steering (CLAS), which decomposes activation steering into a fixed steering direction and a context-dependent steering coefficient. This decomposition preserves the interpretability of the steering direction while enabling the steering strength to vary with context. It also enables CLAS to perform selective steering: steering multiple behaviors across different portions of a single generation, which cannot be achieved using a fixed steering strength. In addition, across a wide range of steering benchmarks and language models, CLAS consistently outperforms standard linear activation steering and matches or exceeds ReFT and LoRA in settings with limited labeled data. Notably, CLAS achieves this using directions that also serve as reliable concept monitors, a property that directions learned via fine-tuning lack. We therefore propose CLAS as an interpretable and accurate method for controlling and specializing large language models.
  </details>

- **[EntropyCache: Decoded Token Entropy Guided KV Caching for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18489)**  `arXiv:2603.18489`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minsoo Cheong, Donghyun Son, Woosang Lim, Sungjoo Yoo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) rely on bidirectional attention, which prevents lossless KV caching and requires a full forward pass at every denoising step. Existing approximate KV caching methods reduce this cost by selectively updating cached states, but their decision overhead scales with context length or model depth. We propose EntropyCache, a training-free KV caching method that uses the maximum entropy of newly decoded token distributions as a constant-cost signal for deciding when to recompute. Our design is grounded in two empirical observations: (1) decoded token entropy correlates with KV cache drift, providing a cheap proxy for cache staleness, and (2) feature volatility of decoded tokens persists for multiple steps after unmasking, motivating recomputation of the $k$ most recently decoded tokens. The skip-or-recompute decision requires only $O(V)$ computation per step, independent of context length and model scale. Experiments on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct show that EntropyCache achieves $15.2\times$-$26.4\times$ speedup on standard benchmarks and $22.4\times$-$24.1\times$ on chain-of-thought benchmarks, with competitive accuracy and decision overhead accounting for only $0.5\%$ of inference time. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement World Model Learning for LLM-based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05842)**  `arXiv:2602.05842`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiao Yu, Baolin Peng, Ruize Xu, Yelong Shen, Pengcheng He, Suman Nath, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance in language-centric tasks. However, in agentic settings, LLMs often struggle to anticipate action consequences and adapt to environment dynamics, highlighting the need for world-modeling capabilities in LLM-based agents. We propose Reinforcement World Model Learning (RWML), a self-supervised method that learns action-conditioned world models for LLM-based agents on textual states using sim-to-real gap rewards. Our method aligns simulated next states produced by the model with realized next states observed from the environment, encouraging consistency between internal world simulations and actual environment dynamics in a pre-trained embedding space. Unlike next-state token prediction, which prioritizes token-level fidelity (i.e., reproducing exact wording) over semantic equivalence and can lead to model collapse, our method provides a more robust training signal and is empirically less susceptible to reward hacking than LLM-as-a-judge. We evaluate our method on ALFWorld and $\tau^2$ Bench and observe significant gains over the base model, despite using no expert data, task-success rewards, or strong-LLM annotations - only an off-the-shelf embedding model as a distance metric. When combined with task-success rewards, our method outperforms direct task-success reward RL by 6.9 and 5.7 points on ALFWorld and $\tau^2$ Bench respectively; it further outperforms expert-data training on ALFWorld and is comparable to it on $\tau^2$ Bench.
  </details>

- **[MBBQ: A Dataset for Cross-Lingual Comparison of Stereotypes in Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07243)**  `arXiv:2406.07243`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vera Neplenbroek, Arianna Bisazza, Raquel Fernández_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative large language models (LLMs) have been shown to exhibit harmful biases and stereotypes. While safety fine-tuning typically takes place in English, if at all, these models are being used by speakers of many different languages. There is existing evidence that the performance of these models is inconsistent across languages and that they discriminate based on demographic factors of the user. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the social stereotypes exhibited by LLMs differ as a function of the language used to prompt them, while controlling for cultural differences and task accuracy. To this end, we present MBBQ (Multilingual Bias Benchmark for Question-answering), a carefully curated version of the English BBQ dataset extended to Dutch, Spanish, and Turkish, which measures stereotypes commonly held across these languages. We further complement MBBQ with a parallel control dataset to measure task performance on the question-answering task independently of bias. Our results based on several open-source and proprietary LLMs confirm that some non-English languages suffer from bias more than English, even when controlling for cultural shifts. Moreover, we observe significant cross-lingual differences in bias behaviour for all except the most accurate models. With the release of MBBQ, we hope to encourage further research on bias in multilingual settings. The dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Four Bridges: Measuring Deception Propensity in a Multi-Agent Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03600)**  `arXiv:2611.03600`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Raphael Casey, Michael Simeone, Alberto Tosato, Florian Ernst, Kemal El Moujahid, James Tamplin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact and coordinate with other agents, yet honesty is mostly measured in single-turn, single-agent benchmarks that pressure the model to deceive. We study deception that emerges in multi-agent interaction without any instruction to deceive. We introduce Kradle, a platform for evaluating AI models, and Four Bridges, an information asymmetry game in which four agents distribute across four rooms: one is the death room, the others hold food worth more if not shared. One agent, the informed agent, knows which room is the death room and can withhold or disclose that information. We evaluate eight frontier models as the informed agent. Deceptive behavior appears in every model and in 73% of all runs (6% to 100% per model), and does not decrease with capability tier within a model family. When the informed agent does disclose, the warning is often challenged, and agents that challenge it enter the death room twice as often as those that do not. Telling all agents that one of them knows the death room collapses deception in six of eight models; telling the informed agent that the room is permanently fatal moves only one. On an open-weight model, an honesty steering vector reduces deception to 0% and adversarial honesty training to 6%. The platform and game provide a testbed for measuring deceptive behavior and comparing interventions.
  </details>

- **[CONFIRM: Governing Scientific Reporting for Trustworthy Agentic Neuroimaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01449)**  `arXiv:2611.01449`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yuxiang Wei, Lijing Zhu, Qizhen Lan, Yanteng Zhang, Vince D. Calhoun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can autonomously run neuroimaging analyses from research questions to reported findings. Existing agentic systems use tools to check that code runs and reported values match its output, but rely on LLM judgment to decide which results warrant scientific reporting. We show that LLM judges endorse site-confounded findings even when the confound is presented as evidence. We present \textbf{CONFIRM}, a framework that separates running an analysis from deciding which findings to report. CONFIRM encodes each hypothesis as a machine-readable, pre-specified analysis plan that fixes the claim and its pass criteria before computing results. It then filters results through five deterministic gates (search history and multiplicity, confounds, power, analytic stability, and replication) under three configurable reporting standards, from least to most demanding: \textit{Screen}, \textit{Replicate}, and \textit{Full}. Failed claims receive diagnoses that guide follow-up analyses. To evaluate reporting decisions at the claim level, we introduce \textbf{NeuroClaimBench}, comprising 489 executable neuroimaging claims with frozen data, including literature-supported findings and 150 controls designed to violate individual methodological requirements. On NeuroClaimBench, CONFIRM falsely supports at most 3\% of controls, compared with 23--38\% for the baselines, while recovering up to 61\% of literature-supported claims. When auditing an existing agent, CONFIRM reduces false support on controls from 23\% to 0\% while retaining the agent's literature-supported claims. Its diagnostic feedback also produces more supported revisions than Self-Refine with fewer LLM calls. Together, CONFIRM and NeuroClaimBench show that basing reporting decisions on pre-specified analysis plans and deterministic gates is essential for trustworthy autonomous scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Switchcraft: AI Model Router for Agentic Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07112)**  `arXiv:2605.07112`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Sharad Agarwal, Pooria Namyar, Alec Wolman, Rahul Ambavat, Ankur Gupta, Qizheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic AI systems that invoke external tools are powerful but costly, leading developers to default to large models and overspend inference budgets. Existing model routers target chat responses or end-task success, not the correctness of the individual tool calls on which agent tasks depend; we present Switchcraft, the first (to the best of our knowledge) model router optimized for strict tool-call correctness in agentic workloads. We construct an evaluation framework on six function-calling benchmarks, holding one out entirely, and fine-tune a calibrated DistilBERT-based classifier, deployed inline under a latency budget, to select the lowest-cost model subject to correctness or abstain when none is likely to answer correctly. Switchcraft reaches 78.3% answer accuracy (86.4% when correct abstentions are credited) at 59% lower cost than the best individual model (75.6%), saving over $3,800 per million queries, and outperforms kNN, MIRT, heuristic, and random routers. On the held-out benchmark, it matches or exceeds every individual model at 5--7X lower cost than the strongest ones. We find two counter-intuitive results: larger models do not consistently outperform smaller ones on tool-use tasks, and nominally cheaper models can incur higher total cost due to token-intensive output.
  </details>

- **[GeoGuesser Bench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Agents on Interactive Geolocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02698)**  `arXiv:2611.02698`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Huanzhi Mao, Yichuan Wang, Michael Koo, Wenyuan Huang, Huy Doan, Jinghan Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language model (VLM) agents that act in the open world need two abilities: world knowledge to interpret what they see, and exploration to find what they cannot yet see. We study how agents combine the two through interactive visual geolocation. GeoGuesser Bench contains 282 tasks in an offline Street View environment where agents rotate, zoom, and move between real panoramas. Its three task types shift the balance from knowledge to exploration: agents (1) report the coordinates of their starting location, (2) name the enclosing street, city, state, or country, or (3) find, read, and count nearby objects. The coordinate tasks in (1) also include 42 real GeoGuessr World Championship rounds, whose expert guesses provide a human reference. We evaluate 23 VLMs: the best accuracy is 62.5% on place naming and 35.0% on object finding, and the best agent averages 4352.4 GeoGuessr points on the championship rounds, against 4770.3 for the better expert in each round. Beyond these headline numbers, world knowledge and exploration are only weakly correlated across models: agents that localize well do not necessarily explore well. To understand where agents fail, we categorize their errors into vision, navigation, and tool-use failures. Further ablations show that the strongest agents, such as Claude Opus 4.8, still perform well with limited views, while weaker agents degrade; customizing the environment thus adjusts task difficulty. The environment's verifiable rewards and restorable state also support training, and our Planner-Worker-Compressor harness cuts planner tokens by 57-61% by compacting the interaction history. We hope GeoGuesser Bench and its environment serve as a high-quality foundation for simulating real-world exploration at scale.
  </details>

- **[AttentionIndex: Accelerating Learned Sparse Attention with a Causal Inverted-File Index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02508)**  `arXiv:2611.02508`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jeongmin Brian Park, Wen-mei Hwu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned sparse attention, used in production models such as DeepSeek-V4 and GLM-5, lets each query attend to only k keys chosen by a lightweight indexer, but the indexer still scores every key in the prefix. At one million tokens, this selection takes more than half of prefill time. Two-stage selection over contiguous blocks, as in HiSA, reduces this cost, but its recall declines as context grows at a fixed candidate budget. We present AttentionIndex, which routes each query over an inverted-file index that groups indexer keys by similarity and refines the candidates with the native indexer score. AttentionIndex builds the index during prefill, making new keys searchable immediately, reusing previous clustering to keep maintenance cheap, and exposing each query only to clusters built from its own prefix. On GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4-Flash at one million tokens, AttentionIndex delivers 1.3-1.8 times the prefill throughput of exhaustive selection, and at throughput within 5% of HiSA, it recovers 17.0 and 10.0 percentage points more of the exhaustive top-k, respectively. AttentionIndex also improves accuracy on long-context benchmarks by up to 18.4 percentage points over HiSA at the same candidate budget.
  </details>

- **[I-PTC: Interactive Programmatic Tool Calling for Stateful Tool-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01446)**  `arXiv:2611.01446`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Huanzhi Mao, Chengkun Cao, Shuo Yuan, Joseph E. Gonzalez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly capable of writing and executing code, suggesting a natural paradigm for tool-augmented agents: expressing tool use as programs rather than isolated calls. Programmatic tool calling (PTC) captures this idea through loops, batching, abstraction, and structured computation, but existing PTC approaches are largely stateless, limiting their ability to reuse intermediate results and adapt over time. This is problematic for real-world tasks, where tool environments and problem-solving processes are inherently stateful. We therefore present I-PTC, an interactive framework that executes stateful PTC: models issue code snippets that call tools, maintain reusable state, and evolve the tool interface through API corrections. To evaluate the effectiveness of I-PTC and the general PTC setting, we introduce PTC-Bench, a benchmark of long-tail, production-style user requests whose difficulty comes from large initial environments, many dependent API interactions, extended planning, and recovery across follow-ups and transient failures. Experiments on PTC-Bench show that I-PTC improves performance on complex multi-step tasks with large tool sets while substantially reducing token consumption over conventional tool-calling baselines and the original PTC baseline, highlighting stateful programmatic tool calling as a promising direction for scalable tool-augmented agents.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Machine-Learning Classifiers for Neoantigen Immunogenicity Prediction and the Limits of SHAP Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00907)**  `arXiv:2611.00907`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ramon Gonzalez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized cancer vaccines require prioritizing, among hundreds of candidate mutant peptides, the few that will induce a T-cell response. This study asks what general-purpose machine learning achieves on that task, and whether the features such models identify as important can be trusted.We make three contributions. The first is a benchmark of sixty models: twelve supervised classifier families, each in five configurations, evaluated on the Immunogenic Tumor Specific Neoantigen database (ITSNdb; 199 experimentally validated neoantigens, 129 immunogenic and 70 non-immunogenic). All sixty are compared under one stratified k-fold cross-validation protocol, in which the data used to choose a configuration are never the data used to score it. The second is an interpretability study that combines SHAP with feature ablation. The third is a shuffled-label control that tests whether a SHAP value has been earned.Two results follow, and both run against current practice. First, the classical linear models lead the ranking. The gradient-boosted ensembles and neural networks that dominate recent work bring no advantage. Second, SHAP can fail on high-cardinality categorical features. Ablation establishes the presenting HLA allele as the dominant input and the source gene as dispensable. SHAP reports the opposite, assigning 68.7% to 81.7% of its total value to the source gene in every model that gives each gene its own weight. Shuffling the gene labels at random among the peptides resolves the contradiction: the meaningless column earns the same SHAP value as the real one. That value measures only that ITSNdb contains 173 genes for 199 peptides. We therefore recommend that SHAP values for high-cardinality categorical annotations in cohorts of this size always be reported against such a shuffled-label control, and we describe one in full.
  </details>

- **[Trail: Accurate Local Smart Home Control Using Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00610)**  `arXiv:2611.00610`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Maleeha Masood, Deepak Vasisht, Indranil Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language driven smart home control remains challenging: cloud-hosted Large Language Models (LLMs) raise privacy concerns, while local Small Language Models (SLMs) can generate invalid actions or misinterpret requests. We introduce Trail (Typed Registry-Anchored Inference for Language commands in Smart Homes), a fully local framework that replaces open-ended command generation with hierarchical typed decisions. Trail exploits the natural structure of a home's action space, selecting a room, device, and operation from candidates derived from the runtime device registry. This ensures valid actions while enabling confidence-based abstention when requests cannot be reliably resolved. We also design a structured memory module that uses typed decisions to support user preferences, device references, and multi-turn interactions. To evaluate Trail, we extend HomeBench and generate three datasets: Trek-Base, which tests natural-language variation; Trek-Robust, which evaluates robustness to changes in the home and its description; and Trek-Memory, which assesses conversational memory across multi-turn interactions. Trail achieves 85.3% accuracy on Trek-Robust versus 69.8% for a finetuned Qwen3.5-4B baseline, while remaining competitive on Trek-Base requests. With memory, Trail resolves 75.6% of the requests that depend on earlier turns, compared with 42.5% for the finetuned baseline given conversation history.
  </details>

- **[A Generalized Sparse Fuzzy Clustering Framework with Automated Cluster Cardinality Estimation Ability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00159)**  `arXiv:2611.00159`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _George Stamatelis, Konstantinos Koutroumbas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fuzzy clustering provides a robust framework for handling ambiguous and overlapping data boundaries, yet its traditional formulation generates entirely dense membership assignments that limit noise resilience and prevent estimating the true number of clusters, when it is unknown. To address these limitations, we introduce in this paper the Generalized Sparse Fuzzy Clustering (GSFC) framework, which incorporates a continuous sparsity penalty into the fuzzy objective function. Because closed-form solutions are precluded by the simultaneous presence of fuzzifier and sparsity exponents, we propose an efficient primal-dual bisection search coupled with specialized analytic pathways to rigorously enforce the partition constraint while organically driving trace memberships to exact zero. This mathematically enforced sparsity empowers the algorithm to autonomously estimate the true number of clusters by dynamically pruning redundant clusters, provided that it is initialized by a larger than the true number of clusters. Furthermore, we integrate a virtual noise sink to insulate outliers from the rest of the data points. The usage of the proposed framework is also extended to line segment detection. Extensive empirical evaluations across synthetic toy datasets, high-noise geometric stress tests, and real-world benchmarks (UCI wine dataset and hyperspectral images) demonstrate that GSFC effectively exceeds conventional fuzzy benchmarks while autonomously resolving cluster cardinality. Additionally, it significantly outperforms existing sparse possibilistic and probabilistic soft-clustering alternatives in line detection tasks.
  </details>

- **[SAT-ACT: Learning CDCL Branching from Outcome-Derived Action Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41290)**  `arXiv:2610.41290`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenchao Sun, Chongyang Tao, Ping Lu, Tongkai Lu, Jianling Gao, Shuai Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning-based approaches have increasingly been explored for SAT solving, with particular attention to improving branching decisions in conflict-driven clause learning (CDCL) solvers. These approaches derive branching guidance either from static structural properties of SAT formulas or from dynamic signals obtained through solver feedback and execution traces. However, these forms of supervision provide limited evidence about state-dependent branching effects, while different outcome measures may favor different branching choices. To address these limitations, we propose SAT-ACT, an outcome-derived learning framework for CDCL branching. We design three complementary outcome comparison criteria to extract action preferences from independent branching action rollouts initiated at the same solver state. The criteria identify preferences supported by the outcomes and leave comparisons unresolved when the evidence is insufficient or conflicting, instead of forcing an ordering using a predefined scalar metric. A state-conditioned network learns branching priorities from the extracted preferences and uses them to guide CDCL search directly without online action rollouts. We further introduce non-dominated heuristic action supervision, which retains the solver-selected action as a training target only when it remains non-dominated under these preferences. Solver-level experiments show that SAT-ACT consistently reduces CDCL search costs on three SAT families and improves PAR-2 on the SAT Competition benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Equation Search: Conjecture-Guided Discovery with SCOPE and ClosureBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18938)**  `arXiv:2610.18938`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxiao Hu, Qian Li, Ding Wang, Siheng Chen, Linfeng Zhang, Jinyue Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most symbolic regression methods optimize candidate equations, but improving an equation's fit does not establish whether its underlying scientific conjecture should be retained, revised, or rejected. A conjecture may admit multiple mathematical realizations, and similarly accurate equations may imply different explanations. This motivates a shift from refining individual expressions to developing and testing the conjectures that guide them. We introduce SCOPE, a framework that sustains multiple conjecture-driven research trajectories. Multiple scientist agents independently pursue conjectures through equation modeling, question formulation, and active experimentation, then exchange evidence to revise their conjectures and research directions. Their findings are consolidated into a scientific report linking models, conclusions, and evidence. Existing equation-discovery benchmarks primarily assess recovered expressions, leaving the evidence and scientific conclusions established through the investigation underexamined. We therefore introduce ClosureBench, an interactive benchmark of 60 closure-discovery tasks across five scientific domains. ClosureBench focuses on closure-discovery tasks to give equation discovery a concrete scientific purpose. Each task embeds an unknown algebraic relation in a known PDE system, where agents control experimental conditions in OpenFOAM to acquire new data for equation discovery. Beyond equation accuracy, ClosureBench proposes three metrics to evaluate the discovery process: evidence faithfulness, mechanism insight, and experimental effectiveness. Compared with diverse baselines, SCOPE achieves higher equation accuracy on both ClosureBench and a real-world scientific discovery case, while producing more accurate, reliable, and effective scientific discoveries.
  </details>

- **[OpenFinGym: A Verifiable Multi-Task Gym Environment for Evaluating Quant Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26350)**  `arXiv:2606.26350`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaicheng Zhang, Wen Ge, Lei Jiang, Weixin Yang, Jordan Langham-Lopez, Jialin Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although large language model agents are increasingly applied to quantitative-finance workflows, their evaluation remains fragmented across isolated tasks, while the financial relevance of benchmark tasks is often overlooked. Yet financial workflows are inherently multi-stage, spanning interdependent tasks such as forecasting, strategy construction, risk management, and trading. Existing platforms typically focus on a single task, and can therefore overstate agent competence and fail to reveal weaknesses in generalization, real-market interaction, and financially meaningful decision-making. We introduce OpenFinGym, a unified gym environment for quantitative-finance agent development that covers forecasting, market generation, real-time trading, and fraud detection under a single execution and verification interface. OpenFinGym additionally provides an automated task-construction pipeline that turns quantitative finance publications into executable task packages; a containerised runtime with a host-side verifier service that supports scalable agent rollouts and prevents runtime train-test leakage; a paper trading engine with a low-latency data-stream design; deferred-resolution support for long-horizon and event-market forecasts; and integration for SFT and RL post-training.
  </details>

- **[Can You Break RLVER? Probing Adversarial Robustness of RL-Trained Empathetic Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07138)**  `arXiv:2605.07138`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Deeraj S K, Sadhana Devarajan, Krishna Mehra, Sudhakar Mishra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable emotion rewards (RLVER) yields strong empathetic language models, but is evaluated with cooperative, honest simulated users. Real users gaslight, escalate, and demand unconditional validation. We introduce the Adversarial Empathy Benchmark (AEB), six adversarial trajectory types whose discriminative rewards penalize formulaic comfort, and the Emotional Consistency Score (ECS), which separates whether a model \emph{improves} the user's emotional state from whether that state stays legible in the transcript. Across eight scenario-matched conditions (480 dialogues; two RLVER and two Qwen2.5 base models, with and without thinking), RLVER-PPO-Think outperforms its untuned same-scale baseline (FS $0.979$ vs.\ $0.761$, $p<10^{-12}$) with no dialogue collapses and 53\% higher hidden-intention detection; the gain is not explained by response length. Yet under an independent cross-family judge (Gemma-3-4b-it), ECS is indistinguishable between the two ($p=0.43$). We call this an outcome--legibility dissociation: RLVER improves emotional outcomes without making the user's state more legible. We do not claim ECS measures internal understanding.
  </details>

- **[Health-SCORE: Improving Open-Ended Health LLMs Using Scalable Rubric-Based Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18706)**  `arXiv:2601.18706`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhichao Yang, Sepehr Janghorbani, Dongxu Zhang, Jun Han, Qian Qian, Andrew Ressler II, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is a powerful approach for improving language models, but applying it to open-ended domains such as healthcare remains challenging because high-quality responses are often multidimensional, context-dependent, and difficult to capture with a single reward signal. Rubrics offer a natural form of supervision by providing a structured representation of the criteria that define a strong response. Existing rubric-based approaches, however, often rely on instance-specific criteria, creating two important limitations: (1) such rubrics are typically unavailable for unseen examples at test time; and (2) constructing new rubrics for every instance incurs substantial reward design costs. We introduce Health-SCORE, a scalable framework that captures recurring principles of response quality in the form of reusable meta-rubrics. Rather than relying on manually constructed instance-level criteria, Health-SCORE captures generalizable principles that can be applied across examples, tasks, and healthcare datasets. These meta-rubrics support two forms of model improvement: they can serve as structured, safety-aware reward signals for reinforcement learning during post-training, and they can be incorporated directly into prompts to guide model behavior at inference time. Across several open-ended healthcare benchmarks, models trained with Health-SCORE achieve performance competitive with models trained using human-authored instance-level rewards, while substantially reducing reward-design costs and improving generalizability.
  </details>

- **[TriPhysBench: Evaluating Physical Intelligence for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01934)**  `arXiv:2611.01934`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhongxiang Sun, Chenghao Yang, Haojie Ding, Boheng Zhang, Fan Yang, Qingsong Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents need physical intelligence to understand their surroundings through physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning. These capabilities must work together to interpret events and make quantitative judgments. Existing benchmarks advance physical understanding and general-purpose tool use, yet the coordination of these capabilities in physical scenarios remains insufficiently characterized. Inspired by Popper's Three Worlds, we introduce TriPhysBench, a benchmark of 410 questions from 200 videos that brings physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning into a shared evaluation setting. Its measurement-grounded and knowledge-grounded subtasks impose complementary demands on obtaining observations and acquiring external specifications, then applying them to quantitative problems. Among six representative vision-language models, the best achieves an answer accuracy of only 50.38%, revealing limited performance on tasks that require these capabilities to work together. We further introduce TriPhys, a framework that learns a unified policy for physical perception, knowledge retrieval, and quantitative reasoning from complete tool-integrated trajectories. The policy uses intermediate reasoning to guide retrieval and computation, then incorporates tool feedback into subsequent physical inference. On 110 questions from videos excluded from training, TriPhys improves answer and solution scores across three Qwen configurations, with a 17.7% relative gain in answer accuracy for TriPhys-27B over its Qwen3.6-27B backbone. This work provides a benchmark and a learning pathway toward embodied agents that move beyond recognizing events to understanding and reasoning about the physical processes behind them.
  </details>

- **[Seeing What Matters, Verifying What Pays: Load-Adaptive Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01617)**  `arXiv:2611.01617`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hong Nie, Zhangchi Gao, Yuqian Liang, Jun Yu, Bo Wang, Jianqing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates vision-language model (VLM) inference without altering the output distribution, yet two factors limit its gains. Existing drafters either lack the image evidence required for visually grounded tokens or pay to condition on every visual token, although a short draft block typically depends on only a few image regions. Moreover, the value of verifying additional candidates varies with GPU load, so no fixed verification structure is efficient across serving conditions. We present Uni-LaSpec, a block-parallel speculative decoding framework that addresses both factors. To improve draft quality, Uni-LaSpec reuses the verifier's own attention: the query retained from the preceding verification ranks the cached, int8-quantized visual keys, and a cross-layer vote selects a few visual tokens that the drafter fuses through gated cross-attention. Acceptance-aware training further weights each draft position by its contribution to the accepted prefix. To allocate verification, a marginal-cost criterion selects the structure per request and per round: draft trees exploit idle verifier capacity under light load, whereas a dynamic head truncates linear drafts under heavy load. Across six VLMs from three model families and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, selecting eight visual tokens retains at least 98.6% of the acceptance obtained from the full image at sub-millisecond selection cost.
