With greedy decoding at concurrency one, Uni-LaSpec achieves up to 5.11x text and 3.24x multimodal throughput over autoregressive decoding. Across six load levels and three GPU types, it outperforms autoregressive decoding in every setting and matches or exceeds the best fixed verification strategy in 123 of 144 settings; under bursty arrivals, it reduces mean end-to-end latency by 47.6% relative to linear verification.
  </details>

- **[MemU-Bench: Benchmarking GUI Agents on Long-Horizon User Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00191)**  `arXiv:2611.00191`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yizhou Liu, Zhengxi Lu, Fei Tang, Yiwen Qiu, Yong Du, Tongbo Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Graphical User Interface (GUI) benchmarks predominantly rely on precise and stateless user instructions. In everyday usage, however, instructions are often \emph{vague and history-dependent}: users omit the exact details of their past operations because they no longer remember them, leaving the agent to recover the missing context from long and noisy interaction histories. To bridge this evaluation gap, we introduce \textbf{MemU-Bench}, a mobile benchmark of \textbf{118 vague tasks} grounded in 29 long-horizon user trajectories, where agents must distill raw interaction streams into memory and retrieve the missing context on demand, with task outcomes verified against serialized application databases. Alongside Success Rate, we incorporate Interaction Efficiency (IE) and Success weighted by Path Length (SPL) to expose agents that succeed by exhaustively searching across different applications rather than by \emph{genuinely recalling} the history. Evaluations show that without user history, state-of-the-art systems struggle under such ambiguity: the best-performing Gemini-3.1-Pro achieves an SPL of only 16.4\%, and end-to-end GUI agents fall below 5\%. To address this, we further propose \textbf{AppMem}, a training-free framework that distills user history into a \emph{structured, app-centric} memory bank, tracks interrupted intents across application switches, and selectively retrieves relevant memories via target-app inference. AppMem raises the SPL of GPT-5.5 from 15.7\% to 66.9\% (\textbf{+51.2\%}), outperforms a naive summary baseline by 9.3\%, and delivers consistent gains across evaluated backbones.
  </details>

- **[The Trinity: Capability-Driven Recursive Self-Improvement for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36077)**  `arXiv:2610.36077`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Weidong Tang, Ruiheng Wang, Jingxuan Zhang, Hexin Wang, Haoyuan Chen, Zhengfeiyang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal intelligence can be viewed as human-guided co-evolution between worlds and agents through interaction. However, scaling this process faces two bottlenecks: constructing heterogeneous, verifiable environments is costly, and manual Harness iteration cannot keep pace with growing task diversity and complexity. A key insight is that tasks with different modalities and surface forms can share the same underlying capability requirements. We therefore introduce The Trinity, a recursive self-improvement framework coupling world generation with Harness evolution through three roles: Creator, Explorer, and Evolver. Drawing on verified execution trajectories, Evolver shifts the basis of task organization from surface categories to shared requirements, reorganizing the original task tree into a hierarchical capability tree. We introduce Capability-Driven Real2Sim2Real: for heterogeneous tasks at the same node, Creator abstracts their common requirements into simplified, verifiable simulated worlds for capability learning, while evaluation on original tasks measures transfer. Along this capability tree, Explorer progressively evolves reusable capability modules through selective inheritance, forming a Harness tree whose validated updates accumulate across iterations within a fixed topology. The resulting Harnesses specialize in different capabilities, motivating a Capability-Aware Harness Router that uses public task information to select compatible module configurations from the tree. Our evolved Harnesses improve mean performance by 34 points across 28 training benchmarks and 26 points across 18 unseen benchmarks, including two real-world embodied tasks.
  </details>

- **[Decentralized Multi-Agent Coordination and Communication in Shared Codebases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00314)**  `arXiv:2611.00314`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Rishabh Mediratta, Lingyun Xiao, Peter Stone, Joydeep Biswas, Elias Stengel-Eskin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents working in shared repositories may receive separate, independently-motivated requests from human developers, creating a setting that is multi-agent by necessity. When this scenario happens, their individual implementations may work separately but conflict when combined. To study this setting, we introduce ParallACT, a benchmark of orchestrator-free multi-agent coding tasks. ParallACT contains both complementary and contradictory tasks. In complementary tasks, each agent's task is separately useful and implementations must share an interface to be productively combined. In contradictory pairs, both agents' tasks require editing the same code in incompatible ways, and naively combining independent solutions fails to produce a useful implementation. We compare a single-agent oracle given the combined set of tasks against multi-agent settings that vary execution order, visibility, awareness of other agents, and access to a communication channel. We find that the oracle solves most tasks while parallel agents perform substantially worse. Their performance drop is notable for complementary tasks, and particularly sharp for contradictory tasks (e.g., from 62% to 16% for Claude Code with Claude Opus 4.8) where merged code typically satisfies only one specification, and the same gap appears in all six further configurations pairing two agent harnesses with three open-weight models. Simply telling an agent that a peer exists does not help significantly, and giving agents access to a communication channel recovers only a small part of the performance, at an additional token cost. ParallACT thus identifies and isolates a core challenge that will become more common as multi-agent coding by independent principals becomes standard, and provides a controlled test of which coordination strategies help, and at what cost.
  </details>

- **[SelfImprove-Bench: Evaluating Generalization in Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02797)**  `arXiv:2611.02797`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Bangrui Xu, Jie Yang, Sidi Chen, Xuanhe Zhou, Jiantao Qiu, Qianyu Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursively self-improving (RSI) systems are increasingly viewed as a promising means of accelerating progress toward artificial general intelligence. They aim to autonomously improve their capabilities by refining their working pipelines and recursively improving the strategies that guide subsequent refinements, with gains generalizing to unseenthis http URL, existing evaluations lack reliable metrics to distinguish capability gains from scenario fitting, sustainable and extensible protocols for unseen tasks and difficulties, and fine-grained attribution of improvement to evolution traces. To this end, we propose SelfImprove-Bench,an RSI evaluation framework that organizes tasks into three primary capabilities (i.e., Exploring, Planning, Reasoning) as scenarios for guiding model evolution. We instantiated the tasks on games for controllability and reproducibility, and pairs them with scalable generation and orchestration driven by task templates and difficulty coefficients. Beyond task-level scores, SelfImprove-Bench organizes evaluation into generalization ladder levels G0 to G4 that measure how far gains transfer from training conditions. Experiments reveal that the evolutionary gains of existing RSI methods are inconsistent across generalization levels.
  </details>

- **[WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03634)**  `arXiv:2611.03634`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaoyang Wang, Qianhui Wu, Boyu Gou, Demi Wang, Jiacheng Zhang, Peichun Hua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Wrong but Useful: Trajectory Value Beyond Answer Correctness in Multi-Agent Messages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14375)**  `arXiv:2608.14375`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Chih-Hsuan Yang, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Cheng-Hau Yang, Weijian Zheng, Fernando Llorente, Xiaolong Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A solution can end with a wrong answer and still help another solver. In multi-agent reasoning, evaluating only a message's proposed answer can therefore miss its contribution to the receiver. We measure this contribution as trajectory value: the change in a fixed integrator's probability of answering correctly when a cached message is present rather than removed. Controlled replay across five benchmarks and two model families separates message effects from identical-input variation. The strongest evidence is for Gemma: in a pre-registered fresh-call re-test, 34 of 73 selected wrong-answer messages pass individual confirmation, exceeding control-calibrated upper bounds on expected false confirmations of 11.4--20.8. The finding is benchmark-dependent and the conservative criterion no longer passes without SciBench. A blinded two-author audit supports the selected answer labels, while neutral replacements show that the helpful effect is not explained solely by message length and position. On held-out replay blocks of the same problems, a message-specific removal rule improves accuracy over the strongest tested correctness-only rule by 1.61 percentage points [1.01, 2.25]. Post hoc analysis localizes most of this gap to pools containing no correct proposal. Evidence for gpt-oss is weaker, and the tested predictors do not establish a practical way to identify useful errors in advance. The results support measuring answer correctness and receiver benefit separately, with value understood as conditional on the message pool, receiver and generation format.
  </details>

- **[When Should LLMs Be Less Specific? Selective Abstraction for Reliable Long-Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11908)**  `arXiv:2602.11908`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shani Goren, Ido Galil, Ran El-Yaniv_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are widely used, yet they remain prone to factual errors that erode trust and limit adoption in high-risk settings. One approach to mitigate this risk is to equip models with uncertainty estimation mechanisms that abstain when confidence is low. However, this binary "all-or-nothing" approach is excessively restrictive in long-form settings, often discarding valuable information. We introduce Selective Abstraction (SA), a framework that enables LLMs to trade specificity for reliability by selectively reducing the detail of uncertain content. We formalize SA through selective risk and coverage, and propose Atom-wise Selective Abstraction, a claim-level instantiation that decomposes responses into atomic claims and replaces uncertain atoms with higher-confidence, less specific abstractions. We develop a novel end-to-end pipeline for evaluating long-form generation that instantiates risk as factual correctness and measures coverage as retained information. Our comprehensive empirical evaluation across five LLMs and two long-form factual benchmarks shows that atom-wise SA consistently improves the risk-coverage trade-off, outperforming seven baselines, including claim removal, self-revision, sampling-based aggregation, and chain-of-verification. It improves AURC by up to 35.61% over claim removal, demonstrating that reducing specificity boosts reliability while preserving substantial information. We further provide a risk-guided procedure for selecting a confidence threshold that satisfies a target risk.
  </details>

- **[Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03377)**  `arXiv:2611.03377`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Ahmad Jarjis Hasan, Jonathan Lwowski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. The scoring rule alone moves accuracy by up to 22 points on identical outputs, yet exactly 0 wherever a model emits clean option letters, so it hides on strong-model leaderboards. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.
  </details>

- **[When No Answer Is Correct: Diagnosing Absent Answer Detection for MLLMs in Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08239)**  `arXiv:2606.08239`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yiheng Wang, Yueqian Lin, Lichen Zhu, Yudong Liu, Hai "Helen" Li, Yiran Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made substantial advancements in video understanding, yet the reliability of their responses remains underexplored. This work presents a diagnostic study of absent answer detection for MLLMs in video understanding, where the correct answer is deliberately excluded from the candidate set and a reliable model is expected to recognize that no valid option exists. We evaluate the absent answer detection behavior under three settings: multiple-choice questions augmented with a ``None of the Above'' option, open-ended generation with a detection instruction, and standard evaluation without any guidance. Across a diverse set of models and benchmarks, we find that MLLMs overwhelmingly select plausible distractors rather than detecting the absent answer. This failure is more pronounced in temporal reasoning tasks and worsens with denser frame sampling. We further explore chain-of-thought prompting as a mitigation strategy and find that while it substantially improves detection rates, performance remains unsatisfactory, suggesting that prompting-based strategies alone are insufficient to fully address this limitation. These findings expose a systematic failure in absent answer detection and highlight the need for explicit detection mechanisms in multimodal systems.
  </details>

- **[Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03618)**  `arXiv:2611.03618`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Taiming Lu, Qi Xu, Zhigang Hua, Zhuang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.
  </details>

- **[DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601)**  `arXiv:2611.03601`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chengxi Li, Shaoxuan Li, Deyao Hong, Qingle Liu, Yi Huang, Puheng Bian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.
  </details>

- **[Online Label-Free Gradient Matching for Continual Language Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03496)**  `arXiv:2611.03496`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Mohammad Reza Rezaei, Ilija Subasic, Pedro Garzon, Radu Paradovschi, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models in customer-facing products must be continually updated, since the data they serve shifts over time. As retraining on the full accumulated data is impractical, each update should instead run on a small set of examples carrying the training signal of all data seen so far. The strongest current methods build this set by gradient matching, which synthesizes examples whose training gradients match those of the full data. However, they require ground-truth labels, run once on a fixed snapshot rather than on a growing stream, and are slow on large data. We introduce \textbf{OLGA}, which lifts these limitations. It distills unlabeled data by drawing the gradient matching targets from the model's own next-token predictions. Across five text classification benchmarks with phi-1.5, it improves accuracy by 10 percentage points over the strongest selection baseline using as few as five examples. OLGA also distills online data streams by keeping a running rehearsal memory of synthetic examples, which is re-distilled together with each new batch as it arrives. In the online setting, it outperforms the strongest selection-based continual baseline by 6.6 stream-AUC percentage points. Finally, our method scales to large data by pairing distillation with selection in a hybrid pipeline, letting selection handle the mild stage of compression and distillation the extreme one. It cuts runtime by 30\% versus distillation alone at similar accuracy, and beats the best selection-only pipeline by up to 12.6 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[CoworkOS:Scaling Agent Coworkers for Real-world Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03178)**  `arXiv:2611.03178`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiajie Jin, Guanting Dong, Chengjun Pan, Xin Deng, Zhuohui Sheng, Guoqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rapid advances in cowork agents are shifting human-AI collaboration toward real-world automation, delivering software, designs, and other artifacts. However, such productivity work demands both high-quality delivery on complex productivity tasks and continued monitoring and decision-making over long horizons, a combination that remains an open challenge. We introduce CoworkOS, a general-purpose harness for long-horizon work that separates task strategy from execution management: the model decides actions, delegation, and acceptance, while the runtime maintains state and feedback. Its three-layer architecture enables direct execution or delegation to Subagents, synchronously or asynchronously, under stable responsibilities. Through a shared application layer, humans and agents inspect and revise the same evolving documents, presentations, CAD models, and Blender scenes, so human-in-the-loop participation can enter both through conversation and through direct modification of the work itself. To sustain continuity across agents and sessions, CoworkOS further introduces hierarchical workspaces and persistent semantic memory, so working files, key decisions, and long-running Job logs remain shareable, reusable, and resumable after interruption. Across 12 benchmarks and 6 categories of real-world applications, CoworkOS proves highly competitive with frontier agent applications such as Codex and DeepSeek Harness. As a sustained-work case study, CoworkOS uses GLM-5.3 as its backbone to conduct RSI-driven automated post-training on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Over five rounds, the model's average score across eight benchmarks rose from 33.19 to 42.95 (+9.76). Looking ahead, we believe CoworkOS opens a meaningful path toward general cowork-agent intelligence.
  </details>

- **[LEANGUARD: Lean-Verified Guardrails for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02332)**  `arXiv:2611.02332`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sreehari Sankar, Debargha Ganguly, Vikash Singh, Mohsen Hariri, Weicong Chen, Yanyan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language-model agents often execute actions that their domain policies forbid. We present LeanGuard, a runtime that checks every proposed tool call against a policy written in Lean4 before the call reaches the environment. Policies combine state predicates with scoped past-time temporal operators over the execution history, evaluation is fail-closed, and the decision procedure is proved equivalent to a LeanLTL semantics. A denied call can be corrected in two ways. With feedback, the agent receives the violated rule and a hint; with proof-guided repair, Lean metaprogramming searches for a nearby call that preserves the user's intent and proves that the policy admits it, without the model. On Tau-Bench with five Qwen models (0.8B-27B), unguarded agents execute at least 33 calls that the Lean policy forbids, while LeanGuard executes none; with feedback, task success does not change significantly. On DTap, LeanGuard reduces direct prompt-injection success from 57.9% to 5.9%, and in telecom, where benign tasks need no writes, benign success is unchanged. Feedback reduces repeated blocked calls and raises task success for four of five models over a bare denial. On replayed denials, repair proves an admissible edit for 88 of 252 denials caused by argument values, in a median of 2,ms.
  </details>

- **[Learning by Doing: Autonomously Compiling and Curating Agent Skills from Verified Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01386)**  `arXiv:2611.01386`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Chalef, Peter Evans, Pavlo Paliychuk, Mehul Patel, Preston Rasmussen, Jack Ryan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for production LLM agents mostly store semantic memory (facts about users and domains) or episodic memory (earlier interactions). Research systems such as Trace2Skill, WikiSkill, and SkillRevise learn procedures from agent runs, package them as Skills, and evaluate them on benchmarks. We describe Zep Agent Skills, a production service that gives deployed agents this procedural memory. The service uses only verified attempts as evidence, cites the supporting events for each field of a Skill, and governs the admission and distribution of each Skill version. The service records each task attempt as a Trace with ordered events, an outcome, and a verification strength. A compiler with separate Reflector and Curator roles turns verified Traces into versioned Skills. Scans and admission gates control which candidates become active, and verified use outcomes change the search rank of each Skill. The Agent Skills format is an open standard that Anthropic originated and donated to the Agentic AI Foundation, so any host of the format can load a compiled Skill. Agents retrieve Skills through lexical search with progressive disclosure, over an API or MCP. We evaluate Zep Agent Skills on $\tau^3$-bench banking, AppWorld, and EnterpriseOps-Gym. Skills compiled from the Traces of a teacher model increase the pass rate of three weaker executor models on the same tasks, at every reasoning effort, by 4.5 to 18.8 points. Most of the gain is on the tasks that the teacher solved. On ALFWorld, Skills compiled from the runs of a small model give a gain similar to or larger than the gain that prior work reports for continuous fine-tuning, with no training.
  </details>

- **[Skill++: Towards Lifelong Agents via Routing-Aware Skill Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00544)**  `arXiv:2611.00544`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hanrong Zhang, Minghao Liang, Jiawei Wu, Yankai Chen, Shicheng Fan, Zhiwei Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents extend their capabilities with skills, each with a name, a description, and a body. Under progressive disclosure, an LLM router selects skills by name and description alone and loads only the selected bodies. Skill libraries grow continually as users and agents add new skills. Because the router relies on this metadata, a similar new skill can displace an earlier one, breaking previously correct routing even though the earlier skill and the router are unchanged. This routing interference is aggravated for public skills, whose descriptions may not match their bodies. We formulate the problem as lifelong skill routing, a class-incremental continual learning problem. A solution must keep earlier skills selectable without suppressing new ones. Existing approaches fall short, since continually retraining the router risks catastrophic forgetting and rewriting methods either ignore similar skills or require per-skill feedback. We therefore propose Skill++, which rewrites only the name and description of each incoming skill, without router training or labeled data. The new description states what the body supports, how the skill differs from its closest predecessor, and what related work it excludes. As a library grows to 731 skills over ten sessions, Skill++ selects all required skills for 76.3% of queries, versus 36.4% under the original metadata. Across three benchmarks and five LLM routers, it outperforms all compared approaches, and its gains carry over to the Codex CLI agent harness.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Context Management for Multi-Turn Human-AI Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00518)**  `arXiv:2611.00518`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Matthew Ho, Chenglong Wang, Baolin Peng, Nebojsa Jojic, Philippe Laban, Michel Galley, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs degrade in multi-turn conversation partly because of context pollution: the model's own earlier outputs stay in context and anchor later turns, so an early misreading of the user's intent can persist after the user corrects it. Assistant omission (AO), dropping every assistant message, removes this pollution but fails when interaction is referential: later turns refer back to drafts, decisions, and tool-call results that exist only in assistant turns. We propose Agentic Conversation Context Curation (AC3), a training-free method in which a separate analyzer (1) consolidates the task specification from the user's messages alone, (2) audits the assistant's prior outputs against it, and (3) edits the context, either appending the analysis or discarding the assistant side and rebuilding the context from the specification. A second finding: pollution is contagious. An analyzer that sees the assistant's reasoning while extracting the specification can do worse than no intervention, and instructing it to ignore them did not close the gap, so we exclude assistant turns structurally. On Lost in Conversation (replaying difficulty-selected instances), where AO is an oracle by construction, AC3 closes 55-84% of the gap between full context and AO on three of four tasks and exceeds AO on the fourth, and an LLM that faithfully summarizes the history does not reproduce the gain even with more compute. On real WildChat conversations, a GPT-5-mini judge prefers AC3's next turn over both full context and AO in every configuration of our main table, though this preference depends on the judge; on CollabLLM results are mixed. In stateful tool use (tau2-bench), AO collapses to 0% on every model; AC3 does not collapse, though it falls slightly behind full context. Detector studies show that Reset removes injected pollution but is not span-selective: the gain comes from rebuilding the specification.
  </details>

- **[Swap, Replicate, Read the Mass: A Protocol for Auditing Demographic Bias in System One Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00496)**  `arXiv:2611.00496`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Alejandro Mozo Quesada, Sergio Gálvez Rojas, Francisco López Valverde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One decision models replace text generation with typed, probability-scored answers and are entering production. Answers execute without a person in the loop, and no multi-model audit exists on established bias benchmarks. We provide that audit: three benchmarks: BBQ (all eleven categories), CrowS-Pairs, and SilverSpoon, adapted to the typed interface, twelve models plus a no-thinking ablation, and 1,638,000 decisions. Every order-sensitive item runs twice with options swapped; native typed models also swap labels; ten configurations repeat under extra seeds and batch 16. We find widespread stereotype alignment, order-dependent answers, and confidence that tracks instability rather than bias. In BBQ, 97 of 143 model-by-category scores are significant and 94 aligned, while the commercial reference abstains on 99.5% of ambiguous items, hiding aligned probability mass. Option swaps flip 0.0% to 72.6% of choices; typed scoring stays below 10% except Laya (22-33%), and untuned letter prompting reaches 73%. CrowS-Pairs preference spans 0.496 to 0.816, and boolean answers can be decided by position (1.00 versus 0.005). SilverSpoon judgments shift toward lower-SES labels in five of eight decision models, inherited from the 9B backbone or induced by tuning. Audits must permute options and reverse questions by default; order-invariant scoring should become a procurement requirement.
  </details>

- **[From Small to Large: Assessing Medical Reasoning, Benchmark Contamination, and Model Size in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00460)**  `arXiv:2611.00460`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Navapat Nananukul, Mayank Kejriwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) now answer medical exam questions at or above expert level, but it is unclear whether such scores reflect clinical competence, or are instead affected by the format of the questions being asked (e.g., multiple-choice versus open-ended), prior exposure to the benchmarks, and the difficulty of the questions. These benchmarks are also public and widely used online, which may be resulting in LLM memorization. Models may perform better on questions that they have seen before. It is also not evident whether smaller models, which are more locally deployable in HIPAA-compliant hospital settings, have achieved human performance. In this study, we evaluate fourteen current models from six leading providers, one smaller and one larger model from each provider with two additional open-weight models, on 3,076 questions from four medical benchmarks (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, PubMedQA, and MedBullets) under standard multiple choice, the same questions with the answer choices withheld, and reconstruction of a hidden question from its choices alone. In the standard multiple-choice setting, the strongest models exceed the human-expert baseline on MedQA-USMLE and PubMedQA, but the smaller models trail their larger models by 8.7 percentage points and only two of the six pass the human-expert bar on the licensing examination. Withholding the answer choices, however, makes every model struggle: mean accuracy falls from 82.3% to 50.9%, and the two largest drops belong to the medically specialised models. On memorization, the strongest models reconstruct one question in four from the answer choices alone, yet accuracy holds on the questions they cannot reconstruct. We further weigh accuracy against inference cost and test whether ensembling several models improves medical question answering.
  </details>

- **[Quantiles, Not Points: Streaming Quantile Matching for Ordinal-Aware LLM-as-a-Judge Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00447)**  `arXiv:2611.00447`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ilija Subasic, Andrew Rabinovich, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge scores are often distributionally shifted from human ratings on any given evaluation task, and post-hoc calibration (mapping LLM to human scores in a small labeled anchor set) has become a popular method to correct for this shift. In this paper we derive the calibration from two positions: that LLMaJ scores are ordinal ratings drawn from a distribution rather than isolated points. We present streaming quantile matching (SQM), a version of a classical method not previously bench marked for LLMaJ calibration, which is built on the two positions by computing the optimal-transport map between the LLM and human score distributions. We develop an evaluation framework and within it we show that unlike point-wise calibrations, SQM respects the ordinality of the human anchor set and achieves the best distributional fidelity and the top overall rank across models and data. We also show that in general, post-hoc calibration narrows the raw error gap between smaller and larger judge models, which opens the possibility of more efficient LLM judges.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Escalation Reliability in Healthcare LLM Agents: A Perturbation-Controlled Benchmark of Workflow-State Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21270)**  `arXiv:2610.21270`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zihao Zheng, Junyi Yao, Baichuan Li, Jiayu Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trustworthy biomedical AI requires reliable action selection when evidence is missing, stale, conflicting, or late, not only accurate answers on static inputs. We instantiate an episode-level evaluation protocol as 400 synthetic healthcare workflow episodes: 40 hand-authored semantic seeds across four task classes, two surface parameterizations, and five controlled variants, scored on ANSWER/ASK CLARIFICATION/ABSTAIN/ESCALATE under a pre-declared 5:1 missed-versus-unnecessary escalation cost. Reference actions are single-author study-policy labels, not clinician-adjudicated ground truth. Across five open-weight configurations (1.7B--9.7B), clean decision accuracy reaches 0.97, yet uncertainty-prompted models miss 32--97% of required escalations. The stronger 9.7B configuration narrows but does not remove the gap, falling from 0.97 clean accuracy to 0.78 under conflict. Verbalized confidence is inconsistent (ECE 0.10--0.54; failure AUROC 0.48--0.77), and a development-tuned confidence-only policy yields no utility gain. A transparent workflow-aware controller improves 5:1 utility for every model and reduces missed escalations to 0.12--0.24, at specificity 0.83--0.93. However, a 30-concept lexical stress test reduces each rule detector's recall from 1.00 on trigger phrases to 0.00 on unseen paraphrases, limiting the controller claim to this benchmark. Seed-clustered statistics, ablations, cross-task transfer, raw outputs, and manifests are packaged for audit. The results motivate explicit workflow-state evaluation while showing that apparently effective routing controls need their own generalization tests.
  </details>

- **[SwarmRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Evolving Scientific Agent Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20431)**  `arXiv:2610.20431`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Qianru Meng, Xi Zhang, Konstantinos Papakostas, Congfeng Cao, Wenxiang Hu, Tianyi Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in scientific research aims to improve agents' research capabilities through their own experimental experience. Scientific agent swarms can combine evidence and implementations from different research directions to guide their agents' subsequent experiments. However, poor experimental results can leave unclear whether to revise a hypothesis or its implementation, while overall program scores can hide components useful to other agents. To address these challenges, we introduce SwarmRSI, a framework that evolves scientific agent swarms for recursive self-improvement. We devise Feedback-Guided Swarm Mutation to refine hypotheses and their implementations using experimental feedback from other agents. We also propose Complementary Swarm Recombination to reuse retained hypotheses and complementary program components across agents, allowing partial results to support new research. Recursive Swarm Evolution integrates both mechanisms into a feedback loop, using new experimental evidence to guide subsequent research. Experiments on OpenProblems, NatureBench and MLE-Bench Lite demonstrate SwarmRSI's state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. Further evaluations show that accumulated research experience improves performance on held-out tasks. Biologists also reproduce its programs for follow-up biological analyses.
  </details>

- **[RAP: Research Attention Prediction in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10092)**  `arXiv:2609.10092`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yingqian Wu, Jingcong Liang, Shengbin Yue, Siyuan Wang, Zhenfei Yin, Junchi Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly support research planning and execution, where judgments about current and anticipated research activity inform decisions. Yet whether they can infer changes across a whole field from the partial evidence they retrieve through online scholarly search remains unclear. We introduce Research Attention Prediction (RAP), a literature search benchmark for evaluating this ability. We build a local search environment from 356,357 dated arXiv papers and construct two complementary evaluation tasks. The benchmark comprises a Core set of 2,160 questions across 54 AI/ML fields and an Extended set covering 269 fields. Our evaluation and analysis of LLM agents show that search substantially improves stronger agents' recognition of recent changes, while forecasting gains remain limited or negative. Agents update forecasts across dates and adjust their investigations to forecasting goals, but these adaptations do not reliably yield accurate predictions of future shifts. Even for recent changes, final judgments can diverge from what the retrieved papers indicate. These findings show that agents can recognise some recent changes, yet reliably anticipating future research activity remains difficult despite changes in their answers and investigations.
  </details>

- **[Humans' ALMANAC: A Human Collaboration Dataset of Action-Level Mental Model Annotations for Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06388)**  `arXiv:2606.06388`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaju Chen, Yuxuan Lu, Jiayi Su, Chaoran Chen, Songlin Xiao, Zheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in LLM agents have enabled complex cognitive capabilities, such as multi-step reasoning, planning, and tool use, that increasingly position these agents as collaborative partners in human workflows. Effective collaboration, however, requires collaborators to continuously maintain and align mental models of their own reasoning, partners' intentions, and shared goals during the collaborative process. Today's agents rarely develop such capabilities since they are primarily optimized for task completion rather than for maintaining the underlying mental models required for collaborative processes. Moreover, the community lacks authentic human collaboration data with action-level mental model annotations that could guide agents toward collaborative competence. To bridge this gap, we present ALMANAC, a dataset of Action-Level Mental model ANnotations for Agent Collaboration built from the Map Task, a classic dyadic routing task from social science. ALMANAC contains 2,987 collaboration actions, each paired with theory-informed mental model annotations that record the participants' self-reasoning, perceived partner intent, and perceived team goal. We benchmark six LLMs on predicting humans' next-turn behavior and mental models. Our results demonstrate ALMANAC's utility in evaluating models' ability to simulate human collaborative behaviors and infer their underlying mental models. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rubric-Guided Process Reward for Stepwise Model Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29310)**  `arXiv:2605.29310`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shenghao Ye, Yu Guo, Zhengheng Li, Qiang Huang, Shuangwu Chen, Wang Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stepwise model routing improves the efficiency of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) by assigning each reasoning step to a suitable model. Recent methods formulate routing as a sequential decision process and train the router with reinforcement learning. However, although they model routing as a process, they still supervise the router with outcome rewards. Such rewards only reflect final answer correctness and fail to evaluate intermediate routing decisions, which can weaken performance and generalization. To address this gap, we propose RoRo, a rubric-guided process reward framework for stepwise model routing. RoRo first collects diverse routing trajectories and constructs preference pairs based on outcome, cost, and process quality. It then trains a Rubricor to generate a query-specific evaluation rubric and a Judge to score routing trajectories under this rubric through alternating optimization. The resulting process rewards are combined with outcome rewards to optimize the routing policy via GRPO. Experiments on five reasoning benchmarks under both same-family and cross-family settings show that RoRo consistently outperforms strong baselines and achieves better accuracy and cost trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Foundation Models to Unlock Real-World Evidence from Nationwide Medical Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02740)**  `arXiv:2605.02740`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fan Ma, Yuntian Liu, Xiang Lan, Weipeng Zhou, Jun Ni, Mauro Giuffrè, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world data increasingly inform regulatory and clinical decisions. Administrative claims, among their major sources, continuously track care and its cost across providers throughout enrollment, yet remain largely unexplored for healthcare foundation models. Here we present ReClaim, a family of generative foundation models built on 43.8 billion claims records from more than 200 million enrollees in the MarketScan Research Databases. ReClaim learns diagnoses, procedures, medications and expenditure jointly through pre-training from scratch, then specializes for downstream prediction tasks through post-training, with a training cutoff at the end of 2022. In predicting more than 1,000 diseases, ReClaim achieved a mean ROC-AUC of 0.769, outperforming disease-specific gradient-boosted models (0.663) and a transformer-based baseline (0.694), and retained this advantage when generalizing to two independent cross-domain electronic health record datasets. ReClaim also outperformed gradient-boosted models in forecasting next-year expenditure and in identifying high-need, high-cost enrollees. ReClaim maintained its advantage in disease prediction and expenditure forecasting on post-cutoff data (2023-2024). Beyond prediction, ReClaim representations used as covariates resulted in improved effect estimates compared with algorithm-derived covariates, when benchmarked against results expected from randomized controlled trials. Together, these results establish nationwide insurance claims as a scalable substrate for healthcare foundation models supporting disease prediction, expenditure forecasting and confounding adjustment in real-world evidence studies. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VeriCypher: Named-Entity Verification for GraphRAG via Text-to-Cypher Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08274)**  `arXiv:2511.08274`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Anton Gusarov, Anastasia Volkova, Valentin Khrulkov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Evgenii Maslov, Ivan Oseledets_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph question answering has traditionally focused on Resource Description Framework (RDF) graphs, SPARQL queries, and triple-based representations. Labeled Property Graphs (LPGs), however, pose a different challenge for LLM-based query generation: executable Cypher queries must align with schema structure, traversal direction, property keys, and literal entity values. This motivated us to introduce VeriCypher, an LLM-based text-to-Cypher framework for question answering over LPGs. It combines query generation conditioned on Cypher-like graph schema summaries with runtime execution, LLM-based query evaluation, and iterative correction where the central mechanism is named-entity verification: the system extracts node labels, relationship patterns, property keys, and literal values from generated queries, checks them against the target graph and schema, and turns mismatches into correction instructions for the next generation step. We evaluate VeriCypher on the CypherBench benchmark across multiple domains and LLM backbones. Compared with an LLM baseline, this method consistently improves answer correctness and query robustness. Ablation results further show that named-entity verification helps correct hallucinated schema elements and entity values, supporting more reliable LLM-based question answering over heterogeneous property graphs.
  </details>

- **[An Agentic Framework for Automated Power System Studies and Grid Connection Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03534)**  `arXiv:2611.03534`  `cs.AI`  
  _MaoXun Huang, Kshitij Kapoor, Eli Louise_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Connecting new generation and demand to the grid depends on two scarce skills: checking applications against a large, changing body of regulation, and running the network studies that decide where and how a project connects. We present Grid Agents, an AI system that brings both together, and the methodology we use to evaluate it. The Grid Ontology is its knowledge system. It represents the regulation, standards and engineering knowledge that grid work depends on, makes it searchable, and evolves as the agents learn from use. Every answer drawn from it rests on evidence the agents cite. Grid Studio is an agent system specialised for power-system engineering. It works in its own engineering environment with domain tools and a study framework, draws on the Grid Ontology, and relies on established solvers for every number it reports. In both, engineers make the decisions. We bring eight published power-system benchmark families, previously scattered across separate papers, into one suite of 519 tasks, and we verify not only each answer but the work behind it, by replaying the agent's tool calls. The Grid Ontology cites the right regulatory evidence more often than a general coding agent or retrieval pipelines, leads on LegalBench-RAG and ranks first on the public leaderboards of the Enterprise RAG Challenge and ViDoRe V3. Grid Studio scores highest on the 519 power-system tasks and is the fastest of all the systems we compare, both by a statistically significant margin. With an optimiser in the loop, its reinforcement packages cost 33-67% less on average than those the agent designs alone.
  </details>

- **[Grading the Unannotated: Insights from the WorkRB Skill Extraction Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03525)**  `arXiv:2611.03525`  `cs.AI`  
  _Warre Veys, Luis Gasco, Jens-Joris Decorte_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill extraction benchmarks score models against a few human-annotated positives and treat every other skill in the vocabulary as equally wrong. At the scale of ESCO (13,939 skills) this binary view can reward models that rank the annotated positive high for the wrong reason, and hides semantically nonsensical predictions. The WorkRB Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026 replaces it with graded relevance: every candidate skill in a model's ranking window carries a 0 to 4 grade, and the leaderboard scores nDCG@100 against these grades. Since ternary judgements proved too coarse for objective annotation, relevance is decomposed into a cascade of four binary questions (same domain, activity mentioned, right granularity, replacement of a gold positive) that a smaller LLM judge can answer reliably; the labels are validated with blind human and frontier-model re-annotation. This paper documents the annotation methodology, the construction of five benchmark datasets (four with vacancy sentences as queries, one with lexically distant skill surface forms), the validation of the LLM cascade, and insights from the submissions. The field converged on multi-stage retrieve-and-rerank pipelines, whose gains come from clearing nonsense out of the ranking window rather than finding more positives: at the top of the leaderboard, the graded ordering is uncorrelated with what binary evaluation would have said. Graded labels expose much bigger gaps between top submissions and baselines than binary evaluation does, and show that semantic-understanding skill recommendation is still wide open. The clear next direction is bringing efficiency back: every submitted system we could size uses at least 42x, typically several hundred times, more parameters than the baselines it beats.
  </details>

- **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412)**  `arXiv:2611.03412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Yeqian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.
  </details>

- **[Top-$k$ Retrieval Leaves Memory Value Undetermined](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03289)**  `arXiv:2611.03289`  `cs.AI`  
  _Arman Behnam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A memory-augmented agent must decide which memories to keep, merge and retrieve, and its only evidence of their value is its retrieval log: the memories it retrieved in each episode and whether it succeeded. Existing methods assign every memory a value from this log, even when the log cannot separate one memory's contribution from another's. We treat the log as an experiment that nobody designed and ask which memory values it determines. Two counts answer this before any outcome is observed: how often each memory was retrieved, and how often each pair was retrieved together. From these counts, we prove that top-$k$ retrieval leaves every memory value undetermined, however many episodes are logged, and that a question with its own memories, asked once, determines at most one of their values. On fourteen standard memory benchmarks, no memory value is determined in any of 55 configurations. Both causes are choices about retrieval. We introduce LEDGER, which estimates each determined Shapley value in closed form with a confidence interval and reports every other value as undetermined. It merges memories with identical histories without losing a determined value, and it retrieves so that the next log determines more. On pooled HotpotQA, LEDGER raises the fraction of memories determined from 0.001 to 0.797, at a cost of 2.6 accuracy points on HotpotQA and with a gain of 2.7 on LoCoMo.
  </details>

- **[ScienceCUA: Resource-to-Skill Construction and Execution-Guided Skill Evolution for Scientific Computer Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03238)**  `arXiv:2611.03238`  `cs.AI`  
  _Daiqiang Li, Jingyi Peng, Rui Hu, Jia Li, Xuyang Liu, Guanren Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-use agents for scientific software require application-specific expertise. Much of this expertise is documented in human-authored software manuals and web pages. However, turning these resources into executable, reusable skills remains challenging due to mismatches between documented and deployed software versions and differences in scope between documentation coverage and task requirements. We introduce ScienceCUA, a two-stage framework combining resource-to-skill construction with execution-guided skill evolution. First, ScienceCUA identifies tasks supported by the software from its documentation, infers operational trajectories, and generalizes them into reusable Skills. Command catalogs, syntax constraints, and shortcuts are organized separately as References for on-demand lookup. Then, ScienceCUA uses execution feedback to drive skill self-evolution by contrasting successful and failed trajectories for the same task, identifying deficiencies, revising existing skills or adding new ones, and updating the skill library only after repeated validation in real environments. To enable reliable evaluation without benchmark noise or task leakage, we introduce two improved benchmarks. ScienceBoard-Verified provides an audited and corrected basis for measuring task performance, while ScienceBoard-VE separates skill evolution from final testing through disjoint evolution and test tasks and further supports native-agent evaluation. Experiments show that the initial Skills and References improve average task success rate by up to 7.23 percentage points over the base model, while skill self-evolution increases the total gain over the baseline to as much as 17.12 percentage points. ScienceCUA turns software documentation into an evolving set of executable skills and queryable references, offering a practical way for general-purpose agents to use scientific software reliably and at scale.
  </details>

- **[Axion 1.0: Open Agent Foundation Models, Simplified](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03233)**  `arXiv:2611.03233`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuqi Jia, Yunjie Tian, Jiayu Zheng, Yijun Liang, Jingchen Sun, Mengyu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Axion 1.0, a fully open agent foundation model that combines strong and broad agentic capabilities with a remarkably simple training recipe. Axion 1.0 is trained in only three stages: two lightweight supervised fine-tuning stages followed by reinforcement learning, without dedicated mid-training, direct preference optimization (DPO), or on-policy distillation (OPD). The first SFT stage develops strong self-contained reasoning through model-aware data construction, where we synthesize challenging problems and adversarially strengthen them based on the base model's own successful solution strategies. The second stage extends these capabilities to tool-dependent reasoning through a scalable pipeline for constructing diverse and automatically verifiable tasks grounded in real-world data sources and deterministic computation. Finally, reinforcement learning further strengthens long-horizon reasoning and interaction with external tools and environments. Despite this compact training recipe, Axion 1.0 achieves strong performance across a broad suite of agentic benchmarks and is competitive with leading commercial models on several of them. Equally importantly, Axion 1.0 is open beyond model weights: we release the complete recipe, including data construction, generation, filtering, verification, prompts, and training code, enabling the entire development process to be reproduced and extended. Axion 1.0 demonstrates that strong agent foundation models can be both open and simple, offering a practical and reproducible path toward advancing open agentic intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Research Agent on MSA Organization to Improve Protein-Protein Complex Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03183)**  `arXiv:2611.03183`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zilu Zhang, Jing Bao, Lifeng Qiao, Sheng Xu, Qiantai Feng, Siqi Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein-complex structure prediction with AlphaFold 3 depends on MSA content, depth, and cross-chain pairing. Current pipelines apply uniform recipes, typically maximizing depth and pairing by species, yet even whether cross-chain pairing helps AF3 remains contested. We show that the value of both depth and pairing is strongly target-dependent, so no single recipe is optimal across interfaces. We introduce an autonomous agent that treats a frozen AF3 as a black-box experimental instrument. For each target, the agent formulates biological hypotheses, analyzes and reorganizes the MSA, and iteratively refines sequence selection and pairing using predictor label-free signals alone. On 1{,}015 protein--protein interfaces from three benchmarks released after AF3's training cutoff, the agent improves overall interface DockQ over AF3's default pipeline and fixed MSA recipes on every benchmark, while using on average 60\% fewer MSA sequences than the default. Gains are largest for inter-species heteromers, where species-based pairing is unreliable: mean DockQ increases from 0.465 to 0.612 on FoldBench and from 0.482 to 0.562 on HD439. The improvements arise from both sequence construction and pairing policy, including deliberatethis http URLresults recast MSA design as a target-specific problem and show that an autonomous agent can adapt it at test time through biological reasoning and self-directed, confidence-guided experimentation.
  </details>

- **[CUA-Master: Scaling Computer-use Agents through Open-World Recursive Improvements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03126)**  `arXiv:2611.03126`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinlei Yu, Gen Li, Ji Zhao, Zhangquan Chen, Congcong Wang, Shuai Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite recent advances in CUAs, their development remains constrained by the limited diversity, controllability, and reliability of interaction data. Moreover, conventional optimization pipelines lack iterative feedback, while existing recursive improvement approaches often rely on self-generated experiences, limiting ability expansion and yielding diminishing returns. To address these challenges, we introduce CUA-Master, a nested double-loop recursive improvement framework. At the system level, an outer world-data-model flywheel continuously incorporates open-world corpus and leverages evolving model weaknesses to guide subsequent data construction and optimization iterations. At the data level, an inner flywheel couples open-world seeding and autocatalytic propagation. The former mines authentic workflows as seed tasks from instructional videos, and employs visual-semantic dual-path trajectory reconstruction; The latter transforms these seeds into reusable primitives for controllable synthesis of diverse, complex, and verifiable tasks. To enable efficient and reliable trajectory collection, we further introduce a feedback-guided snapshot-rollback retry mechanism in various real environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CUA-Master-27B achieves competitive performance across GUI-only and GUI-CLI hybrid benchmarks, scoring 80.2 on OSWorld Verified, 36.9 on OSWorld 2.0, 43.9 on WeaveBench, and 25.0 on Agents' Last Exam. Our framework also substantially improves the quality and efficiency of data synthesis, and exhibits continuous gains across recursive iterations.
  </details>

- **[A Fine-Grained Hierarchy and Benchmark for State Tracking in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03094)**  `arXiv:2611.03094`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jackson Petty, William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theoretical limitations of transformers on state tracking---the ability to track information about entities or variables over long contexts---have motivated new recurrent language modeling (LM) architectures such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN). These architectures have, in particular, been benchmarked against the $S_5$ word problem, a theoretically hard state-tracking task that captures abilities such as code evaluation under arbitrary variable assignment. It, however, remains open whether other, simpler forms of state tracking might both be more relevant in practice and already explain observed differences between these architectures. We propose evaluating state tracking using a hierarchy of word problems with four increasing levels of difficulty: r-trivial, corresponding to immutable assignment to variables in programming; aperiodic, where assignments are mutable; soluble, where assignments are self-referential; and general finite state tracking. Theoretically, we show that the class of problems transformers are thought to length-generalize on does not even contain all aperiodic state tracking. On the other hand, we prove that GDN with positive eigenvalues expresses all aperiodic problems, and the regular languages recognized by certain transformers are precisely all soluble ones, thus providing a fine-grained picture of state tracking capabilities of various architectures. Taking these ideas to practice, we construct StateBench, a 4-tier long-horizon state-tracking benchmark. Our systematic direct training experiments across architectures largely align with theoretical expectation, albeit with interesting observations at very large scale length-generalization. Additionally, we also evaluate pretrained models---both open hybrid and closed frontier ones---on StateBench, finding that none of them robustly solves even soluble state tracking, suggesting a direction for future improvement.
  </details>

- **[PRIME: Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects for Scientific Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03055)**  `arXiv:2611.03055`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fengxian Ji, Xie Yu, Zhexuan Cui, Yuke Li, Zirui Song, Fan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feedback-driven iteration is widely used to improve LLM-generated scientific ideas, but existing systems either leave revision decisions implicit in generation, align revisions with feedback-localized weaknesses, or restrict action selection to system-specific operations. This is problematic because problems, hypotheses, and solutions are interdependent: the location where a weakness manifests does not itself determine the revision scope that will produce a better successor. We term this phenomenon diagnosis-intervention mismatch. We introduce Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects (PRIME), a plug-and-play feedback-to-revision decision layer for existing scientific ideators. PRIME operates in three stages: (1) Structural Attribution interprets feedback over the problem-hypothesis-solution structure without fixing a revision target; (2) Counterfactual Routing constructs alternative scope-conditioned interventions and compares their projected successor utility and risks through lightweight rollouts; and (3) Intervention Execution compiles the selected intervention into an ordered generator-facing plan with semantic actions, preservation constraints, and dependency requirements for successor generation. We validate diagnosis intervention mismatch on 112 human-annotated idea-feedback pairs. Across four benchmarks, two ideators, and fixed- and open-problem settings, PRIME improves successor quality and routing reliability over strong baselines; removing counterfactual routing reduces quality from 3.463 to 1.960 and increases route regret from 0.426 to 1.929, confirming cross-scope effect modeling as the primary source of its gains.
  </details>

- **[Integrating Domain Adaptation and Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Saline-Alkali Land Reclamation Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02883)**  `arXiv:2611.02883`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zuguang He, Congcong Zheng, Wanyue Chen, Wenya Zhou, Zaijin Yang, Long Long, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reclamation of saline-alkali land requires management plans that account for soil and water conditions, treatment evidence, and financial constraints. This study develops Wenyao, an agricultural decision-support system integrating domain adaptation with retrieval-augmented generation. A bilingual corpus of 8,664 research papers was processed using a 110-indicator schema to construct SA-Bench, comprising 4,860 paper-level planning instances. A QwQ-32B model was adapted through low-rank supervised fine-tuning and combined with case retrieval for budget-conditioned planning. Evidence extraction achieved an F1-score of 0.83 on 300 expert-annotated papers. Without retrieval, Wenyao achieved 95.83% accuracy on 936 source-disjoint multiple-choice questions, compared with 87.93% for the strongest general-purpose baseline; removing explanatory supervision reduced accuracy to 89.42%. Matched-evidence comparisons across 69 region-budget cases supported complementary contributions from adaptation and retrieval. In a blind assessment by 31 experts, Wenyao with retrieval obtained the highest mean scores for objectives and projected outcomes, whereas GPT-5 scored highest for cost estimates. These findings support literature-informed reclamation planning with professional review, while ongoing field monitoring will assess soil and crop responses.
  </details>

- **[Think with Skills: Pseudocode Planning Before Execution for Advanced Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02830)**  `arXiv:2611.02830`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boshen Zhou, Boxuan Yue, Xiang Li, Shanchao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model agents often struggle with complex tasks even when equipped with expert-written skills. Such skills encode declarative knowledge, whereas successful execution requires procedural knowledge tailored to the task at hand. Natural language skills may leave conditions and dependencies implicit, and tasks involving multiple skills further require agents to coordinate intermediate results and execution order. We introduce Think with Skills, a method that trains reasoning models to turn relevant skill descriptions into task-specific pseudocode plans before execution. The pseudocode explicitly represents operations, conditions, intermediate results, and dependencies, providing a structured bridge from skill descriptions to agent actions. We construct training data that pairs tasks and skills with pseudocode plans and execution trajectories, use supervised fine tuning to initialize this behavior, and further optimize end-to-end task performance with Group Relative Policy Optimization. On SkillsBench, Think with Skills raises task success from 35.0% to 62.0%. These results show that proceduralizing expert knowledge before execution substantially improves skill use.
  </details>

- **[RxnMiner: A Multimodal Agent for Structured Reaction Data Extraction from Chemistry Figures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02826)**  `arXiv:2611.02826`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinbo Hu, Songren Zhang, Chuanxun Fu, Jinrui Huang, Zhen Hao Wong, Dongxu Cui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reaction data underpin machine learning for reaction prediction, condition recommendation, and retrosynthetic planning, yet extracting these data from literature figures still requires substantial manual effort. Many figures encode multiple experiments through shared templates, substituent tables, and product grids. Recovering concrete reaction records therefore requires molecular recognition, association of evidence with individual experimental entries, and reconstruction of indirectly specified participants. We present RxnMiner, a multimodal agent system that combines an expert-informed extraction procedure with a chemistry toolbox to coordinate these operations. The agent reads molecular structures, resolves shared and entry-specific evidence, and instantiates participants from templates to assemble source-linked reaction records. We also introduce ChemRxn-Bench, a benchmark of 603 reaction figures with 8,547 manually annotated and reviewed reaction records, evaluated under a common protocol for exact molecular identity and reactant-product pairing. On this benchmark, with Qwen3.8-Flash-Next as the backbone, RxnMiner raises stereochemistry-aware reaction macro-F1 from 21.0% under direct prediction to 71.0%, compared with 25.0% for the strongest existing extraction system. Across five backbones, it also outperforms the same base agent without the extraction procedure and chemistry toolbox by 9.4 to 25.5 points. Applied without human intervention to 10,107 literature figures, RxnMiner produces a silver corpus of 88,202 source-linked reaction records.
  </details>

- **[AffToM: Benchmarking Recursive Affective Theory of Mind in Dynamic Multi-Party Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02725)**  `arXiv:2611.02725`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinpeng Hu, Yiduo Jin, Erqiang Wang, Jia Li, Jingjing Wu, Peipei Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding emotion in realistic social interactions requires more than recognizing facial expressions or assigning affective labels. The same event can elicit different emotions depending on what each participant knows, what they believe others know or feel, and how these nested mental states evolve over time. Existing benchmarks largely study higher-order epistemic Theory of Mind (ToM) and multimodal emotion reasoning in isolation, leaving recursive affective reasoning under asymmetric and dynamically changing information underexplored. Therefore, we introduce AffToM, a benchmark for affective ToM with 1,922 test pairs and 19,759 training pairs. AffToM evaluates Individual Affective Reasoning (IAR), which infers emotions from events and socio-cognitive context, and Multi-party Affective Reasoning (MPAR), which recursively tracks participants' nested beliefs and emotions as new evidence emerges. We develop an event-driven multi-agent generation pipeline grounded in dynamic character interaction graphs that explicitly capture event sequences, interpersonal relations, and mental-state updates. Furthermore, we propose AffToM-R1, a two-stage post-training framework combining supervised fine-tuning with perspective-aware rubric-guided reinforcement learning. Experiments show that MPAR remains challenging, with even the strongest evaluated MLLM achieving less than 60% accuracy, while AffToM-R1 improves MPAR accuracy by 13.38 percentage points over its base model. Our dataset is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Answers, Traces, and Efficiency: Evidence for Adaptive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02704)**  `arXiv:2611.02704`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sejin Kim, Sundong Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current AI systems are evaluated as reasoners through final-answer accuracy, reasoning traces, process supervision, and computational or human-relative efficiency. Yet these measures do not establish whether reasoning adapts when tasks or evidence change. We distinguish across-task adaptivity, where acquired abstractions transfer or recombine across novel problems, fromwithin-task adaptivity, where hypotheses and actions are revised in response to new observations, feedback, or counterexamples. Using ARC-AGI as a diagnostic case study rather than an alignment benchmark, we examine what existing measures leave unresolved when test-time search, sampling, or adaptation affects performance. We propose intervention-oriented evaluation that tests abstraction transfer and hypothesis revision under controlled changes to tasks and evidence, with explicit reporting of test-time procedures and budgets. This perspective supports evaluating systems for robustness, responsiveness to corrective feedback, and auditability under changing conditions.
  </details>

- **[InterFuse: Interaction-Guided Adaptive Fusion of Heterogeneous Models for Payment Risk Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02640)**  `arXiv:2611.02640`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoyu Jiang, Weiyu Han, Ze Zhao, Jie Sheng, Ruobing Zuo, Xiao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Payment platforms use view-specific models trained on transaction attributes, behavioral histories, and relational structures to rank transactions under limited review budgets. However, existing fusion methods often focus on prediction scores or interactions among model representations, with less attention to how these interactions should guide aggregation using each model's original prediction and input coverage. To address this limitation, we propose InterFuse, a fusion framework that uses a shared fusion state from cross-model interaction to guide transaction-adaptive aggregation. InterFuse encodes each model's representation, prediction score, and input coverage as a model-specific token, using learned states for unavailable outputs. Availability-aware attention updates these tokens and produces a shared fusion state. This state then guides aggregation over available models using their updated representations, original predictions, and input coverage. We evaluate InterFuse on an industrial payment replay and three public financial benchmarks using the same upstream models across fusion methods. InterFuse achieves the highest top-ranked recall and AUC on all four datasets, improving Recall@1% by 1.11-8.09 percentage points over the strongest baselines. Ablations show the benefits of cross-model interaction and adaptive aggregation, while industrial analyses find larger interaction gains when model predictions disagree. Additional analyses examine robustness to missing outputs and the trade-off between ranking gains and fusion latency.
  </details>

- **[Measuring AI Agents as Teammates with Collaborative Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02633)**  `arXiv:2611.02633`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sean Wu, Aneesh Pappu, Jiaru Zou, Yubin Kim, Yuncong Mao, Hongjian Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in AI is often measured by what a single model can do on its own. Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100.
