Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. The benchmark includes 160 public tasks with code and data, along with 80 held-out tasks for evaluation. As AI agents increasingly work alongside other agents and people, the ability to collaborate effectively is becoming a crucial capability to measure and develop.
  </details>

- **[EEFA: A Framework for the Ethical Evaluation of AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02561)**  `arXiv:2611.02561`  `cs.AI`  
  _Swathi Pallikala, Yashas Hariprasad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid deployment of autonomous AI agents across consequential domains including healthcare, legal services, nancial management, and enterprise workows has created an urgent governance challenge. While the eld of AI evaluation has produced sophisticated benchmarks for measuring agent capability, these frameworks measure exclusively what agents can do rather than how they do it. The ethical dimensions most critical to responsible agent deployment, in- cluding respect for human autonomy, harm avoidance, transparency and non-deception, privacy compliance, and accountability, are systematically absent from current evaluation practice. This paper addresses that gap through three contributions. First, a structured literature review of twelve leading AI agent benchmarks characterizes the ethical dimensions those bench- marks do and do not measure. Second, the Ethical Evaluation Framework for AI Agents (EEFA) is introduced as a comprehensive, operationalizable instrument for ethical agent evaluation, grounded in established AI ethics principles and designed for use by developers, auditors, and regulators. Third, a reproducible validation protocol is specied for applying the EEFA to deployed agent systems, with illustrative test scenarios across all ve framework dimensions. The EEFA is organized across ve primary dimensions: Autonomy Preservation, Harm Avoid- ance, Transparency and Non-Deception, Privacy Compliance, and Accountability and Traceabil- ity. The framework maps directly onto the requirements of the EU AI Act and the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, positioning it as a practical compliance assessment instrument for high-risk AI applications. Empirical application of the framework across diverse benchmarks and agent systems is identied as a priority direction for future work.
  </details>

- **[FORGE: Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02536)**  `arXiv:2611.02536`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fuqiang Wang, Jiahao Fu, Ren Hefeng, Yining Qian, Chuanting Zhang, Cheng Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have demonstrated strong capabilities in structured reasoning and code generation, yet identifying an assembly fault does not guarantee the ability to generate executable rework programs. In interlocking assemblies, a locally plausible repair program may violate blocking dependencies, omit required recovery operations, or terminate in an incorrect configuration. We identify this challenge as fault-conditioned executable rework generation, where models must jointly reason about structural failures and generate programs that restore constrained assembly states. To benchmark this capability, we introduce FORGE-Bench, containing 4,000 tasks across 26 interlocking assemblies with five assemblies held out for evaluating generalization to unseen structures. We further propose FORGE (Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly), a structured state-to-program framework that decomposes rework generation into Rework Structuring and Action Grounding. Rework Structuring captures fault-induced recovery dependencies, while Action Grounding maps these dependencies into state-conditioned executable actions. Experiments show that locally reasonable repair code is insufficient for reliable assembly recovery, and that connecting structural recovery reasoning with executable action realization is essential for verified target restoration.
  </details>

- **[Cumulative and Audited Guardrail Tool Evolution for LLM Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02516)**  `arXiv:2611.02516`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiao Yang, Aishan Liu, Siyuan Liang, Zonghao Ying, Le Wang, Jiakai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in open-world environments where safety risks cannot be fully predefined. However, most existing guardrail mechanisms remain static, relying on fixed rules, prompts, or classifiers that generalize poorly to emerging risks. Here we present EVOLVE, a tool-level self-evolving defense framework that formulates agent security as a process of cumulative security evolution. Inspired by how human societies transform experiences into transferable protective knowledge, EVOLVE externalizes security knowledge into executable guardrail tools that are continuously inherited, independently audited, and iteratively refined through lifelong interaction. Across multiple benchmarks, backbone models, and embodied environments, EVOLVE consistently outperforms existing defenses across diverse risk scenarios with +22\% average F1 score. We further validate the framework in real-world robotic and autonomous agent systems. Our results suggest that scalable agent safety may require continuously evolving defense systems rather than static guardrails.
  </details>

- **[Towards Multi-Value Alignment via Value Localization in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02503)**  `arXiv:2611.02503`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xueqi Ma, Yanbei Jiang, Xingjun Ma, James Bailey, Sarah M. Erfani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning large language models (LLMs) with multiple, potentially conflicting human values remains challenging, as existing multi-objective alignment methods often require costly fine-tuning and provide limited insight into the internal mechanisms underlying value-related behaviors. In this work, we investigate multi-value alignment from a mechanistic interpretability perspective by identifying value-sensitive attention heads (value heads) in LLMs. Using a probing-based value localization framework, we discover sparse sets of attention heads associated with diverse dimensions, spanning both human values (e.g., harmlessness, honesty) and stylistic preferences (e.g., coherence, complexity). Analyses across multiple LLM families reveal functional specialization and structured interactions among value heads, including conflicts between certain values. Targeted head ablations further induce substantial, value-specific behavioral changes, providing causal evidence of their functional importance. Building on these findings, we propose an inference-time activation steering strategy that enables controllable alignment with individual values or combinations of values without updating the underlying model parameters, supported by theoretical analysis. Experiments across multiple value alignment benchmarks demonstrate effective single- and multi-value control while largely preserving non-target model performance. Our findings connect mechanistic interpretability with flexible multi-value alignment, offering a foundation for more interpretable and pluralistic LLM behavior. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmark of THReD: Evaluating an LLM Agent and Its Preprocessing Tools for Turning Electronic Health Records into AI-Ready Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02484)**  `arXiv:2611.02484`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javad Mohammad Alizadeh, Huanmei Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly built into data-analysis software, yet little evidence shows how reliably they preprocess clinical data or whether the tools they invoke give correct, reproducible output. We report a benchmark of THReD, a web-based system that turns electronic health record (EHR) extracts into AI-ready datasets through a chat agent whose proposed steps run only after user confirmation. The agent benchmark has 141 tasks (100 templates) in seven categories, including goal-level requests, failure recovery, requests calling for no action, schema variation and prompt injection through the data, on synthetic tables calibrated to a regional health information exchange; success is judged by checks on the produced data. Over 2,550 episodes with Claude Haiku 5.5 in four context configurations, the deployed agent completed 92.7% (95% CI 88.1-96.4) of tasks on its first answer, abstained on every advice request, recovered from every injected fault, and never proposed an action requested by planted text. Without the data profile, completion fell to 58.0%; goal-level requests were hardest (80.6%). Six experiments on real records of two chronic-disease cohorts and on synthetic data at scale validate the tools: 91-92% of inferred patient classes and 99.7-99.8% of inferred vital-sign labels were correct in masked reconstruction; a dictionary builder combining curated dictionaries with LLM suggestions mapped 99.0% of 2,208 raw variants correctly; all 2,176 replays of 128 saved pipelines gave byte-identical files under pinned dependencies; subgroup differences in inference accuracy vanished within facility and lookup key; and the same-day consolidation rule changed 90-day emergency revisit rates by up to 3.2 points without shifting cohort composition by more than one point. Processing time grew roughly linearly to 5.5 million records while the prompt stayed at about 13 thousand tokens.
  </details>

- **[MAGAR: Multimodal Agents for GUI-Based 3D Mechanical Assembly and Minimal Rework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02472)**  `arXiv:2611.02472`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingxuan Wei, Jiahao Fu, Fuqiang Wang, Xiaoming Wu, Yuwen Liu, Kaiwen Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A GUI command can be correct at the interface level yet still produce an incorrect assembly. An agent must identify the relevant components, respect their structural relationships, and determine which parts should move during recovery. We introduce MAGAR-Bench, a benchmark of 4,000 multimodal samples from 26 assembly families that separates Assembly Decision, Atomic Action, and Minimal Rework. For rework, the objective counts distinct moved parts within the validated recovery candidate set for each state, rather than the number of actions. We also introduce MAGAR, a selective correction framework built on a frozen policy obtained by supervised fine-tuning. Its key design matches correction granularity to output dependencies. Structured candidates revise assembly fields and rework scope, while complete action candidates keep native GUI primitives coupled with their parameters. A learned selector retains the original prediction unless a candidate passes a task-specific intervention threshold. On recorded states from held-out assembly families, MAGAR improves Overall, the task-balanced aggregate, from 37.50 to 41.39, with gains on all three tasks. Across 209 interventions, 70 correct an error and 22 introduce one. These results support task-specific correction while highlighting the remaining tradeoff between fixing errors and preserving reliable predictions.
  </details>

- **[Data-Scarce, Multi-Granular, Multimodal: Benchmark Design for Biosynthetic Gene Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02275)**  `arXiv:2611.02275`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hanlin Xiao, Eriko Takano, Mauricio A. Álvarez, Rainer Breitling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biosynthetic gene clusters (BGCs) are co-located genes that encode the biosynthesis of specialized metabolites, an important source of antibiotics, anticancer agents, immunosuppressants, and agricultural bioactives. Deep learning, particularly self-supervised foundation models, has spurred interest in BGC sequence modeling, but evaluation has lagged behind: existing benchmarks often rely on coarse classification over small curated datasets, providing limited discrimination among models and insight into downstream utility. We propose a benchmark-design framework in which an informative benchmark should be discriminative, separating models with statistical confidence, and applicable, spanning scientifically meaningful tasks under different levels of generalization. We instantiate this framework as BGC-Bench, which derives five tasks from MIBiG, ranging from A-domain substrate prediction to BGC-molecule retrieval, evaluates them under genotype- and phenotype-shift splits, includes an active-learning setting for label-scarce discovery, and is easily extensible to new tasks and encoders. BGCs further provide a shared testbed across granularities and modalities: BGC-Bench compares twelve encoders on nucleotide, amino-acid, or Pfam-domain sequences, including compositional baselines for each input type, and combines BGC and chemical encoders for cross-modal retrieval. No single representation dominates: the best granularity depends on the biological scale of the task, and pretrained encoders do not consistently outperform other representations across tasks and splits. Active-learning experiments show that most tasks remain data-limited, encoder rankings change with labeling budget, and acquisition strategies yield modest gains. These results show that BGC model evaluation requires task-diverse, shift-aware benchmarks, and the framework can be generalized to benchmark design beyond BGCs.
  </details>

- **[Decoupled Predictive Decision Making: A Shared Conditional Predictor for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02075)**  `arXiv:2611.02075`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lang Mo, Xingxuan Zhang, Qi Wang, Mingchao Hao, Hao Yuan, Chunjing Gan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent decisions depend on empirical relationships that deployment observations reveal and task descriptions alone may leave unresolved. We introduce Decoupled Predictive Decision Making (DPDM), an architecture that formulates heterogeneous agent decisions as conditional prediction with a shared pretrained learner. Language models provide semantic representations of current and historical records, while a general-purpose conditional predictor predicts task-defined targets from the represented evidence. Pretraining supplies reusable inference capability; deployment evidence instantiates problem-specific predictions without updating either model's neural parameters. Tool judgments, candidate preferences, and reasoning assessments share an evidence-and-query interface, with predictions mapped to choices by a decision rule. This architecture establishes complementary sources of agent capability: semantic knowledge acquired through language pretraining and empirical inference acquired through structured-data pretraining. A single frozen LimiX-2 predictor serves all five task categories. With Qwen3.5-27B as the semantic encoder, DPDM raises mean accuracy across 50 benchmarks from 78.80% with few-shot prompting to 84.92% under their respective evaluated history budgets, and reaches 82.33% with identical histories and queries. On ToolPRMBench's BFCL and ToolSandbox tasks, absolute accuracy gains are 34.26% and 28.87%, respectively, at the methods' respective evaluated history budgets. Accuracy improves as history grows. With identical imbalanced histories, mean balanced accuracy improves by an absolute 14.51% across five reasoning-step assessment tasks and four encoders. DPDM makes learning from experience an independently improvable foundation-model capability, creating a shared route for advances in conditional prediction to improve decisions across agent tasks.
  </details>

- **[Safety4DM: A Vision-Language Framework for Risk-Aware Dynamic Maps in Vehicle-Road-Cloud Collaborative Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01996)**  `arXiv:2611.01996`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Lu Tao, Xinhui Hu, Jiaqi Gu, Tingting Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic map (DM) is a core information infrastructure for vehicle-road-cloud (VRC) cooperative autonomous driving. It fuses perception data from multiple sources to overcome the inherent line-of-sight and field-of-view limits of single-vehicle perception. However, DM still falls short on driving safety in two ways. First, DM data specifications already define road conditions semantically, but no pipeline yet turns real-world road observations into fine-grained risk entities in the DM. Second, existing DM systems lack a natural language interface for driving risks. To close these gaps, we propose Safety4DM, a safety-oriented language system for VRC dynamic maps. We first build RoadRisk, a real-world road image dataset that covers four types of road conditions and four risk levels. On this dataset, we fine-tune a vision-language model (VLM) for road safety risk perception. The model outputs structured risk representations that the DM can ingest directly. We then introduce a Safety-aware Step-wise Prompting (SafeStep) mechanism as the natural language query interface of the DM. SafeStep answers queries accurately and also generates actionable driving advice. To build and evaluate this interface, we develop SafeVRC-Sim, a VRC cooperative simulation framework, and SafeDM-QA, a safety question-answering benchmark. Experiments show that even the compact Qwen3-VL-4B reaches 89.83% accuracy on risk perception after fine-tuning. SafeStep also plugs easily into multiple LLM families. With medium and large models (20B and above) from five families, it achieves over 93% natural language query accuracy in every case, which shows strong generalization.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Multimodal Emotion Conflict: Temporal Context-Aware Hidden Emotion Detection with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01943)**  `arXiv:2611.01943`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yonghao Wang, Jiaxin Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal emotion recognition commonly combines textual, acoustic, and visual signals to infer affective states. Recent work has begun to study conflicts among modalities, but a conflict does not necessarily indicate that a person is concealing an emotion. It may also result from mixed emotions, individual expressive styles, or contextual factors. This proposal aims to distinguish ordinary emotional inconsistency from emotion concealment and to infer the underlying emotion when supported by evidence. We plan to construct a multimodal benchmark with explicit annotations of expressed emotion, internal emotion, and concealment conditions, and to develop an MLLM-based framework that integrates fine-grained facial Action Units (AUs), cross-modal features, and temporal context.
  </details>

- **[Is Your Long-Horizon Benchmark Really Deep? Measuring and Synthesizing Depth in Terminal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01927)**  `arXiv:2611.01927`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Jiang, Yue Huang, Hang Hua, Yuyang Wu, Zixuan Weng, Olexandr Isayev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon terminal tasks are used to evaluate and train agents. Human-curated datasets are scarce, so many synthesis methods have emerged. They usually measure the length of a task by the turns a model needs to pass it, and its difficulty by labels the LLM sets during generation. We find, however, that the turn count largely reflects the habits of the model, not only the demands of the task, and that the labels barely track how often models pass. We describe a terminal task by its work, the number of operations it requires, and its depth, the number of turns that must happen in sequence because the commands of each turn can only be chosen after reading the output of the previous one. Estimating depth as the smallest turn cap, stated to the agent, under which the task passes, we audit 734 tasks from six existing datasets. Synthesis has mostly added work, not depth: 94% of the solved synthetic tasks pass within three turns, yet depth, unlike the turn count, predicts difficulty beyond work. We propose DEPTH, in which a generation agent writes each task one segment at a time in a real software environment, carrying out each segment before writing it and starting the next from the state it leaves. Our 105 tasks exceed every audited dataset in depth and work, and none of six current models passes half of them. Fine-tuning Qwen3-8B on runs from these tasks improves it on two terminal benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Delivered, Not Used: Evaluating Agent Memory at Its Point of Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01718)**  `arXiv:2611.01718`  `cs.AI`  
  _Changran Xu, Zeju Li, Qiang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task success, retrieval quality, and cost do not show whether an LLM agent used a delivered memory or what followed from that use. We introduce MemUse, which evaluates memory at its point of use in recorded trajectories: each memory delivery is compared with what the agent could already see and followed through the agent's later actions and their consequences. An agentic judge rates each delivered entry's relevance, accuracy, novelty, influence, and observed effect; programmatic checks enforce complete reading of cited evidence, and fixed rules aggregate the ratings into verdicts linked to memory histories and delivery cost. On 8,000 SWE-bench Verified episodes from three memory systems, resolve rates stay within 0.4 percentage points of a no-memory baseline, yet MemUse shows that most delivered entries were irrelevant, most relevant entries went unused, and in two systems most deliveries restated content the agent could still see. It also surfaces effects that outcomes hide: 23.2\% of episodes with beneficial memory use still failed, and 5 of 16 with harmful use still succeeded.
  </details>

- **[Just-in-Time Skill Retrieval via Long-Horizon State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01701)**  `arXiv:2611.01701`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xin Lin, Ziheng Wang, Zerui Chen, Jiayao Ma, Zhimin Wei, Bingshuai Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external skill libraries to support complex task execution. However, determining which skills are needed remains challenging in long-horizon tasks. Existing methods typically select a fixed skill set before execution, implicitly assuming that a task's procedural requirements can be determined from its initial description. This assumption is restrictive for long-horizon interaction, where new requirements emerge from intermediate observations, execution outcomes, and newly revealed constraints. This motivates just-in-time skill retrieval, which dynamically selects skills as execution unfolds. Just-in-time skill retrieval is promising but challenging: the utility of a skill depends not only on the current context, but also on the evolving task state shaped by prior steps. To this end, we propose JitSkill, a state-grounded framework that addresses this challenge through continuous task-state tracking. By connecting emerging requirements with accumulated task progress, JitSkill provides procedural support throughout execution without treating each retrieval decision independently. Experiments across three agent benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness in improving long-horizon task completion. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MemHarmBench: Evaluating Cross-Session Prompt Injection in Memory-Persistent AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01700)**  `arXiv:2611.01700`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhihua Wang, Yunhao Feng, Yifeng Gao, Rui Pan, Baiheng Zhao, Zixing Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents often rely on persistent memory to retain user preferences and operational rules across sessions. However, this capability introduces a new safety risk: malicious instructions from messages, documents, or Web content can be retained in memory and influence future tasks even after the original attack content is removed. Existing attack success metrics, however, fail to capture who is affected and how attacks translate into downstream harm during otherwise normal task execution. We introduce MemHarmBench, a victim-centric benchmark for evaluating cross-session prompt injection in memory-persistent AI agents. MemHarmBench considers three categories of potential victims: users, workspace/project owners, and external stakeholders. Our evaluation first exposes an agent to malicious instructions and tests whether they are retained in memory. We then remove the attack content and initiate a new session with a legitimate task while preserving the agent's memory. We jointly evaluate whether the agent follows the attacker's intent, completes the legitimate task, and exhibits observable execution anomalies. Across multiple agent systems, we find that cross-session attacks can remain behaviorally stealthy: agents often complete tasks normally and exhibit no detectable anomalies while still making critical decisions aligned with the attacker's intent. By connecting malicious memory retention to downstream decisions and affected parties, MemHarmBench provides a systematic framework for exposing safety risks that conventional attack- and task-success metrics may overlook. We publicly release MemHarmBench atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[\textsc{ChatDify}: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01563)**  `arXiv:2611.01563`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration. However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.
  </details>

- **[CivicBench: A Benchmark and Memory Framework for Web-Based Civic Data Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01145)**  `arXiv:2611.01145`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yangyu Wu, Siru Zhong, Haoyuan Liang, Yuxuan Liang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Municipalities publish service requests, geographic boundaries, weather observations, demographic estimates, and operational guidance through independently maintained Web portals. Civic analysis must combine these sources while preserving their schemas, source links, spatial and temporal scopes, and interpretive limits. Tool-using language agents can perform this integration, but existing task benchmarks rarely test whether a computed claim remains supported by the records used. We present CivicBench, a 400-task benchmark over four cities and two years that materializes civic Web data into frozen bundles for reproducible evaluation. Executable contracts score computation, source linkage, coverage, scope, qualified answers, and refusal when the requested conclusion is not supported. We also introduce Task-State Memory (TSM), which compiles tool observations into typed assertions, reconciles records only within compatible scopes, and retrieves a bounded state organized around unresolved constraints. Across nine completed model pairs, eight yield positive mean-score differences with 95\% semantic-contract-cluster bootstrap intervals excluding zero; the ninth interval crosses zero. The benchmark and memory framework make the records behind a claim inspectable at the point of decision.
  </details>

- **[Compile as Hypothesis: A Headroom Typology for Prompt Optimization on Real Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01135)**  `arXiv:2611.01135`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshuai Yang, Changyun Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated prompt compilers search for or rewrite prompts under the implicit assumption that optimization consistently improves downstream task performance. We evaluate this assumption on frontier reasoning models through rigorous paired, same-day measurements. Across four optimizer families (genome search, profile-guided mutation, GEPA, and ESPO) and eight tasks, structured search yields no statistically significant accuracy gains on strong reasoners. However, a discrete structural genome provides essential architectural stability, preventing catastrophic bimodal seed-collapse (where unconstrained search collapses toward the judge floor in 3/5 runs). Through 190-problem evaluations on knowledge-intensive benchmarks, we establish a three-dimensional headroom typology: prompt optimization cannot compensate for intrinsic knowledge deficits or physical token truncation, but successfully resolves protocol and formatting errors. On gpt-6, while free-text reflective search (GEPA) yields a +.14 gain on a task where structured search remains flat, qualitative ablation reveals that ~.035 stems from judge-rubric reconstruction while ~.105 reflects genuine instruction refinement. Our pre-registered AutoAPC-Select deployment gate matches the oracle policy in 5/7 groups and rescues seed-collapse arms with zero regret. Finally, we expose silent backend-mixing in commercial APIs during outage windows and provide automated per-call audit verification tools to ensure empirical evaluation fidelity.
  </details>

- **[HiGenTrans: Hierarchical LLM Knowledge Transfer for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01129)**  `arXiv:2611.01129`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuohang Jiang, Yuxin Chen, Haohao Qu, ZeFei luo, Yunlong Wang, Xu Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative recommendation promises to simplify conventional multi-stage recommender systems by generating item Semantic IDs (SIDs) autoregressively. However, two obstacles limit its effective- ness in industrial settings: interaction-only supervision underuses the semantic knowledge encoded in pretrained language models, while beam-search likelihood ranks token sequences rather than directly estimating item relevance. To address these limitations, we propose HiGenTrans, a three-level framework that introduces LLM knowledge at the input, representation, and decision levels. First, Global Semantic Priming (GSP) adds an LLM-derived user representation to the beginning-of-sequence embedding, provid- ing a semantic prior before SID generation. Second, Hierarchi- cal Representation Transfer (HRT) aligns intermediate LLM representations with depth-matched GenRec layers through mean- centered projections and gated residual connections. Finally, Multi- Signal Decision Calibration (MDC) uses a lightweight mixture- of-experts calibrator to combine semantic relevance, generation confidence, and candidate-set context when reranking beam-search outputs. All semantic features are computed only from the observ- able interaction prefix, preventing target leakage, and can be cached offline. Experiments on three public benchmarks show that HiGen- Trans improves Recall@10 by up to 12.9% and NDCG@10 by up to 33.7% over the strongest baselines. These results demonstrate the value of transferring LLM knowledge at complementary stages of the generative recommendation pipeline.
  </details>

- **[Constraint Dependency Graph Retrieval for Compliance Detection on Long SKILL Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01042)**  `arXiv:2611.01042`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuaitao Zhao, Feng Ni, Lichao Ma, Yuchen Wei, Shuaike Li, Jiaye Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents carry out business operations under enterprise documents we refer to as SKILL documents, which take the form of standard operating procedures (SOPs) that state the conditions to verify and the actions to take before an operation proceeds. Long, densely cross-referenced SKILL documents exceed the context budget of small deployed auditors. Similarity-based retrieval then brings in irrelevant rules while omitting mandatory prerequisites, and the two-stage audit policy provides no trainable signal for the retrieval stage. We present SkillCDG, a compliance detection framework that reformulates each SKILL document as a constraint dependency graph, shifting rule retrieval from similarity-based matching to closure-based selection under the deployment's context budget. Each atomic rule is anchored to its source text span as a graph node, and mandatory prerequisite relations form the sole edge type. Policy-generated retrieval keywords activate seed rules, whose dependency closure yields a compact and conditionally complete rule context within that budget. The same graph also supports a joint optimization scheme. The retrieval stage is trained with group-relative policy optimization using a verifiable reward that combines output-format validity with downstream verdict correctness, with closure non-emptiness serving as a validity gate, while selective on-policy distillation concentrates teacher supervision on high-entropy reasoning tokens, with verdict and termination tokens always included, to improve verdict accuracy. Experiments on three enterprise datasets and two public benchmarks show that SkillCDG improves accuracy and F1 over the full-document and retrieval-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[IJev: Order-Invariant Decisions via Iterated Cyclic Reordering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01015)**  `arXiv:2611.01015`  `cs.AI`  
  _Youzhi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One decision models answer a choice question in one forward pass, but their answer depends on the order in which options are listed, and their probabilities carry a prior over answer labels. An invariant ordering operator is any fixed function that returns the same ordered option list for every input order of a fixed option set. Starting from any such order and averaging a listwise readout over its K cyclic shifts removes the input order, but the decision still depends on the chosen cyclic class: those $K$ shifts are only one of the (K-1)! classes, and different invariant operators can select different classes. We propose IJev, which repeatedly reorders the options by the cyclic-averaged, label-prior-corrected distribution and runs another cyclic average. Every iterate is exactly input-order-invariant. When the iteration does not converge, we average the round distributions rather than return the last round. On five benchmarks and five models, IJev has flip rate zero in every cell. The default uniform average over T=20 rounds substantially improves calibration over a single cyclic average, and its accuracy is comparable. A descriptive choice of horizon and averaging rule is best or tied-best for accuracy in all 25 settings.
  </details>

- **[Pulse: Long-Horizon Tasks Need Streaming Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00988)**  `arXiv:2611.00988`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Jialiang Liang, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly undertake complex, long-horizon tasks that benefit from sustained personalized support through long-term memory. Existing memory-augmented methods retrieve and use memory primarily from the initial request, with limited awareness of ongoing execution and emerging personalization needs. Agents may therefore overlook user preferences and project constraints during multistep tasks. We propose Pulse, a streaming personalization method for long-horizon tasks. An independent, lightweight memory agent continuously monitors execution, identifies emerging personalization needs and potential preference conflicts, and uses user and project memories to provide targeted interventions at appropriate stages. Reinforcement learning optimizes when and how the memory agent intervenes, improving the timeliness and accuracy of personalized support. Pulse scores 71.9% on $\pi$-Bench and 46.4% on MCP-Persona, achieving the highest overall scores among the compared methods on both datasets while improving task execution efficiency.
  </details>

- **[AI Data Analysis: A New Approach - Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00807)**  `arXiv:2611.00807`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jean Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI data analysis today is three fragmented worlds: LLMs understand semantics but cannot compute; BI aggregates precisely but is stateless; ML processes large-scale data on a stateful kernel but lacks semantics. This paper proposes a combination mechanism, named Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis, that fills a still-open gap - the multi-engine joint context: the data context is embedded into LLM conversational analysis, where the LLM serves only as the semantic, reasoning, and orchestration layer (not computation) and connects four engines through the 1M context window - Python and R ML kernels, SQL over relational databases, Cypher over graph databases - yielding two multiplications, "semantics and precise computation" and "relational and graph", under enterprise-grade governance. The novelty is a four-dimensional coordinate system: horizontal (engine kinds), vertical (state flow - the watershed), spatial (business generalization), temporal (iterative convergence); a deterministic grounding method (one-shot injection ofthis http URL+this http URL, M+S) carries the spatial and temporal dimensions. Empirically, all four dimensions are substantiated: horizontally, four engines are quantified - SQL, Cypher (96.5% on CypherBench movie), ML in Python and R (TabReD weather); vertically, state flows across engines and grounding is reused across kernels; spatially, SQL generalizes across three business databases - AdventureWorks 91.3%, financial 95.3%, formula_1 94.6% (average), about 20-23 points above single-shot SOTA 74.45%; temporally, accuracy converges monotonically as notes accumulate. The core invariant - the separation of semantics from computation plus a cross-data-domain persistent context - does not expire as LLMs and agents evolve or engine forms change.
  </details>

- **[BridgeDock: Taming Generative Co-Folding Prior for Molecular Docking with Diffusion Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00795)**  `arXiv:2611.00795`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shikun Feng, Zipeng Yang, Qiuyi Li, Mingze Yin, Xiaoyuan Zhang, Tingjun Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular docking aims to predict the three-dimensional structure of a protein-ligand complex; in the conventional structure-based setting, it is conditioned on an input protein structure and ligand, often starting from an apo receptor in flexible docking or a holo receptor in rigid docking. Recent co-folding models exemplified by AlphaFold 3 achieve strong accuracy in protein-ligand complex prediction, but they typically generate complexes from sequence-derived inputs and therefore do not directly use apo structures as structural priors in docking, leading to a larger conformational search space and weaker robustness in difficult or few-step settings. We present BridgeDock, a framework that adapts pretrained co-folding backbones to flexible docking by modeling the apo-to-holo transition as a diffusion bridge, allowing generation from the initial protein-ligand state rather than from Gaussian noise. To make this bridge formulation compatible with pretrained denoisers, we introduce an alignment mechanism that maps bridge states to the original denoising schedule of the pretrained model. Experiments on standard flexible docking benchmarks show that BridgeDock consistently outperforms strong co-folding baselines. Moreover, BridgeDock is computationally efficient at inference time and remains effective even with very few denoising steps.
  </details>

- **[One Offset, Two Directions: Baseline Adjustment via Subgroup Offsets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00782)**  `arXiv:2611.00782`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaying Zhou, Jiachen Liu, Qingchao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) estimates advantages against the within-prompt mean reward, a baseline shared across the group. In practice, rollouts within a prompt are not always homogeneous, differing in their solution strategies and reasoning trajectories, along which they may group into subgroups. We further find that these heterogeneous subgroups may exhibit distinct reward distributions. The global baseline, however, collapses them onto a single credit axis, where the credit reflects neither a rollout's standing within its subgroup nor the subgroup's standing relative to the rest. We therefore study how to adjust the baseline when the rollouts of a prompt fall into heterogeneous subgroups. We define the \emph{subgroup offset} as the deviation of a subgroup mean from the global mean, and shift the baseline along it by a single signed coefficient $\lambda$. The sign of $\lambda$ yields two opposing directions: \emph{Alignment} ($\lambda{>}0$) adds the offset to the baseline, drawing credit toward a rollout's relative position within its subgroup; \emph{Contrast} ($\lambda{<}0$) subtracts it, drawing a rollout's credit toward the relative quality of the subgroup it belongs to. The adjustment acts on the baseline alone, leaving the reward signal and the optimization objective unchanged. In this work, we induce subgroups from strategies, grouping rollouts that share a strategy into the same subgroup. We evaluate both directions on Qwen3-8B-Base and Olmo3-7B-Base across four competition-level math benchmarks, against a $\lambda{=}0$ anchor that shares the same strategy-conditioned sampling and differs only in the baseline. Against this anchor each direction yields modest but consistent gains in multi-sample coverage, with Contrast the stronger of the two.
  </details>

- **[Decision-Centric Research: A Calibrated Decision Model and Benchmark for Scientific Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00774)**  `arXiv:2611.00774`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shaoning Zeng, Wen Huang, Xinzhong Zhu, Yi Duan, Jianhang Zhou, Jianping Gou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific agents make consequential choices about evidence, next actions and research strategy, but choices embedded in generated text are difficult to evaluate independently. Decision-Centric Research (DCR) is introduced to make individual choices the common unit of supervision, prediction and evaluation through typed predictions over an explicit Research State. DCR defines 24 decision types, ten of which are instantiated in the Research Decision Benchmark (RD-Bench) of 2,000 rule-generated cases. The Decision-Centric Research Model (DCRM) shares a candidate-token readout across types to produce answers and option probabilities in one forward pass. Under a held-out research-domain split, a 0.6B DCRM (state-aware) achieves 89.4% Top-1 accuracy, compared with 27.8% for DCRM (question-only) trained with the same recipe and 36.4% for DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. All 30 type-seed comparisons against DCRM (question-only) are significant after Holm correction. After held-out calibration, the resulting prediction sets contain a mean of 1.515 candidates at 97.1% empirical label coverage, reducing mean set size by 57.4% relative to DCRM (question-only) at similar coverage. These results establish a compact state-aware decision layer with accurate typed prediction and an inspectable uncertainty interface.
  </details>

- **[FastAgent: A Physics-Aware FastFluent-to-Fluent Workflow for Rapid CFD Screening and Industrial Steam Dewaxing Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00645)**  `arXiv:2611.00645`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chen YanKai, Yu QiuHeng, Yuan Man, Zou Ting, Wu Lin, He Jian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial computational fluid dynamics (CFD) becomes costly when complex geometries, long transients and coupled physics must be evaluated across large design spaces. We introduce FastAgent, a physics-aware campaign controller built around FastFluent, a lightweight numerical engine for rapid physical screening. FastFluent supplies stage-specific fields and diagnostics; FastAgent allocates physical models, numerical resolution and simulation horizons by the active quantities of interest (QoIs), while returned Fluent discrepancies update subsequent decisions. Across six public CFD benchmark campaigns, the framework reduces normalised worst-QoI error by 5.68-99.14% and accelerates decision-relevant computation by factors of 1.53-15.36 relative to a matched Generic Agent control. Applied to high-pressure steam dewaxing in investment casting, FastAgent identifies nonuniform condensation heating as the dominant startup mechanism and separates rapid risk-window localisation from high-fidelity melt-completion confirmation. The rapid route requires 81.39% fewer cell-time updates than a fine-grid, small-time-step reference. Structural analysis localises fracture at the observed blade edge. Screening 131,072 outlet layouts and selectively evaluating candidates with Fluent and Abaqus identifies Sparse-6, which reduces simulated crack opening by 96.0% and connected crack extent by 66.7% relative to the Original design. These results demonstrate how rapid numerical evidence and adaptive fidelity control connect large CFD search spaces to mechanism-guided industrial design.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-grounded large language models guide perovskite solar cell design and optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00633)**  `arXiv:2611.00633`  `cs.AI`  
  _YanKai Chen, Zhi Wan, ZiHao Li, Tao Jing_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using large language models to interpret experimental evidence, design discriminating experiments and formulate well-supported mechanistic explanations remains a central challenge in materials research. Here we establish PSM-Bench, an 800-question benchmark developed by perovskite researchers to evaluate scientific reasoning in perovskite photovoltaics. We also introduce OpenPerov, an expert-informed language-model framework that learns from expert-calibrated evidence records reconstructed from 4,532 articles; these records link experimental strategies, controls and observations to mechanistic interpretations. Structured-evidence adaptation outperformed conventional question-answer training, and OpenPerov Flash ranked first among 13 closed-book systems. OpenPerov Pro raised Scientific Mastery further, from 87.6 to 89.1, by revising selected passages with retrieved evidence. On questions derived from subsequent studies, both models also outscored expert-authored answers. In an experimental test of self-assembled monolayer design, OpenPerov selected a K analogue that outperformed the Na analogue preferred by experts. Devices incorporating the selected contact reached a power conversion efficiency of 27.05% and retained 96.74% of their steady-state reference efficiency after 1,000 h of dark thermal ageing at 85 $^\circ$C. These results show that reconstructed scientific reasoning provides an effective basis for building domain models that interpret experiments and inform materials design.
  </details>

- **[To Jev or Not to Jev: When Can System-1 Decision Models Replace LLM Judges?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00505)**  `arXiv:2611.00505`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shreyas Subramanian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models such as TypeSafe's Jev answer typed questions about a text (yes/no, multiple choice, or an ordinal score) with calibrated probabilities in a single forward pass and without generating text, which makes them an inexpensive candidate for LLM-as-a-judge workloads. We evaluate 14 System-1 decision models, selected with a pre-stated protocol, against four generative judges (Claude Haiku 5.5, GPT-5.6 with and without reasoning, and Claude Opus 5.5 with thinking) on eleven established judge benchmarks. Five of the 14 are statistically indistinguishable from GPT-5.6 without reasoning on all five pairwise and best-of-4 benchmarks, and the two strongest, DReX-Max and d1-ultra, are significantly more accurate than Haiku on JudgeBench, LLMBar and RewardBench 2. Conditioned on a reference answer generated once per prompt, eleven models reach 90.3-93.4% on JudgeBench, statistically indistinguishable from Opus 5.5 and GPT-5.6 with reasoning, and on PPE best-of-16 every reference-guided System-1 judge matches GPT-5.6 with reasoning at roughly one-sixteenth of its cost per prompt. Several System-1 judges are better calibrated than every generative judge and correlate more strongly with human helpfulness ratings. Robustness to instructions injected into the judged response is a property of the individual model rather than of the System-1 design: an appended note to the evaluator reverses 0-5% of the correct verdicts of five models but 17-49% for the other nine. Two general System-1 leaderboards that contain no judging tasks predict judging accuracy (Pearson r = 0.95-0.96), a well-calibrated System-1 first stage matches Opus on LLMBar while escalating 17% of items, and same-size controls show that the correctness deficit of a 4B decision model is a reasoning gap rather than a size gap.
  </details>

- **[Few Trials, Many Metrics: Beta Mixtures for Low-Budget AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00474)**  `arXiv:2611.00474`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gustavo Tironi, Felipe Maia Polo, Diego Mesquita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating a stochastic AI system often requires many repeated samples per instance, especially for metrics defined over long horizons. Recent work has shown that modeling instance-level success probabilities with a Beta distribution recovers Pass@$k$ from far fewer samples than direct estimation. We show that this framework supports a broader range of evaluation targets. We fit a mixture of Beta distributions by marginal maximum likelihood, with the number of components chosen by BIC. From that one fit, we compute Pass$^k$ and AUC@$k$ at any horizon in closed form. We also derive delta-method confidence intervals for these metrics and for Pass@$k$, which require no additional model calls. Treating configurations rather than tasks as instances extends the same model to sensitivity analysis. For repeated success metrics, we find that mixture priors yield more accurate estimators than naive estimation across competition mathematics and graduate-level science benchmarks when extrapolating beyond the observed number of trials, and the resulting intervals cover the full-data reference. When prompts are treated as instances, it estimates the distribution of prompt sensitivity more accurately than a comparable item-response baseline without external covariates. Direct estimators remain preferable once trials reach the target horizon.
  </details>

- **[Compression Technique Interactions in Small Language Models: A Factorial Ablation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00464)**  `arXiv:2611.00464`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bhushit Nema, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Anjali Kadiyala_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying small language models on resource-constrained hardware requires compression, yet practitioners lack systematic guidance on how quantization, pruning, and post-compression recovery interact when combined. We present a factorial ablation study evaluating 72 compression configurations across four SLM architectures (2.7B-7B parameters), extended with a 48-configuration ordering analysis, benchmark accuracy validation on 30 compressed models, and robustness checks including multi-seed validation and full-corpus perplexity evaluation. INT8 quantization is effectively lossless (<0.5% accuracy degradation), while NF4 quantization combined with L1 magnitude pruning causes model collapse in all 16 tested combinations. LoRA-based fine-tuning reduces degraded perplexity by up to 97% after aggressive pruning; an ablation against standard fine-tuning without a teacher model shows the recovery is driven by LoRA adaptation itself rather than the distillation signal. Compression ordering shows no significant effect among viable configurations (ANOVA p = 0.96) but determines viability for NF4+pruning combinations. INT8 quantization alone provides 40-45% memory reduction with no quality loss. On-device CPU deployment viathis http URLconfirms these findings transfer off the GPU: 4-bit quantization runs 46-54% faster than 8-bit for a perplexity difference under 0.5 points, while un-structured pruning yields no CPU latency or memory benefit, consistent with its dense storage format.
  </details>

- **[Exo: An Architecture for Self-Improving Systems at Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00463)**  `arXiv:2611.00463`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alexander Krentsel, Sylvia Ratnasamy, Ion Stoica, Martin Casado, Ankur Goyal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses govern how language models act in an environment; designing their policies by hand is difficult, while automated techniques require representative datasets and costly executions. We present Exo, an agent runtime architecture for safe learning during ongoing work that collapses the optimization loop into task execution, allowing agents to revise both their harness and their strategies for further improvement. A minimal protected mechanism layer preserves execution history and governs policy activation while the harness evolves through inspectable code changes. On long-running objectives in an open-world game, Exo reduces model cost by 12-53% relative to Codex and Pi in standalone runs and 45-82% in transfer runs, while matching task completion rates. Compared with the same starting policy without the ability to edit policy, Exo reduces held-out model cost by 35-61% on AppWorld and SREGym with comparable correctness, cutting AppWorld's mean task time by 13% and SREGym's median diagnosis time by 35%. On Continual Learning Bench coding tasks, model cost including learning falls by 14%, with both policies resolving every issue.
  </details>

- **[Dependence-Aware Risk Assessment and Optimal Design of AI-Agent Safeguard Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00386)**  `arXiv:2611.00386`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijia Li, Maochao Xu, Peng Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on safeguards to prevent unauthorized actions while preserving legitimate service. However, existing risk assessments often focus on individual safeguard performance while overlooking dependence among safeguard decisions. This paper develops a dependence-aware framework for risk assessment and optimal design of multi-component AI safeguard systems, jointly selecting configurations and operating policies for sequential layered screening and selective verification. We establish stochastic comparisons of layered-screening error probabilities and invocation costs under lower-orthant ordering. For selective verification, we characterize exactly which policies have error probabilities and review costs that respond monotonically to stronger dependence under supermodular ordering. For a fixed first stage and reviewer, we show that the optimal routing map is a two-threshold rule in the first-stage likelihood ratio. An accompanying open-source software package, Dependence-Aware Policy Optimization Algorithms (DAPOA), facilitates the practical implementation of the proposed framework. Numerical experiments show that ignoring dependence can lead to inaccurate risk assessments and infeasible safeguard designs, with independence-based selections violating security requirements in six of eight scenarios. An empirical study using four LLM safeguards on benchmark-derived agent actions further demonstrates the practical implications of dependence modeling. Under the principal operating setting, dependence-aware selection lowers held-out total cost by 39.6% relative to independence-based selection.
  </details>

- **[Efficient NPN Classification for Large Functions using Graph Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00339)**  `arXiv:2611.00339`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengdi Cao, Hanyu Wang, Wan-Hsuan Lin, Jason Cong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two Boolean functions are considered NPN equivalent if one can be transformed into the other by negating inputs, permuting inputs, or negating the output. Efficient NPN classification is valuable in fields such as logic synthesis, quantum computing, and information theory. However, exact NPN classification demands exhaustive enumeration of all possible input permutations and negations. Its complexity grows exponentially, making it impractical for larger functions with more inputs. In this paper, we introduce the first learning-based formulation for NPN classification by recasting it as NPN action prediction. Our method represents Boolean functions as AND-Inverter Graphs (AIGs) and applies a bidirectional heterogeneous graph neural network to predict input negations, output negation, and input permutations. To mitigate the non-canonicity of logic networks, we further introduce graph rewriting techniques that reduce structural ambiguity and recover useful symmetries before prediction. Experimental results show that NPN action prediction is learnable, that the resulting canonicalization quality improves over heuristic baselines, and that on practical benchmarks our method approaches exact methods on tractable cases while scaling to functions with up to 16 inputs.
  </details>

- **[OmniPiano: Diverse Dexterous Piano-Playing Challenges for Standard, Robust, Safe, and Multi-Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00243)**  `arXiv:2611.00243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fan Xu, Fan Yang, Yifan Xu, Ruiyuan Zhang, Yimou Wu, Nuo Yi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous robotic manipulation remains a major challenge in robotics, exemplified by piano playing, which requires coordinated control of fingers and provides a demanding testbed for reinforcement learning (RL). Although previous work established a benchmark for robotic piano playing, it is restricted to two hands and does not support unified evaluation. To address this gap, we introduce OmniPiano, a benchmark that provides one to up to five Shadow Hands and supports standard, robust, safe, and multi-agent RL within a shared piano-playing task family. OmniPiano is developed for scalable and diverse piano-playing tasks with configurable perturbations, explicit safety constraints, and decentralized cooperation settings. Particularly, to facilitate usability and extendability, OmniPiano adopts a highly modular design and provides comprehensive tasks to support RL study of task performance, robustness, safety, and cooperation in dexterous control. With at least 912 task settings and 36 baseline algorithms, OmniPiano further incorporates LLM-based agents to broaden the evaluation scope and reveal new insights. Extensive evaluations show that state-of-the-art RL algorithms and frontier LLM-based agents still struggle on challenging piano-playing tasks, even under standard RL settings, highlighting substantial room for improvement in dexterous control. The code, dataset, tutorial, and demo are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RE-SciVQA: Improving Scientific VQA With Context-Relevant Retrieval and Lightweight MLLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00242)**  `arXiv:2611.00242`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sheikh Tahmim Mehdi, Muminul Islam Jami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A deep understanding of scientific literature often relies on interpreting figures that contain key information beyond the main text. Leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to extract insights from these figures can significantly accelerate knowledge discovery. Existing works on Scientific Visual Question Answering rely on resource-intensive training pipelines that are often inaccessible and require large computational resources. Instead of designing complex architectures, we propose RE- SciVQA, which focuses on improving the quality of retrieved context and fine-tuning small LLMs with parameter-efficient LoRA training. Using a simple query expansion step, relevant section headers are appended to the original question to improve the retrieval coverage. In addition, a simple adjustment of similarity scores is applied using figure references and caption alignment. On the SciVQA benchmark, RE-SciVQA achieves a ROUGE-1 F1 score of 73.9%, outperforming chart-focused models such as UniChart (14.7%), ChartGemma (6.9%), and Matcha-ChartQA (6.3%). Our relevance-enhanced RAG setup also improves the exact match by 11.9% and the fuzzy match by 29.5% compared with a standard RAG baseline, resulting in an overall gain of 14.3%. We also examine closed-ended infinite visual questions, a category in which chart-understanding models often struggle. Our experiments show that using plot digitization instead of synthetic captions leads to an 18.5% improvement for this question type.
  </details>

- **[Reimagining Text-to-SQL: A Survey of Large Language Model Architectures and Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00171)**  `arXiv:2611.00171`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shikhar Dave, Bhaskarjit Sarmah, Stefano Pasquali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems translate natural language questions into executable SQL queries, democratizing access to relational data. Early approaches relied on handcrafted rules and template matching, while subsequent neural methods introduced encoder-decoder architectures, schema linking, and constrained decoding to handle increasingly complex queries. The emergence of large language models (LLMs), exemplified by systems such as ReFoRCE, Agentar-Scale-SQL, Snowflake's Arctic-Text2SQL-R1, and the modular XiYan-SQL framework, has dramatically advanced the field, pushing execution accuracy to new state-of-the-art levels on challenging benchmarks such as Spider, Spider 2.0, and BIRD. In parallel, modular frameworks decompose the task into sub-problems (schema retrieval, sketch-based generation, repair, and ranking), while agentic systems coordinate multiple specialized agents that communicate and dynamically allocate sub-tasks. This survey systematically reviews these LLM-driven approaches within a unified taxonomy of five paradigms (in-context learning, supervised and parameter-efficient fine-tuning, agentic and tool-augmented orchestration, retrieval-augmented generation, and reinforcement learning) and characterizes them by model granularity (monolithic vs. modular), level of supervision (zero-shot, fine-tuned, or reinforcement-based), and reasoning style (direct decoding vs. decomposed multi-step reasoning). We further analyze evaluation metrics, datasets, and open challenges, including domain generalization, efficiency, and real-world deployment, and we examine the role of the semantic layer in production systems. As LLMs increasingly access sensitive databases, we also discuss responsible AI practices. We survey over 100 papers and technical resources, focusing on work published between 2022 and 2026.
  </details>

- **[Specification-Guided Generation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41511)**  `arXiv:2610.41511`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alessandro Previti, Konstantinos Vandikas, Mario Alviano, Mats Carlsson, Albin Larsson Forsberg, Alexandros Nikou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate structured outputs, but existing constrained-decoding methods mainly enforce syntax and cannot directly enforce satisfaction of a formal specification. We introduce a neuro-symbolic, specification-guided decoding framework that combines LLM generation with symbolic reasoning. At each step, the framework refines the decoding grammar by filtering out values that cannot be extended to an output satisfying the specification. We present a forward-only variant that guarantees specification satisfaction by construction, and a backtracking variant that revises earlier feasible choices when their downstream consequences leave only values that the LLM itself judges unsuitable. The framework is model-agnostic, requires no retraining, and builds on existing grammar-constrained decoding mechanisms. Experiments on configuration and constraint-solving benchmarks show that the forward-only variant reliably produces specification-compliant outputs while reducing repeated LLM inference compared with rejection sampling and incurring modest symbolic-reasoning overhead. The backtracking variant further reduces cases in which the model is forced to select values that it has itself judged unsuitable, thereby substantially improving adherence to the LLM's stated interpretation of the prompt.
  </details>

- **[Prototype-Guided Dynamic Domain Modulation and a Large-Scale Multi-SKU Benchmark for Shampoo Bottle Surface Defect Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38194)**  `arXiv:2610.38194`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuehang Li, Xiao Wang, Qiang Chen, Yangyi Fan, Bo Jiang, Yaowei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial shampoo-bottle surface defect detection must operate across many stock keeping units (SKUs), where differences in bottle geometry, material, label layout, reflectance, and imaging conditions can substantially alter the appearance of the same defect. Existing industrial defect datasets rarely provide both instance-level defect annotations and product-variant identities, making it difficult to study SKU-aware detection under realistic manufacturing conditions. We introduce Bottle-SKU, a large-scale multi-SKU benchmark for shampoo bottle surface defect detection. Bottle-SKU contains 13,119 images, 20,419 instance-level bounding boxes, seven defect categories, and 48 bottle SKUs, enabling evaluation of both shared defect recognition and SKU-specific appearance variation. To address the challenge of SKU-specific appearance variation, we propose Prototype-Guided Dynamic Domain Modulation (ProDMM). ProDMM constructs a visual prototype for each SKU from training images and predicts a soft SKU distribution for each input. The resulting prototype-conditioned representation dynamically generates low-rank Query/Value modulation for the visual backbone and residual conditioning for defect-text-to-visual attention, while preserving the pretrained Grounding DINO detection pathway. The model is trained jointly with detection, SKU-domain classification, and modulation-regularization objectives. Experiments on Bottle-SKU and the public GC10-DET-SKU metallic surface defect dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of prototype-guided domain conditioning for industrial surface defect detection. The source code and model will be released onthis https URL
  </details>

- **[PersonaScienceBench: Evaluating Scientific Agents with Diverse Simulated Researchers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32106)**  `arXiv:2610.32106`  `cs.AI`  
  _Siyang Wu, Weizhi Zhang, Chen Yang, Esther Xu Fei, Jorin Overwiening, Xu Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific agents collaborate with researchers whose expertise and preferences differ, but benchmarks usually hold the collaborator fixed. We introduce PersonaScienceBench: 200 tasks from five benchmarks across four scientific domains. Simulated researchers provide context and feedback according to persona profiles, while task objectives and grading remain fixed. Agents encounter researcher preferences only through conversation. Across eighteen modelconfigurations, task performance, fact recovery, and self-repetition reveal different strengths. Detailed GPT 6 Astra analyses show distinct agent responses across researcher profiles; interaction traces illustrate targeted clar ification and workflow revision. These behaviors can differ even when final scores match. PersonaScienceBench therefore evaluates how agents collaborate with diverse researchers alongside whether they complete the task.
  </details>

- **[PLE: Physical-Engineering's Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30537)**  `arXiv:2610.30537`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rujun Han, Yufan Zhuang, Arijit Ray, Han Yu, Zhongying CuiZhu, George Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language models now score above 90% on popular knowledge benchmarks and are increasingly deployed as agents that carry out multi-hour professional work. Yet no existing benchmark tests whether they can perform the real-world, economically valuable work of a professional physical engineer, such as modelling physics and legal code requirements across a multi-format project corpus, optimising material and installation costs, and issuing 2D/3D engineering design packages that a licensed reviewer would sign off on. Existing engineering benchmarks focus on textbook question answering, isolated tool calls or self-contained toy design problems. As a result, most of these benchmarks are already saturated by leading agents and solving them is far away from delivering real engineering design. We introduce PLE, a benchmark of 85 long-horizon, agentic tasks in professional physical engineering consisting of electrical, civil, structural and mechanical engineering, where solving a task means completing a realistic, billable engineering deliverable. PLE combines two complementary curation paths. Expert-Authored tasks are created end to end by practising engineers from a professional data provider's expert pool. Co-Authored tasks start from problem statements written by practising engineers; an adversarial Creator-Solver agent pipeline constructs each task's realistic project environment, executable grader and reference deliverables before sending them to expert engineers for validation and difficulty intensification. We evaluate a variety of frontier LLMs under seven publicly available agent harnesses. Even the strongest configuration reaches a mean score of only 50.6%, while spending 27M tokens and 63 minutes per task on average, demonstrating the substantial gap between current agents and autonomous physical engineering design.
  </details>

- **[REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26261)**  `arXiv:2610.26261`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanli Ma, Tianmeng Hu, Ke Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. We introduce REFLEX (RNA Ensemble generation calibrated by FLEXibility), a sequence-conditioned generator for RNA backbone-frame ensembles based on a heteroscedastic stochastic bridge on SE(3). During training, experimental B-factors calibrate residue-wise bridge widths, exposing each observed conformer through local perturbation neighborhoods with residue-specific scales while keeping constrained regions sharper. A learned flexibility condition guides the bridge-field network to recover target conformer-specific frames from these calibrated noisy states. At inference, REFLEX uses only the input sequence, requiring no MSA-subsampling heuristics. On a multi-conformer RNA benchmark, REFLEX achieves the best precision-recall trade-off for ensemble coverage across evaluation thresholds, while recovery-stress ablations indicate gains in target-frame recovery under stronger perturbations.
  </details>

- **[RSIFlow: Adaptive Self-Improvement Workflows with an Evolving Meta-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21791)**  `arXiv:2610.21791`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huan Zhang, Mingju Chen, Dongxu Zhou, Can Lv, Sen Cui, Faguo Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. Existing self-improvement methods enhance agent performance through model optimization, harness evolution, and executable artifact refinement, while recursive self-improvement (RSI) further extends optimization to the improvement mechanisms themselves. However, adapting self-improvement workflows to evolving performance bottlenecks while improving workflow orchestration through execution feedback remains a key challenge. We propose \textbf{RSIFlow}, a \textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{I}mprovement framework that integrates adaptive work\textbf{flow} orchestration with meta-agent capability evolution. RSIFlow diagnoses performance bottlenecks from task-agent trajectories and environmental feedback to dynamically select component-specific improvement workflows. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. Experiments across six benchmarks and two backbone models show that RSIFlow achieves the SOTA average performance on all benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of adaptive workflow orchestration and meta-harness evolution. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can the Library Speak? Self-Advertised Method Selection for Formal Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09401)**  `arXiv:2610.09401`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaopeng Yuan, Suijin Wang, Yanli Wang, Haibo Jin, Peng Kuang, Jerry Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based formal provers can retrieve relevant lemmas and prior proofs, but relevance alone does not say whether a mathematical method can be used on the current theorem. A method has prerequisites, a target, an intended action, and obligations that its use leaves to prove. Methods that look equally related to a theorem may therefore differ substantially in whether they offer a plausible next step. We formulate this as an applicability-aware method-selection problem and introduce self-advertisement: before candidates are ranked, a model generates a problem-specific proposal for each one, stating what part of the goal it targets, what action it would take, and what conditions that action requires. We organize 82 reusable methods from Putnam 2000-2014 as Method Contracts, which pair applicability descriptions with Mathlib anchors, a checked example or scaffold, and expected proof obligations. A single batched call elicits proposals across the library; vague or unsupported proposals are demoted, yielding a ranked shortlist accompanied by inspectable claims about each candidate's use. We analyze when similarity-based representations cannot distinguish methods with different applicability, how errors in applicability estimates affect shortlist quality, and what a checked scaffold guarantees under its stated assumptions. Against lexical, embedding, and embedding-plus-LLM reranking baselines, self-advertisement achieves 95.0% hit@5 on Putnam 2015-2025, compared with 84.2% for the strongest reranker. On IMO ProofBench, it achieves 91.7% compared with 88.3%. These results indicate improved coverage of annotated methods in the retrieved shortlists, particularly on Putnam.
  </details>

- **[From Scientific Observations to Mechanisms: Benchmarking Hypothesis Generation by AI Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05197)**  `arXiv:2610.05197`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaxun Xie, Qingqing Long, Meng Xiao, Wei Ju, Yuanchun Zhou, Hengshu Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-driven mechanistic hypotheses are essential to scientific discovery because they explain how underlying processes produce observed phenomena. AI agents and AI scientists increasingly support scientific data analysis. However, their ability to turn empirical findings into mechanistic hypotheses remains insufficiently examined. To address this gap, we introduce MechHypoBench, the first benchmark for evaluating whether AI agents and AI scientists can generate such hypotheses from empirical data. It combines paper-derived mechanisms from 14 scientific fields with real-world datasets containing 17.98 million records. The construction retains the observational complexity of empirical data while providing a specified underlying mechanism. Agents analyze the observations and propose open-form hypotheses. We develop an evaluation framework that assesses open-form mechanistic hypotheses through their consequences under withheld conditions. Experiments with general agents and AI scientists reveal a substantial gap between generated hypotheses and the underlying mechanisms.
  </details>

- **[STAGE: Stateful Translation to Agentic Graph Execution with Policy-Scoped Context and Deterministic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22538)**  `arXiv:2608.22538`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mengxi Luo, Changjia Chen, An Cao, Wanyi Dai, Zirong Huang, Shixiong Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise agents are expected to deliver correct and consistent outcomes while executing complex procedures, adhering to governing policies, and operating within practical resource budgets. We present \textsc{Stage}, an integrated execution framework combining typed task nodes, shared execution memory, document tools, and deterministic orchestration. We evaluate \textsc{Stage} on SOP-Bench Referral Abuse, the Retail and Telecom domains of $\tau^2$-bench, and two proprietary banking workflows, Smart Dispute and Paystub Fraud Detection. Using the same underlying model in each comparison, \textsc{Stage} improves single-run success and repeated-run reliability over the baseline in all 18 benchmark--model pairs. Success across all three runs (\(\mathrm{Pass}^{3}\)) increases by 7.5--66.0 percentage points. Fixed-graph ablations demonstrate the contributions of localized policy context and coordinator-owned routing. Further analysis of Smart Dispute shows improved coverage of required operations across procedure lengths. These findings show that integrating local reasoning, persistent state, and procedural control can improve reliable enterprise workflow completion without changing the underlying model.
  </details>

- **[Solver-Aware Decompositions for Programming-by-Example: When Dividing Requires Knowing How to Conquer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03461)**  `arXiv:2608.03461`  `cs.AI`  
  _Janis Zenkner, Tobias Sesterhenn, Tim Grams, Christian Bartelt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decomposition-based Programming-by-Example splits a task into subtasks: a decomposer predicts intermediate subgoals, and a learned synthesizer generates a program for each subgoal. Execution-decomposition approaches train the decomposer to imitate ground-truth (GT) subgoals and thereby treat decomposition quality as a property of the task alone.
